### [1] [Hyperbolic lattices with mass disorder: Phases and phase transitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02192)
*Sheersh Sen,Christopher A. Leong,Bitan Roy*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: Nearest-neighbor tight-binding models on three hyperbolic lattices (plaquette-centered {10,3}, {8,3}, and {8,4}) show vanishing, finite, and diverging zero-energy DOS (Dirac liquid, Fermi liquid, flat band). Under mass disorder, the Dirac liquid undergoes a semimetal-to-metal transition then an Anderson metal-to-insulator transition; the other two show only the Anderson transition. Critical exponents resemble those for on-site potential disorder.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To scrutinize how mass disorder impacts the electronic states and DOS of hyperbolic lattices that host distinct zero-energy DOS behaviors (Dirac liquid, Fermi liquid, flat band), exploiting their emergent bipartite nature within NN-TBMs.

Method: Extensive numerical calculations of average and typical DOS via the kernel polynomial method for NN-TBMs on plaquette-centered {10,3}, {8,3}, and {8,4} hyperbolic lattices on a Poincaré disk with open boundary conditions.

Result: The hyperbolic Dirac liquid remains stable against weak mass disorder, undergoes a semimetal-to-metal quantum phase transition at moderate disorder, then an Anderson metal-to-insulator transition at stronger disorder. The Fermi liquid and flat band systems display only the Anderson metal-to-insulator transition. Critical exponents near all these transitions are close to those mediated by on-site potential disorder.

Conclusion: Mass disorder drives a two-stage transition sequence in the hyperbolic Dirac liquid, while Fermi liquid and flat band systems exhibit only the Anderson transition; the critical exponents are near those induced by on-site potential disorder, indicating a degree of universality.

Abstract: Nearest-neighbor (NN) tight-binding models (TBMs) on plaquette-centered $\{ 10,3\}$ (Schläfli symbol), $\{ 8,3\}$, and $\{ 8,4\}$ hyperbolic lattices on a Poincaré disk with open boundary conditions display a vanishing, a finite, and a diverging density of states (DOS) near zero energy (band center), respectively, yielding a Dirac liquid, a Fermi liquid, and a flat band. Emergent bipartite nature of these lattices within the framework of NN-TBMs, allows us to scrutinize the impact of mass disorder on the electronic states and DOS therein. From extensive numerical calculations of the average and typical DOS using the kernel polynomial method, we show that hyperbolic Dirac liquid remains stable against weak mass disorder, undergoing a semimetal-to-metal quantum phase transition at moderate disorder, followed by an Anderson metal-to-insulator transition at even stronger disorder. The remaining two systems display only the latter transition. Critical exponents near all these transitions are found to be close to the ones mediated by on-site potential disorder.

</details>

### [2] [Walshness: an intrinsic neural-network representability metric for quantum states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00505)
*Nisarga Paul,Kehuang Chen,Jessica K. Jiang,Haimeng Zhao,Di Luo*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces "Walshness," a complexity metric quantifying whether a quantum many-body state admits a compact neural-network representation, proving that efficient NQS representability is equivalent to bounded Walshness, and providing an algorithm to find the optimal basis by minimizing a classical spin model's energy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding when neural networks can efficiently represent physical quantum states remains limited because of the nonlinear parameterization of NQS, the intricate sign structure of quantum states, and the sensitive dependence on basis choice. A rigorous, intrinsic complexity metric connecting quantum states to their neural-network representability is needed.

Method: The authors define Walshness as a complexity metric of a quantum state. They rigorously prove that a general quantum many-body state admits an efficient NQS representation if and only if it has bounded Walshness, for various NQS architectures. They also provide an algorithm that finds the optimal basis by minimizing the energy of a corresponding classical spin model.

Result: The proposed algorithm empirically improves the learnability of various physical quantum states, including ground states of the transverse-field Ising model and mixed-field toric code, often reducing infidelity by orders of magnitude. It also recovers and generalizes the basis choice given by the well-known Marshall sign rule in frustrated antiferromagnets.

Conclusion: The work provides an intrinsic complexity metric for quantum states with provable connections to their neural-network representability, offering both theoretical guarantees and practical basis-selection improvements.

Abstract: Neural quantum states (NQS) have emerged as powerful representations of quantum states with rapidly expanding applications across quantum many-body physics. Yet our understanding of when neural networks can efficiently represent physical quantum states remains limited, in part due to the nonlinear parameterization of NQS, the intricate sign structure of quantum states, and the sensitive dependence on basis choice. We introduce Walshness, a complexity metric of a quantum state which quantifies whether it admits a compact neural-network representation. We rigorously prove that a general quantum many-body state admits an efficient NQS representation if and only if it has bounded Walshness for various NQS architectures. We provide an algorithm for finding the optimal basis by minimizing the energy of a corresponding classical spin model. This allows us to empirically improve the learnability of various physical quantum states, including ground states of the transverse-field Ising model and mixed-field toric code, often reducing infidelity by orders of magnitude, and recovers and generalizes the basis choice given by the well-known Marshall sign rule in frustrated antiferromagnets. Our results provide an intrinsic complexity metric for quantum states with provable connections to their neural-network representability.

</details>


### [3] [Beyond Light Cones: State Preparation Complexity in Quantum Spin Glasses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02166)
*Omar Al-Ghattas,David Gamarnik,Bobak T Kiani*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a method for analyzing state preparation complexity in dense quantum p-spin Hamiltonians using "effective profile complexity" derived from the metric entropy of Pauli profiles, yielding circuit-complexity, depth-width, entanglement-depth, and MPS bond-dimension lower bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing complexity bounds for ground-state preparation rely only on circuit lightcones, which are insufficient for dense Hamiltonians where non-local structure or trailing Clifford layers (e.g., first-level reverse magic) can spread local observables across the system and defeat small-lightcone arguments.

Method: Define the class's effective profile complexity from the metric entropy of its Pauli profiles (expectations of all Pauli operators supported on exactly p qubits). Combine an adaptation of the nonsymmetric quantum de Finetti theorem of Berta et al. with Gaussian process entropy bounds to relate this complexity to ground-state energy separation and benchmarking against product states.

Result: Classes with uniformly bounded quadratic effective profile complexity stay separated from the ground-state energy by a positive multiple of sqrt(n) for large fixed p; subquadratic-complexity classes cannot beat a suitable benchmark class at leading order. Achieving near-ground-state energy requires Omega(n^2/log n) one- and two-qubit gates even with arbitrary discardable ancillas. The framework also yields depth-width tradeoffs, entanglement-depth and MPS bond-dimension lower bounds, and obstructions at every fixed level of Parham's magic hierarchy with total circuit width O(n). Clifford+T circuits with o(n) T-gates gain no leading-order energy advantage over product stabilizer states.

Conclusion: Effective profile complexity provides a lightcone-independent framework for proving state-preparation lower bounds in dense p-spin Hamiltonians, unifying gate-count, depth-width, entanglement-depth, MPS bond-dimension, and magic-hierarchy obstructions under a single entropy-based measure.

Abstract: We introduce a method for studying state preparation complexity in dense quantum $p$-spin Hamiltonians on $n$ qubits, going beyond bounds based only on circuit lightcones. The key input is the class's effective profile complexity, which is derived from the metric entropy of its Pauli profiles. These profiles record expectations of all Pauli operators supported on exactly $p$ qubits. Classes with uniformly bounded quadratic effective profile complexity remain separated from the ground-state energy by a positive multiple of $\sqrt n$ for sufficiently large fixed $p$. At subquadratic effective profile complexity, the class cannot outperform a suitable benchmark class at leading order, with product states providing a universal benchmark. The proof combines an adaptation of a nonsymmetric quantum de Finetti theorem of Berta et al. (arXiv:1810.12197) with Gaussian process entropy bounds.
  Applying this framework, we show that attaining near-ground-state energy requires $Ω(n^2/\log n)$ one- and two-qubit gates, even with arbitrary discardable ancillas. We also obtain depth-width tradeoffs, entanglement-depth and matrix product state bond-dimension lower bounds, and obstructions for both orientations at every fixed level of Parham's magic hierarchy (arXiv:2504.19966), with total circuit width $O(n)$. In first-level reverse magic, a shallow circuit is followed by an unrestricted Clifford circuit. The latter can spread local observables across the system, preventing a direct application of small-lightcone bounds. For this first-level class, our bounds also allow arbitrarily many clean ancillas at fixed shallow-circuit depth. A sharper benchmark shows that Clifford+$T$ circuits with $o(n)$ $T$-gates have no leading-order energy advantage over product stabilizer states, even with unrestricted Clifford operations and arbitrary discardable ancillas.

</details>


### [4] [Beyond Lie Algebras: Lie-Wedge Stratification and Pure-State Stabilizability in Single-Channel Qubit Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00046)
*Yibin Wang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Drift-free single-qubit systems with bidirectional Hamiltonian controls and at most one traceless dissipator fall into thirteen structural strata based on the generated algebra and its Lie-wedge geometry; these strata determine pure-state stabilizability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lie algebras describing control generator combinations erase the distinction between reversible control and irreversible dissipation in open systems. Lie wedges retain this information, enabling a more refined analysis of control capabilities.

Method: The authors study drift-free single-qubit systems with bidirectional Hamiltonian controls and at most one independently actuated dissipator generated by a traceless jump operator. They classify these systems into thirteen structural strata defined by the generated algebra and its Lie-wedge geometry, characterized by the control algebra and four channel properties.

Result: The systems fall into thirteen structural strata. In each wedge, reversible directions are precisely the coherent controls. In five strata, stabilizability (existence of a generator with a unique globally attractive pure state) depends on additional channel data. In one exceptional stratum, no single control-rotated dissipative direction suffices, but averaging two such directions yields stabilization.

Conclusion: Lie wedges reveal control capabilities that the generated Lie algebra misses, providing a finer characterization of open-system control and stabilization.

Abstract: Lie algebras describe how control generators combine, but in open systems they erase the distinction between reversible control and irreversible dissipation. Lie wedges retain this information. We study drift-free single-qubit systems with bidirectional Hamiltonian controls and at most one independently actuated dissipator generated by a traceless jump operator. In this work, we prove that they fall into thirteen structural strata defined by the generated algebra and its Lie-wedge geometry. The control algebra and four channel properties determine the stratum; in each wedge, the reversible directions are precisely the coherent controls. We then use the stratification to determine whether a wedge contains a generator with a unique globally attractive pure state. In five strata, stabilizability depends on additional channel data. In one exceptional stratum, no single control-rotated dissipative direction suffices, but averaging two such directions in the wedge yields stabilization. Lie wedges therefore reveal control capabilities that the generated Lie algebra misses.

</details>


### [5] [Sharp Target-Domain Certificates for Quantum-Kernel Advantage under Distribution Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00080)
*Roberto Fernández-Barrios,Iker Pastor-López,Asier González-Santocildes,Pablo García Bringas*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper derives an assumption-free sharp identified interval for the finite-batch predictive advantage of a fixed quantum candidate over the best member of a prespecified classical-kernel family, given partially/unaudited labels, and validates it via retrospective and prospective audits across security shifts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing quantum predictive-advantage claims under shift typically presuppose known target labels, which biases or invalidates comparisons. The authors seek an assumption-free characterization of advantage that remains valid when target labels are only partially available, and a principled way to separate genuine quantum advantage from protocol artifacts.

Method: They derive the sharp identified interval for the finite-batch advantage of a fixed candidate over the best member of a prespecified classical-kernel family under any bounded loss, with unrestricted completions of the unaudited labels. Zero-one accuracy admits exact closed-form partial-label updates. They evaluate across eight security shifts against 115 classical kernels, then apply retrospective auditing and prospective corroboration with predefined criteria and protocol controls.

Result: Across eight security shifts, zero-label upper endpoints against 115 classical kernels span 0.002-0.088. Retrospective auditing reduces every endpoint to 0.010 using 0-33 of 500 labels, including entangling-ZZ models. Prospectively, two eligible tasks met criteria: four task-classifier medians require 0-3 labels (overall median 0, maximum 76), and all 20 realized effects are negative; a third task fails its feature gate. Protocol controls reverse an apparent advantage, and finite-shot noise can raise predictive distinctness without yielding useful advantage.

Conclusion: The framework cleanly separates three distinct questions: protocol validity, target predictive indispensability, and quantum relevance. This separation prevents conflating statistical artifacts or invalid protocols with genuine quantum predictive advantage.

Abstract: Quantum predictive advantage under shift usually requires known target labels. We derive the assumption-free sharp identified interval for the finite-batch advantage of a fixed candidate over the best member of a prespecified fixed classical-kernel family under any bounded loss and unrestricted completions of the unaudited labels; zero-one accuracy has exact closed-form partial-label updates. Across eight security shifts, zero-label upper endpoints against 115 classical kernels span 0.002-0.088. Retrospective auditing reduces every endpoint to 0.010 with 0-33 of 500 labels, including entangling-ZZ models. Prospective corroboration on two eligible tasks met predefined criteria: four task-classifier medians require 0-3 labels (overall median 0; maximum 76), and all 20 realized effects are negative; a third task fails its feature gate. Protocol controls reverse an apparent advantage, while finite-shot noise can increase predictive distinctness without useful advantage. The framework separates protocol validity, target predictive indispensability, and quantum relevance.

</details>


### [6] [Exact Posterior Prediction from Product Haar Measurements and a Randomized-Mesh Maximum-Likelihood Bridge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00117)
*Naqueeb Ahmad Warsi,Ayanava Dasgupta,Masahito Hayashi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper analyzes Bayes-optimal prediction of one unmeasured copy of an unknown pure quantum state given copies measured by the one-copy Haar POVM, deriving exact permanent-based formulas for the posterior mean, proving minimaxity, and establishing relative-entropy guarantees with sample complexity scaling as (d/ε)log(d/ε) in the fixed-dimensional asymptotic regime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predicting an unmeasured copy of an unknown pure quantum state from independently measured copies is a fundamental quantum-inference problem. Under the one-copy Haar POVM with quantum relative-entropy loss, the Bayes predictive state is the full-rank posterior mean, but exact finite-sample evaluations and tight error guarantees are lacking. The work aims to characterize this fixed separable observation exactly and obtain sharp relative-entropy and posterior-purity bounds without relying on deterministic finite-sample uniqueness or a triangular mesh schedule.

Method: The posterior mean is evaluated exactly via permanents of minors of the outcome Gram matrix, with positivity and normalization proved and minimaxity established among decision rules for the fixed outcomes. A Hilbert-Schmidt projection of the posterior mean and an exactly analyzed linear-inversion competitor yield a finite-sample posterior-purity bound and an explicit relative-entropy guarantee. A random orientation on a finite projective mesh is fixed across the sample, and a selected finite-alphabet MLE in the resulting reference experiment connects the product data to a regular ray-valued model; a nested recovering mesh uniformly recovers the fine Fisher information.

Result: The permanent representation of the posterior mean is positive and normalized, and the posterior mean is minimax among rules based on the fixed outcomes. A finite-sample posterior-purity bound and a relative-entropy guarantee with sample count O((d^2/ε)log(d/ε)) are proved. Each sufficiently fine fixed mesh yields a regular finite-alphabet model whose selected MLE has Haar-averaged inverse-Fisher coefficient a_k, and refining the mesh makes a_k approach d-1, giving epsilon-optimal leading relative-entropy and posterior-overlap bounds with the sharper asymptotic scale (d/ε)log(d/ε). Entropy and posterior-purity comparisons with exact collective benchmarks are also obtained.

Conclusion: The Bayes predictive state for fixed one-copy Haar POVM outcomes admits an exact, provably positive and normalized permanent representation and is minimax among outcome-based decision rules. Finite-sample and asymptotic relative-entropy guarantees are established, with the refined fixed-mesh MLE achieving the sharper asymptotic rate (d/ε)log(d/ε). This rate is asymptotic and not claimed as a uniform finite-sample guarantee, and no deterministic finite-sample MLE uniqueness or triangular mesh schedule is required.

Abstract: We study prediction of one unmeasured copy of an unknown finite-dimensional pure quantum state after independently measuring the observed copies with the one-copy Haar POVM. For the resulting fixed separable observation, the Bayes predictive state under quantum relative-entropy loss is the full-rank posterior mean. We evaluate it exactly through permanents of minors of the outcome Gram matrix, prove positivity and normalization of the permanent representation, and establish minimaxity among decision rules based on these fixed outcomes. Using Hilbert--Schmidt projection of the posterior mean and an exactly analyzed linear-inversion competitor, we prove a finite-sample posterior-purity bound and an explicit relative-entropy guarantee with sample count $O((d^2/ε)\log(d/ε))$. Using the exact collective benchmarks proved in a companion paper, we also obtain entropy and posterior-purity comparisons. To connect the fine product data to a regular ray-valued experiment without assuming deterministic finite-sample uniqueness, we draw one random orientation for a finite projective mesh, retain it for the entire sample, and apply a selected finite-alphabet MLE in the resulting reference experiment. A nested recovering mesh uniformly recovers the fine Fisher information. Consequently every sufficiently fine fixed mesh gives a regular finite-alphabet model. Its selected MLE has Haar-averaged inverse-Fisher coefficient $\overline a_k$, and refining the mesh after the fixed-mesh large-sample limit makes $\overline a_k$ approach $d-1$. This yields epsilon-optimal leading relative-entropy and posterior-overlap bounds for the fine posterior and the sharper fixed-dimensional asymptotic scale $(d/ε)\log(d/ε)$. The latter is not asserted as a uniform finite-sample guarantee. No deterministic finite-sample MLE uniqueness or triangular mesh schedule is used.

</details>


### [7] [Global and nonlocal magic of quantum many-body scars](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00129)
*Lukas Ebner,Giovanni Cataldi,Kaidi Xu,Jad C. Halimeh,Emanuele Tirrito*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies nonstabilizerness (magic) as a diagnostic of quantum many-body scars in (1+1)D Abelian Z2 and U(1) lattice gauge theories, deriving an exact closed-form nonlocal trace-distance magic from the Schmidt spectrum and showing scars exhibit extensive global magic yet anomalously large nonlocal magic versus ergodic states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonergodic features of chaotic quantum many-body systems are usually characterized via local observables, fidelity, and entanglement entropy, which may miss genuinely quantum resources. The authors aim to use nonstabilizerness (magic), especially global and nonlocal measures, to expose scar structure beyond entanglement-based diagnostics.

Method: Analytical and numerical study of quantum many-body scars near infinite temperature in (1+1)-dimensional Abelian Z2 and U(1) lattice gauge theories. The authors derive an exact closed-form expression for nonlocal trace-distance magic from the Schmidt spectrum, reducing optimization over local unitaries to finite maximization over stabilizer-compatible Schmidt ranks, and analyze constrained Hilbert spaces and stabilizer-incompatible Schmidt ranks.

Result: Scar eigenstates can exhibit extensive global magic despite anomalously low entanglement, while retaining anomalously large nonlocal magic relative to ergodic states. Constrained Hilbert spaces can generate irreducible nonlocal magic through stabilizer-incompatible Schmidt ranks, even for a flat entanglement spectrum.

Conclusion: Magic, particularly nonlocal trace-distance magic, serves as a sensitive probe of quantum many-body scars and constrained dynamics, revealing nonergodic quantum resources that entanglement entropy alone does not capture.

Abstract: Nonergodic features of chaotic quantum many-body systems are commonly characterized through local observables, fidelity, and entanglement entropy. Here, using global and nonlocal measures of nonstabilizerness (magic), we study quantum many-body scars near infinite temperature in $(1+1)$-dimensional Abelian $\mathbb{Z}_2$ and U$(1)$ lattice gauge theories, both analytically and numerically. We derive an exact closed-form expression for the nonlocal trace distance magic solely from the Schmidt spectrum, reducing the optimization over local unitaries to a finite maximization over stabilizer-compatible Schmidt ranks. We find that scar eigenstates can exhibit extensive global magic despite their anomalously low entanglement, while retaining anomalously large nonlocal magic compared with ergodic states. We further show that constrained Hilbert spaces can generate irreducible nonlocal magic through stabilizer-incompatible Schmidt ranks, even for a flat entanglement spectrum.

</details>


### [8] [Stellar rank under the contraction of SU(1,1) to the Heisenberg-Weyl group](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00133)
*Chon-Fai Kam*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how the zero structure of Husimi functions of single-mode bosonic pure states behaves under the contraction of SU(1,1) to the Heisenberg-Weyl group, characterizing stellar rank via a weight sequence and various limit properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On the plane, the stellar rank (number of Husimi zeros) characterizes Gaussian states via vanishing rank, but on the Poincaré disk the zero set leaves a zero-free factor undetermined, so classification at fixed Bargmann index fails. The authors therefore shift to studying how the zero structure behaves in the contraction limit.

Method: The authors study the contraction of SU(1,1) to the Heisenberg-Weyl group, tracking how Husimi-function zeros redistribute on a single scale. The analysis is driven by a weight sequence w_k(m)=(2k)^m/(2k)_m whose monotonicity in k controls all estimates. They use normal-family and Segal-Bargmann space arguments, upper semicontinuity of the rank, and a two-sided Harnack estimate.

Result: Rescaled states form a normal family; every nonzero limit point lies in the Segal-Bargmann space, and those with finitely many zeros are exactly the finite-rank Fock states. Rank is upper semicontinuous, with equality precisely when all zeros scale as O(1/sqrt(2k)). A rank-r family degenerates iff the amplitudes on the lowest r+1 weight vectors tend to zero. Mass escapes at the edge of the rescaled disk via a first-order transition of the semiclassical Husimi potential at an explicit critical coupling. The contraction limit retains exactly the lowest 2r+3 amplitudes, and this number cannot be lowered.

Conclusion: The contraction limit provides a well-defined framework for understanding Husimi-zero structure, with the stellar rank governing Gaussianity, degeneration, and mass escape, and a sharp, non-improvable bound of 2r+3 retained amplitudes.

Abstract: Under the contraction of $SU(1,1)$ to the Heisenberg-Weyl group, the zeros of the Husimi function of a state redistribute according to a single scale, and we determine which of them survive. The stellar rank of a single-mode bosonic pure state, the number of these zeros, characterises the Gaussian states on the plane through the vanishing of the rank. On the Poincaré disk, where the same construction applies to the discrete series of $SU(1,1)$, it does not, because the zero set leaves a zero-free factor undetermined. We therefore ask how the zero structure behaves in the contraction limit rather than how it should be classified at fixed Bargmann index. The entire difference between the two Hilbert spaces reduces to one weight sequence $w_k(m)=(2k)^m/(2k)_m$, whose monotonicity in $k$ drives every estimate below. The rescaled states form a normal family, every nonzero limit point lies in the Segal-Bargmann space, and those with finitely many zeros are exactly the finite-rank Fock states. The rank is upper semicontinuous, with equality precisely when all zeros scale as $O(1/\sqrt{2k})$, the scale of the coherent-state labels. A two-sided Harnack estimate pins the profile on compact sets. Separately, a family of rank $r$ degenerates if and only if the amplitudes on the lowest $r+1$ weight vectors tend to zero. Mass escapes at the edge of the rescaled disk instead, by a first-order transition of the semiclassical Husimi potential at an explicit critical coupling. Finally, the contraction limit retains exactly the lowest $2r+3$ amplitudes, and this number cannot be lowered.

</details>


### [9] [Symmetry Discovery in Quantum Learning: Observable-Level and Task-Level Inference from Finite Measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00157)
*Zeyu Chen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops a finite-measurement theory for inferring the correct symmetry group of a quantum learning model from candidate transformations, characterizing observable-invisible transformations via stabilizers and Lie algebras, deriving an inverse-gap shadow statistic with optimal qubit scaling, and linking task validation to group-average asymmetry and a subsequent release decision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Symmetry reduces the capacity of a quantum learning model, but the imposed group must match both the measured information and the label transformation. When the symmetry is mis-specified relative to the probe and label structure, the model loses capacity or encodes the wrong invariance. The paper addresses the need to determine, from finite measurements, which symmetry group the data and task actually support.

Method: The authors establish a finite-measurement theory for inferring the group from candidate transformations. The central structural result identifies observable-invisible transformations with the stabilizer of a projected state when the probe span is invariant. Recovered generators are assembled into a valid subgroup, and the continuous invisible space is identified with its Lie algebra. For finite dictionaries, an unbiased shadow statistic distinguishes zero from positive squared expectation discrepancies with inverse-gap measurement rate. Task validation tests either the joint distribution via a characteristic kernel or its encoded mean via a classical-quantum discrepancy. An exact group-average identity relates the encoded mean to joint-state asymmetry, converted to binary task breaking mass. Projection bias quantifies excessive symmetry cost, an ℓ1 readout bound quantifies capacity gained by relaxing symmetry, and Fisher-orthogonality is shown at an invariant pure-state backbone.

Result: Observable-invisible transformations coincide with the stabilizer of a projected state under invariant probe span; recovered generators form a valid subgroup with invisible space given by its Lie algebra. The unbiased shadow statistic attains an inverse-gap measurement rate, improving the inverse-square-gap rate of uniform discrepancy estimation. A commuting qubit lower bound proves the gap dependence optimal at fixed snapshot scale, and simultaneous intervals support data-dependent tolerances. The group-average identity relates encoded mean to joint-state asymmetry and specifies its conversion to binary task breaking mass, while projection bias and the ℓ1 readout bound quantify symmetry over- and under-specification. Retained and nontrivial breaking sectors are Fisher-orthogonal at an invariant pure-state backbone. Ising-chain calculations connect finite-shot recovery, label-dependent symmetry, and physical sector drift.

Conclusion: The results determine which symmetry the measurements support and provide the statistical and geometric basis for a subsequent release decision.

Abstract: Symmetry reduces the capacity of a quantum learning model, but the imposed group must match both the measured information and the label transformation. We establish a finite-measurement theory for inferring this group from candidate transformations. The central structural result identifies observable-invisible transformations with the stabilizer of a projected state whenever the probe span is invariant. It turns recovered generators into a valid subgroup and identifies the continuous invisible space with its Lie algebra. For finite dictionaries, an unbiased shadow statistic distinguishes zero from positive squared expectation discrepancies with an inverse-gap measurement rate, improving the inverse-square-gap rate of uniform discrepancy estimation. A commuting qubit lower bound proves the gap dependence optimal at fixed snapshot scale, and simultaneous intervals support data-dependent tolerances. Task validation then tests either the joint distribution through a characteristic kernel or its encoded mean through a classical--quantum discrepancy. An exact group-average identity relates the latter to joint-state asymmetry and specifies its conversion to binary task breaking mass. Projection bias quantifies the cost of excessive symmetry, while an $\ell_1$ readout bound quantifies the capacity gained by relaxing it. At an invariant pure-state backbone, retained and nontrivial breaking sectors are Fisher-orthogonal. Ising-chain calculations connect finite-shot recovery, label-dependent symmetry, and physical sector drift. These results determine which symmetry the measurements support and provide the statistical and geometric basis for a subsequent release decision.

</details>


### [10] [Projector Form of the Quantum Brachistochrone and Its Relation to Two-Boundary Quantum Algorithm Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00169)
*Masayuki Ohzeki*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper establishes a link between the time-optimal quantum evolution framework of Carlini et al. and the coherent two-boundary optimal-control approach recently introduced for quantum algorithm design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two distinct formalisms exist for optimal quantum evolution: the time-optimal quantum evolution of Carlini et al. and the coherent two-boundary optimal-control formulation for quantum algorithm design. Their precise relationship is unclear, motivating the need for clarification.

Method: The authors analyze and compare the two formulations, examining how the time-optimal quantum evolution framework relates to the coherent two-boundary optimal-control formulation.

Result: A clarified relationship between the time-optimal quantum evolution of Carlini et al. and the coherent two-boundary optimal-control formulation for quantum algorithm design is established.

Conclusion: The two approaches are shown to be connected, providing a unified understanding useful for quantum algorithm design.

Abstract: We clarify the relation between the time-optimal quantum evolution of Carlini et al. and the coherent two-boundary optimal-control formulation recently proposed for quantum algorithm design.

</details>


### [11] [Entanglement cost of quantum depolarization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00187)
*Kun Fang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work determines the exact entanglement cost of every qubit isotropic state and every two-qubit Bell-diagonal state, using tuned qubit semigroups to convert log-Sobolev entropy bounds into supporting lines of Wootters' function, extended to many copies via a tensorization theorem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Entanglement cost is defined asymptotically and regularized, making exact evaluation difficult even for isotropic (depolarized maximally entangled) states. The paper aims to resolve this long-standing evaluation problem.

Method: The proof uses a family of suitably tuned qubit semigroups to transform a general log-Sobolev entropy bound into supporting lines of Wootters' function. A recent exact tensorization theorem of Dong et al. extends these bounds to arbitrarily many copies, giving a general lower bound on entanglement cost. For qudit isotropic states, a general lower bound is derived from the full depolarizing 2-to-3 norm.

Result: The exact entanglement cost is determined for all qubit isotropic states and all two-qubit Bell-diagonal states. Matching the lower bound with entanglement of formation yields the exact cost for a broader class of two-qubit states. For qudit isotropic states, the bound substantially improves on the PPT-relative entropy of entanglement and nearly matches entanglement of formation, leaving a gap of at most 2% of log d that vanishes as d grows.

Conclusion: Exact qubit results and qudit bounds extend to the entanglement cost of preparing quantum depolarizing channels under both parallel and sequential strategies.

Abstract: Entanglement cost is the asymptotic rate of Bell pairs required to prepare a quantum state. Its regularized definition has made exact evaluation difficult, even for isotropic states (i.e., depolarized maximally entangled states). In this work, we determine the entanglement cost of every qubit isotropic state and, more generally, every two-qubit Bell-diagonal state. Our proof uses a family of suitably tuned qubit semigroups to transform a general log-Sobolev entropy bound into supporting lines of Wootters' function. A recent exact tensorization theorem of Dong et al. [arXiv:2606.17729] extends these bounds to arbitrarily many copies, yielding a general lower bound on entanglement cost. Matching this bound with the entanglement of formation also determines the exact cost for a broader class of two-qubit states. For qudit isotropic states, we derive another general lower bound from the full depolarizing $2\to 3$ norm. This bound substantially improves on the PPT-relative entropy of entanglement and nearly matches the entanglement of formation, leaving a gap of at most $2\%$ of $\log d$ that vanishes as $d$ grows. Finally, the exact qubit results and qudit bounds extend to the entanglement cost of preparing quantum depolarizing channels under both parallel and sequential strategies.

</details>


### [12] [Simultaneous Perturbation as a Spectral Filter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00201)
*Masayuki Ohzeki*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how SPSA's finite-width Rademacher random perturbation interacts with the structured Fourier landscapes of parameterized quantum circuits, deriving the exact mode-wise estimator response, its variance, a Fokker-Planck description of the stochastic dynamics, and showing the non-conservative mean field accelerates convergence to the steady state and global minimum.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parameterized quantum circuits require repeated measurements to estimate objective gradients, making the number of circuit evaluations a central resource. SPSA is widely used because it estimates the full gradient from only two cost evaluations, independent of the number of parameters, but the effect of its finite-width random perturbation on the structured Fourier landscapes of quantum circuits is not well understood.

Method: The authors analyze the random perturbation for structured Fourier landscapes generated by quantum circuits. For a finite update width, they derive the exact mode-by-mode response of the mean Rademacher estimator, showing selective suppression of modes involving many parameters. For general generators, they show the filtered mean field need not be conservative, derive its sampling variance, compare it with Gaussian random-direction smoothing, and formulate the stochastic dynamics via a Fokker-Planck equation.

Result: The finite-width Rademacher estimator selectively suppresses high-order Fourier modes involving many parameters. The filtered mean field is generally non-conservative, and its sampling variance and comparison with Gaussian random-direction smoothing are derived. The non-conservative field yields acceleration toward the steady state and the global minimum of the cost function by gradually decreasing the SPSA parameters.

Conclusion: SPSA's finite-width random perturbation acts as a structured mode filter on quantum-circuit Fourier landscapes, producing a non-conservative mean field whose dynamics—captured by a Fokker-Planck equation—accelerate convergence to the steady state and the global minimum as the SPSA parameters are gradually decreased.

Abstract: Parameterized quantum circuits require repeated measurements to estimate objective gradients, making the number of circuit evaluations a central resource. Simultaneous perturbation stochastic approximation (SPSA) is widely used because it obtains a full gradient estimate from only two cost evaluations, independently of the number of parameters. We analyze the random perturbation for the structured Fourier landscapes generated by quantum circuits. For a finite update width, we derive the exact mode-by-mode response of the mean Rademacher estimator and show that it selectively suppresses modes involving many parameters. For general generators, the filtered mean field need not be conservative. We derive its sampling variance, compare it with Gaussian random-direction smoothing, and formulate the resulting stochastic dynamics through a Fokker--Planck equation. The non-conservative field generates acceleration to the steady state and the global minimum of the cost function by gradually decreasing the parameters in SPSA.

</details>


### [13] [Minimax Quantum State Tomography with Periodic Clifford Measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00210)
*Hongru Zhao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper characterizes the minimax expected trace norm risk of quantum state tomography over polynomial spectral decay classes using randomized nonadaptive single-copy measurements, showing that two estimators (operator norm minimum distance and measurement-weighted projected least squares) achieve minimax rates adaptively, with logarithmic circuit depth under a periodic Clifford ensemble.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum state tomography underpins state preparation characterization and measurement prediction, yet existing guarantees are often non-adaptive and limited in scope. The authors seek minimax optimal, adaptive estimators over realistic spectral decay classes that require no prior knowledge of the state's rank, eigenbasis, or spectral structure, while remaining efficiently implementable.

Method: The analysis uses randomized nonadaptive single-copy measurements from a two-layer periodic Clifford ensemble under an explicit sufficient block size condition. Two estimators are studied: operator norm minimum distance and measurement-weighted projected least squares. The core technical tool is a sharp (in dimension) uniform fourth-moment bound on projectors for the periodic Clifford ensemble, valid for arbitrary test states including entangled-across-blocks states. Covariance concentration and positivity give adaptive oracle bounds, and a shared rotation information argument on spectral tail packings yields matching lower bounds. Polynomial-time reconstruction algorithms, numerical comparisons, and Lean formalizations are provided.

Result: Statewise expected trace norm oracle inequalities are established with bounds depending on each state's actual spectral tail, requiring no prior structural knowledge. Both estimators attain minimax expected trace norm rates up to constants over polynomial spectral decay classes, and consequently achieve the minimax rate over every fixed-rank constrained class while remaining adaptive. For admissible logarithmic block sizes, the guarantees hold with logarithmic elementary gate depth.

Conclusion: The work provides minimax-optimal, adaptive quantum state tomography for polynomial spectral decay classes via periodic Clifford measurements with logarithmic circuit depth, supported by sharp fourth-moment bounds, matching lower bounds, efficient algorithms, numerical evidence, and formalized proofs.

Abstract: Quantum state tomography provides a foundation for characterizing state preparation and predicting measurement outcomes. We characterize the minimax expected trace norm risk of quantum state tomography over classes with polynomial spectral decay under randomized nonadaptive single copy measurements. Under an explicit sufficient block size condition for the two layer periodic Clifford ensemble, we establish statewise expected trace norm oracle inequalities for operator norm minimum distance and measurement weighted projected least squares, with bounds that depend on each state's actual spectral tail, while the estimators require no prior knowledge of the state's structural characteristics, including its rank, eigenbasis, or spectral structure. Over these polynomial spectral decay classes, both estimators attain the minimax expected trace norm rates up to constants. As a direct consequence, the same estimators attain the minimax rate over every fixed rank constrained class, while remaining adaptive to the unknown structural characteristics of the state. For admissible logarithmic block sizes, the periodic Clifford measurements attain these guarantees with logarithmic elementary gate depth. The main technical ingredient is a uniform bound on the fourth moment of projectors for the periodic Clifford ensemble. The bound is sharp in dimension and holds for arbitrary test states, including those entangled across blocks. Covariance concentration and positivity yield adaptive oracle bounds, while a shared rotation information argument on spectral tail packings establishes matching lower bounds. We also provide polynomial time reconstruction algorithms, numerical comparisons, and Lean formalizations of all labeled theoretical results.

</details>


### [14] [Physical-Work Fluctuation Relations from Accessible Quantum Macrostates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00246)
*Borhan Ahmadi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Coarse thermodynamic information measured at the nonequilibrium endpoint selects one member of an exact family of fluctuation relations, providing a statistically exact control on the standard Jarzynski estimator and lowering the finite-confidence sampling cost, as demonstrated in a finite Bose-Hubbard system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Jarzynski's equality relates nonequilibrium work to equilibrium free-energy differences, but its exponential average converges slowly because rare trajectories dominate the average, creating a heavy sampling burden.

Method: The authors use endpoint mean energy together with a coarse spatial record to define a maximum-entropy state, which selects one specific member from an exact family of fluctuation relations. This member acts as an exact statistical control for the ordinary Jarzynski estimator, and remains valid even when final energy and retained record do not commute.

Result: In a finite Bose-Hubbard system, the proposed control substantially reduces the finite-confidence sampling cost. Additionally, a small controlled departure from exact endpoint matching yields a square-root information-sampling tradeoff. The method preserves the same microscopic trajectories and free-energy target.

Conclusion: Coarse endpoint thermodynamic information can reduce the sampling cost of Jarzynski-based free-energy estimation without altering the underlying trajectories or target, via a physically selected fluctuation relation acting as an exact control.

Abstract: Jarzynski's equality recovers an equilibrium free-energy difference from nonequilibrium work trajectories, but its exponential average can converge very slowly because rare trajectories carry large weight. We show that coarse thermodynamic information measured at the nonequilibrium endpoint can reduce this sampling burden while keeping the same microscopic trajectories and the same free-energy target. The endpoint mean energy and a coarse spatial record define a maximum-entropy state and select one member of an exact family of fluctuation relations. That physically selected member provides an exact statistical control for the ordinary Jarzynski estimator, even when the final energy and retained record do not commute. In a finite Bose--Hubbard system, this control substantially lowers the finite-confidence sampling cost, while a small controlled departure from exact endpoint matching produces a square-root information--sampling tradeoff.

</details>


### [15] [Exact replica-sector hierarchy of multi-resolvent correlations in random free fermions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00261)
*Zhiqiang Huang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper constructs an exact replica-sector decomposition organizing higher-order connected cumulants of channel-resolved spectral functions in random free fermions, providing a microscopic benchmark for higher-order eigenstate thermalization fluctuations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The spectral weight of a many-body eigenstate in a given channel is not captured by the smooth one-point ETH envelope, and the higher-order correlations—connected cumulants of three or more channel-resolved spectral functions—lacked a systematic organization. This work aims to supply that missing organization.

Method: Using random free fermions where each channel overlap is a squared Slater minor of a Haar-orthogonal one-body eigenvector matrix, the r-resolvent moment is shown to admit an exact replica-sector decomposition—a sum over double-coset sectors of the orthogonal Weingarten algebra, with permutation sectors as replica contraction classes. Sector coefficients are obtained via an exact occupancy-matrix assembly: a signed convolution over slot permutations, decomposition into connected blocks, and a finite-state chain transfer matrix with local transition rules independent of r and k.

Result: The sector theorem holds at arbitrary finite multiplicity and is realized exactly at r=3 (k=2,3,4), r=4 and r=5 (k=2). The structure reproduces the physical hierarchy: the fully coincident sector projects to Selberg moment ratios q_r, levels are nested by exact marginalization, and sectors correspond to multi-energy spectral cumulants behind resolvent cumulants, with irreducible self-energy vertices of the Feshbach ladder as the connected kernel.

Conclusion: Multi-resolvent correlations of random free fermions are organized level by level by a single replica-sector algebra, offering an exact microscopic benchmark for the higher-order fluctuation sector of eigenstate thermalization with explicit microscopic sector geometry.

Abstract: The spectral weight with which a many-body eigenstate contributes to a given channel is not fixed by the smooth one-point ETH envelope, and its higher-order correlations---the connected cumulants of three and more channel-resolved spectral functions---have lacked a systematic organization. We construct one. In random free fermions, where every channel overlap is a squared Slater minor of a Haar-orthogonal one-body eigenvector matrix, the $r$-resolvent moment admits an exact replica-sector decomposition: a sum over the double-coset sectors of the orthogonal Weingarten algebra, in which the permutation sectors are the replica contraction classes and each sector value is determined by the overlap pattern of the channels and of the eigenstates. The sector coefficients follow from an exact occupancy-matrix assembly---a signed convolution over the slot permutations, a decomposition into connected blocks, and a finite-state chain transfer matrix whose local transition rules are independent of $r$ and $k$---so the sector theorem holds at arbitrary finite multiplicity, realized exactly at $r=3$ ($k=2,3,4$), $r=4$ and $r=5$ ($k=2$). The structure carries the physical hierarchy: the fully coincident sector is the projection that yields the Selberg moment ratios $q_r$, the levels are nested by exact marginalization, and the sectors are the multi-energy spectral cumulants behind the resolvent cumulants, with the irreducible self-energy vertices of the Feshbach ladder as the connected kernel. The multi-resolvent correlations of random free fermions are therefore organized, level by level, by one replica-sector algebra---an exact microscopic benchmark for the higher-order fluctuation sector of eigenstate thermalization, with its microscopic sector geometry made explicit.

</details>


### [16] [Exact Critical Curve for Uniform Stabilizer-State Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00266)
*Masahito Hayashi,Yimin Lu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper resolves the exact critical crossover for identifying an unknown pure n-qubit stabilizer state from a uniform ensemble under arbitrary collective measurements without side information, showing that at k=n+c the optimal success probability converges to an explicit positive series P∞(c), with P∞(0)=0.1760837896…, quadratic-exponential decay below threshold, and 1−P∞(c)∼2^−c above threshold.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The sample complexity of identifying an unknown pure stabilizer state has been a longstanding open problem in quantum information. Prior work lacked an exact characterization of the critical copy number and the precise achievable success probability in the n+O(1) regime.

Method: The authors analyze the uniform ensemble of pure n-qubit stabilizer states under arbitrary collective measurements without side information. They prove that the pretty-good measurement is exactly optimal for every finite n and k, and diagonalize the stabilizer Gram operator using Clifford tensor-power duality together with a defect filtration, including the exceptional binary sectors.

Result: For k=n+c with fixed integer c, the optimal success probability converges to an explicit positive series P∞(c). In particular, P∞(0)=0.1760837896…, the two tails obey a quadratic-exponential law below threshold, and 1−P∞(c)∼2^−c above threshold. The resulting integer-scale law quantifies the n+O(1) copy threshold.

Conclusion: The exact critical crossover for stabilizer-state identification is fully resolved, establishing the n+O(1) copy threshold, the optimality of the pretty-good measurement, and explicit asymptotic success probabilities governed by an integer-scale law.

Abstract: The sample complexity of identifying an unknown pure stabilizer state has been a longstanding problem in quantum information. In this paper, we resolve the entire exact critical crossover for the uniform ensemble of pure $n$-qubit stabilizer states under arbitrary collective measurements and without side information. For $k=n+c$, with fixed integer $c$, the optimal success probability converges to an explicit positive series $P_{\infty}(c)$. In particular, $P_{\infty}(0)=0.1760837896\ldots$, while its two tails obey a quadratic-exponential law below threshold and $1-P_{\infty}(c)\sim2^{-c}$ above threshold. The pretty-good measurement is exactly optimal for every finite $n$ and $k$. The proof diagonalizes the stabilizer Gram operator through Clifford tensor-power duality and a defect filtration, including the exceptional binary sectors. The resulting integer-scale law quantifies the $n+O(1)$ copy threshold.

</details>


### [17] [A Frame-Spread Lower Bound for Quantum Entropy Estimation under Fixed Rank-One Measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00270)
*Xinyu Song*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Estimating von Neumann entropy from a fixed rank-one measurement requires Ω(d²) samples for constant accuracy when frame spread is bounded; minimax MSE is at least c₁log²{d²/(κ_ν n)} below n ≈ c₂d²/κ_ν.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work shows bounded-risk entropy tomography needs O(d³log³d) observations for finite equal-weight exact designs and the Haar covariant measurement. There is a gap: the intermediate minimax regime is open, and lower bounds for general fixed rank-one measurements (beyond designs) were not established.

Method: Analyze minimax mean-squared error for estimating von Neumann entropy of a d-dimensional quantum state from n independent outcomes of a fixed rank-one measurement. Introduce the normalized trace-zero frame spread κ_ν ∈ [1, d+1], which equals 1 for exact complex projective 2-designs. Derive a lower bound on the minimax MSE in terms of d, κ_ν, and n.

Result: For any fixed rank-one measurement and n ≤ c₂d²/κ_ν, minimax MSE ≥ c₁log²{d²/(κ_ν n)}. For bounded frame spread and every fixed 0<a<2, minimax risk is of order log²d for all n ≲ d^{2-a}. Consequently, fixed uniform accuracy demands Ω(d²) observations. Existing upper bounds give bounded risk from O(d³log³d) observations for finite equal-weight exact designs and Haar covariant measurement.

Conclusion: A fundamental Ω(d²) sample lower bound holds for entropy estimation with fixed rank-one measurements of bounded frame spread, sharply separating this sample-cost regime from the O(d³log³d) achievable upper bounds and leaving the intermediate minimax regime open. Results apply only to fixed measurements repeated independently; adaptive, collective, and common-randomness-reusing protocols are excluded.

Abstract: We study estimation of the von Neumann entropy of a $d$-dimensional quantum state from independent outcomes of a fixed rank-one measurement. Let $κ_ν\in[1,d+1]$ denote the normalized trace-zero frame spread; $κ_ν=1$ for exact complex projective $2$-designs. For any fixed rank-one measurement and $n\le c_2d^2/κ_ν$, the minimax mean-squared error is at least $c_1\log^2\{d^2/(κ_νn)\}$. For bounded frame spread and every fixed $0<a<2$, the minimax risk is of order $\log^2 d$ throughout $n\lesssim d^{2-a}$; hence fixed uniform accuracy requires $Ω(d^2)$ observations. Existing tomography bounds give bounded risk from $O(d^3\log^3 d)$ observations for finite equal-weight exact designs and the Haar covariant measurement, while the intermediate minimax regime remains open. The results concern a fixed measurement repeated independently across copies; adaptive and collective protocols, and protocols that reuse common randomness across copies, are not covered.

</details>


### [18] [Frame dependence of Kochen-Specker contextuality for relativistic spin systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00274)
*Jonte R. Hance,Ruben Campos Delgado*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Kochen-Specker contextuality is Lorentz invariant on the full Hilbert space but not when restricted to spin; momentum acts as an environment that applies momentum-dependent Wigner rotations, degrading spin observables and breaking contextuality operator identities, with quantified thresholds for several contextuality/nonlocality witnesses and a q-plate implementation that makes key results testable without motion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper asks whether Kochen-Specker contextuality is Lorentz invariant. On the full Hilbert space, boosts act unitarily, so contextuality is preserved; however, physically relevant spin-only descriptions may not inherit this invariance, raising the question of whether contextuality and related nonlocality certificates survive relativistic boosts when momentum is traced out.

Method: The authors analyze boosts restricted to spin, treating momentum as an environment that applies momentum-dependent Wigner rotations. They derive the degradation of spin observables and the breaking of operator identities underlying state-independent contextuality. For spin 1/2, they identify a scalar loss parameter Δ, the packet average of sin²(θ_W/2), where θ_W is the Wigner angle; for general spin, they relate the degradation rate of a ray to its transverse spin variance. They evaluate this channel on specific contextuality and nonlocality tests: the Free Will Theorem's SPIN axiom, the Yu-Oh set, the Peres-Mermin square, singlet CHSH, and a GHZ-state OR gate. They also propose a q-plate implementation on photon polarization, with retardation replacing rapidity.

Result: In the spin-only account, the Free Will Theorem's SPIN axiom fails for every Δ>0. The Yu-Oh set loses contextuality between Δ≈0.008 and 0.016, with state independence lost first. The Peres-Mermin square loses contextuality at Δ≈0.059 or 0.074 depending on orientation. Singlet CHSH loses nonlocality for Δ≥0.159. A GHZ-state OR gate loses Raussendorf's contextuality certificate at Δ≈0.206. A q-plate implements the same channel exactly on photon polarization, so the nonlocality, GHZ, and aimed-source results are testable without motion.

Conclusion: Kochen-Specker contextuality is Lorentz invariant only in the full Hilbert-space description; restricted to spin, momentum-induced Wigner rotations break the relevant operator identities and degrade contextuality and nonlocality. The loss is quantified by Δ (spin 1/2) or transverse spin variance (general spin), with specific thresholds for well-known tests. Because a q-plate realizes the channel on photon polarization, several predicted effects can be experimentally tested without requiring actual motion.

Abstract: We ask whether Kochen-Specker contextuality is Lorentz invariant. On the full Hilbert space, it is, since boosts act unitarily. Restricted to spin, it is not: momentum acts as an environment applying momentum-dependent Wigner rotations, leaving spin observables unsharp and breaking the operator identities behind state-independent contextuality. For spin $1/2$ one scalar governs the loss, the packet average $Δ$ of $\sin^2(θ_W/2)$ (where $θ_W$ is the Wigner angle); for any spin, a ray degrades at a rate set by its spin variance transverse to the boost. In the spin-only account, the Free Will Theorem's SPIN axiom fails for every $Δ>0$; the Yu-Oh set loses contextuality between $Δ\approx0.008$ and $0.016$, with state independence going first; the Peres-Mermin square loses contextuality at $Δ\approx0.059$ or $0.074$ by orientation; singlet CHSH loses nonlocality for $Δ\geq0.159$; and a GHZ-state OR gate loses Raussendorf's contextuality certificate at $Δ\approx0.206$. A $q$-plate implements the channel exactly on photon polarisation, with a retardation in place of the rapidity, so the nonlocality, GHZ and aimed-source results are testable without motion.

</details>


### [19] [Nonlinear collective-spin dynamics for quantum-enhanced sensing in solid-state platforms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00286)
*Le Bin Ho*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This tutorial presents a materials-oriented framework for understanding collective spin sensing via nonlinear collective-spin dynamics, linking phase-space reshaping of spin states to microscopic interactions in solid-state platforms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Collective spin systems in solid-state materials offer a route to quantum-enhanced sensing beyond the standard quantum limit, but a unified, intuitive understanding connecting idealized collective dynamics to realistic material properties is needed.

Method: The authors develop a conceptual framework based on nonlinear collective-spin dynamics, using phase-space representations to show how nonlinear interactions reshape collective spin states, redistribute quantum fluctuations, and generate metrologically useful states such as spin-squeezed and entangled states. They then relate these ideal dynamics to microscopic interactions and real solid-state systems.

Result: The framework explains how interaction strength, disorder, decoherence, and experimental control influence sensing performance, and enables comparison of platforms such as nitrogen-vacancy centers in diamond, donor spins in silicon, and magnetic materials in terms of key material properties.

Conclusion: The tutorial provides a materials-oriented understanding of nonlinear collective-spin sensing rather than a comprehensive platform comparison or practical sensing guide, highlighting material properties, advantages, and limitations across solid-state spin platforms.

Abstract: Collective spin systems in solid-state materials are promising platforms for quantum-enhanced sensing. In systems such as nitrogen-vacancy centers in diamond, donor spins in silicon, and magnetic materials, interactions among many spins can generate collective quantum correlations that improve measurement precision beyond the standard quantum limit. This tutorial develops an intuitive, materials-oriented framework for understanding these effects through nonlinear collective-spin dynamics. We show how nonlinear interactions reshape collective spin states in phase space, redistribute quantum fluctuations, and generate metrologically useful states such as spin-squeezed and entangled states. We then connect these ideal collective dynamics to microscopic interactions and realistic solid-state systems, discussing how interaction strength, disorder, decoherence, and experimental control affect sensing performance. Different platforms and experiments are compared to highlight key material properties, advantages, and limitations. The tutorial focuses on a materials-oriented understanding of nonlinear collective-spin sensing, rather than a comprehensive comparison of experimental platforms or a practical guide to sensing experiments.

</details>


### [20] [Quantum gates from the middle convolution of twisted Burau representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00293)
*Haru Negami*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies braid representations from character-twisted Burau inputs via the Katz-Long-Moody (multiplicative middle convolution) construction, determining the spectra, decomposition, and Clifford/entangling properties of the output, and providing an explicit qubit-efficient unitary synthesis with error bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Character-twisted Burau inputs under the KLM construction yield a rich family of braid representations tied to KZ-type equations, but their fine structure (spectra, invariant summands, irreducibility) and their quantum-information-theoretic properties (non-Cliffordness, entanglement, density of the image) were not fully understood, nor was there an explicit resource-efficient unitary realization.

Method: Fix q=e^{2πia}, τ=e^{2πic} with a,c>0, nc+(n+1)a<1; use known positive-definite invariant forms for λ=e^{2πil}, 0<l<1-nc-(n+1)a. Analyze the M=n(n+1)-dimensional output via spectral decomposition into trivial, reduced Burau, and Long-type summands; prove non-Cliffordness in Weyl-Heisenberg frames and entangling behavior under linear independence; construct an orthonormal basis with block structure and express generators via rank-one phase reflections and O(M)-length one-qubit+CNOT word, with finite-alphabet approximation and error bounds.

Result: The generator spectrum recovers (q,τ); the output decomposes into two trivial, two reduced Burau, and an n(n-1)-dimensional Long-type summand (generically irreducible for n=3,4). Irrational a or c makes every generator non-Clifford in every finite-dimensional Weyl-Heisenberg frame; Q-linear independence of 1,a,c makes every generator entangling for every nontrivial bipartition. The full braid image is not projectively dense. Irreducibility at nonexceptional parameters forces commuting free-group inputs. An explicit orthonormal basis gives each generator one 2(n+1) block, n-2 blocks of size two, and identity elsewhere; each is a product of 2n rank-one phase reflections and an O(M)-length word in one-qubit unitaries and CNOTs with no ancillas, including direction preparation and inverse. Finite-alphabet approximation, conditioning, and error bounds are derived, with a matched-resource comparison to a Quantum Shannon reference compiler. The gate bound is exponential in encoded qubit count and excludes classical preprocessing.

Conclusion: The KLM construction over character-twisted Burau inputs yields structured, realizable braid representations with strong non-classical (non-Clifford, entangling) properties yet a non-dense image; explicit unitary synthesis is possible without ancillas, though the gate cost scales exponentially in encoded qubits and omits classical preprocessing.

Abstract: We study braid representations obtained from character-twisted Burau inputs by the Katz-Long-Moody (KLM) construction, which corresponds to multiplicative middle convolution for KZ-type equations. For $n\ge2$, let $q=e^{2πia}$, $τ=e^{2πic}$, where $a,c>0$ and $nc+(n+1)a<1$. Known form and signature results give a positive-definite invariant form for $λ=e^{2πil}$, $0<l<1-nc-(n+1)a$. For fixed input, unitary realizations are unique up to simultaneous unitary conjugation. We address two questions.
  First, we determine spectra and invariant summands of the $M=n(n+1)$-dimensional output. The generator spectrum recovers $(q,τ)$. The output decomposes into two trivial and two reduced Burau summands, and an $n(n-1)$-dimensional summand isomorphic to Long's reduced construction, generically irreducible for $n=3,4$. If $a$ or $c$ is irrational, every generator is non-Clifford in every finite-dimensional Weyl-Heisenberg frame; if $1,a,c$ are linearly independent over $\mathbb{Q}$, every generator is entangling for every nontrivial bipartition. The full braid image is not projectively dense. For general positive-definite unitary inputs, irreducibility of the full braid output at a nonexceptional parameter forces the free-group input operators to commute.
  Second, we construct one orthonormal basis giving each generator one block of size $2(n+1)$, $n-2$ blocks of size two, and identity elsewhere. Each generator is a product of $2n$ rank-one phase reflections and, in a fixed binary encoding, an $O(M)$-length word in one-qubit unitaries and CNOTs, without auxiliary qubits and including direction preparation and its inverse. We give finite-alphabet approximation, conditioning and error bounds, and a matched-resource comparison with a Quantum Shannon reference compiler. The gate bound is exponential in the encoded qubit count and excludes classical preprocessing.

</details>


### [21] [Hidden-State Updates and observable-record composition in retrocausal models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00357)
*D. M. Theshan N. Weerasinghe*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper distinguishes two composition requirements for hidden-variable models that reproduce context-independent individual readouts but may fail to preserve observable records under device connection or reuse: an invariant space (from normalization defects of nonnegative instrument matrices) characterizing unit continuation mass under finite adaptive policies, whose annihilator gives the maximal admissible positive cone closed under outcome-conditioned updates; and a separate observation space characterizing indistinguishability of context-dependent hidden preparations under joint readout.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hidden-variable models can match individual, context-independent readouts yet still fail to preserve observable records once devices are connected or reused, so a principled account of composition is needed to separate what such models can and cannot reproduce.

Method: The work formalizes two composition requirements. For finite families of nonnegative instrument matrices it defines an invariant space generated by normalization defects, characterizing unit continuation mass under every finite adaptive policy, with its annihilator giving the maximal admissible positive cone closed under outcome-conditioned updates. A separate observation space characterizes indistinguishability of context-dependent hidden preparations under joint readout. Examples are tested via exact rational calculations, exhaustive finite-policy experiments, continuous source sampling, and quadrature; a wrapped-Cauchy spin source with a stipulated unchanged-source passive extension yields a setting-dependent agreement probability with contrast ε²sech(2g)/4.

Result: A finite-state example passes every fixed sequence but fails under outcome-dependent stopping. For the wrapped-Cauchy spin source, two individually fair probes acquire a setting-dependent agreement probability with contrast ε²sech(2g)/4. Protection under one and two identical uses forces a continuous binary passive response to be constant, while measurable two-valued exceptions remain possible.

Conclusion: The criteria apply to specified instrument families and accessible records; they do not construct a microscopic detector nor establish consistency under unrestricted network composition.

Abstract: A hidden-variable model can reproduce context-independent individual readouts while failing to preserve observable records when devices are connected or reused. We distinguish two composition requirements. For a finite family of nonnegative instrument matrices, an invariant space generated by normalization defects characterizes unit continuation mass under every finite adaptive policy. Its annihilator defines the maximal admissible positive cone closed under outcome-conditioned updates. A separate observation space characterizes indistinguishability of context-dependent hidden preparations under joint readout. A finite-state example passes every fixed sequence but fails under outcome-dependent stopping. For a wrapped-Cauchy spin source with an explicitly stipulated unchanged-source passive extension, two individually fair probes acquire a setting-dependent agreement probability with contrast $ε^2\operatorname{sech}(2g)/4$. More generally, protection under one and two identical uses forces a continuous binary passive response to be constant; measurable two-valued exceptions remain possible. Exact rational calculations, exhaustive finite-policy experiments, continuous source sampling, and quadrature test the stated examples. The criteria apply to specified instrument families and accessible records; they do not construct a microscopic detector or establish consistency under unrestricted network composition.

</details>


### [22] [Finite-blocklength classical communication over the quantum erasure channel with and without classical feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00440)
*Mark M. Wilde*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper derives the exact optimal success probability for transmitting a fixed number of classical messages through finite uses of the quantum erasure channel with classical feedback and no shared entanglement, showing it equals E[min{1,d^K/M}] with K~Binomial(n,1-p), and analyzes achievability, relative majorization, hypothesis-testing converses, gaps without feedback, and error exponents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum channels are subject to erasure, and feedback is a powerful resource in classical communication. There is a need to characterize the fundamental limits of classical message transmission through quantum erasure channels with feedback, particularly the exact finite-blocklength success probability, its achievability, and how feedback affects error exponents relative to the no-feedback case.

Method: The authors compute the optimal success probability via a converse combining an elementary dimension bound for noiseless quantum communication with classical feedback (proved by fixing classical controls without conditioning the sender's state on the observed transcript), allowing arbitrary adaptive quantum encoders, quantum memories, and receiver instruments. They construct a classical protocol transmitting base-d digits of the message, repeating each digit until acknowledged or blocklength is reached. They prove a relative-majorization property of erasure-channel outputs, evaluate a hypothesis-testing converse, and analyze product-state codes with tetrahedral qubit states. They also derive message-size formulas and examine strong-converse bounds and error exponents.

Result: For input dimension d, erasure probability p, blocklength n, and M equiprobable messages, the optimal success probability is E[min{1,d^K/M}] with K~Binomial(n,1-p); the classical acknowledgment-based protocol attains this bound for every integer M. A hypothesis-testing converse recovers the same numerical bound without feedback, but need not be achievable without feedback: four uses of the qubit erasure channel with four messages yield a strict gap. A product-state code using tetrahedral qubit states outperforms every classical binary erasure code with these parameters. The bounded-remainder normal approximation and the average-success strong-converse exponent are unchanged without feedback. The feedback-assisted error exponent below capacity is determined and agrees with the no-feedback exponent above a critical rate, with no-feedback bounds given at lower rates.

Conclusion: Classical feedback does not increase the optimal success probability for this finite-blocklength quantum erasure channel task beyond the derived bound, and a simple acknowledgment-based classical strategy is optimal. While the no-feedback hypothesis-testing converse gives the same numerical bound, it is not always achievable (strict gap in the 4-use qubit case), and quantum product-state codes can beat classical binary erasure codes. Feedback-assisted and no-feedback error exponents coincide above a critical rate, with distinct behavior at lower rates.

Abstract: We determine the optimal success probability for transmitting a fixed number of classical messages through a finite number of uses of the quantum erasure channel, assisted by noiseless classical feedback and without initial shared entanglement. For an input dimension $d$, an erasure probability $p$, a blocklength $n$, and $M$ equiprobable messages, the optimal success probability is equal to $E[\min\{1,d^K/M\}]$, where $K$ is binomial with parameters $n$ and $1-p$. The converse allows arbitrary adaptive quantum encoders, quantum memories, and receiver instruments. Its main ingredient is an elementary dimension bound for noiseless quantum communication with classical feedback, proved by fixing the classical controls without conditioning the sender's state on the observed transcript. A classical protocol that transmits the base-$d$ digits of an integer representing the message, repeating each digit until the receiver acknowledges its reception or the prescribed blocklength is reached, attains the bound for every integer $M$. We also establish a relative-majorization property of erasure-channel outputs and exactly evaluate a hypothesis-testing converse, recovering the same numerical bound without feedback. That converse need not be achievable without feedback: four uses of the qubit erasure channel and four messages give a strict gap. A product-state code employing tetrahedral qubit states nevertheless outperforms every classical binary erasure code with these parameters. We give an explicit message-size formula and show that the bounded-remainder normal approximation and the average-success strong-converse exponent are unchanged without feedback. We also determine the feedback-assisted error exponent below capacity, prove that it agrees with the no-feedback exponent above a critical rate, and give no-feedback bounds at lower rates.

</details>


### [23] [Quantum Secret Sharing and Error Correction vs No-Cloning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00441)
*Steven Chien,Ishani Mukherjee,Mark Zhandry*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies quantum secret sharing (QSS) with multiple identical copies of the input state, showing that no-cloning only partially characterizes feasibility. It gives positive constructions for thresholds when t>(n-k+1)/2 and a new "Clique Path" obstruction ruling out t≤(n-1)/k, with tight matching bounds for k=2, leaving k≥3 open.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical secret sharing supports all access structures, and threshold schemes are efficient. In the quantum setting, however, the no-cloning theorem severely restricts feasibility: for threshold access structures, QSS is possible iff t>n/2. The authors aim to understand whether relaxing the problem by providing multiple identical input copies can circumvent this single-copy no-cloning obstruction, and whether no-cloning still exactly characterizes feasibility.
