Method: The paper introduces a many-copy variant of QSS: the protocol receives k identical copies of the input state but must recover only one copy. It analyzes feasibility using connections to k-colorability of graphs derived from the access structure. Positively, it constructs QSS for thresholds whenever t>(n-k+1)/2. Negatively, it develops a new obstruction called the Clique Path obstruction, applicable to arbitrary access structures, which for thresholds implies impossibility whenever t≤(n-1)/k. The results are also connected to quantum error correction for erasure channels.

Result: For k copies, no-cloning implies impossibility when t≤n/(k+1). The paper shows this is not tight: a construction achieves QSS for t>(n-k+1)/2, while the Clique Path obstruction rules out t≤(n-1)/k. For k=2 these upper and lower bounds exactly match. A gap remains for k≥3.

Conclusion: Multiple copies of the input allow QSS to go slightly beyond the single-copy no-cloning obstruction, but no-cloning does not exactly characterize the many-copy case. Both feasibility and obstruction results reduce to k-colorability of graphs from the access structure, yield tight bounds for k=2, and imply analogous obstructions for quantum error correction over erasure channels. Closing the gap for k≥3 is left as an open direction.

Abstract: Secret sharing is ubiquitous throughout cryptography. All possible access structures are classically feasible, and in the case of threshold access structures, the protocols are even very efficient. However, when moving to the quantum setting, the no-cloning theorem shows that many access structures are impossible. In fact, no-cloning exactly characterizes feasibility: for thresholds, quantum secret sharing is possible if and only if the threshold $t$ is strictly more than $n/2$.
  In this work, we propose a variant of quantum secret sharing (QSS) where two or more identical copies of the input state are provided, but the output is only required to recover one copy. This notion circumvents the simple one-copy no-cloning obstruction, though the natural $k$-copy generalization still gives much milder obstructions. For thresholds using $k$ copies, no-cloning implies that QSS is impossible whenever $t\leq n/(k+1)$.
  It is tempting to hypothesize that no-cloning continues to exactly characterize the many-copy case. However, we show that this is not the case. We give positive results showing that multiple copies allow for going slightly beyond the single-copy obstruction: for thresholds, we construct QSS whenever $t>(n-k+1)/2$. On the other hand, we give a novel obstruction we call the Clique Path obstruction, which applies to arbitrary access structures. For thresholds, it shows that QSS is impossible whenever $t\leq (n-1)/k$. Our upper and lower bounds exactly match for $k=2$. Both our results leverage connections to the $k$-colorability of certain graphs derived from the access structure. We leave closing the gap for $k\geq 3$ copies as a fascinating direction for future work.
  Secret sharing is closely related to error correction, which can also be considered in the many-copy setting. Our results imply similar obstructions for quantum error correction for erasure channels.

</details>


### [24] [Robust classical and quantum communication over almost-iid channels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00442)
*Liuhang Ye,Bjarne Bergh,Nilanjana Datta*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proves that the asymptotic classical and quantum capacities of channels that are "almost-iid" along a reference channel Φ match those of Φ itself, with a single universal code achieving vanishing error across the whole almost-iid class.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard iid paradigm models n channel uses as the tensor power Φ^{⊗n}, but this exact tensor-power behaviour is an idealization. The paper asks whether asymptotic capacities survive when channels are only approximately iid (Choi-almost-iid sequences), motivated by the almost-iid states appearing in Renner's exponential de Finetti theorem.

Method: The authors consider sequences of Choi-almost-iid channels whose normalized Choi states are MSR almost-iid along the normalized Choi state of Φ, with defect parameters r_n = o(n). They exploit the exponential de Finetti theorem for quantum channels (from a companion paper), in which Choi-almost-iid channels play the same role as almost-iid states do in the state version.

Result: The universal unassisted classical and quantum capacities of the Choi-almost-iid class along Φ are shown to equal those of Φ. Universality is within the class: for any rate below the relevant capacity, a code sequence depending only on Φ and the rate—not on the specific channel sequence—achieves vanishing error for every sequence in the class.

Conclusion: Asymptotic capacities of a channel Φ are robust to deviations from exact tensor-power behaviour: they remain achievable for all channel sequences that are almost-iid along Φ, using a single universal code per rate.

Abstract: The independent and identically distributed (iid) paradigm for asymptotic communication through quantum channels describes $n$ uses of a channel $Φ$ by $Φ^{\otimes n}$. Since exact tensor-power behaviour is an idealization, we ask whether the asymptotic capacities of $Φ$ remain achievable for channel sequences that are almost-iid along $Φ$. We consider sequences of Choi-almost-iid channels whose normalized Choi states are Mazzola-Sutter-Renner (MSR) almost-iid along the normalized Choi state of $Φ$, with defect parameters $r_n=o(n)$. In Renner's exponential de Finetti theorem for quantum states, almost-iid states appear in the approximating mixture and motivate the MSR almost-iid class. Choi-almost-iid channels play the corresponding role in an exponential de Finetti theorem for quantum channels established in a companion paper. In this paper, we prove that the universal unassisted classical and quantum capacities of the class of Choi-almost-iid channels along $Φ$ equal those of the channel $Φ$. Here universality is within this class: for each rate below the relevant capacity, a code sequence depending on $Φ$ and the rate, but not on the particular channel sequence, achieves vanishing error for every sequence in the class.

</details>


### [25] [Flexible rigidity: Compiling generic trusted-preparation protocols into sequential device-independent protocols](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00444)
*Amir Arqand,Srijita Kundu,Ernest Y. -Z. Tan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A generic compiler converts device-dependent trusted-preparation protocols into sequential device-independent protocols, enabled by new Renyi-divergence-based rigidity theorems achieving constant rate under constant noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Device-independent protocols are highly desirable for security but hard to construct, while device-dependent trusted-preparation protocols are easier to design. The paper aims to bridge this gap by systematically compiling trusted-preparation protocols into sequential device-independent ones, overcoming the scaling obstacle that previous rigidity theorems based on trace distance or fidelity face.

Method: The compiler requires the trusted-preparation protocol to admit an entanglement-based realization matching the optimal strategy of a non-local game satisfying a weak rigidity condition. The core technical tool is a new class of rigidity theorems based on Renyi divergences. The generic sequential proof uses the sharp-divergence chain rule of Fawzi and Fawzi (2021); in the memoryless-device special case rates are improved via a novel subadditivity property for a modified channel sandwiched divergence; a chain rule for iterated-mean divergences is also proven.

Result: The compiler is applied to obtain sequential device-independent protocols for uncloneable encryption, copy-protection of point functions, and blind randomness expansion. These protocols achieve a constant rate (ratio of trusted states prepared to device uses) while tolerating a constant level of noise per device usage.

Conclusion: New Renyi-divergence-based rigidity theorems overcome the fundamental scaling obstacle of prior trace-distance/fidelity rigidity theorems, enabling constant-rate sequential device-independent compilation with constant noise tolerance. The modified channel sandwiched divergence subadditivity and the iterated-mean divergence chain rule are of independent interest and offer prospects for further rate improvements.

Abstract: We give a generic compiler for turning a wide class of device-dependent protocols with trusted state preparation into sequential device-independent protocols. In order for our compiler to apply, the trusted-preparation protocol needs to satisfy certain minimal conditions: having an entanglement-based realization in the first place, and the entanglement-based realization corresponding to the optimal strategy of any non-local game that satisfies a fairly weak rigidity condition. We apply our generic compiler to give sequential device-independent protocols for uncloneable encryption, copy-protection of point functions, and blind randomness expansion.
  Our technical contribution is a new class of rigidity theorems based on Rényi divergences, with flexible cryptographic applications. They allow our compiler to achieve a constant rate (i.e., the ratio of trusted states prepared to device uses), while tolerating a constant level of noise per device usage. This overcomes a fundamental scaling obstacle faced by previous rigidity theorems for trace distance or fidelity. For the generic sequential case, our proof is based on the sharp-divergence chain rule by Fawzi and Fawzi (2021), which suffices for a constant but fairly low rate. In the special case when the devices retain no memory, we improve the rates by deriving a novel subadditivity property for a modified channel sandwiched divergence, which may be of independent interest. Finally, as a prospect for future improvements, we also prove a chain rule for the iterated-mean divergences from Brown, Fawzi and Fawzi (2021).

</details>


### [26] [Learning Local Fermionic Lindbladians under Parity Superselection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00448)
*Tim Möbus,Daniel Stilck França,Cambyse Rouzé*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops learning algorithms for parity-covariant, k-mode-local Lindbladians on m fermionic modes, circumventing parity superselection constraints that forbid direct measurement of odd (single-Majorana) observables. Using even preparations/measurements with short-time evolution and internal markers, it recovers canonical coefficients via signed Fierz inversion and achieves entrywise error ε with sample complexity Õ(ᾱ²ε⁻²log(m/δ)), with matching lower bounds for short-time experiments and extensions to the unknown-graph setting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parity superselection forbids direct measurement of odd Majorana observables, which obstructs standard tomographic/learning approaches to fermionic Lindbladians. The work aims to learn time-independent, parity-covariant, k-mode-local Lindbladians without external modes or a priori knowledge of nonzero coefficient locations, addressing both sample complexity and fundamental lower bounds.

Method: The approach uses even preparations and measurements with uninterrupted short-time evolution. Internal markers convert odd probes into even observables, and pair measurements among three separated markers calibrate their dynamical contributions. Signed Fierz inversion recovers canonical coefficients, and semidefinite fitting yields a valid generator. For the unknown-graph setting, one idle ancillary mode per system mode and potentially nonlocal pair operations are employed under a coefficient-degree bound and controlled weak-coefficient tails.

Result: For finite-range models on known bounded-degree graphs with marker access and weighted-strength bound ᾱ, entrywise error ε requires Õ(ᾱ²ε⁻²log(m/δ)) samples; diamond-norm error ε costs an extra factor m², matching short-time lower bounds up to logarithmic and fixed geometric factors. Without a supplied graph, total absolute coefficient error ≤ ε per mode is achieved with Õ_k(ᾱ²d²m^{⌊k/2⌋}ε⁻²log(m/δ)) samples under approximate coefficient-degree bound d. Geometric models allow prediction of fixed-support even observables with logarithmic sample complexity. All guarantees hold with probability ≥ 1−δ; finite-volume and exponential-tail extensions are provided.

Conclusion: The paper provides provably sample-efficient protocols for learning parity-covariant local Lindbladians under the constraint of parity superselection, with matching lower bounds for short-time experiments. It extends to unknown graph structures and geometric models, and includes finite-volume and exponential-tail generalizations.

Abstract: Parity superselection forbids direct measurement of odd Majorana observables. We learn time-independent, parity-covariant, $k$-mode-local Lindbladians on $m$ modes using even preparations and measurements with uninterrupted short-time evolution. Internal markers turn odd probes into even observables, and pair measurements among three separated markers calibrate their dynamical contributions. Signed Fierz inversion recovers canonical coefficients, while semidefinite fitting yields a valid generator. For finite-range models on known bounded-degree graphs with suitable marker access and a supplied weighted-strength bound $\barα$, entrywise error $\varepsilon$ is achieved using $\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\barα^2\varepsilon^{-2}\log(m/δ))$ samples, without external modes or known nonzero coefficient locations. Recovery to diamond-norm error $\varepsilon$ costs an additional factor $m^2$, matching lower bounds for short-time experiments on fresh systems up to logarithmic and fixed geometric factors. Without a supplied graph, one idle ancillary mode per system mode and potentially nonlocal pair operations give total absolute coefficient error at most $\varepsilon$ per mode using $\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_k(\barα^2\mathsf d^2m^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}\varepsilon^{-2}\log(m/δ))$ samples, under a supplied approximate coefficient-degree bound $\mathsf d$ and controlled weak-coefficient tails. All guarantees hold with probability at least $1-δ$. For geometric models, fixed-support even observables can be predicted with logarithmic system-size sample complexity at fixed time and accuracy. We also give finite-volume and exponential-tail extensions.

</details>


### [27] [Clifford-hierarchy stabilizer formalism with applications to twisted quantum doubles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00464)
*Christopher Fechisin,Kieran Cooney,Mathi Raja,Victor V. Albert,Dominic J. Williamson,Kyle Kawagoe,Seth Musser*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces the $X\mathcal C^{(k)}_d$ stabilizer formalism, a generalization of Pauli stabilizers, to systematically describe beyond-Pauli topological codes and their logical gates, including twisted quantum double phases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent work shows quantum double models can circumvent the Bravyi-Koenig bound via code-switching protocols where non-Pauli stabilizer phases appear as intermediate codes. However, a concrete stabilizer framework for such beyond-Pauli topological codes and their logical gates is lacking.

Method: The authors define the $X\mathcal C^{(k)}_d$ stabilizer formalism, combining Pauli-$X$ operators with diagonal operators from a fixed level $k$ of the Clifford hierarchy on prime-dimensional qudits. They classify a large class of these stabilizer states and apply the framework to 2D twisted quantum double phases ${\cal D}^\omega(\mathbb{Z}_p^n)$.

Result: $X\mathcal C^{(k)}_d$ stabilizer states are uniform superpositions over affine-linear subspaces with diagonal phases; when stabilizers need level-$k$ Clifford-hierarchy operators, the diagonal gates producing the phases sit one level higher. Every 2D twisted quantum double ${\cal D}^\omega(\mathbb{Z}_p^n)$ with $\omega$ a product of type-III cocycles and $p$ odd admits a Clifford ($k=2$) stabilizer realization; phases with type-I or type-II cocycles are conjectured to require level-$p$ stabilizers. A pair of qudit toric codes can be deterministically prepared in a logical magic state by measuring $X\mathcal C^{(k)}_d$ stabilizers.

Conclusion: The framework provides a concrete stabilizer formalism for topological codes beyond Pauli stabilizer models and their logical gates, with implications for code-switching, twisted quantum double phases, and magic-state preparation.

Abstract: Recent work has shown that quantum double models can be used to circumvent the Bravyi-Koenig bound. In particular, this is achieved through code-switching protocols in which non-Pauli stabilizer phases appear as intermediate codes. We introduce the $X\mathcal C^{(k)}_d$ stabilizer formalism, which generalizes the Pauli stabilizer formalism by combining Pauli-$X$ operators with diagonal operators drawn from a fixed level $k$ of the Clifford hierarchy on prime-dimensional qudits. This yields a natural class of beyond-Pauli stabilizer models, including many twisted quantum double phases. We classify a large class of these $X\mathcal C^{(k)}_d$ stabilizer states, showing that they are uniform superpositions over affine-linear subspaces with diagonal phases. In particular, when the stabilizers of a state require operators from level $k$ of the Clifford hierarchy, we show that the diagonal gates required to produce the phases in the wavefunction sit one level higher. We further show that every 2D twisted quantum double ${\cal D}^ω(\mathbb{Z}_p^n)$ with $ω$ a product of type-III cocycles and $p$ an odd prime admits a realization using only Clifford ($k=2$) stabilizers, and we conjecture that phases where $ω$ contains a type-I or type-II cocycle require level-$p$ Clifford-hierarchy stabilizers for $p$-dimensional qudits. Finally, we demonstrate that a pair of qudit toric codes may be deterministically prepared in a logical magic state by measuring $X\mathcal C^{(k)}_d$ stabilizers. Together, these results provide a concrete stabilizer framework for topological codes beyond Pauli stabilizer models and their logical gates.

</details>


### [28] [Factor Code Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00469)
*Abhisek Sahu,Jeremy van der Heijden,Mark Van Raamsdonk,Rana Zibakhsh*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies when a network of quantum isometric codes can be promoted to a "factor code network" by assigning unital *-homomorphisms, characterizing feasibility via Hilbert-space dimension divisibility, network topology (tree vs. diamond), and principle-angle multiplicities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum codes defined by isometries naturally carry logical-to-physical operator maps (*-homomorphisms). While single factor codes are understood, it is unclear how to consistently assign such operator maps across a whole network of nested/related codes. The paper motivates this by asking when a code network (a poset-indexed family of Hilbert spaces with compatible isometries) can be upgraded to a factor code network with compatible operator maps, a question relevant to compositional and hierarchical quantum coding.

Method: The authors formalize a factor code as a pair (T, φ) where T is an isometry and φ is a unital *-homomorphism satisfying φ(O)T = TO, with φ(B(H_code)) being a von Neumann factor subalgebra of B(H_phys). They define a code network as isometries T_ji on a poset satisfying T_kjT_ji = T_ki, and seek unital maps φ_ji with (T_ji, φ_ji) a factor code and φ_kjφ_ji = φ_ki. They analyze tree-structured networks, diamond-shaped networks (using principle angles between subspaces and their multiplicities), incomparable predecessors (commutation, generation, intersection of image algebras), and commuting-square structures.

Result: Divisibility conditions on Hilbert-space dimensions are sufficient for promotion of any code network with a tree structure but not in general. For a diamond-shaped network, promotion is possible if and only if the principle angles between T_42 H_2 and T_43 H_3 all have multiplicity divisible by dim H_1. For incomparable i_1, i_2 < j, the paper characterizes when φ_{j i_1}(B(H_{i_1})) and φ_{j i_2}(B(H_{i_2})) commute, when they generate all of B(H_j), and when they intersect only in multiples of the identity. For i < j_1, j_2 < k, it characterizes when φ_{k j_1}(B(H_{j_1})) ∩ φ_{k j_2}(B(H_{j_2})) = φ_{k i}(B(H_i)) and when these algebras with B(H_k) form a nondegenerate commuting square.

Conclusion: Factor code networks can be promoted under dimension divisibility for tree-structured networks, but general networks require finer conditions. The diamond case is governed exactly by principle-angle multiplicities relative to dim H_1, and the algebra-relation questions (commutation, generation, intersection, commuting squares) admit precise characterization in terms of the underlying subspace geometry.

Abstract: For a quantum code defined by an isometry $T:H_{\rm code} \to H_{\rm phys}$, an operator map is a *-homomorphism $\varphi: B(H_{\rm code}) \to B(H_{\rm phys})$ from the space of operators on the logical Hilbert space to the space of operators on the physical Hilbert space satisfying $\varphi({\cal O}) T = T {\cal O}$ for every operator ${\cal O}$ on $H_{\rm code}$. This can additionally be taken to be unital $\varphi(I_{code}) = I_{phys}$ if and only if $\dim H_{\rm code}$ divides $\dim H_{\rm phys}$. In this case, $\varphi(B(H_{\rm code})$ is a von Neumann factor subalgebra of $B(H_{\rm phys})$ and we call the pair $(T,\varphi)$ a {\it factor code}.
  A code network is a family of Hilbert spaces $\{{\cal H}_i\}$ indexed by a partially ordered set with isometries $T_{ji}:{\cal H}_i \to {\cal H}_j$ for $i < j$ satisfying $T_{kj} T_{ji} = T_{ki}$ for $i < j < k$. In this paper, we ask when this can be promoted to a {\it factor code network} by defining unital operator maps $\varphi_{ji}$ so that $(T_{ji}, \varphi_{ji})$ is a factor code and $\varphi_{kj} \varphi_{ji} = \varphi_{ki}$. We show that the divisibility conditions on Hilbert space dimensions are sufficient for any code network with a tree structure but not in general. For a diamond-shaped network, we show that promotion is possible if and only if the principle angles between $T_{42} H_2$ and $T_{43} H_3$ all have multiplicity divisible by $H_1$.
  For incomparable $i_1,i_2 < j$, we characterize when $\varphi_{j i_1} (B(H_{i_1}))$ and $\varphi_{j i_2} (B(H_{i_2}))$ commute, when they generate all of $B(H_j)$, and when they intersect only on multiples of the identity. For $i < j_1,j_2 < k$, we characterize when $\varphi_{k j_1} (B(H_{j_1})) \cap \varphi_{k j_2} (B(H_{j_2})) = \varphi_{k i} (B(H_{i}))$ and when these algebras together with $B(H_k)$ form a nondegenerate commuting square.

</details>


### [29] [Mixed-state phases induced by power-law quantum channels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00480)
*Christopher Fechisin,Tsung-Cheng Lu,Zhi-Yuan Wei,Jeet Shah,Yu-Xin Wang,Alexey V. Gorshkov,Cheng-Ju Lin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A long-range quantum channel enables a phase transition to strong-to-weak spontaneous symmetry breaking (SW-SSB) in 1D, producing a phase diagram with trivial, mixed SPT, and SW-SSB phases, linked to logical information loss via a classical statistical mechanics mapping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inherently mixed quantum orders like SW-SSB have no pure-state analogue, but finite-depth local channels are generally expected to be unable to induce a transition to SW-SSB in one spatial dimension, creating an obstruction the authors seek to circumvent.

Method: Introduce a long-range quantum channel performing two-body dephasing on site pairs with power-law-decaying probability, apply it to a one-parameter family of matrix product states interpolating between trivial and SPT phases, and analyze the resulting phases via Rényi-2 diagnostics and mappings to a classical statistical mechanics model.

Result: The phase diagram hosts trivial, mixed SPT, and SW-SSB phases. The mixed SPT phase is distinct from prior decohered SPT phases, hosting two logical qubits and nonvanishing Rényi-2 string order despite vanishing conventional string order. Mappings to a classical statistical mechanics model connect loss of logical information to the onset of SW-SSB.

Conclusion: Long-range channels circumvent the local-channel obstruction to SW-SSB in 1D. The mixed states can be tuned to high-purity regimes, reducing measurement overhead and enabling experimental realization and detection on near-term quantum hardware.

Abstract: Open quantum systems can exhibit inherently mixed quantum orders, such as strong-to-weak spontaneous symmetry breaking (SW-SSB), which have no analogue in pure states. It is generally expected that a finite-depth local quantum channel cannot induce a phase transition to SW-SSB in one spatial dimension. We circumvent this obstruction by introducing a long-range quantum channel, which implements two-body dephasing on pairs of sites with probability falling off according to a power-law in the site separation. Applying this channel to a one-parameter family of matrix product states interpolating between trivial and symmetry-protected topological (SPT) phases, we construct a phase diagram that hosts trivial, mixed SPT, and SW-SSB phases with respect to Rényi-2 diagnostics. Notably, the mixed SPT phase is distinct from previously studied decohered SPT phases: it hosts two logical qubits and exhibits nonvanishing Rényi-2 string order, despite vanishing conventional string order. Through a series of mappings to a classical statistical mechanics model, we establish a direct connection between the loss of logical information and the onset of SW-SSB in this model. Finally, we consider the feasibility of experimental realization of the phenomena we have studied. We emphasize that our family of mixed states can be tuned to regimes of high purity, substantially reducing measurement overhead and enabling their experimental realization and detection on near-term quantum hardware.

</details>


### [30] [Causal Trade-Offs in Superluminal Extensions of Relativity. A no-go theorem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00494)
*Amrapali Sen,Matthias Salzger,V. Vilasini*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops a causal modelling framework to study Dragan and Ekert's superluminal reference frame proposal and proves a no-go theorem: permitting superluminal frames avoids superluminal signalling only under severe constraints (restricted spacetime access, frame-dependent observables, or frame-dependent/free-variable-free physics). Objective uncertainty alone does not resolve the conflict.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dragan and Ekert recently proposed superluminal reference frames as a way to reconcile relativistic symmetry with quantum uncertainty, suggesting objective uncertainty could eliminate the conflict between superluminal transformations and superluminal signalling. This work aims to formally test whether that proposal actually succeeds.

Method: The authors develop a causal modelling framework to formally analyze superluminal reference frames and derive a no-go theorem characterizing the conditions under which such theories can avoid superluminal signalling.

Result: A theory permitting superluminal reference frames can avoid superluminal signalling only if it severely constrains which spacetime regions are accessible to operational protocols, if the choice of reference frame has observable consequences, or if free variables do not exist or their existence depends on the reference frame. The no-go result shows Dragan and Ekert's objective uncertainty proposal does not, by itself, eliminate the conflict.

Conclusion: The paper clarifies the limitations of uncertainty-based resolutions and the causal trade-offs faced by superluminal extensions of relativity. Since superluminal signalling might be avoided by relaxing other fundamental assumptions, the proposed objective uncertainty may not be necessary at all.

Abstract: Extensions of relativity that include superluminal reference frames have recently been proposed by Dragan and Ekert as a way to reconcile relativistic symmetry with quantum uncertainty. In this work, we develop a causal modelling framework to formally study this proposal and prove a no-go theorem demonstrating that a theory permitting superluminal reference frames can avoid superluminal signalling only if it severely constrains which spacetime regions are accessible to operational protocols, if the choice of reference frame has observable consequences or if free variables do not exist or their existence depends on the reference frame. Crucially, our result goes beyond the familiar intuition that superluminal transformations may lead to superluminal signalling; our no-go result shows that Dragan and Ekert's proposal of introducing objective uncertainty does not, by itself, eliminate this conflict. In fact, because superluminal signalling might instead be avoided by relaxing other fundamental assumptions in the presence of superluminal reference frames, the proposed objective uncertainty may not be necessary at all. Our results clarify the limitations of uncertainty-based resolutions and the causal trade-offs faced by superluminal extensions of relativity.

</details>


### [31] [Quantum Algorithms for OPI Variants Beyond Locality and Classical Decodability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00502)
*Seyoon Ragavan,Noah Shutty*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper extends Regev's quantum reduction framework by relaxing two limitations: it introduces a quantum decoder for finding nonzero solutions to By=0 over codes with a two-fold multiplication property, and it enables classical decoding under global histogram-local constraints via a Fourier stability argument, yielding efficient quantum algorithms for optimal polynomial intersection variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior applications of Regev's reduction that resisted dequantization depended on (1) efficient classical decoders and (2) coordinate-wise constraints specifying allowed values per coordinate. The paper aims to break past each of these restrictions separately to broaden the reach of the framework.

Method: First contribution: a quantum decoder to find solutions y in (F_q\{0})^m to By=0 for B in F_q^{n x m}, adapting the Chen-Liu-Zhandry template to codes spanned by rows of B that satisfy a "two-fold multiplication property" (coordinate-wise products of codeword pairs span a space of dimension much smaller than m). Second contribution: retains classical decoding but admits global constraints on symbol frequencies, studying "histogram-local" constraints that specify allowed occurrence counts for each symbol; for broad families it shows a uniformly random satisfying vector stays satisfying with constant probability after resampling a uniformly random coordinate.

Result: The first contribution solves the nonzero-solution problem for codes with the two-fold multiplication property, extending beyond the m=Ω(n^2) random-matrix regime (fixed prime q>2) previously handled by Chen-Liu-Zhandry and now classically covered. The second contribution establishes stability of histogram-local constraints, providing sufficient Fourier mass at decodable Hamming weights and yielding efficient quantum algorithms for variants of optimal polynomial intersection (OPI).

Conclusion: Regev's reduction can be pushed beyond its classical-decoder and coordinate-wise-constraint assumptions via two independent routes: a quantum decoder exploiting algebraic structure of codes, and a Fourier-analytic stability property for histogram-local constraints enabling classical decoding. Both expand the class of problems admitting efficient quantum algorithms, notably variants of OPI.

Abstract: Regev's reduction is a quantum algorithmic framework for finding codewords satisfying nonlinear constraints by decoding the dual code. To date, applications that have not been dequantized have relied on efficient classical decoders and coordinate-wise constraints specifying a set of allowed values for each coordinate. We push past these two restrictions separately via two separate contributions.
  Our first contribution uses a quantum decoder to find solutions $\mathbf{y}\in(\mathbb{F}_q\setminus\{0\})^m$ to $\mathbf{B}\mathbf{y}=0$, where $\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{F}_q^{n\times m}$. For fixed prime $q>2$, Chen, Liu, and Zhandry (Eurocrypt 2022) solve this problem for random matrices with $m=Ω(n^2)$, a regime now covered by classical algorithms. We adapt their template to codes (spanned by the rows of $\mathbf{B}$) with a ``two-fold multiplication property'': the coordinate-wise products of pairs of codewords span a space of dimension much smaller than $m$.
  Our second contribution retains classical decoding but allows global constraints on symbol frequencies. We study ``histogram-local'' constraints, which specify the allowed numbers of occurrences of each symbol. For broad families of these constraints, we show that a uniformly random satisfying vector remains satisfying with constant probability after resampling a uniformly random coordinate. This stability provides sufficient Fourier mass at decodable Hamming weights, yielding efficient quantum algorithms for variants of optimal polynomial intersection (OPI).

</details>


### [32] [Graph Neural Post-selection for Quantum Error Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00504)
*Conor Carty,Tamas Noszko,Joschka Roffe,Roberto Bondesan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Graph neural networks predict decoder failure from syndromes alone (using existing belief-propagation posteriors for qLDPC codes), enabling post-selection without extra decoder runs, achieving large logical error rate reductions on surface and bivariate bicycle codes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-selection improves logical reliability by rejecting failure-prone shots, but conventional approaches require running the decoder to know which shots fail. A method that predicts decoder failure directly from syndromes would avoid additional decoder executions and enable efficient post-selection, especially for high-rate qLDPC codes.

Method: Graph neural networks that predict decoder failure using only syndromes, and for high-rate qLDPC codes, existing belief-propagation posteriors as additional input. Evaluated on rotated surface code and bivariate bicycle (BB) code memories under uniform depolarising circuit-level noise.

Result: Retaining 90% of shots yields ~3700x and ~740x logical error rate reductions for [[72,12,6]] and [[144,12,12]] BB codes at realistic physical error rates. For the distance-5 surface code at p=0.003, the neural post-selector matches the leading complementary gap method's logical error suppression without running the decoder.

Conclusion: GNN-based failure prediction enables decoder-free neural post-selection that substantially suppresses logical errors and is competitive with state-of-the-art methods while avoiding extra decoder executions.

Abstract: Post-selection improves the logical reliability of quantum computation by rejecting shots which are likely to result in logical failure. We introduce graph neural networks that predict decoder failure without additional decoder executions, using only syndromes, and for high-rate quantum low-density parity-check (qLDPC) codes, existing belief-propagation posteriors. We evaluate rotated surface and bivariate bicycle (BB) code memories under uniform depolarising circuit-level noise. Retaining 90% of shots gives approximately 3700x and 740x reductions in logical error rates for [[72,12,6]] and [[144,12,12]] BB codes at realistic physical error rates. For the distance 5 surface code at physical noise p=0.003, our neural post-selector achieves logical error suppression comparable to the leading complementary gap method without running the decoder.

</details>


### [33] [Noisy Quantum Query Complexity via Fractional Block Sensitivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00506)
*Mehil Agarwal,Shravas Rao,Fang Song*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies quantum query complexity under imperfect oracle access. Using a fractional block sensitivity (fbs) framework, it derives lower bounds for negligent oracles, hybrid coherent/classical algorithms, and IID dephasing noise, and introduces a general variational resource for time-varying dephasing models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real quantum oracles are imperfect, so understanding how noise and limited access models degrade or preserve quantum query speedups is important. Prior work lacked a unified lower-bound framework across such imperfect oracle models.

Method: The authors build lower bounds on fractional block sensitivity (fbs). For negligent oracles (correct query w.p. 1-p), they prove bounds in terms of fbs(f,0^n) and construct a partial function f' preserving quantum query complexity under negligence. For hybrid Q coherent and C classical queries they prove C+Q^2=Ω(fbs(f)), and for IID dephasing they prove Ω(p·fbs(f)). They also introduce time-varying dephasing models with a variational resource lower-bounded by fbs(f), reducible to convex optimization.

Result: Negligence need not destroy quantum speedups: transformed partial functions retain exponential quantum speedups even under negligent queries, ruling out a general fbs(f) lower bound for partial functions. General bounds in terms of fbs(f) hold for all Boolean functions in the hybrid and IID dephasing models. The variational resource recovers the hybrid and IID dephasing bounds and determines query complexity of unstructured search under growing dephasing rates.

Conclusion: Fractional block sensitivity provides a common framework for quantum query lower bounds under imperfect oracle access, yielding both general bounds and a flexible variational resource for new noise schedules.

Abstract: We study quantum query complexity under several models of imperfect oracle access, and develop lower bounds through a common framework based on fractional block sensitivity (\(\fbs\)).
  For a negligent oracle that applies the correct query with probability \(1-p\), we prove a lower bound in terms of \(\fbs(f,0^n)\). We also show, perhaps surprisingly, that negligence need not destroy quantum speedups: any function \(f\) can be transformed into a partial function \(f'\) whose negligent query complexity essentially preserves the quantum query complexity of \(f\). This gives partial functions with exponential quantum speedups even under negligent queries, and in particular rules out a general lower bound in terms of \(\fbs(f)\) for partial functions in this model.
  For two other models, we obtain general lower bounds in terms of \(\fbs(f)\) for all Boolean functions. For hybrid algorithms using \(Q\) coherent and \(C\) classical queries, we prove the tradeoff \(C+Q^2=Ω(\fbs(f))\). For an IID dephasing noisy model where each query dephases the query-index register at rate \(p\) independently, we prove \(Ω\!\left(p\,\fbs(f)\right)\) queries are necessary.
  Finally, we introduce a broader family of time-varying dephasing models and identify a variational resource that is always lower bounded by \(\fbs(f)\). Computing this resource reduces to a convex optimization problem, providing a simple way to derive lower bounds for new noise schedules. As applications, we recover the hybrid and IID dephasing bounds and determine the query complexity of unstructured search when the dephasing rate grows over time.

</details>


### [34] [Blind Unforgeability implies Plus-One Unforgeability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00517)
*Mehil Agarwal,Calliope S. Reimann,Fang Song*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proves that quantum blind-unforgeability implies quantum plus-one unforgeability, thereby establishing that blind-unforgeability is strictly stronger than plus-one unforgeability in the quantum setting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The relationship between quantum blind-unforgeability and quantum plus-one unforgeability was previously an open question. Since plus-one unforgeability is known not to imply blind-unforgeability, resolving whether the converse implication holds is necessary to fully characterize the relative strength of these two quantum security notions.

Method: The authors introduce a new smoothing technique that coherently rescales the query histories of a quantum-query algorithm in a black-box manner. This allows transforming any successful plus-one attacker into a blind-unforgeability attacker with only inverse-polynomial loss. The approach departs from earlier attempts based on extracting a useful query history, which the authors show inevitably fails in general, and instead coherently reshapes how query histories interfere.

Result: Quantum blind-unforgeability implies quantum plus-one unforgeability. Combined with the known fact that plus-one unforgeability does not imply blind-unforgeability, this establishes that blind-unforgeability is strictly stronger than plus-one unforgeability in the quantum setting.

Conclusion: The open question is settled: blind-unforgeability is strictly stronger than plus-one unforgeability in the quantum setting. The smoothing technique provides a conceptual shift in how quantum-query algorithms are analyzed, moving from query-history extraction to coherent reshaping of query-history interference.

Abstract: We prove that quantum blind-unforgeability implies quantum plus-one unforgeability, settling an open question in the literature. Since plus-one unforgeability is known not to imply blind-unforgeability, our result establishes that blind-unforgeability is \emph{strictly stronger} than plus-one unforgeability in the quantum setting.
  The implication is proven using a new \emph{smoothing} technique that coherently rescales the query histories of a quantum-query algorithm in a black-box manner. This enables us to transform any successful plus-one attacker into a blind-unforgeability attacker with only inverse-polynomial loss. Our proof represents a conceptual shift from merely extracting a useful query history as in earlier attempts, which we show inevitably fails in general, to first coherently reshaping how query histories interfere.

</details>


### [35] [Near-Optimal Ground-State Preparation without Controlled Hamiltonian Evolutions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00520)
*Dhrumil Patel,Steven T. Flammia,Raul Garcia-Patron*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work resolves whether controlled Hamiltonian evolution is necessary for near-optimal ground-state preparation, showing it is not: two algorithms using only uncontrolled evolution queries achieve matching complexity, and the framework extends to energy and property estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ground-state preparation, energy estimation, and property estimation are fundamental quantum simulation tasks. Existing near-optimal algorithms rely on block encodings or controlled Hamiltonian evolution, raising the question of whether controlled Hamiltonian evolution is necessary or whether the same scaling can be achieved in the weaker access model providing only uncontrolled Hamiltonian evolution.

Method: The paper gives two algorithms accessing the Hamiltonian H only through queries to U_H and its inverse (with ||H|| ≤ α_H, τ=Θ(1/α_H)). The first uses fresh copies of the input state with ground-state weight ≥ η and spectral gap ≥ γ, requiring Õ(α_H/(γη)) queries. The second uses coherent access to the input-state preparation unitary and its inverse, improving to Õ(α_H/(γ√η)). Both rely on a mechanism called the spectral descent principle, starting from |ψ⟩ and progressively descending the energy spectrum toward the ground state until the desired fidelity is reached. The framework is extended to ground-state property and energy estimation with explicit complexity guarantees.

Result: The first algorithm requires Õ(α_H/(γη)) queries; the second achieves Õ(α_H/(γ√η)), matching the known lower bound up to logarithmic factors. This shows algorithms with additional access to controlled Hamiltonian evolution cannot improve the query complexity for ground-state preparation beyond logarithmic factors.

Conclusion: Controlled Hamiltonian evolution is not necessary for near-optimal ground-state preparation: uncontrolled evolution queries suffice to match the lower bound. The spectral descent principle provides a simple, general mechanism that also yields complexity guarantees for ground-state property and energy estimation.

Abstract: Ground-state preparation, energy estimation, and property estimation are fundamental tasks in quantum simulation. Existing ground-state preparation algorithms achieve near-optimal complexity using block encodings or controlled Hamiltonian evolution, raising a natural question: is controlled Hamiltonian evolution necessary, or can the same scaling be attained in the weaker access model that provides access to only uncontrolled Hamiltonian evolution? We resolve this question by giving two algorithms that access the input Hamiltonian $H$ only through queries to $U_H$ and its inverse $U_H^\dagger$, where $\|H\|\leα_H$ and $τ=Θ(1/α_H)$. Given an input state with ground-state weight at least $η$ and a spectral gap of $H$ of at least $γ$, our first algorithm uses fresh copies of the input state and requires $\widetilde O(α_H/(γη))$ queries to $U_H$ and $U_H^\dagger$, while our second algorithm uses coherent access to the input-state preparation unitary and its inverse to improve this complexity to $\widetilde O(α_H/(γ\sqrtη))$. The latter bound matches the known lower bound up to logarithmic factors, showing that algorithms with additional access to controlled Hamiltonian evolution cannot improve the query complexity for ground-state preparation beyond logarithmic factors. Both algorithms are based on a simple mechanism that we call the spectral descent principle, in which the algorithm starts from the input state $|ψ\rangle$ and progressively descends through the energy spectrum, moving closer to the ground state until the desired fidelity is reached. We also extend this framework to ground-state property and energy estimation and give explicit complexity guarantees for both tasks.

</details>


### [36] [First-quantized quantum simulation with non-local potentials by matrix-product-state encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00521)
*Kazuki Tsuoka,Taichi Kosugi,Masari Watanabe,Hirofumi Nishi,Yu-ichiro Matsushita*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes encoding non-local pseudopotentials via matrix product states (MPS) to efficiently implement non-local potentials in first-quantized quantum simulations, achieving reduced circuit depth demonstrated on 1D hydrogen ionization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: First-quantized quantum simulations offer lower space and gate complexities than second-quantized approaches, and pseudopotentials can further reduce gate requirements by decreasing the number of simulated electrons. However, implementing pseudopotentials in quantum circuits is challenging due to their non-local nature.

Method: The authors proposed a method to efficiently implement non-local potentials using matrix product state (MPS) encoding. This approach enables efficient implementation of a Trotter step operator with circuit depth O(deg(V_loc) n^{deg(V_loc)} + 8L n n_atom χ² + 2n²), where deg(V_loc) is the degree of the polynomial local potential, n_atom is the number of atoms, L is the maximum number of projector functions across all atoms, and n is the number of qubits for the binary-encoded wave function in one-dimensional space.

Result: The approach was applied to a model of ionization of a one-dimensional hydrogen atom driven by an ultrashort laser pulse. MPS encoding significantly reduced the required circuit depth compared to a direct unitary decomposition.

Conclusion: MPS encoding provides a pathway for practical first-quantized simulations by efficiently implementing non-local potentials with reduced circuit depth.

Abstract: Quantum simulations based on first-quantized approaches can offer lower space and gate complexities than second-quantized approaches. The gate requirements for these simulations can be further reduced by employing pseudopotentials, a technique widely used in classical quantum chemistry calculations to decrease the number of simulated electrons. However, implementing pseudopotentials in quantum circuits remains challenging due to their non-local nature. In this study, we proposed a method to efficiently implement non-local potentials using matrix product state (MPS) encoding. MPS encoding allows us to efficiently implement a Trotter step operator with a circuit depth of $O(\mathrm{deg}(V_{\mathrm{loc}}) n^{\mathrm{deg}(V_{\mathrm{loc}})} + 8Lnn_{\mathrm{atom}}χ^2 + 2n^2)$, where $\mathrm{deg}(V_{\mathrm{loc}})$ is the degree of the polynomial local potential, $n_{\mathrm{atom}}$ is the number of atoms in the system, $L$ is the maximum number of projector functions across all atoms, and $n$ is the number of qubits used to represent the binary-encoded wave function in one-dimensional space. We demonstrated the effectiveness of this approach by applying it to a model of the ionization of a one-dimensional hydrogen atom driven by an ultrashort laser pulse. Our results showed that MPS encoding significantly reduces the required circuit depth compared to a direct unitary decomposition, providing a pathway for practical first-quantized simulations.

</details>


### [37] [Good Quantum Locally Testable Codes from Lossless Cubical Complexes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00525)
*Itay Cohen,Itai Leigh,Assaf Reiner,Amnon Ta-Shma,Elad Tzalik*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work develops a higher-dimensional lossless expansion approach, introducing a level chain complex on Boolean cube level sets and proving a local-to-global theorem: one-dimensional lossless expansion in directional graphs implies small-set coboundary expansion of the global level complex. As an application, sufficiently imbalanced two-sided lossless four-dimensional cubical complexes yield asymptotically good quantum locally testable codes (qLTCs).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sipser and Spielman built LDPC codes from bipartite spectral or one-sided lossless expanders. In higher dimensions, spectral expansion underpinned asymptotically good classical LTCs and qLDPC codes (Dinur–Evra–Livne–Lubotzky–Mozes; Panteleev–Kalachev), while Lin and Hsieh instead used two-dimensional lossless cubical complexes. This paper aims to push the lossless approach to higher dimensions and to understand the implications of high-dimensional lossless cubical complexes, rather than constructing them directly.

Method: Rather than constructing high-dimensional lossless cubical complexes, the authors investigate their consequences. They associate to a high-dimensional cubical complex a level chain complex, whose chain groups are supported on level sets of the Boolean cube rather than on its cells. The main technical tool is a local-to-global theorem showing that suitable one-dimensional lossless expansion in the directional graphs implies small-set coboundary expansion of the global level complex.

Result: The central technical result is a clean local-to-global theorem for the level chain complex: one-dimensional lossless expansion in directional graphs implies small-set coboundary expansion of the global level complex. Consequently, sufficiently imbalanced, two-sided lossless four-dimensional cubical complexes give rise to asymptotically good quantum locally testable codes.

Conclusion: The higher-dimensional lossless framework, via the level chain complex and its local-to-global principle, connects one-dimensional lossless expansion to global coboundary expansion and yields asymptotically good qLTCs from imbalanced two-sided lossless four-dimensional cubical complexes. The authors expect this local-to-global principle to find further applications.

Abstract: Sipser and Spielman constructed LDPC codes from either bipartite \emph{spectral} expanders or one-sided \emph{lossless} expanders. In higher dimensions, \emph{spectral} expansion similarly played a central role in the constructions of asymptotically good classical LTCs and qLDPC codes by Dinur, Evra, Livne, Lubotzky, and Mozes and by Panteleev and Kalachev. Alternatively, Lin and Hsieh constructed classical LTCs and qLDPC codes from two-dimensional \emph{lossless} cubical complexes.
  In this work we develop the higher-dimensional \emph{lossless} approach. We do not construct the required high-dimensional lossless cubical complexes; rather, we investigate what their existence would imply. We associate with a high-dimensional cubical complex a \emph{level chain complex}, whose chain groups are supported on the level sets of the Boolean cube rather than on its cells. Our main technical contribution is a clean local-to-global theorem for this structure: suitable one-dimensional lossless expansion in the directional graphs implies small-set coboundary expansion of the global level complex. As a consequence, sufficiently imbalanced, two-sided lossless four-dimensional cubical complexes give rise to asymptotically good quantum locally testable codes. We expect the local-to-global principle developed here to have further applications.

</details>


### [38] [Polynomial-time local-unitary equivalence of graph states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00527)
*Yuxuan Zhang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A deterministic polynomial-time algorithm decides local-unitary equivalence of graph states in Õ(n^6.38) bit operations, constructs the unitaries, and counts local-Clifford classes within an LU class.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Local-unitary (LU) equivalence of quantum states—whether two states differ only by independent single-qubit basis changes—has had open complexity status for graph states for over a decade. A prior quasipolynomial algorithm by Claudet and Perdrix left the polynomial-time question unresolved.

Method: Building on Claudet and Perdrix's quasipolynomial algorithm, the enumeration of vertex subsets is replaced by a compact constraint system generated from pairs and triples of vertices; the remaining graph transformation is recovered by solving linear equations over the binary field, with these new steps costing Õ(n^5) bit operations and the inherited graph preprocessing dominating the overall bound.

Result: Decides LU equivalence for graphs on n labelled vertices in Õ(n^6.38) bit operations and constructs exact single-qubit unitaries when equivalent; the number of local-Clifford classes inside any LU class is a power of two computable within the same bound, which also determines whether single-qubit Clifford gates reach every graph state in an LU class and yields a counterexample otherwise. The method further decides LU equivalence of stabilizer codes encoding one logical qubit.

Conclusion: LU equivalence of graph states is decidable in polynomial time, resolving a decade-old open problem, and the approach extends to counting LC classes within LU classes and to stabilizer codes with one logical qubit.

Abstract: Local-unitary (LU) equivalence asks whether two quantum states differ only by independent changes of basis on their qubits. For graph states, whether this relation can be decided in polynomial time has remained open for over a decade. We give a deterministic algorithm that decides LU equivalence for graphs on $n$ labelled vertices in $\widetilde O(n^{6.38})$ bit operations and constructs exact single-qubit unitaries whenever the states are equivalent. Building on Claudet and Perdrix's quasipolynomial algorithm, we replace the enumeration of vertex subsets by a compact system of constraints generated from pairs and triples. The remaining graph transformation is found by solving linear equations over the binary field. These new steps cost $\widetilde O(n^5)$ bit operations; the inherited graph preprocessing sets the overall bound. We also count the local-Clifford (LC) classes of graph states within any LU class: their number is a power of two, computable within the same bound. For any given graph state, this decides whether single-qubit Clifford gates reach every graph state in its LU class, and supplies a counterexample when they do not. The method also decides LU equivalence of stabilizer codes encoding one logical qubit.

</details>


### [39] [Quantum Krylov Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00537)
*Shunji Matsuura,Yoji Kawamura,Joseph Salfi,Satoshi Iso*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Quantum Krylov Learning (QKL) is a restricted-access Hamiltonian learning framework that reconstructs a probe-accessible operator Krylov Jacobi matrix from single-temperature probe autocorrelators, requiring no symmetry, parametric model, or controlled-unitary gates, with an end-to-end polynomial sample-complexity guarantee.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Hamiltonian of a natural quantum system is usually inferred indirectly via proposed microscopic models and comparison with experiment. Direct extraction from measurements is preferable, but in quantum sensing and boundary spectroscopy one only has access through controllable probes rather than the full system. Existing restricted-access Hamiltonian learning protocols typically presuppose a special symmetry, a known parametric form, or controlled operations beyond free evolution, which limits their applicability.

Method: QKL uses single-temperature probe autocorrelators and their operator Krylov structure to extract model-independent spectral information about an unknown system. Instead of fitting a parametrized Hamiltonian, it reconstructs the operator Krylov Jacobi matrix representing the dynamical modes accessible to the probe, assuming the unobserved system is at or near infinite temperature. Under this condition the Jacobi matrix is reconstructed directly from the autocorrelation of a probe observable without oracle access to the Hamiltonian. Required operations are free evolution under the unknown H plus probe state preparation and measurement, so the learning core needs no symmetry, no parametric model, and no controlled-unitary gates.

Result: The paper proves a Lieb--Robinson bound for the operator Krylov chain and derives a data-driven criterion for the required observation window. It also establishes an end-to-end sample complexity guarantee: under generic conditions on the spectral gaps and weights, the number of measurement shots needed to reach spectral precision epsilon is polynomial in 1/epsilon.

Conclusion: QKL provides a model-independent, restricted-access route to Hamiltonian spectral learning from probe autocorrelators, combining a Lieb--Robinson bound, an observation-window criterion, and polynomial sample complexity under generic spectral assumptions, thereby removing the symmetry, parametric-model, and controlled-operation requirements of prior approaches.

Abstract: The Hamiltonian of a natural quantum system is usually determined indirectly, by proposing microscopic models and comparing their predicted observables with experiment. A more direct approach is to extract information about the Hamiltonian from measurements on the system itself. In many settings, however, direct access to the full system is unavailable and measurements can be performed only through controllable probes, as in quantum sensing and boundary spectroscopy. Existing Hamiltonian learning protocols under such restricted access typically require a special symmetry, a known parametric form, or controlled operations beyond free evolution. We introduce Quantum Krylov Learning (QKL), a restricted-access framework that avoids these requirements. QKL uses single temperature probe autocorrelators and their operator Krylov structure to extract model-independent spectral information about the unknown system. Rather than fitting a parametrized Hamiltonian, it reconstructs the operator Krylov Jacobi matrix representing the dynamical modes accessible to the probe. The method assumes that the unobserved system is at, or near, infinite temperature. Under this condition, the Jacobi matrix can be reconstructed directly from the autocorrelation of a probe observable, without oracle access to the Hamiltonian. The required operations are free evolution under the unknown $H$, together with probe state preparation and measurement. The learning core therefore requires no symmetry, no parametric model, and no controlled-unitary gates. We show a Lieb--Robinson bound for the operator Krylov chain and derive a data-driven criterion for the required observation window. We also establish an end-to-end sample complexity guarantee: under generic conditions on the spectral gaps and weights, the number of measurement shots required to achieve spectral precision $\varepsilon$ is polynomial in $1/\varepsilon$.

</details>


### [40] [Unifying and Extending Strong Simulation of Quantum Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00547)
*Floris Geerts,Rihan Hai,Matthias Lanzinger,Reinhard Pichler,Emanuel Sallinger,Daniel Unterberger*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper establishes functional aggregate queries (FAQs) as a unifying language for exact classical quantum circuit simulation, where circuits become sum-product queries evaluated via an InsideOut scheme. This single framework recovers treewidth, rank-width, and Clifford-circuit tractability, and introduces a new criterion—tensor layout symmetry width—that captures tractable regimes beyond existing parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical simulation of quantum circuits has produced many different tractability guarantees (treewidth, rank-width, Clifford circuits), but these results come from disparate formalisms. The paper seeks a single unifying algorithmic foundation that explains and connects these distinct sources of simulation tractability within one scheme, and that can systematically reveal new tractable regimes.

Method: The authors model a quantum circuit as a functional aggregate query: factors encode gates, internal wire variables are aggregated (marginalized), and free boundary variables index transition amplitudes. Simulation is performed by an InsideOut evaluation scheme, where the query specifies what is computed while the evaluation plan, semiring, and representation of intermediate factors determine the cost. Different factor representations (explicit, compressed, affine-quadratic) and semirings instantiate different tractability guarantees.

Result: The framework recovers known results: with explicit factor representations, FAQ evaluation recovers the treewidth bound for tensor-network contraction and gives finer sparsity-sensitive bounds via fractional covers; over a formal phase semiring, compressed intermediates recover rank-width-based simulation for compatible quadratic phase representations; and for Clifford circuits, affine-quadratic factors are closed under multiplication and marginalization, remaining polynomial in size, giving polynomial-time exact amplitude computation with no bounded-width assumption. It also yields a new criterion, tensor layout symmetry width, which combines local cut-rank with separator symmetry via exact tree-tensor representations; the authors give a constructive evaluation bound and exhibit a circuit family with bounded tensor layout symmetry width but unbounded phase-graph rank-width and circuit line-graph treewidth.

Conclusion: Representation-aware FAQ evaluation serves as a common algorithmic foundation for classical quantum circuit simulation and provides a systematic route to identifying new tractable regimes, as demonstrated by the tensor layout symmetry width parameter that strictly extends existing width-based tractability criteria.

Abstract: We establish functional aggregate queries (FAQs) as a unifying language for exact classical simulation of quantum circuits. A circuit becomes a sum-product query: factors encode gates, internal wire variables are aggregated, and free boundary variables index transition amplitudes. The central insight is that distinct sources of simulation tractability can be exploited within the same InsideOut evaluation scheme. The query specifies what is computed; the evaluation plan, semiring, and representation of intermediate factors determine the cost.
  This view unifies structural and algebraic simulation guarantees. With explicit factor representations, FAQ evaluation recovers the treewidth bound for tensor-network contraction and yields finer sparsity-sensitive bounds via fractional covers. Over a formal phase semiring, compressed intermediate factors recover rank-width-based simulation for compatible quadratic phase representations. For Clifford circuits, affine-quadratic factors are closed under multiplication and marginalization and remain polynomial in size, yielding polynomial-time exact amplitude computation without any bounded-width assumption.
  Beyond these recoveries, the framework yields a new tractability criterion: tensor layout symmetry width. This parameter combines local cut-rank with separator symmetry through exact tree-tensor representations. We give a constructive evaluation bound and exhibit a circuit family with bounded tensor layout symmetry width but unbounded phase-graph rank-width and circuit line-graph treewidth. These results establish representation-aware FAQ evaluation as a common algorithmic foundation for classical simulation and a systematic route to new tractable regimes.

</details>


### [41] [A polynomial-time classical sampler for noisy quantum circuits from statistical mechanics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00548)
*Jon Nelson,Joel Rajakumar,Chao Yin,Yifan F. Zhang,Michael J. Gullans*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proves that geometrically local circuits with unital gates and single-qubit depolarizing noise become classically simulable at depths scaling only as Ω(p⁻¹ log p⁻¹), independent of system size, using a polymer-model mapping, cluster expansion, and hypercontractivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical simulation of noisy quantum circuits helps delimit where quantum advantage is possible. Prior classical sampling algorithms for general circuits required depth growing logarithmically with system size, so noise needed to trivialize the global state. The authors aim to show a much weaker condition—local noise accumulation at system-size-independent depth—already suffices for efficient classical sampling.

Method: They consider geometrically local circuits built from unital operations with interspersed single-qubit depolarizing noise of strength p. They map the circuit's output state to a polymer model in statistical mechanics, then combine a convergent cluster expansion with hypercontractivity of the depolarizing channel to establish approximate classical samplability.

Result: They prove that any such geometrically local, unital circuit with single-qubit depolarizing noise at strength p can be approximately sampled by a polynomial-time classical computer once the depth reaches Ω(p⁻¹ log p⁻¹). This depth is independent of system size, improving on previous results requiring depth growing logarithmically in system size.

Conclusion: Local accumulation of noise at depth independent of system size is already enough to enable efficient classical sampling. This tightens the boundary of quantum advantage for noisy circuits and provides a rigorous classical-simulation guarantee via statistical-mechanical techniques.

Abstract: Developing classical simulation algorithms for noisy quantum circuits is essential to delineating the limits of quantum advantage. Existing classical sampling approaches for general circuits require circuit depths to grow logarithmically with system size, so that noise drives the global output state close to a trivial state. Here we show that local accumulation of noise at a depth independent of system size is already sufficient. Specifically, we prove that any geometrically local circuit composed of unital operations and interspersed with single-qubit depolarizing noise of strength $p$ after $Ω(p^{-1} \log p^{-1})$ depth can be approximately sampled from a polynomial-time classical computer. Our proof maps the output state of the circuit to a polymer model in statistical mechanics, and combines a convergent cluster expansion with hypercontractivity of the depolarizing channel.

</details>


### [42] [Optimal two-mode bosonic loss codes from finite group symmetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00561)
*Argyris Giannisis Manes,Mahadevan Subramanian,Liang Jiang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies photon-loss correction for fixed-total-photon-number qubit encodings in two bosonic modes, uncovering finite-group structure in optimal codes and deriving Knill-Laflamme conditions for finite-group-invariant codes, yielding analytic constructions, maximum loss distance certificates, and a conjectured infinite-distance code family with minimal photon number.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Photon loss is the dominant noise process in bosonic quantum hardware such as superconducting cavities. Fixed-total-photon-number qubit encodings in two bosonic modes preserve the loss-detection advantage of dual-rail qubits while enabling photon-loss correction. However, optimal code constructions and their fundamental distance limits are not well understood, motivating a systematic search and analytical characterization.

Method: Numerical optimization of entanglement fidelity over arbitrary encoders and decoders for total photon numbers 4≤n≤25, revealing finite-group structure in all best-found codes. Derivation of necessary-and-sufficient Knill-Laflamme conditions for arbitrary finite-group-invariant codes, reducing construction to equations on representation multiplicity spaces. Explicit construction of the minimum number of operators needed to impose all loss-correction constraints for every finite subgroup of SU(2) and every tensor rank. Exact MacWilliams-Farkas certificates for loss distance, plus identification and construction of a binary-polyhedral sequence with photon number n_d=⌈(3d²+1)/4⌉ through distance d=10, and a symmetry-reduced constraint count.

Result: Of the best-found codes, 11 correspond to two-dimensional irreducible representations and 11 to reducible ones. The analytic constructions reproduce most numerical codes and predict constructions missed by the initial search. Exact MacWilliams-Farkas certificates show maximum possible loss distance in 21 of 22 sectors, and the distance bound is proven monotone in total photon number. The constructed (n,d)=(28,6),(49,8),(76,10) codes give the smallest reported n for their respective distances, and all members through d=9 attain the fixed-n LP distance bound.

Conclusion: The constructed codes, exact certificates, and symmetry-reduced constraint count provide evidence for an infinite code family conjectured to attain every loss distance at the minimum photon number, linking finite-group representation theory to optimal bosonic quantum error correction.

Abstract: Photon loss is a dominant noise process in bosonic quantum hardware, including superconducting cavities. Fixed-total-photon-number qubit encodings in two bosonic modes retain the loss-detection advantage of dual-rail qubits while supporting photon loss correction. Optimizing entanglement fidelity in this setting for $4\leq n\leq25$ over arbitrary encoders and decoders reveals finite-group structure in every best-found code: 11 correspond to two-dimensional irreducible representations and 11 to reducible ones. Motivated by this emergence, we derive the necessary-and-sufficient Knill-Laflamme conditions for arbitrary finite-group-invariant codes, reducing their construction to equations on representation multiplicity spaces. For every finite subgroup of $SU(2)$ and every tensor rank, we explicitly construct the minimum number of operators required to impose all corresponding loss-correction constraints. These results produce analytic counterparts to most numerical codes and predict constructions missed by the initial search. Exact MacWilliams-Farkas certificates show that our codes achieve the maximum possible loss distance in 21 of the 22 sectors, and we prove that the resulting distance bound is monotone in total photon number. Beyond the scan, we identify a binary-polyhedral sequence with photon number $n_d=\lceil(3d^2+1)/4\rceil$ and construct each corresponding code through distance $d=10$. To our knowledge, the constructed $(n,d)=(28,6),(49,8),(76,10)$ codes give the smallest reported $n$ for their respective distances; all members through $d=9$ attain the fixed-$n$ LP distance bound. Together, these constructions, certificates, and a symmetry-reduced constraint count provide evidence for an infinite code family conjectured to attain every distance at the minimum photon number.

</details>


### [43] [Characterizing unitaries via quasi-process functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00579)
*Nasra Daher Ahmed,Ravi Kunjwal*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Every n-qubit unitary can be canonically represented as a quasi-process function unitary (qPFU); this causality-inspired representation yields two sufficient criteria (MECC and Unambiguity), circuit-depth bounds via Hamming-graph clique partitioning, and a unitary purification recipe for quantum causality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper connects two seemingly unrelated areas: the well-known decomposition of n-qubit unitaries into controlled single-qubit unitaries, and quasi-process functions from the study of indefinite causal order. It aims to understand quantum nonlocality without entanglement (QNLWE) via unitaries encoding the trade-off between causal order and locality, and to provide a versatile, causality-inspired tool for analyzing quantum circuit structure.

Method: The control conditions of a controlled-single-qubit decomposition are shown to define a quasi-process function, giving a canonical quasi-process function unitary (qPFU) representation. From this, two sufficient (not necessary) criteria for a unitary to be a process function unitary (PFU) are derived: mutual exclusivity of control conditions (MECC) and Unambiguity. The inequivalence of these criteria is proven. Circuit depth of MECC unitaries is characterized through clique-partitioning problems on Hamming graphs, and this correspondence yields depth bounds. A construction of unitary purifications of the associated process function is also provided.

Result: A canonical qPFU representation of every n-qubit unitary is established. Two sufficient criteria, MECC and Unambiguity, are obtained for PFU status, shown to be inequivalent (neither implies the other). Circuit depth of MECC unitaries is characterized via clique-partitioning on Hamming graphs, giving bounds on the depth of n-qubit MECC unitaries. MECC unitaries also yield a recipe for unitary purifications of the associated process function, producing a large class of unitary processes relevant to quantum causality.

Conclusion: The causality-inspired qPFU representation provides a canonical framework linking unitary decomposition, quantum nonlocality without entanglement, and process functions/classical causal loops. It offers criteria for PFU identification, depth bounds, and a purification construction, making it a versatile tool for analyzing quantum circuit structure in broader domains.

Abstract: It is well-known that every n-qubit unitary can be decomposed into a product of controlled single-qubit unitaries. We show that the control conditions in such a decomposition naturally define a quasi-process function, an object that originally came up in the study of indefinite causal order. Here quasi-process functions yield a representation of every n-qubit unitary in a canonical form, namely, as a quasi-process function unitary (qPFU). We use this representation to show how quantum nonlocality without entanglement (QNLWE) can be understood in terms of unitaries that encode the trade-off between causal order and locality noted in R. Kunjwal and Ä. Baumeler, arXiv:2202.00440. Specifically, we obtain two sufficient (but not necessary) criteria---mutual exclusivity of control conditions (MECC) and Unambiguity---for a unitary to be a process function unitary (PFU), i.e., it admits a qPFU representation where the quasi-process function is a process function, an object that can model paradox-free classical causal loops. We also show the inequivalence of these criteria, neither implying the other. We then show that the circuit depth of MECC unitaries admits a characterization in terms of clique-partitioning problems on Hamming graphs and use this correspondence to provide bounds on the circuit depth of n-qubit MECC unitaries. Finally, we show how MECC unitaries provide a recipe for constructing a unitary purification of the associated process function, thereby providing a large class of unitary processes of interest in quantum causality. The generality of this causality-inspired representation of unitaries makes it a versatile tool for analyzing the structure of quantum circuits in other domains.

</details>


### [44] [Quantum key distribution using generalized contextuality against post-quantum eavesdroppers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00595)
*Daniel Centeno,Roberto D. Baldijão,Maria Ciudad Alañón,Yujie Zhang,Pedro Lauand,Elie Wolfe*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether generalized contextuality in prepare-and-measure experiments can certify secret keys analogously to Bell nonlocality, treating operational equivalences as theory-independent constraints against an unrestricted (post-quantum) eavesdropper.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bell nonlocality enables device-independent key certification even against no-signaling-constrained eavesdroppers, but relies on entanglement and spacelike separation. The authors ask whether generalized contextuality—a nonclassicality notion accessible in single-system prepare-and-measure setups—can play an analogous cryptographic role.

Method: Alice and Bob establish operational equivalences among local preparation and measurement procedures, treated as theory-independent constraints any channel intervention (including Eve's) must respect. Eve is otherwise unrestricted (not assumed quantum). The optimal guessing probability against individual attacks is characterized as the solution of a finite linear program, yielding a worst-case asymptotic key rate from observed statistics and operational equivalences alone.

Result: Contextuality is proven necessary for a positive key rate but not sufficient: the (3,2)-parity-oblivious random access code, which yields secret key against a quantum eavesdropper, yields zero certifiable key rate against a post-quantum one. Conversely, operational equivalences lacking analogues among no-signaling constraints can strictly increase the asymptotic key rate, illustrated via the prepare-and-measure CHSH protocol with an aligned key-generating setting.

Conclusion: Generalized contextuality can certify secret keys under an adversarial model where operational equivalences constrain even post-quantum eavesdroppers.
