However, contextuality alone is neither sufficient nor the sole resource: specific operational equivalences beyond the no-signaling analogue can enhance key rates, while certain contextuality-based schemes provide no advantage against post-quantum adversaries.

Abstract: Bell nonlocality allows secret key to be certified without trusting the internal operation of the devices, and even against eavesdroppers constrained only by the no-signaling principle. We ask whether generalized contextuality, a notion of nonclassicality available in prepare-and-measure experiments on a single system, can play an analogous role. In our approach, Alice and Bob first establish operational equivalences among their local preparation and measurement procedures, which are then treated as theory-independent constraints that any physical intervention on the communication channel, including that of an adversary, must respect. Eve is otherwise unrestricted, that is, her intervention on the transmitted system is not assumed to be described by quantum theory. Within this adversarial model, we show that Eve's optimal guessing probability against individual attacks is the solution of a finite linear program, and hence that a worst-case asymptotic key rate follows from the observed statistics and the operational equivalences alone. We prove that contextuality is necessary for a positive key rate. However, we also show that it is not sufficient. Concretely, the (3,2)-parity-oblivious random access code, known to yield secret key against a quantum eavesdropper, yields none certifiable key rate against a post-quantum one. Conversely, operational equivalences with no analogue among no-signaling constraints can strictly increase the asymptotic key rate. We illustrate this with the prepare-and-measure version of the standard CHSH protocol with an aligned key-generating setting.

</details>


### [45] [Late-Time Power Laws in Fluorescence Decays: Quantum Decay or Alternative Mechanisms?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00603)
*Francesco Giacosa,Anna Kolbus,Krzysztof Kyziol,Magdalena Plodowska,Milena Piotrowska,Karol Szary,Arthur Vereijken*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Time-resolved fluorescence spectroscopy data from erythrosine B and eosine Y are used to test whether late-time power-law decays in survival probability arise from quantum-mechanical unstable-state dynamics or from alternative statistical and energy-migration mechanisms; the fitted non-quantum parametrizations fail to reproduce the sharp exponential-to-power-law transition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In quantum mechanics, an unstable system's survival probability is predicted to show a late-time power-law tail, but similar behavior can emerge in complex systems through nonextensive statistical models, continuous lifetime distribution models, and energy-migration models applied to fluorescence. This work aims to distinguish these competing interpretations by confronting them with experimental fluorescence decay data.

Method: Time-resolved fluorescence spectroscopy experiments were performed using erythrosine B and eosine Y as fluorophores. The measured survival probability data were fitted with specific parametrizations from quantum-mechanical theory and from alternative descriptions, including nonextensive statistical models, continuous lifetime distribution models, and energy-migration models.

Result: The alternative statistical and energy-migration parametrizations fitted to the data cannot reproduce certain features expected within the quantum-mechanical description, notably the sharp transition from the exponential decay regime to the power-law regime.

Conclusion: The sharp exponential-to-power-law transition observed in the fluorescence data favors the quantum-mechanical interpretation of the late-time survival probability over the tested nonextensive statistical, continuous lifetime distribution, and energy-migration explanations.

Abstract: In quantum mechanics, the properties of an unstable system are closely related to its corresponding energy distribution. One of the predictions of the theory is the emergence of a late-time power law in the survival probability $P(t)$ of the unstable state. However, in complex systems, similar effects may emerge due to other phenomena. This is the case for nonextensive statistical models, for some continuous lifetime distribution models, and also for certain energy-migration models applied to fluorescence. In this work, these alternative interpretations are confronted with data from time-resolved fluorescence spectroscopy experiments with erythrosine B and eosine Y as fluorophores. It turns out that some of the features expected within the quantum-mechanical description, such as the sharp transition from the exponential to the power-law regime, cannot be reproduced by the specific statistical and energy-migration parametrizations fitted in the analysis.

</details>


### [46] [Engineering non-ergodic properties in two dimensional quantum many-body systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00635)
*Nyayabanta Swain,Gabriel Lemarié,Shaffique Adam*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Using an eigenstate-to-Hamiltonian construction, the authors engineer 2D square-lattice spin Hamiltonians whose interaction geometry (axial vs. diagonal) controls Hilbert-space structure, yielding tunable crossover from ergodic to non-ergodic dynamics and signatures of quantum many-body scars.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most known non-ergodic quantum many-body systems are found via intuition, analogy, or numerical search rather than systematic Hamiltonian design. This leaves few general methods for engineering robust non-ergodic dynamics in disorder-free interacting systems, especially in higher dimensions where enhanced connectivity generally disfavors ergodicity breaking.

Method: The authors employ an eigenstate-to-Hamiltonian construction. Starting from a common parent model and input eigenstate, they systematically construct two 2D quantum spin Hamiltonians on the square lattice that differ in interaction geometry: axial versus diagonal.

Result: Interaction geometry and coupling pattern control Hilbert-space structure and dynamics. The axial model forms a connected Hilbert-space network and shows an anisotropy-driven crossover from ergodic to non-ergodic behavior via resonance suppression. The diagonal model exhibits Hilbert-space fragmentation and, within its largest irreducible sector, quantum many-body scar signatures—towers of highly localized, low-entanglement eigenstates and long-lived coherent revivals.

Conclusion: The work establishes a direct link between Hamiltonian design, Hilbert-space network structure, and emergent non-ergodic dynamics, offering a systematic route to engineer tunable non-ergodic properties in disorder-free higher-dimensional quantum many-body systems.

Abstract: Non-ergodic quantum many-body dynamics offers a route to persistent quantum coherence far from equilibrium, beyond the conventional expectations of thermalizing statistical mechanics. Most known examples have been identified through intuition, analogy, or numerical search, rather than by systematic Hamiltonian design. This leaves few general methods for engineering robust non-ergodic dynamics in disorder-free interacting quantum many-body systems, especially in higher dimensions where enhanced connectivity generally disfavors ergodicity breaking. Here, we use an eigenstate-to-Hamiltonian construction approach to systematically engineer two-dimensional quantum spin Hamiltonians with tunable non-ergodic properties. Starting from a common parent model and input eigenstate, we construct two target Hamiltonians on the square lattice that differ in their interaction geometry: axial versus diagonal. We show that the resulting interaction geometry and coupling pattern control the Hilbert-space structure and dynamics. The axial model forms a connected Hilbert-space network and exhibits an anisotropy-driven crossover from ergodic to non-ergodic behavior through the suppression of resonances. In contrast, the diagonal model exhibits Hilbert-space fragmentation and, within its largest irreducible sector, signatures of quantum many-body scar dynamics including towers of highly localized, low-entanglement eigenstates, and long-lived coherent revivals. These results establish a direct link between Hamiltonian design, Hilbert-space network structure, and emergent non-ergodic dynamics.

</details>


### [47] [Operator-language Feynman rules for driven-dissipative quantum systems: from mean field to non-Gaussian photon correlations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00667)
*Peter Ehlers,Phi Hung Nguyen,Daniel Soh*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops Feynman rules for Lindblad master equations in quantum-optics operator language, splitting the expansion into a kinematic shift algebra on Wick-ordered eigenoperators and a dynamical resolvent part, enabling perturbative propagators for Gaussian generators and beyond.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lindblad master equations for driven, dissipative quantum-optical systems are rarely exactly solvable, and existing perturbative or closure schemes (e.g., Gaussian cumulant closures) fail to capture genuinely non-Gaussian correlations. The authors seek a systematic diagrammatic perturbation theory that retains the operator structure of quantum optics and works across cavities, atoms, and lattices.

Method: They construct Feynman rules built on a fixed algebra of elementary operator moves with closed-form propagators for perturbation theory about an exactly solvable (mostly Gaussian) generator L0. The expansion separates into (i) a kinematic part, the shift algebra on Wick-ordered eigenoperators O_mn = :(c†)^m c^n:, yielding a vertex table for a given interaction, and (ii) a dynamical part entering only via the resolvent of L0, which is diagonal and additive for any stable, diagonalizable Gaussian L0 (thermal and squeezed reservoirs, correlated decay), and also applicable to two-level emitters. The method extends to the counting-field-tilted generator for photon-counting cumulants.

Result: In a driven Kerr cavity the loop expansion coincides with the semiclassical expansion and is asymptotic. For a strongly driven two-level atom, dressed lines replace a finite-radius series by one line per Mollow component, accurate deep into saturation. For Kerr rings and chains, cost is polynomial in the number of sites at fixed order. On a three-site ring, the diagrams recover the non-Gaussian part of the three-photon correlation missed by the Gaussian cumulant closure; to tenth order they achieve the smallest g^(3) error among compared methods for U ≤ 0.25κ, at cost below a fourth-order closure, though third- and fourth-order closures are more accurate at moderate coupling. Extended to the counting-field-tilted generator, the rules give photon-counting cumulants of a disordered eight-site chain beyond direct diagonalization; quantum-jump trajectories at three couplings agree within 1.6 standard errors, and at the strongest coupling within one, where they place the third cumulant six standard errors above the Gaussian value.

Conclusion: The proposed operator-language Feynman rules provide a systematic, scalable perturbative framework for Lindblad master equations, unifying kinematic and dynamical structure, capturing non-Gaussian correlations beyond Gaussian closure, delivering accurate Mollow-resolved atomic responses and photon-counting statistics for extended lattice systems, at polynomial cost in system size.

Abstract: We construct Feynman rules for Lindblad master equations in the operator language of quantum optics: a fixed algebra of elementary operator moves and closed-form propagators for perturbation theory about an exactly solvable, in most cases Gaussian, generator. The expansion separates into a kinematic part (the shift algebra on the Wick-ordered eigenoperators $O_{mn}=:(c^\dagger)^m c^n:$, from which the vertex table for a specified interaction is assembled) and a dynamical part that enters only through the resolvent of the unperturbed generator, diagonal and additive for any stable, diagonalizable Gaussian $L_0$, including thermal and squeezed reservoirs and correlated decay, and equally available for a two-level emitter, driven or undriven. In the driven Kerr cavity the loop expansion is the semiclassical expansion and the series is asymptotic. In a strongly driven two-level atom, dressed lines replace a series of finite radius by one accurate deep into saturation, one line per Mollow component. For Kerr rings and chains the cost is polynomial in the number of sites at fixed perturbative order. On a three-site ring the diagrams recover the non-Gaussian part of the three-photon correlation that the Gaussian cumulant closure lacks; carried to tenth order they give the smallest $g^{(3)}$ error of the methods compared for $U\leq0.25κ$ at a cost below that of a fourth-order closure, and third- and fourth-order closures are more accurate at moderate coupling. The same rules, extended to the counting-field-tilted generator, give the photon-counting cumulants of a disordered eight-site chain beyond direct diagonalization; quantum-jump trajectories at three couplings agree with them to within $1.6$ standard errors, within one at the strongest coupling, where they place the third cumulant six standard errors above the Gaussian value.

</details>


### [48] [Quantum Circuit Pruning: From NISQ Architectures to Fault-Tolerant Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00669)
*Pau Escofet,Carmen G. Almudéver,Sergi Abadal,Eduard Alarcón*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A routing-aware pruning strategy removes parametric two-qubit gates when their computational value is outweighed by routing/fidelity costs, yielding up to 48.6% gate reduction and 47.7% fidelity gains on NISQ, with feasibility in FTQC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parametric two-qubit gates incur fidelity and routing costs; some contribute little computationally, especially for distant qubits, so a unified pruning criterion across NISQ and FTQC is needed.

Method: Each two-qubit gate is evaluated by comparing an architecture-independent lower bound on the fidelity cost of omission against a hardware-specific routing execution cost model. An analytical characterization of the pruning boundary is derived as a function of rotation angle, qubit distance, and hardware noise.

Result: Benchmark simulations show up to 48.6% gate count reduction and up to 47.7% fidelity improvement in the NISQ regime; an FT feasibility analysis indicates pruning remains relevant across code distances and physical error rates.

Conclusion: Routing-aware pruning is a practical, unified compilation strategy applicable across the quantum computing stack.

Abstract: We present a routing-aware pruning strategy for quantum circuits that selectively removes parametric two-qubit gates whose computational contribution is outweighed by the fidelity cost of their execution, and assess it across both Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) and fault-tolerant quantum computing (FTQC) architectures. Each two-qubit gate of a circuit is evaluated by comparing an architecture-independent lower bound on the fidelity cost of omitting the gate against a hardware-specific model for the routing execution cost in each setting. We additionally provide an analytical characterization of the pruning boundary as a function of rotation angle, qubit distance, and hardware noise. Simulations on benchmark circuits demonstrate gate count reductions of up to 48.6% and fidelity improvements of up to 47.7% in the NISQ regime, while a feasibility analysis on FT hardware indicates that pruning is expected to remain relevant across a range of code distances and physical error rates. These results establish routing-aware pruning as a practical and unified compilation strategy across the quantum computing stack.

</details>


### [49] [Beyond Feasibility: Finite-Depth Accessibility in Constrained QAOA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00736)
*Rushikesh Ubale,Gregory T. Byrd,Yasar Mulani,Sangram Deshpande*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces finite-depth accessible sets $R_p(M,x_0)$ for constrained QAOA, proposes an objective ceiling $f_R^\star$ and a reachable-quality diagnostic $Q_R$, and shows empirically that $Q_R$ correlates strongly with realized shallow-QAOA performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Constraint-preserving mixers keep QAOA within a feasible subspace, but feasibility and global mixer connectivity alone do not determine which feasible configurations are actually reachable by a particular shallow circuit with finite depth and a fixed mixer schedule. A diagnostic is needed that captures objective-relevant accessibility at the level of the executed circuit.

Method: The authors study constrained QAOA as an executed circuit with a specified feasible initial state, finite depth, and finite schedule of mixer interactions. They define the finite-depth accessible set $R_p(M,x_0)$, show that the ideal QAOA circuit's computational-basis support lies within it, and derive a parameter-independent objective ceiling $f_R^\star$ plus a normalized reachable-quality diagnostic $Q_R$. They evaluate the framework using resource-matched block-local XY mixer schedules on constrained portfolio optimization and synthetic block-constrained quadratic problems, including controlled, dependence-aware, and compilation-only analyses.

Result: Across 207 favorable-tail CVaR QAOA configurations, $Q_R$ shows the strongest association with realized shallow-QAOA performance among the structural diagnostics considered, with Pearson and Spearman correlations of 0.7003 and 0.8057; the relationship remains positive under controlled and dependence-aware analyses. The distinction persists across changes in feasible initialization, portfolio size, and objective family. A compilation-only study shows that for dense portfolio Hamiltonians, phase-separator routing dominates the compiled two-qubit resource scale, and mixer schedules with comparable compiled two-qubit cost can yield markedly different finite-depth accessible quality.

Conclusion: Objective-relevant finite-depth accessibility, captured by $Q_R$, is a useful complementary diagnostic for resource-bounded constrained QAOA, distinct from feasibility and global mixer connectivity.

Abstract: Constraint-preserving mixers keep QAOA within a feasible subspace, but feasibility and global mixer connectivity do not determine which feasible configurations are available to a particular shallow circuit. We study constrained QAOA at the level of the circuit actually executed: a specified feasible initial state, finite depth, and finite schedule of mixer interactions. We define the finite-depth accessible set $R_p(M,x_0)$ and show that the computational-basis support of the ideal QAOA circuit is contained within it. This gives a parameter-independent objective ceiling $f_R^\star=\max_{x\in R_p}f(x)$ and a normalized reachable-quality diagnostic $Q_R$, separating accessible volume from the objective quality contained within it.
  We evaluate this framework using resource-matched block-local XY mixer schedules for constrained portfolio optimization and synthetic block-constrained quadratic problems. Across 207 favorable-tail CVaR QAOA configurations, $Q_R$ shows the strongest association with realized shallow-QAOA performance among the structural diagnostics considered, with Pearson and Spearman correlations of $0.7003$ and $0.8057$; the relationship remains positive under controlled and dependence-aware analyses. The distinction persists across changes in feasible initialization, portfolio size, and objective family.
  A compilation-only study further shows that, for the dense portfolio Hamiltonians considered here, phase-separator routing dominates the compiled two-qubit resource scale, while mixer schedules with comparable compiled two-qubit cost can exhibit markedly different finite-depth accessible quality. These results motivate objective-relevant finite-depth accessibility as a complementary diagnostic for resource-bounded constrained QAOA.

</details>


### [50] [Negative quasiprobabilities redistribute (and complex ones reduce) the information in unabsorbed photons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00740)
*Jonte R. Hance*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Negative Kirkwood–Dirac quasiprobabilities do not enable interferometric or readout advantages for interaction-free measurement; for a single photon, Fisher information per absorbed photon never exceeds the direct-transmission value, and the imaginary parts of the distribution are output-basis dependent, admitting a fully real, optimal representation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Negative Kirkwood–Dirac quasiprobabilities have been credited with the quantum advantage of interaction-free measurement and postselected metrology. This paper tests that claim on the task interaction-free measurement was designed for: learning about a sample while letting it absorb as little light as possible.

Method: The Kirkwood–Dirac distribution of path and output is used to characterize the linear response to a weak sample, with its real part responding to absorption and its imaginary part to phase. The Fisher information about transmittance per absorbed photon is computed for a pure single photon passing once through an arbitrary interferometer and readout.

Result: For a pure single photon through any interferometer, the Fisher information about transmittance per absorbed photon equals the direct-transmission value minus a sum of squared imaginary parts: no interferometer or readout beats direct transmission. A basis exists in which every term is real, which is optimal. A single photon gains information per absorbed photon only through a phase response—requiring a thick or inhomogeneously broadened sample, or many passes when absorptions go uncounted, as shown by a critically coupled resonator.

Conclusion: Negativity plays no role beyond concentrating information in weakly lit outputs. For detecting a weak absorber, negativity gives a single-shot advantage that does not accumulate over multiple trials; advantage instead accumulates in the presence of outputs that are dark without the object, a case that requires no negativity.

Abstract: Negative Kirkwood--Dirac quasiprobabilities are credited with the quantum advantage of interaction-free measurement and postselected metrology. We test that claim on the task interaction-free measurement was designed for: learning about a sample letting it absorb as little light as possible. The Kirkwood--Dirac distribution of path and output gives the linear response to a weak sample: its real part responds to absorption, its imaginary part to a phase. For a pure single photon passing once through any interferometer, the Fisher information about transmittance per absorbed photon is the direct-transmission value minus a sum of squared imaginary parts: no interferometer or readout beats direct transmission. Negativity plays no role beyond concentrating information in weakly lit outputs. The imaginary parts of the Kirkwood-Dirac distribution depend on the output basis here, not on the sample, so a basis exists in which every term is real, which is optimal. A single photon gains information per absorbed photon only through a phase response, which for an optical line needs a thick or inhomogeneously broadened sample, or through many passes when absorptions go uncounted, as shown by a critically coupled resonator. For detecting a weak absorber, negativity gives a single-shot advantage that does not accumulate over multiple trials; advantage instead accumulates when there are outputs which are dark without the object, a case which needs no negativity.

</details>


### [51] [esQueranto: Differentiable Structured Quantum Light for Automated Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00750)
*Marcello Armezzani,Tareq Jaouni,Pontus Lindgren,Sören Arlt,Mario Krenn,Xuemei Gu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces esQueranto, a differentiable JAX-based simulator unifying photon-number quantum optics with structured-light propagation, enabling AI-driven experiment design and automated discovery in quantum optics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: For centuries, experiment design has been exclusively human. A new paradigm is emerging where AI and computational methods design experiments themselves. Realizing automated scientific discovery requires simulators expressive enough to represent diverse physical processes and couplings from which new experiments can be constructed. Existing simulators lack a unified description bridging quantum-optical states and structured-light propagation.

Method: The authors present esQueranto, a differentiable software implemented in JAX that brings photon-number quantum optics and structured-light propagation into a common description. This allows spatial evolution of light to directly influence non-classical quantum states and their interference. JAX provides automatic differentiation and hardware-accelerated evaluation for optimization and automated search.

Result: The framework is demonstrated across a broad range of quantum-optical applications that exercise complementary aspects of its physical description, showing it can model coupled quantum and structured-light phenomena within a single differentiable simulator.

Conclusion: By combining quantum and structured-light physics in one differentiable simulator, esQueranto takes an important step toward the dream of a foundational simulator in physics, supporting automated experiment design and scientific discovery.

Abstract: Uncovering new phenomena in nature requires experiments. For centuries, their design has been exclusively a human endeavor. Today, a new paradigm is emerging in which artificial intelligence and computational methods can design experiments themselves. Realizing this form of automated scientific discovery requires simulators that are sufficiently expressive to represent the diverse physical processes and couplings from which new experiments can be constructed. In this direction, we introduce \textsc{esQueranto}, a differentiable software that brings photon-number quantum optics and structured-light propagation into a common description, allowing the spatial evolution of light to directly influence non-classical quantum states and their interference. \textsc{esQueranto} is implemented in JAX, providing automatic differentiation and hardware-accelerated evaluation for optimization and automated search. We demonstrate the framework across a broad range of quantum-optical applications that exercise complementary aspects of its physical description. By combining quantum and structured-light physics within a single differentiable simulator, \textsc{esQueranto} takes an important step towards the dream of a foundational simulator in physics.

</details>


### [52] [Conditioning on Subalgebras - Entropy Duality and Generalized Quantum Stein's Lemma in von Neumann Algebras](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00772)
*Bjarne Bergh,Li Gao,Mizanur Rahaman*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper establishes duality relations for conditional entropies (Umegaki, sandwiched-, and Petz-Rényi divergences) relative to subalgebras of arbitrary von Neumann algebras, extending finite-dimensional results to infinite dimensions and general subalgebras, and applies this to an infinite-dimensional quantum Stein's lemma for composite hypothesis testing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing duality relations for conditional entropies are largely restricted to tensor-product subsystems in finite dimensions. There is a need to extend these dualities to general subalgebras and to the fully infinite-dimensional setting, and to provide an operational interpretation of relative entropy to a subalgebra beyond finite dimensions.

Method: The authors derive duality relations for conditional entropies relative to subalgebras of arbitrary von Neumann algebras, covering the Umegaki relative entropy, sandwiched Rényi divergences, and Petz-Rényi divergences. They then apply these dualities to composite quantum hypothesis testing.

Result: Duality relations are established that extend familiar conditional-entropy dualities from tensor-product subsystems to general subalgebras in finite dimensions, and lift the framework to the infinite-dimensional setting. An infinite-dimensional extension of quantum Stein's lemma is obtained for the case where the alternative hypothesis is the state space of a finite-index subalgebra.

Conclusion: The relative entropy to a subalgebra admits an operational interpretation as the optimal asymptotic error exponent in composite quantum hypothesis testing, marking the first such result beyond finite dimensions.

Abstract: We establish duality relations for conditional entropies relative to subalgebras of arbitrary von Neumann algebras, including the Umegaki relative entropy as well as the sandwiched- and Petz-Rényi divergences. Even in finite dimensions, these results extend the familiar dualities for conditional entropies from tensor-product subsystems to general subalgebras, while also lifting the framework to the fully infinite-dimensional setting. As an application, we establish an infinite-dimensional extension of quantum Stein's lemma when the alternative hypothesis is the state space of a finite-index subalgebra, which gives an operational interpretation of the relative entropy to a subalgebra as the optimal asymptotic error exponent in composite quantum hypothesis testing-the first beyond finite dimensions.

</details>


### [53] [Real-Time Adaptive Filtering and the Boxcar Limit in Superconducting Qubit Readout](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00783)
*Hans Johnson,Tanay Roy,Leonardo Bove,David van Zanten,Silvia Zorzetti,Jafar Saniie*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Adaptive FIR filtering (LMS on FPGA) does not improve superconducting-qubit readout fidelity: fixed FIR filters collapse to a scaled boxcar, and the frozen LMS filter retains only 99.4% of the boxcar discrimination SNR. Real gains come from optimizing window start/duration (+2.4–5.6%) and per-sample weighting (up to +3.0%), with sequential detection and matched-filter templates (≈30 shots/class) offering early decisions near boxcar fidelity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard dispersive readout baseline is a boxcar averager (uniform average over a fixed window plus thresholding). The paper asks when additional digital processing—adaptive filtering, per-sample weighting, sequential detection—actually improves state-assignment fidelity rather than merely denoising the trace.

Method: An adaptive FIR filter trained in real time via the LMS algorithm on an FPGA quantum controller; measured readout shots analyzed offline. Frozen filter compared against boxcar averaging, plus tests of per-sample weighting and sequential detection. Fixed FIR filters analyzed theoretically (kernel collapse). Hardware sweeps locate the early-decision crossover. Matched-filter templates built from few labeled shots. Experiments on a 2D transmon (Q1, 8 µs operating point) and a 3D ancilla qubit (Q2).

Result: For fixed FIR filters, summing filtered samples equals the boxcar sum times one complex number (up to a small edge correction), scaling signal and noise equally—no discrimination gain (boxcar limit). The frozen LMS filter reduces trace noise but yields no resolved fidelity improvement; each filtered shot carries 99.4% of the boxcar discrimination SNR on Q1 at 8 µs. Optimizing readout window start/duration improves contrast fidelity by +2.4% to +5.6% across four readout lengths. Per-sample weighting adds up to +3.0% over full-window integration, but only +0.33% beyond the tuned window at 8 µs. On Q2 the sequential test decided most shots early with fidelity within 0.5 percentage points of boxcar; hardware sweeps place the crossover window near 1 µs. About 30 labeled shots per class suffice for a matched-filter template nearly as good as one from hundreds.

Conclusion: The results form an operating-point record that cleanly separates waveform denoising from decision improvement: adaptive/fixed FIR filtering does not raise discrimination fidelity, while window tuning, per-sample weighting, and sequential detection provide the real gains, with early-decision behavior governed by an operating-point crossover near 1 µs.

Abstract: In dispersive readout of superconducting qubits, the common baseline is a boxcar averager: a uniformly weighted average over a fixed window followed by threshold-based state assignment. We ask when additional digital processing improves fidelity. We evaluated an adaptive finite-impulse-response (FIR) filter trained in real time by the least-mean-squares (LMS) algorithm on an FPGA quantum controller, and analyzed measured readout shots offline to compare the frozen filter with boxcar averaging and to test per-sample weighting and sequential detection.
  For the fixed FIR filters tested, summing the filtered samples gives the boxcar sum multiplied by one complex number, apart from a small edge correction (the kernel collapse). This scales state separation and noise equally, so discrimination cannot improve (the boxcar limit).
  The frozen LMS filter reduces trace noise without a resolved fidelity improvement at the tested readout lengths. Replayed on the same shots offline, each filtered shot carries 99.4% of the boxcar shot's discrimination signal-to-noise ratio at the 8 $μ$s operating point of the 2D transmon qubit (Q1).
  On Q1, optimizing the readout window's start and duration improves contrast readout fidelity by +2.4% to +5.6% across four readout lengths. Per-sample weighting adds up to +3.0% over full-window integration, and only +0.33% beyond the tuned window at 8 $μ$s.
  Remaining results are from a 3D ancilla qubit (Q2), which sits above the early-decision crossover that Q1 sits below. Hardware sweeps locate the crossover window near 1 $μ$s. Offline, the sequential test decided most shots early, with fidelity within 0.5 percentage points of the boxcar. About 30 labeled shots per class build a matched-filter template nearly as good as one from hundreds.
  The results form an operating-point record that separates waveform denoising from decision improvement.

</details>


### [54] [Remote state preparation of a single-spin state via hybrid spin-photon entanglement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00832)
*Yunge Jiang,Yehan Yu,Lili Song,Yan Mu,Chaoyun Peng,Mingfeng Wang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A remote-state-preparation protocol for a stationary electron-spin qubit encoded in hybrid spin–coherent-state entanglement is proposed. By coupling the transmitted pulse to an auxiliary spin, homodyne detection, and a spin projective measurement, it avoids coherent-state-superposition projection and photon-number-resolving detection, achieving deterministic preparation with one bit of classical communication and optimized fidelity above the classical benchmark over tens of kilometers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Remote state preparation exploits prior knowledge of a target state to reduce communication resources for quantum-state distribution. Conventional continuous-variable/traveling-qubit schemes require projections onto superpositions of nonorthogonal coherent states and photon-number-resolving detection, which are experimentally demanding; moreover, single-photon encodings fail probabilistically under photon loss.

Method: Hybrid entanglement between an electron spin and a coherent-state light pulse is generated via spin-dependent reflection from a spin-cavity system. The pulse is transmitted, coupled to an auxiliary spin, then homodyne-detected and subjected to a spin projective measurement, thereby circumventing direct coherent-state-superposition projection. Analytical modeling quantifies coherent-state nonorthogonality, fiber loss, and spin dephasing; a diamond nitrogen-vacancy center implementation is discussed.

Result: In the ideal limit, deterministic preparation of a particular class of target states is achieved with only one bit of classical communication. Coherent-state encoding is naturally photon-loss tolerant: loss attenuates the amplitude, causing gradual fidelity reduction rather than probabilistic failure. Larger coherent-state amplitude improves distinguishability and fidelity but increases which-branch information leakage from loss, yielding an optimal amplitude at each transmission distance. For experimentally relevant parameters, the optimized average fidelity stays well above the classical benchmark over tens of kilometers.

Conclusion: The protocol provides a loss-tolerant, experimentally feasible route to remote state preparation of stationary spin qubits, avoiding demanding coherent-state-superposition measurements and photon-number-resolving detection, with fidelity optimized against distance-dependent loss-induced leakage.

Abstract: Remote state preparation is a fundamental quantum-information protocol that exploits prior knowledge of a target state to reduce the communication resources required for quantum-state distribution. Here, we propose a remote-state-preparation protocol for a stationary electron-spin qubit using hybrid entanglement between the electron spin and a coherent-state light pulse, generated by spin-dependent reflection from a spin-cavity system. Unlike single-photon encodings, the coherent-state encoding is naturally tolerant of photon loss: a loss channel attenuates the coherent-state amplitude, leading to a gradual reduction in preparation fidelity rather than probabilistic protocol failure. The central difficulty---implementing the projection onto superpositions of nonorthogonal coherent states required in the conventional protocol---is circumvented by coupling the transmitted pulse to an auxiliary spin, followed by homodyne detection and a spin projective measurement. The protocol therefore avoids direct coherent-state-superposition measurements and photon-number-resolving detection. In the ideal limit, deterministic preparation of a particular class of target states can be achieved using only one bit of classical communication. We analytically quantify the effects of coherent-state nonorthogonality, fiber loss, and spin dephasing on the preparation fidelity. Increasing the coherent-state amplitude improves state distinguishability and hence the preparation fidelity, but also enhances which-branch information leakage caused by photon loss, resulting in an optimal amplitude at each transmission distance. For experimentally relevant parameters, the optimized average fidelity remains well above the classical benchmark over distances of tens of kilometers. We also discuss a possible implementation using diamond nitrogen-vacancy centers.

</details>


### [55] [Neural Fourier Surrogates for Data Reuploading Quantum Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00841)
*Oliver Knitter,Jonathan Mei,Sang Hyub Kim,Chi Chen,Masako Yamada,Martin Roetteler*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces Neural Fourier Surrogates (NFS), a stochastic classical neural network that learns coefficients over the same finite Fourier series support as quantum neural networks, serving as a natural classical baseline for evaluating QNN performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The exact boundary between classical and quantum advantage in quantum machine learning remains poorly understood. Direct comparisons between QNNs and existing classical models are limited because they operate over fundamentally different function classes, preventing broader insight into the distinction between the two paradigms.

Method: Inspired by Neural Quantum States and Random Fourier Features, the authors propose Neural Fourier Surrogates (NFS), a stochastic classical neural network architecture that efficiently learns coefficients over the same finite Fourier series support as QNNs, enabling a like-for-like comparison.

Result: On tabular benchmark datasets, NFS proves an effective classifier broadly competitive with established classical baselines (including a comparable Random Fourier Features model) and achieves performance comparable to data-reuploading QNNs. Additional analysis compares the learned Fourier spectra of QNNs and NFS on synthetic data.

Conclusion: NFS establishes a natural classical baseline for evaluating QNN performance, offering a fairer, function-class-matched framework for probing the boundary between classical and quantum advantage.

Abstract: For quantum machine learning, the exact boundary between classical and quantum advantage is still poorly understood. Direct comparison between quantum neural networks (QNNs) and existing classical models, which encompass fundamentally different function classes, often fails to provide broader insight into the difference between the two. Inspired by the techniques of Neural Quantum States and Random Fourier Features, this work introduces Neural Fourier Surrogates (NFS), a stochastic classical neural network architecture for efficiently learning coefficients over the same finite Fourier series support as quantum neural networks. Testing on a selection of tabular benchmark datasets, we find that NFS is an effective classifier architecture broadly competitive with established classical baselines, including a comparable Random Fourier Features model, and possessing comparable performance to data-reuploading QNNs; combined with additional analysis comparing the learned Fourier spectra of QNNs and NFS on synthetic data, these results establish NFS as a natural classical baseline for evaluating QNN performance.

</details>


### [56] [Beyond IP = PSPACE and QIP = PSPACE: Interactive Proofs in Arbitrary Physical Theories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00847)
*Kishor Bharti*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Interactive proof power is characterized across general probabilistic theories: classical-message protocols always decide PSPACE, quantum-message protocols resist stronger-than-quantum provers, but arbitrary system messages can strictly exceed PSPACE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The equalities IP = PSPACE and QIP = PSPACE (the latter with three messages) motivate the question of how much of an interactive proof's power derives from the underlying physical theory, rather than from the interaction structure itself.

Method: Interactive proofs are studied in general probabilistic theories subsuming classical and quantum theory. The analysis distinguishes what prover and verifier exchange (classical messages, quantum messages, or arbitrary systems) and how the theory defines efficient operations, under stated standard assumptions.

Result: With only classical messages, protocols in every theory satisfying standard assumptions decide exactly PSPACE. With a quantum verifier and quantum messages, a prover using any theory containing quantum theory does not increase maximum acceptance probability, so the three-message PSPACE result holds against such provers. When messages may be arbitrary systems, the interactive-proof class can strictly exceed PSPACE.

Conclusion: Interactive proof power is governed by both the exchanged message type and the theory's efficient operations: classical and quantum-message settings yield PSPACE, while arbitrary-system messages can surpass it.

Abstract: The equalities IP = PSPACE and QIP = PSPACE, the latter achievable with three messages, raise a basic question: how much of an interactive proof's power comes from the underlying physical theory? We study interactive proofs in general probabilistic theories, which include classical and quantum theory. The answer depends on what the prover and verifier exchange and how the theory specifies efficient operations. When they exchange only classical messages, protocols in every theory satisfying our standard assumptions decide exactly PSPACE. For protocols with a quantum verifier and quantum messages, allowing a prover to use any theory containing quantum theory does not increase the maximum acceptance probability. Thus, the three-message PSPACE result remains valid against such provers. When messages may be arbitrary systems, the interactive-proof class can strictly exceed PSPACE.

</details>


### [57] [From Steane to A7: Quantum Codes from Invariant States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00865)
*Ian Teixeira*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A two-parameter family of single-error-correcting ((7,4,3))_4 seven-ququart codes with transversal A7-tilde symmetry is constructed by building invariant parent states via Schur-Weyl sector weight equations, yielding non-Clifford transversal gates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To realize the finite component of a two-qubit super-golden gate set, one needs a code whose transversal local symmetry supports non-Clifford operations. Direct codespace search is hard, so the paper seeks a systematic construction linking permutation t-transitivity and local unitary t-group symmetry to error-correcting distance.

Method: Rather than searching codespaces directly, construct invariant parent states. For n systems of local dimension q with local symmetry a unitary t-group and permutation symmetry t-transitive, maximal mixing of all marginals on at most t sites is expressed as linear equations in Schur-Weyl sector weights. Each feasible solution gives parents of pure distance at least t+1; puncturing one site yields a q-dimensional code of distance at least t with the prescribed local symmetry acting transversally. The Steane and nonstabilizer Fake Steane codes are analyzed to explain residual continuous freedom: their eight-qubit purifications lie on a circle where error correction fixes two invariant-sector norms but leaves a relative phase free. Replacing Clifford symmetry by A7-tilde <= SU(4) while keeping the permutation group gives three fixed sector norms and two free relative phases, producing the ququart family.

Result: A two-parameter family of single-error-correcting seven-ququart codes with transversal A7-tilde symmetry is obtained, encoding two logical qubits in ((7,4,3))_4 codes and supporting non-Clifford operations by applying the same gate to each physical ququart.

Conclusion: Invariant parent states with t-transitive permutation symmetry and a local unitary t-group reduce code construction to linear Schur-Weyl sector-weight equations; puncturing yields transversal-symmetry codes, and the degree of continuous freedom (free relative phases) depends on the prescribed symmetry, yielding new ququart codes realizing part of the super-golden gate set.

Abstract: We construct a two-parameter family of single-error-correcting seven-ququart codes with transversal $\tilde{A_7}$ symmetry, realizing the finite component of a two-qubit super-golden gate set. These $((7,4,3))_4$ codes encode two logical qubits and support non-Clifford operations by applying the same gate to each physical ququart.
  Our method constructs invariant parent states rather than searching directly for a codespace. For $n$ systems of local dimension $q$, suppose the local symmetry is a unitary $t$-group and the permutation symmetry is $t$-transitive. Maximal mixing of all marginals on at most $t$ sites is then equivalent to linear equations in Schur--Weyl sector weights. Every feasible solution yields parents of pure distance at least $t+1$; puncturing one site produces a $q$-dimensional code of distance at least $t$ with the prescribed local symmetry acting transversally.
  Steane and the nonstabilizer Fake Steane code reveal why the construction leaves continuous freedom. They share the same prescribed transversal Clifford and permutation symmetries, and their eight-qubit purifications lie on a circle: error correction fixes two invariant-sector norms but leaves the relative phase free. Keeping the same permutation group and replacing Clifford symmetry by $\tilde{A_7}$ $\leq SU(4)$ gives three fixed sector norms and two free relative phases, producing the new ququart family.

</details>


### [58] [A Quantum Phase Neural Network with Multi-Inputs/Single-Output](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00868)
*Shuang Cong,Jinmin Yang,Sajede Harraz*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A 35-input/single-output quantum phase neural network (QPNN) is proposed to recognize the 7×5 pixel letters "A" and "B", using quantum phase encoding, rotation and controlled-NOT gates, a derived phase-weight learning rule with adaptive learning rate, and analytical zero-error phase solutions, verified on Qiskit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional multi-input recognition faces the "exponential wall": representing all 2^35 complex exponential amplitudes is intractable. The work seeks an implementation that avoids generating the full amplitude space while supporting practical multi-input recognition.

Method: Each node takes binary (0/1) inputs; the 35 binary inputs are normalized and quantum-phase encoded into a superposition described by 35 quantum phases. A quantum rotation gate introduces adjustable phases, and a controlled-NOT gate entangles the relative phase between two inputs. The input-output relation is derived analytically as a polynomial function of adjustable rotation angles, together with a phase-weight learning training algorithm using an adaptive learning rate. Recognition probability 1 solutions are designed, yielding non-unique analytical expressions for all zero-error adjustable phases. Performance is tested on the Qiskit platform.

Result: Zero-error adjustable phase solutions achieving recognition probability 1 are obtained, and these solutions are shown to be non-unique. Experimental verification on Qiskit confirms the derived behavior.

Conclusion: Phase-driven computation converts probability calculation into an analytical polynomial function of adjustable rotation angles, circumventing the need for all 2^35 complex exponential amplitudes and offering an effective route to mitigate the "exponential wall", providing a new implementation scheme for practical multi-input recognition.

Abstract: A 35 input/single output quantum phase neural network (QPNN) is proposed to recognize the English letters' A 'and' B 'composed 7*5 of pixels. Each node of the proposed QPNN has the input with 0 or 1. Through normalization and quantum phase encoding, each set of 35 with 0 or 1 digital inputs is encoded into a quantum superposition state described by 35 quantum phases. Then, a quantum rotation gate is used to introduce adjustable phase, and a controlled NOT gate is used to entangle the relative phase between the two inputs. The relationship between the input and output of the network is derived. This paper also derived the phase weight learning training algorithm with adaptive learning rate. The solutions with the recognition probability of 1 is designed, and the analytical expressions for all adjustable phase solutions with zero errors are not unique. The experimental performance is verified on the Qiskit platform. This paper uses phase drive to convert the probability calculation into an analytical polynomial function with adjustable rotation angles in the network, avoiding the bottleneck of generating all 2^35 complex exponential amplitudes and providing an effective way to solve the "exponential wall" problem, which provides a new implementation solution for the practical application of multi-input recognition problems.

</details>


### [59] [A Gate-Based Quantum Computing Framework for Codon Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00887)
*Fatemeh Ghasemi,Kion Kim*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work introduces a gate-based quantum computing framework for codon optimization by mapping the objective to an Ising Hamiltonian and Pauli operators, then solving it with VQE, SVQE, and QAOA, reproducing exact ground states on a three-amino-acid benchmark and demonstrating execution on IBM Quantum hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Codon optimization is a challenging combinatorial optimization problem relevant to synthetic biology, protein expression, and biotechnology. Although quantum annealing has been explored for it, gate-based quantum algorithms remain largely unexplored, leaving a gap this study aims to address.

Method: The optimization objective is formulated as an Ising Hamiltonian and expressed in terms of Pauli operators suitable for gate-based processors. The framework is implemented and investigated using VQE, SVQE, and QAOA, with benchmarking against exact diagonalization, classical optimization, and quantum annealing. Ansatz selection, optimization strategies, initialization schemes, and hardware execution settings are evaluated, and hardware demonstrations run on IBM Quantum processors.

Result: On a three-amino-acid benchmark, the implementations reproduce the exact ground-state solutions and identify optimized ansatz selections, optimization strategies, initialization schemes, and hardware settings yielding reproducible performance on current noisy quantum hardware. Hardware demonstrations on IBM Quantum processors show practical implementation of the workflow.

Conclusion: As a work in progress, the study establishes a reproducible computational framework for gate-based quantum codon optimization and provides a foundation for investigating larger protein sequences as quantum hardware and algorithms advance.

Abstract: Codon optimization is a challenging combinatorial optimization problem with important applications in synthetic biology, protein expression, and biotechnology. While quantum annealing has previously been explored for this problem, the application of gate-based quantum algorithms remains largely unexplored. In this work, we present a gate-based quantum computing framework for codon optimization by formulating the optimization objective as an Ising Hamiltonian and expressing it in terms of Pauli operators suitable for gate-based quantum processors. The proposed framework is investigated using the Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE), Sampling Variational Quantum Eigensolver (SVQE), and the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA), with benchmarking against exact diagonalization, classical optimization, and quantum annealing. Using a three-amino-acid benchmark, the proposed implementations successfully reproduce the exact ground-state solutions while identifying optimized ansatz selections, optimization strategies, initialization schemes, and hardware execution settings that provide reproducible performance on current noisy quantum hardware. Hardware demonstrations are performed on IBM Quantum processors to evaluate the practical implementation of the proposed workflow. As a work in progress, this study establishes a reproducible computational framework for gate-based quantum codon optimization and provides the foundation for future investigations of larger protein sequences as quantum hardware and algorithms continue to advance.

</details>


### [60] [Telecom-Band Optical Transitions of Erbium Atoms in Rare-Gas Solids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00918)
*Evgenii Zaitsev,Alec Dinerstein,Shan Zou,Charles Peretti,Yutian Wen,Wei Guo,Gregory V. Hartland,Yizhong Huang,Dafei Jin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Neutral Er atoms are isolated in solid Ne and Ar at 6 K, showing narrow telecom-band inner-shell emission with microsecond 4f→5d decay, sub-1 cm⁻¹ matrix shift, sub-22 GHz inhomogeneous linewidth, and PL lifetimes up to 78.9 ms near the radiative limit—establishing rare-gas solids as promising hosts for long-lived Er telecom emission.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rare-earth atoms like Er possess inner-shell near-infrared transitions, some in telecom bands, which are largely host-insensitive due to shielding by filled outer shells. Cryogenic rare-gas solids (solid Ne and Ar) offer high chemical purity and a nearly nuclear-spin-free environment, making them attractive hosts for preserving optical and spin coherence—yet Er in such matrices remains largely uncharacterized.

Method: Neutral Er atoms were isolated in solid Ne and Ar at 6 K, and their emission was characterized via steady-state and time-resolved photoluminescence (PL) spectroscopy. The study examined near-infrared transitions between 1000–1500 nm, including 4f→5d and 4f→4f inner-shell emission, analyzed crystal-field splitting, matrix shift, inhomogeneous linewidth, and PL lifetime, and applied thermal annealing to probe host effects.

Result: Several near-infrared transitions (1000–1500 nm) were observed in both hosts, including inner-shell emission in telecom O and E bands. The 4f→5d transition near 1299 nm forms a narrow-component multiplet consistent with crystal-field splitting and decays on a microsecond timescale. The 4f→4f transition near 1437 nm shows a matrix shift below 1 cm⁻¹ in both hosts. In solid Ne, its inhomogeneous linewidth is bounded to <22 GHz by spectrometer resolution, with PL lifetime up to 78.9 ms, close to the estimated radiative limit. Thermal annealing extends the 1437 nm lifetime in Ne and suppresses phonon sidebands relative to zero-phonon lines in Ar.

Conclusion: Rare-gas solids are promising hosts for long-lived telecom-band Er emission, providing a foundation for future spin-photon interfaces and hybrid quantum architectures.

Abstract: Rare-earth (RE) atoms, such as erbium (Er), have inner-shell optical transitions in the near-infrared, some of which are in the telecom bands. These transitions are largely insensitive to the host environment due to effective shielding by the filled outer shells. Cryogenic rare-gas (RG) solids, such as solid neon (Ne) and argon (Ar), combine high chemical purity with a nearly nuclear-spin-free environment, making them attractive solid hosts for preserving optical and spin coherence. In this work, we isolate neutral Er atoms in solid Ne and Ar at 6 K and characterize their emission by steady-state and time-resolved photoluminescence (PL) spectroscopy. In both hosts, we observe several near-infrared transitions between 1000--1500 nm, including inner-shell emission in the telecom O and E bands. The $4f \rightarrow 5d$ transition near 1299 nm forms a multiplet of narrow components, consistent with crystal-field splitting, and decays on a microsecond timescale. The $4f \rightarrow 4f$ transition near 1437 nm shows a matrix shift below 1 cm$^{-1}$ in both hosts. In solid Ne, its inhomogeneous linewidth is bounded by the spectrometer resolution to < 22 GHz, and its PL lifetime reaches up to 78.9 ms, close to the estimated radiative limit. Thermal annealing extends the 1437 nm lifetime in Ne and suppresses the phonon sidebands relative to the zero-phonon lines in Ar. These results identify RG solids as promising hosts for long-lived telecom-band Er emission and provide a foundation for future spin-photon interfaces and hybrid quantum architectures.

</details>


### [61] [TATVA: A Reinforcement Learning Framework for Quantum Circuit Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00945)
*Om Bhamare,Aryan Jadhav,Manas Shinde,Prithvi Shinde*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: TATVA is a reinforcement learning system that synthesizes compact, high-fidelity quantum circuits by treating circuit design as a sequential decision problem, using parallel DQN and PPO agents with Qiskit simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-fidelity quantum state preparation is critical, as errors propagate into final computational results. Traditional circuit design relies on predefined rules and mathematical decompositions, which struggle to handle diverse and complex target states and often require many gates. There is a need for automatic synthesis that achieves accurate target states with fewer gates.

Method: TATVA formulates circuit synthesis as a sequential decision problem where an agent selects one gate at a time and computes fidelity after each applied gate, choosing the gate yielding the highest fidelity. It employs a parallel architecture with two RL agents—Deep Q-Network (DQN) and Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO)—integrated with Qiskit's statevector simulator. After reaching target fidelity, a post-hoc optimization step reduces circuit depth while preserving fidelity.

Result: The system synthesizes circuits up to 5 qubits, achieving a fidelity of 0.999999 while producing compact circuits. Circuits are evaluated using fidelity, success rate, gate count, and circuit depth. Unseen target states (not used in training) are used to assess learning effectiveness and generalization beyond training states.

Conclusion: TATVA demonstrates that reinforcement learning enables automatic quantum circuit synthesis with high fidelity, compact gate counts, and generalization to unseen target states, offering a viable alternative to traditional rule-based and decomposition-based design.

Abstract: One of the most important initial steps in quantum computing is high-fidelity quantum state preparation, because errors in it can affect the final computational result. Therefore, one of the major challenges is to design a quantum circuit that can achieve the desired target state accurately with a smaller number of gates. Traditionally, circuits are designed using predefined rules and mathematical decompositions, but this makes it difficult to design circuits for different and complex target states. This paper presents TATVA, a reinforcement learning (RL) system for synthesizing quantum circuits. TATVA views circuit synthesis as a sequential decision problem in which the agents select one gate at a time and calculate the fidelity after each applied gate, with the gate producing the highest fidelity selected for the circuit. The system introduces a parallel architecture that uses two RL agents, Deep Q-Network (DQN) and Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), along with Qiskit's statevector simulator. The system can synthesize up to 5 qubits while achieving a fidelity of 0.999999 and producing compact circuits. After achieving the desired fidelity, it performs a post-hoc optimization step that reduces the circuit depth while preserving the achieved fidelity. The circuit is finalized using fidelity, success rate, gate count, and circuit depth. Target states that are not used during training are used to test whether the system has learned effectively and can generalize beyond the training states. This approach aims to enable automatic quantum circuit synthesis with high fidelity using reinforcement learning.

</details>


### [62] [Sublinear-depth Quantum Simulation of Electrons with Atomic Orbitals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00950)
*Jakob Günther,Aram W. Harrow*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a quantum simulation algorithm for electronic structure in atomic-orbital basis, achieving a Trotter step depth of O(polylog(N)) without ancillas and a total gate count of N^{5/3+o(1)}, matching the best known scaling across any basis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Atomic orbitals are the standard compact, flexible choice for modeling molecular electrons, but they yield Hamiltonians with up to O(N^4) terms and little structure, making it hard to match the complexity of plane-wave and real-space-grid methods. The work aims to close this complexity gap for quantum simulation of atomic-orbital models.

Method: The approach implements a Trotter step for atomic orbitals in O(polylog(N)) depth without ancillas by combining three ingredients: a rigorous treatment of orbital localization, a hierarchical Hamiltonian decomposition based on the Fast Multipole Method, and shallow quantum Fourier arithmetic circuits. Trotter error is explicitly bounded.

Result: The algorithm achieves sublinear simulation depth (O(polylog(N)) per Trotter step) and a total gate count of N^{5/3+o(1)}, matching the best scaling known for any basis, all without ancilla qubits.

Conclusion: Atomic-orbital-based electronic structure simulation on a quantum computer can be made as efficient as the best plane-wave and real-space approaches, resolving the long-standing complexity disadvantage of atomic orbitals while keeping the number of orbitals per atom constant.

Abstract: Discretizing electronic degrees of freedom into atomic orbitals is the default choice to accurately model electrons in molecules in a compact and flexible way. We consider the task of simulating such electronic structure models on a quantum computer for $N$ orbitals, keeping the number of orbitals per atom constant. Atomic orbitals give rise to Hamiltonians with potentially up to $\mathcal{O}(N^4)$ terms and little structure, which made it challenging to match the complexity of methods based on plane waves and real-space grids. In this work, we show that a Trotter step for atomic orbitals can be implemented in depth $\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{polylog}(N))$ without ancillas by combining a rigorous treatment of orbital localization, a hierarchical Hamiltonian decomposition based on the Fast Multipole Method, and shallow quantum Fourier arithmetic circuits. By bounding the Trotter error, we obtain sublinear simulation depth, and our total gate count $N^{5/3 + o(1)}$ matches the best scaling known for any basis.

</details>


### [63] [Exact $T$-counts of Toffoli layers from an isotropy bound](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01024)
*Arul Rhik Mazumder*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proves that a layer of m disjoint CCZ gates requires exactly 6m+1 T gates in any Hadamard-free Clifford+T circuit with clean ancillas, establishing the first general-m optimality proof via an isotropy constraint on T-gate vectors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The T-count dominates the cost of fault-tolerant Clifford+T computation. While Campbell and Howard gave constructions matching certain low-T-count values, no proof established optimality for general m. This paper aims to prove that the 6m+1 lower bound for a layer of m disjoint CCZ gates is optimal for all m.

Method: The authors prove optimality using an isotropy constraint: for a pure-cubic phase, the vectors recording which T gates touch each qubit span a totally isotropic subspace. This yields a general isotropy floor δ ≥ 2(n−d*)−r for every diagonal level-three gate, computable from the phase polynomial in polynomial time. A separate parity argument raises the floor to 2ν+1 on non-Clifford pure-cubic gates, where ν is stabilizer nullity equal to n−d* on this class.

Result: A layer of m disjoint CCZ gates needs exactly 6m+1 T gates in every Hadamard-free Clifford+T circuit with clean ancillas, matching Campbell and Howard's construction and recovering their 4m+3 for a fan-out of m Toffolis from one control. On the TODD optimizer output for 24 benchmark circuits, the floor certifies 193 of 311 merged phase-polynomial blocks optimal (186–193 across five seeds), versus 113 for nullity.

Conclusion: The isotropy floor provides a provably optimal lower bound for general m, is never below stabilizer nullity, and holds under stated conditions for circuits with internal Hadamards forming unitarily uncomputed temporary AND blocks. Under adaptive feedforward, only t ≥ ν is proved.

Abstract: The $T$-count is the dominant cost of fault-tolerant Clifford+$T$ computation. We prove that a layer of $m$ disjoint CCZ gates, the diagonal core of a parallel Toffoli layer, needs exactly $6m+1$ $T$ gates in every Hadamard-free Clifford+$T$ circuit with clean ancillas. Campbell and Howard gave the matching construction. To our knowledge this is the first proof that it is optimal for general $m$ (for $m=1$ the value $7$ is classical, and Campbell and Howard state the value $13$ at $m=2$). On every controlled unitary the same floor comes within one of their exact count, and it recovers their $4m+3$ for a fan-out of $m$ Toffolis from one control. The proof rests on an isotropy constraint: for a pure-cubic phase, the vectors recording which $T$ gates touch each qubit span a totally isotropic subspace. In general the constraint gives the isotropy floor $δ\ge2(n-d^{\ast})-r$ for every diagonal level-three gate, computed from the phase polynomial in polynomial time. The floor is never below stabilizer nullity $ν$, which equals $n-d^{\ast}$ on this class, and a separate parity argument raises it to $2ν+1$ on non-Clifford pure-cubic gates. On the output of the TODD optimizer for the $24$ benchmark circuits it completes, the floor certifies $193$ of its $311$ merged phase-polynomial blocks optimal for their Hadamard layering ($186$ to $193$ across five optimizer seeds), against $113$ for nullity. The floor also holds, under stated conditions, for circuits whose only internal Hadamards form unitarily uncomputed temporary AND blocks, while under adaptive feedforward only $t\geν$ is proved.

</details>


### [64] [Device-Independent Conference Keys from Parity-Extended Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01025)
*Suvradip Chakraborty,Ronak Ramachandran,Aniruddha Sen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces Parity-$G$ games, a construction extending any two-player non-local game $G$ (with an optimal strategy where one player measures Pauli observables) to $N$ players while preserving quantum and classical values. Applied to the Mermin–Peres Magic Square Game, it yields the Parity Magic Square Game and the first device-independent conference key agreement (DI-CKA) protocol based on a pseudo-telepathy game, proven secure against coherent attacks and outperforming the Parity-CHSH-based protocol at low noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing DI-CKA protocols are each restricted to a single Bell inequality, typically a multiparty CHSH variant, whereas DI-QKD has benefited from a much richer landscape of non-local games. It was unclear how to transfer this diversity of games to the conference (multiparty) setting, limiting the design space and performance of DI-CKA protocols.

Method: The authors define Parity-$G$ games, which extend any two-player game $G$ to $N$ players for every $N$, provided $G$ admits an optimal strategy in which one player measures Pauli observables. The extension preserves the quantum and classical values of $G$, so security of the resulting $N$-party protocol reduces to analysis of the two-player game. They instantiate the framework with the Mermin–Peres Magic Square Game to obtain the Parity Magic Square Game, an $N$-player pseudo-telepathy game won ideally in every round, and use it to construct a DI-CKA protocol proven secure against coherent attacks.

Result: The framework recovers the Parity-CHSH game of Ribeiro, Murta and Wehner (Phys. Rev. A, 2018) as a special case. The Parity Magic Square Game yields the first DI-CKA protocol based on a pseudo-telepathy game; it is proven secure against coherent attacks, produces up to two key bits per round, and achieves a higher key rate than the Parity-CHSH-based DI-CKA protocol at low noise.

Conclusion: Parity-$G$ games provide a general, modular route to constructing multiparty non-local games and DI-CKA protocols from two-player games, broadening the design landscape beyond CHSH-type inequalities. The resulting pseudo-telepathy-based DI-CKA protocol demonstrates security against coherent attacks and improved low-noise key rates, establishing pseudo-telepathy games as a viable resource for conference key agreement.

Abstract: Device-independent conference key agreement (DI-CKA) lets a group of parties establish a shared secret key from untrusted quantum devices, with security certified by non-locality. Existing DI-CKA protocols are each built around a single Bell inequality, typically a multiparty variant of the CHSH game. DI-QKD protocols, in contrast, have been built from a much richer landscape of non-local games, and it has remained unclear how to carry this landscape over to the conference setting. We introduce $\textit{Parity-$G$ games}$, which extend any two-player game $G$ to $N$ players, for every $N$, provided $G$ has an optimal strategy in which one player measures Pauli observables. The extension preserves the quantum and classical values of $G$, and the security of the resulting $N$-party protocol follows from an analysis of the two-player game alone. Our framework recovers the Parity-CHSH game of Ribeiro, Murta and Wehner (Phys. Rev. A, 2018) as a special case. Applied to the Mermin--Peres Magic Square Game, it yields a new $N$-player pseudo-telepathy game, the $\textit{Parity Magic Square Game}$, which ideal devices win in every round. We use it to construct the $\textit{first}$ DI-CKA protocol based on a pseudo-telepathy game. We prove the protocol secure against coherent attacks. It produces up to two key bits per round, and at low noise its key rate exceeds that of the DI-CKA protocol based on the Parity-CHSH game.

</details>


### [65] [Function-like pseudorandom unitaries generate pseudorandom quantum processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01038)
*Zitai Xu,Rahul Arvind,Kishor Bharti,Tobias Haug,Dax Enshan Koh,Wai-Keong Mok,Andrew Tanggara,Alexey V. Gorshkov*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces pseudorandom function-like unitaries (PRFUs), a primitive that uses a single short key to generate an entire reusable family of independently random-looking quantum operations indexed by public labels, extending quantum pseudorandomness from individual unitaries to efficiently generated families.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Haar-random unitaries model generic random quantum evolution but have exponential description and circuit complexity. Pseudorandom unitaries emulate a single Haar-random unitary efficiently, yet many tasks require an entire reusable family of independently random-looking operations, which existing primitives do not provide.

Method: The authors define PRFUs indexed by public labels generated from one short key, distinguishing classical- and coherent-label access and establishing security under adaptive quantum queries. For classical labels, they give a generic construction from a post-quantum pseudorandom function plus a pseudorandom unitary. For coherent labels, they use an indexed path-recording framework to analyze interference across labels and prove security of a construction based on quantum-secure function and permutation primitives.

Result: PRFUs are shown to generate pseudorandom channels and quantum combs with private memory, secure against adaptive interventions and concurrent sessions. Combined with unitary gluing, they yield a one-key family of pseudorandom unitaries whose supported register widths can be chosen after key generation. Applications include nonce-resolved quantum authentication, coherently masked QRAM queries, and efficient emulation of random multi-time dynamics.

Conclusion: The work extends quantum pseudorandomness from individual operations to efficiently generated families of random-looking quantum dynamics, providing reusable, key-efficient families with broad cryptographic and simulation applications.

Abstract: Haar-random unitaries provide a canonical model of generic random quantum evolution, but they typically have exponential description and circuit complexity. Although pseudorandom unitaries efficiently emulate a single Haar-random unitary, many tasks require an entire reusable family of independently random-looking operations. We introduce pseudorandom function-like unitaries (PRFU), which generate such a family indexed by public labels using only a single short key. We distinguish classical- and coherent-label access and establish security under adaptive quantum queries. For classical labels, we provide a generic construction from a post-quantum pseudorandom function and a pseudorandom unitary. For coherent labels, we use an indexed path-recording framework to analyze interference across labels and prove security of a construction based on quantum-secure function and permutation primitives. We further show that PRFUs generate pseudorandom channels and quantum combs with private memory, which are secure against adaptive interventions and concurrent sessions. Combining PRFUs with unitary gluing yields a one-key family of pseudorandom unitaries whose supported register widths can be chosen after key generation. Further applications include nonce-resolved quantum authentication, coherently masked QRAM queries, and efficient emulation of random multi-time dynamics. Our results extend quantum pseudorandomness from individual operations to efficiently generated families of random-looking quantum dynamics.

</details>


### [66] [Learned Parallel Bit-Flipping Sequential Belief Propagation Decoding of Quantum LDPC Codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01068)
*Mohsen Moradi,Taejoon Kim,Remi A. Chou*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes a learned parallel bit-flipping sequential BP decoder for QLDPC codes that combines learned sequential BP with quaternary bit-flipping candidate exploration to improve reliability while maintaining low latency via parallelism.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard flooding BP suffers convergence failures on QLDPC codes due to short cycles, degeneracy, and symmetric decoding trajectories. Learned sequential BP improves convergence via a reinforcement-learning policy for variable-node update orders, but a single learned trajectory remains sensitive to unfavorable local Pauli decisions.

Method: The decoder first runs learned sequential BP for a fixed number of iterations. If the syndrome is unsatisfied, it constructs quaternary bit-flipping candidates, each changing the current Pauli decision of one qubit, using a new quaternary score combining syndrome gain, a quantized Pauli log-likelihood penalty, and Q-table lookahead at hypothetical post-flip states. Selected candidates initialize independent learned sequential BP continuations from the same decoder state, and these continuations run in parallel.

Result: Simulations on representative QLDPC codes over the depolarizing channel show that the proposed decoder improves the reliability of learned sequential BP while having a parallel low-latency structure, since testing several candidates mainly increases parallel hardware resources rather than sequential decoding latency.

Conclusion: The learned parallel bit-flipping sequential BP decoder enhances the reliability of learned sequential BP for QLDPC codes while preserving a low-latency, parallel-friendly structure suitable for low-overhead fault-tolerant quantum computation.

Abstract: Quantum low-density parity-check (QLDPC) codes are promising candidates for low-overhead fault-tolerant quantum computation, but their practical use requires fast, low-complexity, and reliable decoders. Belief propagation (BP) is attractive because of its local message-passing structure, yet standard flooding BP often suffers from convergence failures on QLDPC codes due to short cycles, degeneracy, and symmetric decoding trajectories. Learned sequential BP improves convergence by using a reinforcement-learning policy to choose variable-node update orders, but a single learned trajectory can still be sensitive to unfavorable local Pauli decisions. We propose a learned parallel bit-flipping sequential BP decoder with a new quaternary score combining syndrome gain, a quantized Pauli log-likelihood penalty, and Q-table lookahead at hypothetical post-flip states.
