Our decoder first runs learned sequential BP for a fixed number of iterations. If the syndrome is not satisfied, it constructs quaternary bit-flipping candidates, each corresponding to changing the current Pauli decision of one qubit. The selected candidates initialize independent learned sequential BP continuations from the same decoder state. Since these continuations are independent, they can be executed in parallel, so testing several candidates mainly increases parallel hardware resources rather than sequential decoding latency. Simulations on representative QLDPC codes over the depolarizing channel show that the proposed decoder improves the reliability of learned sequential BP while having a parallel low-latency structure.

</details>


### [67] [The stationarity test: a framework for learning quantum many-body systems from their thermal states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01074)
*Thiago Bergamaschi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a "stationarity test" algorithm that connects quantum Gibbs sampling to Hamiltonian learning, enabling first-of-their-kind time-efficient, nearly sample-optimal algorithms for structure learning of lattice Hamiltonians at all temperatures and for learning coefficients from thermal metastable states, plus a refinement of the thermal area law.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning the Hamiltonian interactions of a quantum system from samples of its Gibbs states is a foundational problem at the intersection of quantum learning theory and many-body physics. Existing approaches lack a unified, rigorous framework for structure learning across all temperatures and for learning from metastable states.

Method: The authors draw connections to the quantum Gibbs sampling literature and introduce the stationarity test: guess a Hamiltonian and measure the rate-of-change of local observables under the associated detailed-balanced quantum Markov chain. Technically, the analysis relies on new approximate locality and convexity properties for the quantum Fisher information of these Gibbs sampling algorithms.

Result: The stationarity test yields: (1) the first learning algorithm for the underlying interaction graph (structure learning) of lattice Hamiltonians at all temperatures from Gibbs state copies; (2) the first algorithm learning lattice Hamiltonian coefficients from thermal metastable states only, modeled as local minima of the free energy; (3) a refinement of the recent area law for thermal metastable states in the thermodynamic limit. Algorithms are rigorous, time-efficient, and nearly sample-optimal in system size and accuracy.

Conclusion: Connecting quantum Gibbs sampling to Hamiltonian learning via the stationarity test provides a unified, rigorous, and near-optimal framework, with applications spanning structure learning at all temperatures, learning from metastable states, and refining thermal area laws.

Abstract: The task of learning the Hamiltonian interactions governing a quantum system, given samples of its thermal (or 'Gibbs') states, is a foundational question at the intersection of quantum learning theory and many-body physics. In this paper, we draw connections to the quantum Gibbs sampling literature to introduce a natural learning algorithm we call the stationarity test: which simply "guesses" the Hamiltonian, and measures the rate-of-change of local observables, under the associated detailed-balanced quantum Markov chain [CKG23]. We leverage the stationarity test to address the following applications:
  1. To give the first learning algorithm for the underlying interaction graph, i.e. structure learning, of lattice Hamiltonians at all temperatures, given copies of their Gibbs states.
  2. To give the first learning algorithm for the coefficients of a lattice Hamiltonian, given only copies of its thermal metastable states, modeled as the "local minima" of the free energy.
  3. In addition, we present a refinement to the recent area law for thermal metastable states [BCV25], which holds in the thermodynamic limit.
  Our learning algorithms are rigorous, time-efficient, and nearly sample-optimal in system size and accuracy. At a technical level, our arguments are based on new approximate locality and convexity properties for the quantum Fisher information of these Gibbs sampling algorithms.

</details>


### [68] [Quantum synchronization in atom-cavity coupled systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01094)
*Katha Haldar,Anushree Dey,Saikat Ghosh,Bimalendu Deb*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A driven spin-1 atomic ensemble interacting with two driven cavity modes can exhibit limit cycles or quantum synchronization depending on drive strengths, diagnosed via generalized two-mode Husimi-Q functions, U(1) symmetry breaking, Wigner distributions, and Wehrl mutual information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and visualize emergent synchronized and limit-cycle dynamics in a driven-dissipative interacting CQED system, and to relate field synchronization to information-theoretic quantities for potential quantum metrology applications.

Method: A CQED setup: an ensemble of spin-1 atoms driven by a classical field interacting with two driven cavity modes. Visualization and characterization via generalized two-mode field Husimi-Q function using SU(2) coherent states, spin-1 Husimi-Q function, U(1) symmetry breaking analysis, Wigner quasi-probability distribution, and coherence properties of the reduced field density matrix; Wehrl mutual information between modes is computed.

Result: Both the atomic and two-mode field subsystems exhibit limit-cycle or quantum synchronization depending on cavity drive strengths and system parameters. Field synchronization is corroborated by U(1) symmetry breaking, Wigner function, and coherence indicators. Field synchronization and Wehrl mutual information are mutually exclusive.

Conclusion: The system reveals an intriguing interplay between limit-cycle behavior and quantum synchronization, highlighting emergent quantum phenomena in driven dissipative interacting systems; the results are relevant for extracting quantum advantages in quantum metrology with coupled atom-cavity systems.

Abstract: We consider a cavity quantum electrodynamics (CQED) setup where an ensemble of spin-1 atoms driven by a classical field interacts with two driven cavity field modes. We show that the two sub-systems - the atoms and the two-mode cavity field, exhibit limit-cycle or quantum synchronization depending on the cavity drive strengths and other system parameters. In order to visualize the limit cycle and synchronization in the field, we introduce generalized two-mode field Husimi-Q function using SU(2) coherent states. For atomic spin states, we make use of spin-1 Husimi-Q function to visualize the same. We further corroborate the occurrence of field synchronization and limit cycle by analyzing $U(1)$ symmetry breaking, Wigner quasi-probability distribution, coherence properties of reduced field density matrix. Our results reveal intriguing interplay between the limit-cycle and quantum synchronization behavior, highlighting emergent quantum phenomena in driven dissipative interacting quantum systems. Additionally, we calculate Wehrl mutual information between the two modes and find that the field synchronization and Wehrl mutual information are mutually exclusive. Our study would be important to extract quantum advantages in quantum metrology using coupled atom-cavity systems.

</details>


### [69] [Constant-Per-Layer-Depth MPS-Pretrained Ansatz for Noisy Distributed Quantum Processors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01099)
*Seongpyo Hong,Woodo Lee,Yong-Su Kim,Seung-Sup B. Lee,Junghyun Lee*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper compares three MPS-pretraining circuit architectures (ladder, mixed-canonical, brick-wall) at equal per-layer resources for variational quantum eigensolvers, and extends them to modular multi-QPU processors. The shallower brick-wall layout minimizes circuit duration, decoherence, and ZNE overhead, giving the best noisy performance; distributed MPS-pretrained circuits with nearest-neighbor QPU scheduling maintain constant per-layer depth and match or beat single-processor brick-wall when inter-QPU communication is fast relative to coherence time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Distributed quantum processors can scale variational algorithms beyond single-device limits, but circuit depth, communication overhead, and noise constrain performance. The work seeks hardware-aware co-design of tensor-network pretraining, circuit scheduling, and communication topology for noisy modular hardware.

Method: The authors compare ladder, mixed-canonical, and brick-wall realizations of matrix-product-state (MPS) pretraining under matched per-layer two-qubit-block resources. They then extend MPS-pretrained circuits to modular processors with one communication qubit per QPU, using a nearest-neighbor QPU-path schedule that holds per-layer depth constant as QPUs are added. Distributed circuits use inter-QPU links to realize long-range interactions of the target Hamiltonian.

Result: Ideal VQE performance and gradient scales are comparable across the three architectures, but the shallower brick-wall architecture reduces circuit duration, idle-time decoherence, and ZNE overhead, yielding superior noisy and ZNE-assisted performance. Distributed MPS-pretrained circuits match or outperform the single-processor brick-wall under noise when inter-QPU communication idle time is short compared with the coherence time.

Conclusion: Hardware-aware co-design of tensor-network pretraining, circuit scheduling, and communication topology is established as a guiding principle for variational quantum computation on noisy modular hardware.

Abstract: Distributed quantum processors could scale variational algorithms beyond single devices, but circuit depth, communication overhead, and noise limit their performance. We compare ladder, mixed-canonical, and brick-wall realizations of matrix-product-state (MPS) pretraining with matched per-layer two-qubit-block resources. Despite comparable ideal variational quantum eigensolver performance and gradient scales, the shallower brick-wall architecture reduces circuit duration, idle-time decoherence, and zero-noise-extrapolation (ZNE) overhead, yielding superior noisy and ZNE-assisted performance. We then extend MPS-pretrained circuits to modular processors with one communication qubit per quantum processing unit (QPU); a nearest-neighbor QPU-path schedule keeps the per-layer depth constant as QPUs are added. Distributed circuits whose inter-QPU links realize long-range interactions of the target Hamiltonian match or outperform the single-processor brick-wall under noise when communication idle time is short compared with the coherence time. These results establish hardware-aware co-design of tensor-network pretraining, circuit scheduling, and communication topology as a principle for variational quantum computation on noisy modular hardware.

</details>


### [70] [Dissipation-Sensitivity Trade-Off in Dissipative Bosonic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01112)
*Kohei Yoshimura,Ryusuke Hamazaki*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A universal trade-off is derived between steady-state entropy production rate and quantum Fisher information gain relative to a thermal state, for driven single-mode bosonic systems in a thermal bath.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum states outperform thermal states for metrology, but maintaining non-thermal, more sensitive states requires dissipation. Whether a fundamental cost constrains this sensitivity gain was previously unclear.

Method: For Gaussian states, a rigorous lower bound on the steady-state entropy production rate is derived in terms of quantum Fisher information gain over a thermal reference. Kerr nonlinearities are numerically tested to probe beyond-Gaussian validity.

Result: Steady-state entropy production rate is lower-bounded by the quantum Fisher information gain; numerical Kerr tests indicate the bounds extend beyond the Gaussian regime.

Conclusion: A new link is established between steady-state thermodynamics and quantum metrological performance, framing sensitivity as thermodynamically costly.

Abstract: We discover a universal trade-off between the dissipation required to maintain a quantum state more sensitive than a thermal one and the sensitivity gain for a driven single-mode bosonic system under a thermal environment. Specifically, for Gaussian states, we rigorously derive a lower bound on the entropy production rate in the steady state in terms of the quantum Fisher information gain relative to a thermal reference state. Numerical investigations of Kerr nonlinearities further support that these bounds can hold beyond the Gaussian regime. Our results establish a new connection between steady-state thermodynamics and quantum metrological performance.

</details>


### [71] [Multivariate Quantum Signal Processing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01125)
*Guang Hao Low*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops Multivariate Quantum Signal Processing (MQSP), a transfer-function framework for designing matrix-valued transformations of multiple noncommuting, nonnormal block-encoded matrices, analogous to multiport IIR filters, yielding efficient quantum circuits and improved/resolved query complexities for several multi-oracle problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Univariate QSP/QSVT provide powerful approximation-theoretic guarantees for single block-encoded matrices, but many open problems involve multiple noncommuting, nonnormal matrices (multi-oracle settings) whose joint transformations lack an efficient design methodology and analytic characterization. A framework extending signal processing to the multivariate, matrix-valued setting is needed to resolve these multi-oracle query complexities.

Method: The authors formulate Multivariate Quantum Signal Processing, equipping quantum transducers with the analytic structure of multiport Infinite-Impulse-Response (IIR) filters and establishing a transfer-function methodology for quantum query algorithm design. They derive efficiently computable analytic bounds valid uniformly across the oracle promise, give a complete characterization of achievable transfer functions, and build a modular library of analytic IIR components.

Result: The framework yields efficient quantum circuits with logarithmic error dependence and near-unity success probability. It improves or resolves per-oracle query complexity for: (1) Hamiltonian simulation for H=sum_j H_j with cost ~ t<λ,C>_{1/2}+O(||C||_1 log(1/ε)); (2) sparse simulation with optimal time-norm Θ(√d ||H||_{1→2} t) and additive error dependence; (3) linear systems and (4) differential equations under minimal matrix assumptions with optimal weighted state-preparation and multi-term cost; (5) ground-state preparation and energy estimation; (6) generalized eigenvalue problems. Applications include exact simulation of downfolded Hamiltonians, classical-data-input reduction for multiscale graph classification via Kron reduction, and online generalization via anisotropic Kalman filtering of streaming quantum data.

Conclusion: MQSP generalizes the approximation-theoretic guarantees of univariate QSP/QSVT to the multivariate, matrix-valued regime, providing a principled and modular design methodology that resolves or improves per-oracle query complexity across a broad set of multi-oracle problems and enables new applications in Hamiltonian downfolding, graph classification, and online quantum-data processing.

Abstract: We develop Multivariate Quantum Signal Processing for designing matrix-valued transformations of multiple noncommuting nonnormal block-encoded matrices. Our framework equips quantum transducers with the analytic structure of multiport Infinite-Impulse-Response (IIR) filters and establishes a transfer-function methodology for designing quantum query algorithms. Efficiently computable analytic bounds that hold uniformly across the oracle promise yield efficient quantum circuits with logarithmic error dependence and near-unity success probability, paralleling the approximation-theoretic guarantees of univariate QSP/QSVT. We provide a complete characterization of achievable transfer functions and a principled library of analytic IIR components. Our modular designs greatly improve or resolve the per-oracle query complexity of open multi-oracle problems: (1) Hamiltonian simulation under $H=\sum_j H_j$ with weighted quasinorm cost $\approx t\langle\vecλ,\vec C\rangle_{1/2}+\mathcal{O}(\|\vec C\|_1\log(1/ε))$ for block-encoding normalizations $\vecλ$ and costs $\vec C$; (2) sparse simulation with optimal time--norm $Θ(\sqrt{d}\|H\|_{\rm 1\to2}t)$ and additive error dependence; (3) solving linear systems and (4) differential equations under minimal matrix assumptions with optimal weighted state-preparation and multi-term cost; (5) ground-state preparation and energy estimation; (6) generalized eigenvalue problems. We apply these to exactly simulate downfolded Hamiltonians with leading cost $t\langle\vecλ_{\rm eff},\vec C_{\rm eff}\rangle_{1/2}$ further reduced by fractional dynamic correlation energy contributions from coarse-grained Hamiltonians, and show similar classical-data-input-reduction results for multiscale graph classification via Kron reduction. We also show the online generalization with anisotropic Kalman filtering of streaming quantum data.

</details>


### [72] [Classical Hardness of Learning Functions of Hamiltonians](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01141)
*Sota Hashimoto,Akinori Kawachi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper rigorously proves average-case classical hardness for two distribution-specific Hamiltonian function learning problems (for cosine and exponential functions) under the assumption that factoring random RSA moduli is average-case hard.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Morohoshi et al. proposed a physically motivated quantum machine learning problem—predicting Tr[f(H)ρ] from classical descriptions of H and ρ with unknown f (Hamiltonian function learning)—and gave an efficient quantum learning algorithm under suitable conditions, but left open a rigorous proof of average-case classical hardness. This paper fills that gap.

Method: The authors give a rigorous reduction-based proof for two specific distributions tied to f_cos,π(λ)=cos(πλ) and f_exp,β(λ)=e^{-βλ}. They show that an efficient classical randomized learner under squared loss, whose output hypotheses are evaluable in classical polynomial time, would yield a classical randomized polynomial-time algorithm for factoring random RSA moduli.

Result: Under the assumption of average-case hardness of factoring random RSA moduli, both distribution-specific Hamiltonian function learning problems are average-case classically hard for efficient classical randomized learners with polynomially evaluable hypotheses under squared loss.

Conclusion: The work establishes rigorous average-case classical hardness for Hamiltonian function learning, complementing the known efficient quantum algorithm and supporting a quantum advantage for this learning problem.

Abstract: Morohoshi, Nakayama, Manabe, and Mitarai proposed a physically motivated quantum machine learning problem in which the goal is to predict quantities of the form $\operatorname{Tr}[f(H)ρ]$ from classical descriptions of a Hamiltonian $H$ and a quantum state $ρ$, where $f$ is an unknown function. We call this problem Hamiltonian function learning in this paper. They constructed an efficient quantum learning algorithm under suitable conditions, while leaving a rigorous proof of average-case classical hardness open. In this paper, we rigorously prove the average-case classical hardness for two distribution-specific Hamiltonian function learning problems for $f_{\cos,π}(λ)=\cos(πλ)$ and $f_{\exp,β}(λ)=e^{-βλ}$ discussed in the paper of Morohoshi et al. under the assumption of the average-case hardness of factoring random RSA moduli. More specifically, we show that an efficient classical randomized learner under squared loss whose output hypotheses are evaluable in classical polynomial time for either problem would yield a classical randomized polynomial-time algorithm for factoring random RSA moduli.

</details>


### [73] [Robustness hierarchy of bipartite quantum correlations under noisy dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01163)
*Shakib Daryanoosh*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A robustness framework quantifies Bell nonlocality, EPR steering, and entanglement, orders their robustness measures, links robustness to noise-induced transition times, and expresses instantaneous robustness decay along Lindblad dynamics as a directional derivative.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum correlations degrade under noise, so quantitative measures are needed that track not only their presence but also their degradation during physical evolution.

Method: Develop a robustness framework for the hierarchy of Bell nonlocality, EPR steering, and entanglement. Establish an ordering of the corresponding robustness measures and relate robustness to noise-induced transition times. Formulate instantaneous robustness decay along Lindblad dynamics as a directional derivative determined by both the evolution and the robustness functional.

Result: An ordering of robustness measures across the correlation hierarchy is established, robustness is connected to noise-induced transition times, and robustness decay is characterized as a directional derivative. Applications to isotropic depolarizing noise yield resource transition times, while amplitude damping reveals the directional dependence of steering robustness.

Conclusion: The framework offers an approach to characterizing the degradation and lifetime of bipartite quantum correlations under noisy dynamics.

Abstract: Quantum correlations are fragile under noise, motivating quantitative measures that track not only their presence but also their degradation during physical evolution. We develop a robustness framework for the hierarchy of Bell nonlocality, EPR steering, and entanglement, establishing an ordering of the corresponding robustness measures and relating robustness to noise-induced transition times. We further formulate the instantaneous robustness decay along Lindblad dynamics as a directional derivative determined by both the evolution and the robustness functional. Applications to isotropic depolarizing noise and amplitude damping illustrate, respectively, the resulting resource transition times and the directional dependence of steering robustness. The framework provides an approach to characterizing the degradation and lifetime of bipartite quantum correlations under noisy dynamics.

</details>


### [74] [Vector chiral order and dynamical quantum phase transitions in an Ising chain with dimerized anisotropic Gamma interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01167)
*Yu-Hong Yan,Shi-Qiao Wu,Kun-Liang Zhang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A dimerized anisotropic Gamma interaction in a transverse-field Ising chain generates vector chiral order and dynamical quantum phase transitions, with a gapless critical line showing non-standard criticality and quench-path-dependent DQPT critical times.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper explores how dimerized off-diagonal (Gamma) exchange, beyond conventional Ising interactions, can enrich equilibrium ordering and dynamical critical phenomena in a quantum Ising chain, motivating the study of Gamma anisotropy compet ing with the transverse field.

Method: The authors study a transverse-field Ising chain augmented with dimerized anisotropic Gamma interactions, with and without Ising interactions, using the vector spin chirality and vector chiral order parameter to map the phase diagram, and analyzing spin-spin correlations, subsystem entanglement entropy, and dynamical quantum phase transitions under quenches.

Result: The phase diagram contains positive and negative vector chiral phases plus a paramagnetic phase. Without Ising interactions, a gapless critical line appears with no finite-size drift, showing spin-spin correlations and entanglement entropy behavior distinct from Ising criticality. DQPT critical times form periodic or aperiodic sequences depending on the quench path.

Conclusion: Dimerized off-diagonal exchange enriches both equilibrium ordering and dynamical critical behavior in a quantum Ising chain, yielding vector chiral order, an unconventional gapless critical line, and tunable DQPT critical-time sequences.

Abstract: We investigate a transverse-field Ising chain with dimerized anisotropic Gamma interactions and demonstrate that, both in the absence and presence of Ising interactions, the competition between Gamma anisotropy and the transverse field gives rise to vector chiral order and dynamical quantum phase transitions. The phase diagram comprises positive and negative vector chiral phases and a paramagnetic phase, characterized by the vector spin chirality and the vector chiral order parameter. In the absence of Ising interactions, we identify a gapless critical line that exhibits no finite-size drift, along which the spin-spin correlations and subsystem entanglement entropy display behavior distinct from that of Ising criticality. Furthermore, we find that the critical times of dynamical quantum phase transitions form periodic or aperiodic sequences, depending on the quench path. Our findings highlight how dimerized off-diagonal exchange enriches both equilibrium ordering and dynamical critical behavior in a quantum Ising chain.

</details>


### [75] [Rapid mixing of quantum spin chains at any finite temperature](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01190)
*Leeseok Kim*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proves that a quasi-local quantum Gibbs sampler mixes rapidly to the Gibbs state of any 1D finite-range Hamiltonian at all finite temperatures in O(log(n/ε)) time, with O(polylog(n/ε)) circuit depth for state preparation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Preparing quantum Gibbs states is a central task in quantum computing and many-body physics, and early proposals for heat-bath Gibbs samplers (Kastoryano and Brandão) lacked a rigorous rapid-mixing guarantee. This work aims to establish fast convergence and efficient circuit depth for the 1D case.

Method: Each update traces out a contiguous constant-length block and reconstructs it using the Petz recovery map. The analysis directly bounds the distance between the updated state and the target Gibbs state using the quantum Wasserstein distance of order 1.

Result: The sampler mixes to the Gibbs state of any 1D finite-range Hamiltonian at all finite temperatures in O(log(n/ε)) time, and the Gibbs states can be prepared with O(polylog(n/ε)) depth quantum circuits.

Conclusion: The proposed techniques are expected to facilitate the analysis and design of other Gibbs samplers.

Abstract: We prove that a quasi-local quantum Gibbs sampler rapidly mixes to the Gibbs state of any 1D finite-range Hamiltonian at all finite temperatures in $\mathcal{O}\left(\log (n/\varepsilon)\right)$ time. Each update traces out a contiguous block of constant length and reconstructs it using the Petz recovery map, following early proposals for quantum heat-bath Gibbs samplers by Kastoryano and Brandão~\cite{kastoryano2016quantum}. Our proof directly bounds the distance between the updated state and the target Gibbs state using quantum Wasserstein distance of order $1$. We further provide $\mathcal{O}(\text{polylog}(n/\varepsilon))$ depth of quantum circuits to prepare these Gibbs states. We hope these techniques will facilitate the analysis and design of other Gibbs samplers.

</details>


### [76] [Fermionic Gaussian Scrooge Ensembles in Deep Thermalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01209)
*Ning Sun,Pengfei Zhang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces the fermionic Gaussian Scrooge ensemble as the universal description of deep thermalization in generic free-fermion systems, proving its emergence in the SYK$_2$ model and demonstrating it numerically in charge-conserving random Gaussian circuits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep thermalization studies show that measuring part of a chaotic many-body wave function yields a projected ensemble converging to the Scrooge ensemble. However, free-fermion systems are nonchaotic and their states remain Gaussian, so the standard Scrooge ensemble cannot apply. The authors are motivated to find the correct universal ensemble describing deep thermalization in generic free-fermion systems.

Method: The authors define the fermionic Gaussian Scrooge ensemble as a distortion of the Gaussian Haar ensemble by the average density matrix. They analyze its moments, revealing an enlarged symmetry of Gaussian states in the replicated Hilbert space. They then test this ensemble in two settings: analytically for the SYK$_2$ model's projected ensemble at arbitrary evolution times, and numerically for random Gaussian circuits with charge conservation at long times.

Result: The projected ensemble of the SYK$_2$ model is analytically proven to follow the fermionic Gaussian Scrooge ensemble at arbitrary evolution times. Numerical evidence shows the same ensemble emerges at sufficiently long times in random Gaussian circuits with charge conservation.

Conclusion: The fermionic Gaussian Scrooge ensemble is established as a universal description of deep thermalization in free-fermion systems, extending the deep thermalization framework beyond chaotic systems to nonchaotic Gaussian dynamics.

Abstract: Measuring part of a many-body wave function generates a projected ensemble of pure quantum states on the unmeasured subsystem. Recent advances in deep thermalization have shown that, in chaotic systems, this ensemble universally converges to the maximally random ensemble compatible with its average density matrix, known as the Scrooge ensemble. By contrast, free-fermion systems are nonchaotic, and their quantum states are constrained to remain Gaussian. Motivated by this distinction, we introduce the fermionic Gaussian Scrooge ensemble to describe deep thermalization in generic free-fermion systems. This ensemble is defined as a distortion of the Gaussian Haar ensemble by the average density matrix, and its moments reveal an enlarged symmetry of Gaussian states in the replicated Hilbert space. We demonstrate the emergence of the fermionic Gaussian Scrooge ensemble in two complementary settings: (1) we analytically prove that the projected ensemble of the SYK$_2$ model follows the fermionic Gaussian Scrooge ensemble at arbitrary evolution times; (2) we provide numerical evidence that it emerges at sufficiently long times in random Gaussian circuits with charge conservation. Our results establish the fermionic Gaussian Scrooge ensemble as a universal description of deep thermalization in free-fermion systems.

</details>


### [77] [Experimental quantification of quantum coherence for a set of quantum states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01227)
*Tianle Zheng,Liangsheng Li,Wenting Zhou,Chengjie Zhang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper provides a direct, basis-independent experimental verification of quantum coherence for a set of quantum states, using a Sagnac interferometer, and shows that BB84 states exhibit maximal set coherence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum coherence is a fundamental resource, but previous verifications often rely on specific bases. A basis-independent, experimental validation for a set of quantum states is needed to confirm the theory and enable protocol applications such as BB84 and quantum secure direct communication.

Method: The authors experimentally quantify quantum coherence for two sets of experimental states in a basis-independent manner using a Sagnac interferometer.

Result: Experimental results match the theory of quantum coherence for a set of quantum states perfectly. The BB84 protocol states are shown to exhibit maximal set coherence.

Conclusion: The verified framework supports applications of set coherence in quantum key distribution and probabilistic quantum cloning, paving the way for broader use of quantum coherence for sets of quantum states.

Abstract: We present a direct experimental verification of quantum coherence for a set of quantum states in a basis-independent manner. We find that the theory of quantum coherence for a set of quantum states matches our experimental results perfectly and can be applied to protocols like BB84 and quantum secure direct communication. Using a Sagnac interferometer, we experimentally quantify the quantum coherence for two sets of experimental states. Moreover, we introduce a novel application of set coherence, illustrating that the states employed in the BB84 protocol exhibit maximal set coherence. Our results pave the way for wider applications of quantum coherence for a set of quantum states, including quantum key distribution protocols and probabilistic quantum cloning.

</details>


### [78] [Linear-Time Encodable Quantum Codes near the CSS GV Bound](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01277)
*Rachel Yun Zhang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The authors construct quantum CSS codes whose rate-distance tradeoff approaches the CSS Gilbert-Varshamov bound, with a log-depth linear-gate encoder, viewed as a quantum analogue of classical repeat-multiple-accumulate codes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical repeat-multiple-accumulate codes offer excellent rate-distance tradeoffs with simple, low-complexity encoders that admit efficient encoding; translating these benefits to the quantum setting is desirable but nontrivial. Existing quantum CSS codes approaching the CSS GV bound often lack such simple, shallow encoding circuits. The work is inspired by Brehm and Resch to bridge this gap.

Method: The construction builds quantum CSS codes as a quantum analogue of classical repeat-multiple-accumulate codes. The encoder consists of a single constant-depth outer quantum circuit followed by several classical accumulation and inverse-accumulation layers, yielding logarithmic depth and a linear number of gates.

Result: The resulting codes achieve a rate-distance tradeoff approaching the CSS GV bound while being encodable by a quantum circuit of logarithmic depth using a linear number of gates.

Conclusion: A family of quantum CSS codes approaching the CSS GV bound is obtained with a remarkably simple encoder structure, demonstrating that near-optimal quantum codes can also be efficiently encodable at low circuit depth.

Abstract: We construct quantum CSS codes with rate-distance tradeoff approaching the CSS GV bound, which can be encoded by a quantum circuit of logarithmic depth and a linear number of gates. Our construction is heavily inspired by a work of Brehm and Resch and can be viewed as a quantum analogue of classical repeat multiple accumulate codes. The encoder has a particularly simple structure, consisting of a single constant depth outer quantum circuit followed by several classical accumulation and inverse-accumulation layers.

</details>


### [79] [Preservability of Measurement Incompatibility: Purification, Activation, and a No-Go Theorem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01327)
*Chao-Hsien Wu,Franco Nori,Huan-Yu Ku*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies purification and activation of measurement incompatibility preservability, introducing robustness-based resource monotones, proving pre-filtering cannot activate it (no-go theorem), and showing stochastic activation via post-filtering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Measurement incompatibility is a fundamental quantum resource, but system-environment interactions can degrade or destroy it via measurement-incompatibility-annihilating channels. Recent work characterized noisy dynamics' ability to preserve incompatibility within the resource theory of measurement incompatibility preservability, motivating the need to understand purification and activation of this preservation.

Method: The authors introduce robustness-based resource monotones as figures of merit within the resource theory of measurement incompatibility preservability. They analyze pre-filtering operations for strengthening preservability and post-filtering operations for activation, examining whether these operations can activate preservability from an incompatibility-annihilating channel.

Result: Pre-filtering operations can strengthen measurement incompatibility preservability but cannot activate it from an incompatibility-annihilating channel, establishing a no-go theorem. However, an incompatibility-annihilating channel can be stochastically activated via post-filtering operations.

Conclusion: The results provide a practical framework for exploiting measurement incompatibility in quantum information processing, clarifying the roles of pre- and post-filtering in preserving and activating this resource.

Abstract: Measurement incompatibility is a fundamental quantum resource that enables advantages in many quantum information tasks, including cryptography and communication. However, unavoidable interactions between a system and its environment can degrade or even completely destroy measurement incompatibility; such a process is referred to as a measurement-incompatibility-annihilating channel. Recently, the capability of noisy quantum dynamics to preserve measurement incompatibility has been characterized within the resource theory of measurement incompatibility preservability. This motivates studying how to purify the preservation of measurement incompatibility and how to activate it from a measurement-incompatibility-annihilating channel. To this end, we first introduce our figures of merit as robustness-based resource monotones within this resource theory. We demonstrate that while pre-filtering operations can strengthen measurement incompatibility preservability, they cannot activate it from an incompatibility-annihilating channel, establishing a no-go theorem. Furthermore, we explicitly demonstrate that an incompatibility-annihilating channel can be stochastically activated via post-filtering operations. Our results provide a practical framework for exploiting measurement incompatibility in quantum information processing.

</details>


### [80] [Achieving Identical Stored Energy in Cascaded Collisional Quantum Battery Charging: Analytical Result](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01332)
*Longyan Gong,Jing Zhang,Yongtao Li*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work studies a two-qubit quantum battery in a cascaded collision model and uses an adaptive measurement on the charger after each collision to suppress trial-to-trial charging fluctuations, achieving full suppression for excited-state chargers while proving such full suppression is impossible for general superposition chargers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reliable quantum batteries require uniform charging across distinct trials, but intrinsic quantum stochasticity fundamentally undermines this uniformity. This paper aims to overcome trajectory-dependent stored-energy fluctuations in quantum battery charging.

Method: The authors model a two-qubit quantum battery within cascaded collision models. They propose an adaptive measurement performed on the charger after each collision, which produces two conditional battery states with equal expected stored energy. Analytical treatment is carried out for chargers prepared in the excited state and in general superposition states, supported by numerical simulations.

Result: For chargers in the excited state, the authors analytically prove that complete suppression of trajectory-dependent stored-energy fluctuations is attainable. For chargers in general superposition states, full suppression is shown to be fundamentally prohibited due to battery-phase-dependent recursive dynamics. Numerical simulations agree excellently and quantitatively with the theoretical predictions.

Conclusion: Adaptive measurement on the charger offers a feasible pathway toward high-uniformity quantum-battery charging, with the caveat that complete fluctuation suppression is limited to excited-state chargers and forbidden for general superposition chargers.

Abstract: Charging uniformity across distinct trials is an essential requirement for reliable quantum batteries. Nevertheless, intrinsic quantum stochasticity poses a fundamental challenge to this goal. This work investigates a two-qubit quantum battery within the framework of cascaded collision models. We propose an adaptive measurement on the charger after each collision, yielding two conditional battery states of equal expected stored energy. For chargers prepared in the excited state, we analytically prove that complete suppression of trajectory-dependent stored-energy fluctuations is attainable; for chargers prepared in general superposition states, our theory reveals that full suppression is fundamentally prohibited owing to battery-phase-dependent recursive dynamics. Numerical simulations are in excellent quantitative agreement with theoretical predictions. These findings provide a feasible pathway toward high-uniformity quantum-battery charging.

</details>


### [81] [Vacuum entanglement in a time-dependent electric field](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01394)
*Álvaro Álvarez-Domínguez,Luis J. Garay,Mercedes Martín-Benito,Iker Sanz-González*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The vacuum correlations of a charged scalar field between two disjoint regions are studied under a time-dependent homogeneous electric field. The field increases state mixedness and total correlations but reduces distillable entanglement between regions, which vanishes at finite time for intense non-perturbative pulses. The background also induces anisotropic, direction-dependent correlation distribution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how an external electric background, via the Schwinger effect (particle-antiparticle creation), modifies quantum correlations and entanglement structure of the field vacuum across spatially separated regions.

Method: Construct localized observables in two disjoint regions of a charged scalar field vacuum. Consider a time-dependent homogeneous electric field and relate the time evolution of correlations to particle-antiparticle creation through the Schwinger effect, including analysis of background-induced anisotropy.

Result: The electric field increases the mixedness of the states and enhances total correlations, while distillable entanglement between the regions decreases and vanishes at a finite time for sufficiently intense pulses in the non-perturbative pair-production regime. The induced anisotropy makes the correlation distribution acquire a nontrivial directional dependence.

Conclusion: External electric fields can enhance overall correlations while destroying usable (distillable) entanglement between disjoint regions, with a marked directional, anisotropic character—linking entanglement degradation directly to Schwinger pair production.

Abstract: We analyze the correlations of the vacuum state of a charged scalar field by constructing localized observables in two disjoint regions. We consider a time-dependent homogeneous electric field and examine the time evolution of correlations by relating it with the particle-antiparticle creation through the Schwinger effect. Our results show that the electric field increases the mixedness of the states and enhances total correlations, while the distillable entanglement among the regions decreases, vanishing at a finite time for sufficiently intense pulses within the non-perturbative pair-production regime. We also discuss the effect of anisotropy induced by the external background, showing that the distribution of correlations acquires a nontrivial directional dependence.

</details>


### [82] [Optimal initial states for quantum Fisher information in linearized cavity optomechanics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01417)
*Wangjun Lu,Qing Yu,Ying Li,Cuilu Zhai,Rui Zhang,Zhao-Hui Peng,Shiqing Tang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper determines optimal initial states of a linearized cavity-optomechanical system for estimating the single-photon coupling via quantum Fisher information, deriving closed-form optima in the beam-splitter regime, a Heisenberg bound quadratic in energy saturated by two-mode NOON states, and extensions to the blue sideband, cavity loss, and a realistic quantum-coherent coupling experiment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Estimating the single-photon coupling is central to cavity optomechanics, but the optimal probe states for this metrological task had not been systematically characterized. The authors aim to identify the initial states that maximize the quantum Fisher information for coupling estimation.

Method: The authors analyze a linearized cavity-optomechanical system, using quantum Fisher information as the figure of merit. In the red-detuned (beam-splitter) regime, for pure inputs the Fisher information reduces to four times the variance of the excitation-exchange operator, turning the problem into variance maximization. They employ a Lagrange-dual argument to bound the coherence of a phase-matched squeezed vacuum for fixed mechanical reference, and perform exact diagonalization in truncated Fock spaces. Joint optimization at fixed total excitation number is treated, with extensions to the blue sideband, cavity loss (renewal-optimal duration), and a state-of-the-art experiment.

Result: With a Fock mechanical mode the probe is degenerate: any state saturating the photon-number constraint is optimal, and a many-phonon Fock reference gives linear per-photon sensitivity enhancement. For a fixed mechanical reference the optimum is a phase-matched squeezed vacuum. Jointly optimizing both modes at fixed total excitation, the quantum Fisher information obeys a Heisenberg bound quadratic in energy, saturated by equal superpositions of the two extremal generator eigenstates, i.e. a two-mode NOON state after a balanced beam splitter, while balanced product Fock states achieve only linear enhancement. Exact diagonalization confirms all results, with the single-photon input as the simplest saturating case. The blue sideband exhibits an unbounded two-mode-squeezing spectrum making the entangled optimum impossible, so the balanced product Fock state is exactly optimal at definite energy. Under cavity loss, information accumulation becomes linear on the cavity-lifetime timescale with a renewal-optimal duration of about 2.5 lifetimes. For a state-of-the-art quantum-coherent coupling experiment, the per-hour relative precision lies between 1e-6 and 1e-5, with number and thermal references being the most robust.

Conclusion: Optimal initial states for estimating the single-photon coupling are identified: a degenerate Fock-referenced family in the beam-splitter regime, a phase-matched squeezed vacuum for fixed mechanical reference, and a two-mode NOON state saturating a quadratic Heisenberg bound when both modes are jointly optimized. Extensions show that on the blue sideband the balanced product Fock state is exactly optimal, that cavity loss makes information accumulation linear with an optimal duration near 2.5 lifetimes, and that a realistic experiment can reach relative precision of roughly one to ten parts in a million per hour, with number and thermal references most robust.

Abstract: We find the optimal initial states of a linearized cavity-optomechanical system for estimating the single-photon coupling by quantum Fisher information. In the red-detuned (beam-splitter) regime the interaction exchanges excitations between the two modes, and for pure inputs the Fisher information is four times the variance of the excitation-exchange operator, reducing the optimization to variance maximization with a closed-form solution. With the mechanical mode in a Fock state the probe is degenerate: any state saturating the photon-number constraint is optimal, and a Fock reference with many phonons enhances the per-photon sensitivity linearly. For an arbitrary fixed mechanical reference it becomes a phase-matched squeezed vacuum, from an exact bound on that coherence obtained by a Lagrange-dual argument. Jointly optimizing both modes at fixed total excitation number, the quantum Fisher information obeys a Heisenberg bound quadratic in the energy, saturated by equal superpositions of the two extremal eigenstates of the generator, equivalently a two-mode NOON state after a balanced beam splitter; balanced product Fock states reach only a linear enhancement. Exact diagonalization in truncated Fock spaces confirms all the results, the familiar single-photon input being the simplest saturating case. Three extensions are worked out: the blue sideband has an unbounded two-mode-squeezing spectrum, so the entangled optimum becomes impossible and the balanced product Fock state is exactly optimal at definite energy; under cavity loss the information accumulation turns linear on the cavity-lifetime timescale, at a renewal-optimal duration of about two and a half lifetimes; and for a state-of-the-art quantum-coherent coupling experiment the per-hour relative precision lies between one part in a million and ten parts in a million, number and thermal references being the most robust.

</details>


### [83] [Robust and leakage-resilient device-independent oblivious transfer in MiniQCryp](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01421)
*Zhili Chen,Rahul Jain,YaoNan Zhang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Assuming post-quantum one-way functions, this paper constructs device-independent (DI) oblivious transfer (OT) and bit commitment, where honest parties use only trusted classical computation to operate untrusted quantum devices that may share arbitrary entanglement and behave non-IID. The protocols are simulation-based secure against quantum polynomial-time adversaries and compose sequentially. One skeleton serves both primitives, tolerating a constant fault rate under isolated laboratories with coordinate-local measurements, or an inverse-polylogarithmic rate under polylogarithmic adaptive leakage and arbitrary joint measurements. Since OT is complete, this yields DI secure computation with abort for any efficiently computable classical functionality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Device-independent cryptography removes the need to trust quantum devices, but prior DI OT and commitment constructions either assumed IID behavior, lacked simulation-based security, required stronger trust, or tolerated only vanishing fault rates. This work aims to achieve simulation-based, sequentially composable DI OT and bit commitment from post-quantum one-way functions, robust to non-IID devices, arbitrary entanglement, and noisy or leaking devices.

Method: A single protocol skeleton serves both OT and commitment, in two regimes: (1) isolated laboratories with coordinate-local measurements in the honest receiver's device, tolerating a constant rate of honest-device faults; (2) polylogarithmically many qubits of adaptive leakage between laboratories and arbitrary joint measurements, tolerating an inverse-polylogarithmic rate. Each elementary DI call uses a fresh, isolated batch of polylogarithmically many device coordinates, and total device use in the compiled OT protocol is polynomial. The commitment extractor changes a public parity relation via classical equivocation without altering the device execution or its leakage. A commit-and-prove functionality, disjoint audits, and an affine consistency check link certified correlations to ideal OT. Sender security relies on a selector-aware parallel-repetition bound for the Magic Square game, derived from the two-round threshold theorem of Kundu and Tan.

Result: The construction yields DI OT and bit commitment with simulation-based security against quantum polynomial-time adversaries, sequentially composable with efficient simulators. The commitment has efficient simulators against both parties and yields DI coin tossing with abort. Because OT is complete for secure computation, the result gives a DI protocol (with abort) for every efficiently computable classical functionality on a fixed number of parties, secure against static corruption of any proper subset. Fault tolerance is achieved at a constant rate in the isolated-laboratory regime and an inverse-polylogarithmic rate in the leakage regime.

Conclusion: Device-independent oblivious transfer and bit commitment, and consequently general DI secure computation with abort, are achievable from post-quantum one-way functions under simulation-based, sequentially composable security, tolerating non-IID devices, arbitrary entanglement, constant or inverse-polylogarithmic noise depending on the regime, and adaptive leakage. This establishes DI completeness for classical functionalities on a fixed number of parties with static corruption.

Abstract: Assuming post-quantum one-way functions, we construct device-independent (DI) oblivious transfer (OT) and bit commitment: honest parties use only trusted classical computation to operate untrusted quantum devices, which may share arbitrary entanglement and behave non-IID. Security is simulation-based against quantum polynomial-time adversaries and composes sequentially with efficient simulators. One protocol skeleton serves both, in two regimes. With isolated laboratories and coordinate-local measurements in the honest receiver's device, it tolerates a constant rate of honest-device faults. With polylogarithmically many qubits of adaptive leakage between the laboratories and arbitrary joint measurements, it tolerates an inverse-polylogarithmic rate. Each elementary DI call uses a fresh, isolated batch of polylogarithmically many device coordinates, and total device use in the compiled OT protocol is polynomial. The commitment has efficient simulators against both parties and yields DI coin tossing with abort.
  Because OT is complete for secure computation, the construction yields a DI protocol, with abort, for every efficiently computable classical functionality on a fixed number of parties, secure against static corruption of any proper subset of them.
  The commitment's extractor changes a public parity relation through classical equivocation and leaves the device execution, hence its leakage, unchanged. A commit-and-prove functionality, disjoint audits, and an affine consistency check link the certified correlations to ideal OT. Sender security rests on a selector-aware parallel-repetition bound for the Magic Square game, which we derive from the two-round threshold theorem of Kundu and Tan.

</details>


### [84] [Unflattening by Flattening -- How Input Distributions Shape Output Variance in Angle-Encoded Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01446)
*Melvin Strobl,Gabriel Mejia,Eileen Kuehn,Achim Streit*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper analyzes when barren plateaus are avoided for angle-encoded product states by linking output variance to the input's "algebraic purity" (overlap with the circuit's dynamical Lie algebra), showing matchgate and off-diagonal circuit families retain output variance of order 1/n, and provides a classical preprocessing certificate for the off-diagonal family.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Barren plateaus cause loss gradients in parameterized quantum circuits to vanish exponentially with qubit number, impeding training. Prior understanding of when gradients survive does not fully account for how the input state distribution, via its structure relative to the circuit's dynamical Lie algebra, governs output variance.

Method: The authors introduce algebraic input purity—the input's overlap with the circuit's dynamical Lie algebra—to explain how input distributions affect output variation for angle-encoded product states. They analyze matchgate and off-diagonal circuit families under specified readouts and Haar or exact group 2-design sampling, counting diagonal Pauli strings to identify zero-output endpoints. They also construct a classical preprocessing certificate using a shared random rotation accepted only when the dataset's mean purity passes a computable threshold, and study binary and ternary weighted encodings.

Result: Matchgate circuits on n qubits retain output variance of order 1/n for every pure product input; the off-diagonal family gives zero output on computational-basis inputs at any depth and for every parameter choice. Independent uniform angles yield mean variance of the same order as matchgates. The preprocessing certificate certifies mean output variance of order 1/n for the off-diagonal family before circuit execution with a constant expected number of trials, and weighted encodings recover independent-angle mean purity from one uniform scalar input. Numerical experiments examine training behavior and larger-algebra extensions.

Conclusion: The variance guarantee is for output variance averaged over inputs; successful learning additionally depends on the task and on information preserved by the encoding.

Abstract: Barren plateaus hinder training of parameterized quantum circuits by making loss gradients exponentially small in the number of qubits. For angle-encoded product states, we show how the input distribution affects output variation through algebraic input purity, i.e. the input's overlap with the circuit's dynamical Lie algebra. With the specified readouts and Haar or exact group 2-design sampling, matchgate circuits on $n$ qubits retain output variance of order $1/n$ for every pure product input. The off-diagonal family instead has zero output on computational-basis inputs at any depth and for every parameter choice. Independent uniform angles yield mean variance of the same order as matchgates. A count of diagonal Pauli strings identifies these zero-output endpoints. For any fixed dataset of nonzero inputs with at least $2n$ coordinates, we construct a classical preprocessing certificate. A shared random rotation is accepted only when the dataset's mean purity passes a computable threshold. This certifies mean output variance of order $1/n$ for the off-diagonal family before circuit execution, with a constant expected number of trials. Binary and ternary weighted encodings also recover the independent-angle mean purity from one uniform scalar input. Numerical experiments examine training behavior and extensions to larger algebras. The guarantee concerns output variance averaged over inputs, while successful learning also depends on the task and information preserved by the encoding.

</details>


### [85] [Unbounded separation between definite and indefinite causal order in finite-dimensional quantum metrology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01462)
*Yanglin Hu,Zi-Shen Li,Giulio Chiribella,Yuxiang Yang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper shows arbitrarily large (unbounded) advantages of indefinite causal order over definite causal order for finite-dimensional quantum systems in the finite sample regime, establishing an energy-saving separation in geometric phase estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Indefinite causal order is known to provide an unbounded advantage in quantum metrology, but this advantage was believed to be specific to infinite-dimensional systems. Recent findings suggested that in finite dimensions advantages are fundamentally limited to bounded constant factors. This paper challenges that belief by investigating whether arbitrarily large advantages can arise in finite-dimensional settings.

Method: The authors establish an unbounded separation between definite and indefinite causal order in estimating a geometric phase associated to two sets of N displacements generated by discrete position and momentum operators on a d-dimensional quantum system. They prove an approximate Weyl relation for discrete Gaussian wavepackets as a key technical tool, and analyze the required probe energy under a bounded number of measurement shots ν.

Result: For any given constant R, there exist values of N and d=Ω(N²) such that a strategy with indefinite order uses an initial probe with R times less energy than every definite-order strategy achieving the same mean squared error, provided the number of measurement shots satisfies ν=O(exp(πd/16)/poly(d)). The energy saving grows arbitrarily large with the problem parameters.

Conclusion: Arbitrarily large advantages of indefinite causal order do arise for finite-dimensional systems in the finite sample regime, contrary to recent suggestions that finite-dimensional advantages are bounded. This demonstrates that indefinite order offers an energy saving that grows without bound with the parameters of the problem.

Abstract: Indefinite causal order is known to offer enhancements in quantum metrology, notably including an unbounded advantage in the measurement of a geometric phase of a harmonic oscillator. This advantage, however, is specific to the infinite dimensional setting, and its finite dimensional analogue remains elusive, with recent findings suggesting that advantages in finite dimensions may be fundamentally limited to bounded constant factors. Here we show that, in fact, arbitrarily large advantages arise for finite dimensional systems in the finite sample regime. Specifically, we establish an unbounded separation between definite and indefinite causal order in the estimation of a geometric phase associated to two sets of $N$ displacements generated by discrete position and momentum operators on a $d$-dimensional quantum system: for any given constant $R$, there exist values of $N$ and $d=Ω(N^2)$ such that a strategy with indefinite order uses an initial probe with $R$ times less energy than the probe required by every strategy with definite order achieving the same mean squared error, whenever the number of measurement shots $ν$ is bounded as $ν=\mathcal{O}(\exp(πd/16)/\mathrm{poly}(d))$. In other words, indefinite order offers an energy saving that grows arbitrarily large with the parameters of the problem. To prove this result, we establish an approximate Weyl relation for discrete Gaussian wavepackets, which is of independent technical interest.

</details>


### [86] [Zero- Versus Infinite-Temperature Damping in Variational Quantum Circuits: Feature Scale, Sampling Cost, and Frame Gauge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01466)
*Vu-Quoc-Minh Nguyen,Tuan-Vu Truong,Hoang-Long Nguyen,Trung-Khanh Le*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The Pauli twirl of amplitude damping equals generalized amplitude damping at infinite temperature; comparing them in variational circuits isolates AD's zero-temperature non-unital bias, which mainly rescales features. A trainable output scale recovers most accuracy (AD leads twirls by ≤~3 points), but the gap reappears as a shot-cost: at 10^3 shots/image, a 4-qubit AD classifier at p=0.3 stays within 1.5 points of noiseless accuracy while twirled ones lose up to 33. The damping direction is a gauge only under complete entangling layers with trainable boundaries, becoming physical inside decomposed two-qubit gates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To isolate and characterize the effect of amplitude damping's (AD) non-unital, zero-temperature bias in variational quantum circuits, by using the Pauli twirl of AD—which is generalized amplitude damping at infinite temperature—as a controlled reference that preserves AD's contraction but removes its non-unital term.

Method: Analytical and numerical study of variational circuits under AD versus its Pauli twirls: study feature behavior under random parameters (floor values, closed forms, temperature dependence ~tanh(ħω/2k_BT), per-layer shrinkage up to eight qubits); introduce a trainable output scale; benchmark classifier accuracy and shot-noise robustness (10^3 shots/image); analyze the gauge status of the damping direction with complete entangling layers/trainable boundaries and inside decomposed two-qubit gates.

Result: Features under AD settle on a floor, set by the last layer at strong damping, with closed form and temperature dependence tanh(ħω/2k_BT) at fixed T1; under twirls they shrink by a constant factor per layer (up to eight qubits). A trainable output scale removes most accuracy differences, leaving AD ahead by at most ~3 percentage points; however, the removed gap reappears as measurement-shot cost: at 10^3 shots/image, a 4-qubit AD classifier at p=0.3 stays within 1.5 points of noiseless accuracy while twirled classifiers lose up to 33. At weaker damping, separation depth grows as ~(np)^{-1}ln(1/p). The damping direction is a gauge when damping follows complete entangling layers with trainable boundaries, and becomes physical inside a decomposed two-qubit gate (eigensolver).

Conclusion: AD's advantage over its twirls is largely a feature-scaling effect that a trainable output scale can absorb in accuracy terms, but the cost is deferred to shot statistics, where twirled circuits degrade substantially. The gauge nature of the damping direction depends on circuit structure—it is unphysical under complete entangling layers with trainable boundaries but physical within decomposed two-qubit gates—clarifying when zero-temperature bias matters versus when it can be gauged away.

Abstract: The Pauli twirl of amplitude damping (AD) is generalized amplitude damping at infinite temperature: it keeps the contraction of AD and removes its non-unital term, so comparing the two in variational circuits isolates the zero-temperature bias, which acts mainly through the scale of the features. For random parameters, features under AD settle on a floor, which at strong damping is set by the last layer, has a closed form, and at fixed $T_1$ falls with temperature as $\tanh(\hbarω/2k_BT)$; under the twirls they shrink by a constant factor per layer, up to eight qubits. A trainable output scale removes most of the resulting accuracy differences, leaving AD ahead of its twirls by at most about three percentage points in our simulations; what it removes reappears as a cost in measurement shots: trained and tested with $10^3$ shots per image, a four-qubit classifier under AD at $p=0.3$ stays within 1.5 points of noiseless accuracy, while the twirled classifiers lose up to 33. At weaker damping the separation depth grows roughly as $(np)^{-1}\ln(1/p)$. The damping direction is a gauge when the damping follows complete entangling layers and the circuit boundaries are trainable; in an eigensolver it becomes physical inside a decomposed two-qubit gate.

</details>


### [87] [Continuous-Process Randomized Compilation for Quantum Process Tensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01521)
*He Wang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces continuous-process randomized compilation (CPRC) within the continuous process tensor (cPT) framework to tailor errors in continuous-time, memory-bearing quantum processes, extending randomized compiling beyond discrete circuits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional discrete randomized compiling (RC) applies random control frames only at logical-gate boundaries, so it cannot capture intra-instrument control evolution or persistent system-environment coupling. In memory-bearing environments, errors correlate across time rather than being confined to single gates, necessitating a continuous-time treatment.

Method: The authors formulate CPRC in the cPT framework, unifying random unitary control trajectories, pointwise-covariant finite-duration instruments, and environmental dynamics on a single time axis. They study four protocols: fixed Pauli refresh, bounded Cayley paths, unitary Brownian motion, and Ornstein-Uhlenbeck driving. Analysis includes a closed-frame counterexample, an exact static-bath solution, and derivation of a total-variation error bound for adaptive output records under bounded centered coupling and mixing conditions.

Result: (1) Pointwise covariance makes each trajectory reproduce the target logic exactly absent system-environment coupling, while a closed-frame counterexample shows endpoint-only compensation yields first-order instrument errors. (2) Independent Pauli conjugations over full intervals diagonalize error histories at boundaries, but classical labels retain environment-induced correlations, confirmed by an exact static-bath solution. (3) A total-variation error bound is derived for full adaptive output records, and convergence of trajectory-averaged cPT coefficients is proven for finite non-Gaussian environments and Gaussian baths with time-integrable covariance, including the uncut Drude spectrum. Numerical validation in a qubit model uses output probabilities, operator conditional mutual information, and all two-particle coefficients for qubit and thermal Drude baths.

Conclusion: CPRC extends randomized compiling from discrete circuits to continuous-time, memory-bearing quantum processes, providing a unified framework, quantitative tools, implementable schemes, and benchmarks for non-Markovian error tailoring.

Abstract: Traditional discrete randomized compiling (RC) tailors errors with random control frames at logical-gate boundaries, but fails to capture intra-instrument control evolution or persistent system-environment coupling. In memory-bearing environments, errors correlate across times, not just single gates. We build continuous-process randomized compilation (CPRC) in the continuous process tensor (cPT) framework, unifying random unitary control trajectories, pointwise-covariant finite-duration instruments, and environmental dynamics on one time axis. We study four protocols: fixed Pauli refresh, bounded Cayley paths, unitary Brownian motion, Ornstein-Uhlenbeck driving. We obtain three key results: (1) Pointwise covariance ensures each trajectory exactly reproduces the target logic without system-environment coupling; a closed-frame counterexample proves endpoint-only compensation causes first-order instrument errors. (2) Independent Pauli conjugations over full intervals diagonalize error histories at boundaries, but classical labels retain environment-induced correlations, confirmed by an exact static-bath solution. (3) We derive a total-variation error bound for full adaptive output records under bounded centered coupling and mixing conditions. We prove convergence of trajectory-averaged cPT coefficients in fixed particle sectors for finite non-Gaussian environments and Gaussian baths with time-integrable covariance, including the uncut Drude spectrum. We numerically validate the theory in a qubit model via output probabilities, operator conditional mutual information, and all two-particle coefficients for both qubit and thermal Drude baths. This work extends RC from discrete circuits to continuous-time memory-bearing quantum processes, offering a unified framework, quantitative tools, implementable schemes, and benchmarks for non-Markovian error tailoring.

</details>


### [88] [Efficient learning of quantum interactions from thermal metastable states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01538)
*Bingrun Wang,Qi Ye,Chi-Fang Chen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper reformulates finite-temperature Hamiltonian learning by replacing intractable Gibbs states with physically realistic metastable states of detailed-balanced Lindbladians, extending Gibbs-state learning protocols to this setting with nearly optimal sample and computational complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning quantum interactions from finite-temperature many-body systems is a central task in emerging quantum platforms. While efficient protocols exist for learning from lattice quantum Gibbs states, these assume access to exact Gibbs states, which are computationally intractable to prepare and may not faithfully represent generic finite-temperature quantum systems. In practice, a system coupled to a heat bath can remain stuck at an approximate stationary state (metastable state) long before true equilibration, motivating a physically and algorithmically consistent alternative.

Method: The authors formulate learning from metastable states of detailed-balanced master equations (Lindbladians) arising from system-bath interactions. They distill the algorithmic mechanism and structural condition underlying Gibbs-state learning and extend it fully to metastable states. They also sharpen notions of metastability and develop a unified framework for finite-temperature learning.

Result: The extended protocol attains nearly optimal sample and computational complexity in both the system size and the precision.

Conclusion: Learning from metastable states of detailed-balanced Lindbladians provides a physically and algorithmically consistent alternative to Gibbs-state learning, achieving near-optimal complexity and offering a unified framework for finite-temperature learning.

Abstract: Learning quantum interactions from finite-temperature many-body systems is a central task in emerging quantum platforms. Recently, the problem of learning from lattice quantum Gibbs states has found rigorous, efficient protocols. Nevertheless, exact Gibbs states, as the input premise, are in fact computationally intractable to prepare and may not faithfully represent generic finite-temperature quantum systems. In contrast, a system coupled to a heat bath can be stuck at an approximate stationary state (metastable state) long before it truly equilibrates. Here, we formulate a physically and algorithmically consistent alternative: learning from such metastable states of detailed-balanced master equations (Lindbladians) arising from system-bath interactions. We distill the algorithmic mechanism and structural condition underlying Gibbs-state learning and extend it in full to metastable states, attaining nearly optimal sample and computational complexity (in the system size and the precision). More broadly, we sharpen notions of metastability and develop a unified framework for finite-temperature learning.

</details>


### [89] [Molecular Dynamics with Nuclear Effects on Quantum Computers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01549)
*Lukas Haßfurth,Juliane Heitkämper,Elias Walter,Birger Horstmann*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces a hybrid quantum-classical ab-initio molecular dynamics algorithm using the nuclear-electronic orbital (NEO) method to capture nuclear quantum effects, validated on H2, H2O, and the Zundel ion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nuclear quantum effects are essential for describing proton transfer and hydrogen bonding, yet incorporating them into quantum chemistry calculations is computationally prohibitive on classical hardware. Quantum computers offer a promising alternative due to their linear scaling of space requirements with system size.

Method: A hybrid quantum-classical algorithm for ab-initio molecular dynamics that embeds nuclear quantum effects via the nuclear-electronic orbital (NEO) method. Ground state energies and forces are evaluated on a quantum computer using a variational quantum eigensolver (VQE), while molecular geometries are updated classically. Compact hardware-efficient ansatz circuits are compared against more accurate UCCSD ansatzes.

Result: Simulations of H2, H2O, and the Zundel ion H5O2+ reproduce vibrational spectra compared with experimental data. Including nuclear quantum effects renders the proton shuttling motion in the Zundel ion effectively barrierless, and frequency errors improve significantly. Hardware-efficient ansatz results are comparable to UCCSD.

Conclusion: The algorithm demonstrates the feasibility of executing NEO-based ab-initio molecular dynamics with nuclear quantum effects on near-term quantum devices, as compact hardware-efficient circuits achieve accuracy comparable to UCCSD.

Abstract: Nuclear quantum effects are critical for describing proton transfer and hydrogen bonding, but their incorporation into quantum chemistry calculations is often computationally prohibitive on classical hardware. A promising alternative are quantum computers due to their linear scaling in the space requirements with system size. We introduce a novel hybrid quantum-classical algorithm for ab-initio molecular dynamics that incorporates nuclear quantum effects via the nuclear-electronic orbital method. The proposed algorithm evaluates ground state energies and forces on the quantum computer using a variational quantum eigensolver, while the molecular geometries are updated classically. We validate our approach through simulations of $\text{H}_2$, $\text{H}_2\text{O}$ and the Zundel ion $\text{H}_5\text{O}_2^+$, comparing the simulated vibrational spectra with experimental data. Upon inclusion of nuclear quantum effects, the proton shuttling movement in the Zundel ion becomes effectively barrierless, and errors in the simulated frequencies improve significantly. Employing compact hardware-efficient ansatz circuits we achieve results comparable to the more accurate UCCSD ansatzes, which hints towards the feasibility of executing our algorithm on near-term quantum devices.

</details>


### [90] [Efficient certification of time-reversal symmetry requires entanglement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01555)
*Zhenhuan Liu,Zhenyu Du,Yifan Tang,Zi-Wen Liu,Jens Eisert,Ingo Roth*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper formulates a Bell-inequality-like test of time-reversal symmetry using only forward access and trusted quantum operations, showing that entanglement converts temporal input-output relations into measurable spatial exchange symmetry. For n-qubit unitary dynamics, distinguishing time-reversal-symmetric circular ensembles from Haar-random dynamics requires Ω(min{2^{n/2}, 2^{n-e}}) queries for any classically adaptive protocol, where e relates to probe and measurement logarithmic entanglement negativities; maximally entangled probes and SWAP measurements reduce this to a constant number of queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time-reversal symmetry is a fundamental principle of physics, yet testing it is challenging because it conventionally requires reversing the arrow of time—an operation not generally available in experiments. The paper aims to overcome this by exploiting quantum entanglement to convert temporal input-output relations into a measurable spatial exchange symmetry, enabling a forward-access-only test.

Method: The authors formulate a Bell-inequality-like test of the antiunitary time-reversal symmetry that uses only forward access and trusted quantum operations. They analyze n-qubit unitary dynamics and prove query-complexity lower bounds for classically adaptive protocols seeking to distinguish time-reversal-symmetric circular ensembles from Haar-random dynamics. They define e = min{e_s, e_m}, with e_s and e_m the probe and measurement logarithmic entanglement negativities. They also develop a general time-reversal symmetry test for arbitrary fixed, compatible probes and measurements, relating its query complexity to their logarithmic negativities, and optimize the probe and measurement to match the lower-bound scaling in the high-entanglement regime.

Result: Any classically adaptive protocol needs Ω(min{2^{n/2}, 2^{n-e}}) queries to reliably distinguish time-reversal-symmetric circular ensembles from Haar-random dynamics.
