Maximally entangled probes combined with SWAP measurements reduce this cost to a constant number of queries. The general test's query complexity is tied to the logarithmic negativities of the probe and measurement, and optimized probe/measurement choices match the lower-bound scaling in the high-entanglement regime.

Conclusion: The results establish a quantitative connection between entanglement and time-reversal symmetry, bridging two central concepts in quantum information science and fundamental physics. Entanglement serves as a resource that converts temporal input-output relations into spatial exchange symmetry, enabling efficient tests of an antiunitary symmetry without backward time access.

Abstract: Time-reversal symmetry is a fundamental principle of physics describing the invariance of physical laws under reversal of the direction of time. We formulate a Bell-inequality-like test of this antiunitary symmetry using only forward access and trusted quantum operations: entanglement converts temporal input--output relations into measurable spatial exchange symmetry. For $n$-qubit unitary dynamics, we prove that reliably distinguishing the time-reversal-symmetric circular ensembles from Haar-random dynamics requires $Ω(\min\{2^{n/2},2^{n-e}\})$ queries for any classically adaptive protocol. Here, $e=\min\{e_{\mathrm s},e_{\mathrm m}\}$ with $e_{\mathrm s}$ and $e_{\mathrm m}$ representing the probe and measurement logarithmic entanglement negativities, respectively. Maximally entangled probes and SWAP measurements reduce this cost to a constant number of queries. Furthermore, we develop a time-reversal symmetry test for arbitrary fixed, compatible probes and measurements, relate its query complexity to their logarithmic negativities, and match the lower-bound scaling in the high-entanglement regime by optimizing the probe and measurement. Our results establish a quantitative connection between entanglement and time-reversal symmetry, bridging two central concepts in quantum information science and fundamental physics.

</details>


### [91] [Adversarial Robustness in Fake Quantum Simulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01574)
*Marc Maußner,Volker Reers*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A dual-phased study benchmarked Qiskit Aer simulation throughput across hardware architectures and evaluated PGD attacks plus adversarial retraining for QML classifiers under realistic noise, projecting up to 8 qubits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: QML models deployed on noise-model-based fake simulators face open questions about runtime scalability and adversarial robustness under realistic noise, which this work aims to address.

Method: Phase one benchmarks computational throughput of Qiskit's Aer simulation engine across varying hardware architectures for medium-scale simulations; phase two evaluates Projected Gradient Descent (PGD) attacks and adversarial retraining strategies under realistic noise conditions.

Result: The study quantifies runtime tradeoffs and scaling behavior for medium-scale simulations projected up to 8 qubits, and shows that high adversarial-to-benign retraining ratios (50/50) are essential for practical robustness of 4-qubit classifiers under realistic noise.

Conclusion: Practical QML robustness on noisy fake-backend simulators requires substantial adversarial retraining (50/50 ratio), while simulation cost scales with qubit count and hardware architecture.

Abstract: This paper investigates the performance scalability and adversarial robustness of Quantum Machine Learning (QML) models deployed on noise-model-based fake simulators. We conduct a dual-phased study, first benchmarking the computational throughput of Qiskit's Aer simulation engine across varying hardware architectures, and second, evaluating the effectiveness of Projected Gradient Descent (PGD) attacks and adversarial retraining strategies under realistic noise conditions. Our results quantify the runtime tradeoffs and scaling behavior for medium-scale simulations (projected up to 8 qubits) and demonstrate that high adversarial-to-benign retraining ratios (50/50) are essential for achieving practical model robustness for 4-qubit classifiers under realistic noise conditions.

</details>


### [92] [Electromagnetic Side-Channel Vulnerability in QKD Equipment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01609)
*Mikio Fujiwara,Katsumi Fujii,Fumie Ono,Masakazu Ono,Akihisa Tomita*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Electromagnetic emissions from a time-bin decoy-state BB84 prototype QKD device were measured at 0 m and 3 m in an anechoic chamber; the transmitter dominated leakage, with distinguishable quantum-state signatures at the third harmonic of the 1241.6 MHz clock.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world QKD network nodes may be exposed to side-channel attacks such as electromagnetic leakage, so empirically characterizing the EM emanations of practical QKD hardware is necessary.

Method: Measured electromagnetic spectra and waveforms from a prototype time-bin decoy-state BB84 QKD device (Y and Z bases), at distances of 0 m and 3 m from transmitter and receiver inside an anechoic chamber.

Result: The transmitter was the primary source of electromagnetic leakage; distinct differences were observed between the quantum states generated at the third harmonic of the 1241.6 MHz clock.

Conclusion: The prototype QKD device exhibits measurable state-dependent EM leakage, highlighting a practical side-channel vulnerability in actual QKD network nodes.

Abstract: Actual quantum key distribution network nodes may be vulnerable to side-channel attacks such as electromagnetic leakage. In this study, we measured electromagnetic emissions from a prototype quantum key distribution device implementing a time-bin decoy-state BB84 protocol with Y and Z bases. We measured the electromagnetic spectra and waveforms at distances of 0 m and 3 m from the transmitter and receiver inside an anechoic chamber. The results showed that the transmitter was the primary source of electromagnetic leakage, and distinct differences were observed between the quantum states generated at the third harmonic of the 1241.6 MHz clock.

</details>


### [93] [Dissipation-Enabled Operator-Norm Locality Bounds for Bose-Hubbard Hamiltonians](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01669)
*Marius Lemm,Tim Möbus*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Local dissipation restores an operator-norm Lieb-Robinson bound for bosonic lattice systems, yielding almost-ballistic information propagation uniform in the initial state, with applications to cat-code dissipation, local adiabatic and channel approximations, thermodynamic limits, and efficient digital quantum simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Closed bosonic lattice systems generally lack operator-norm Lieb-Robinson bounds uniform in the initial state, because the information propagation velocity can grow with local boson occupancy, which can be macroscopically large. This prevents transferring tools and results from quantum spin systems—such as local approximations and simulation guarantees—that rely on state-independent Lieb-Robinson bounds.

Method: The authors study the dissipative Bose-Hubbard model governed by a Lindbladian with on-site l-photon loss (l > 2). Dissipation rapidly depletes highly occupied sites, regularizing the state on a Sobolev-type scale of local particle moments. They derive moment bounds that diverge as time approaches zero but remain integrable near zero at sufficiently low orders, and use these to establish the Lieb-Robinson bound. They extend the framework to cat-code dissipation, proving for initial states in the code space a local adiabatic approximation uniform in total volume.

Result: An almost-ballistic operator-norm Lieb-Robinson bound is established for the dissipative Bose-Hubbard model, uniform in the initial state. Consequences include a local channel approximation, existence of a thermodynamic limit, and efficient digital quantum simulation. For cat-code dissipation, a volume-uniform local adiabatic approximation is proven for code-space initial states.

Conclusion: Local dissipation regularizes bosonic lattice dynamics so that operator-norm Lieb-Robinson bounds hold uniformly in the input state, mirroring quantum spin systems but in contrast to closed Bose-Hubbard systems. This makes state-independent local-approximation, thermodynamic-limit, and simulation results available for dissipative bosonic lattice models.

Abstract: Closed bosonic lattice systems do not generally admit operator norm Lieb--Robinson bounds uniformly in the initial state. The reason is that the information propagation velocity can grow with the local boson occupancy, which can be macroscopically large. Here, we show that local dissipation restores an operator-norm Lieb--Robinson bound. We consider the dissipative Bose--Hubbard model described by a Lindbladian operator with on-site $\ell$-photon loss, $\ell>2$ and, for our main result, we establish an almost-ballistic Lieb-Robinson bound. Dissipation rapidly depletes highly occupied sites, thus regularizing the state on the Sobolev-type scale of local particle moments. The resulting moment bounds diverge as time approaches zero but remain integrable near zero at sufficiently low orders. We extend these ideas to treat cat-code dissipation and, for initial states in the code space, we prove a local adiabatic approximation uniform in the total volume. Further consequences include local channel approximation, a thermodynamic limit, and efficient digital quantum simulation. The point is that these applications are now available \textit{uniformly in the input state}, as for quantum spin systems, but in contrast to closed Bose--Hubbard systems.

</details>


### [94] [A Code-Agnostic Graph Neural Network Decoder from the Detection Error Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01683)
*Federico Alberto Astolfi,Guido Pupillo*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: POLYMECHANON is a GNN decoder for quantum error correction that takes only the detection error model (DEM) as input, represented as a tripartite graph of detectors, error mechanisms, and logical observables. It generalizes across stabiliser codes and noise models, outperforming correlated MWPM and BP+OSD on several benchmarks, with noise-rate-independent decoding time and effective confidence-based post-selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing quantum error correction decoders, such as MWPM and BP+OSD, are typically tailored to specific code families or noise models and require redesign for new codes. The authors aim for a single, code-agnostic decoder architecture whose behavior is determined by data (the DEM) rather than design choices, enabling decoding of any stabiliser code under any noise model expressible as a DEM without redesigning the decoder.

Method: The DEM is represented as a tripartite graph of detectors, error mechanisms, and logical observables. Input features are computed from the quantum code itself rather than being arbitrary design choices. A GNN then decodes on this graph; the same architecture is trained on the DEM and applied to different stabiliser codes and noise models. Evaluation covers rotated surface codes (phenomenological and circuit-level noise), a family of high-rate qLDPC codes, timing comparisons against BP+OSD, cross-code generalization, and confidence-based post-selection.

Result: On the rotated surface code, it beats correlated MWPM under both phenomenological and circuit-level noise, with up to 25% fewer logical failures. On high-rate qLDPC codes, it matches BP+OSD on smaller codes and surpasses it on larger ones, with up to 16% fewer logical failures on [[130,4,6]]. Its decoding time is independent of physical error rate, unlike BP+OSD: on [[130,4,6]], effective cost is a few ms per shot on one GPU versus tens of ms for BP+OSD on one CPU core, making it a real-time decoding candidate for neutral-atom processors. A single model trained across code families outperforms uncorrelated MWPM on trained graph-like codes and stays within 10% of BP+OSD on others, but tends to fail to generalize to unseen, especially larger, codes. Confidence-based post-selection reduces surface-code logical error rate by over an order of magnitude while retaining over 90% of shots.

Conclusion: A DEM-driven GNN decoder can serve as a largely code- and noise-agnostic approach: only the DEM changes, so new codes and noise models can be decoded without redesigning the decoder. It offers competitive accuracy, noise-rate-independent runtime suitable for real-time decoding, and a probabilistic output enabling effective post-selection, though generalization to unseen codes remains limited.

Abstract: We present POLYMECHANON, a graph neural network (GNN) decoder for quantum error correction whose only input is the detection error model (DEM) of a quantum code under a given noise model. We represent the DEM as a tripartite graph of detectors, error mechanisms and logical observables, where every input feature is computed by using the quantum code as data rather than design choice. In this way, the same architecture decodes in principle any stabiliser code, under any noise model that can be expressed as a detection error model, once trained on the DEM. We test this approach along four directions. Firstly, on the rotated surface code the decoder outperforms correlated MWPM under both phenomenological and circuit-level noise, with up to $25\%$ fewer logical failures. Secondly, on a family of high-rate qLDPC codes it matches BP+OSD on the smaller codes and surpasses it on the larger ones, with up to $16\%$ fewer logical failures on $[\![130,4,6]\!]$. Thirdly, its decoding time does not depend on the physical error rate, whereas that of BP+OSD grows with it: on $[\![130,4,6]\!]$ its effective cost per shot is of the order of a few ms on a single GPU against a few tens of milliseconds for BP+OSD on a single CPU core, and its fixed computational graph makes it a candidate for real-time decoding on neutral-atom processors. Finally, a single model trained across codes of different families outperforms uncorrelated MWPM on the graph-like codes seen during training and stays within $10\%$ of BP+OSD on the others, while it tends to fail to generalize to unseen codes, particularly larger ones. Its probabilistic output enables confidence-based post-selection, lowering the logical error rate by more than an order of magnitude on the surface code while keeping more than $90\%$ of the shots. Since only the DEM changes, new codes and noise models can be decoded without redesigning the decoder.

</details>


### [95] [Mentor-Initiated Asymmetric Bidirectional Quantum Teleportation Protocol for Arbitrary Qubit States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01684)
*Sugata Adhya,Benchao Yang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A mentor-initiated bidirectional asymmetric quantum teleportation protocol is proposed that, unlike prior schemes, permits teleportation of an arbitrary two-qubit state from Bob to Alice. The entangled resources are built from standard quantum gates, and the protocol is demonstrated via a Qiskit quantum-circuit implementation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing bidirectional asymmetric quantum teleportation schemes restrict the two-qubit state sent from Bob to Alice to a Bell-like form α|00⟩+β|11⟩, limiting the class of states that can be teleported.

Method: The authors generalize the mentor-initiated bidirectional asymmetric quantum teleportation framework to allow an arbitrary two-qubit state to be teleported from Bob to Alice. The required entangled resources are constructed explicitly using standard quantum gates, and the protocol is implemented as a quantum circuit in Qiskit.

Result: The protocol succeeds in teleporting an arbitrary two-qubit state from Bob to Alice within the mentor-initiated bidirectional asymmetric setting, as confirmed by the Qiskit circuit implementation using gate-constructed entangled resources.

Conclusion: The proposed protocol removes the Bell-like-state restriction of previous schemes, broadening the applicability of mentor-initiated bidirectional asymmetric quantum teleportation, with a practical Qiskit-based validation.

Abstract: In this paper, a mentor-initiated bidirectional asymmetric quantum teleportation protocol is proposed. Unlike existing schemes that restrict the two-qubit state transmitted from Bob to Alice to a Bell-like form $α|00\rangle+β|11\rangle$, our protocol allows an arbitrary two-qubit state to be teleported from Bob to Alice within the mentor-initiated framework. The required entangled resources are constructed using standard quantum gates, and the protocol is illustrated through its quantum-circuit implementation in Qiskit.

</details>


### [96] [Predicting electric-field noise in ion traps using fluctuation electrodynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01693)
*Markus Teller,Da An,Alberto M. Alonso,Philip C. Holz,Philipp Schindler,Hartmut Häffner,Tracy E. Northup*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A method predicts electric-field noise from bulk dielectric and metallic fluctuations in arbitrary surface ion-trap geometries, using energy loss as the only material input, and matches measured noise within a factor of four.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Electric-field noise causes gate errors in trapped-ion quantum computers, yet it has been difficult to predict, limiting noise-informed trap design.

Method: A predictive model is formulated for electric-field noise arising from fluctuations in the bulk of dielectric and metallic materials. It is valid for arbitrary trap geometries and requires only energy loss as the relevant material property. The model is applied to a trap with a floating electrode and to typical surface-trap designs, with analysis of electrode shielding.

Result: In the floating-electrode trap, the dielectric contribution dominates. The model predicts the observed noise within a factor of four. For typical surface traps, electrode structures are analyzed in terms of how they shield the noise.

Conclusion: Bulk-material noise can be quantitatively predicted for arbitrary trap geometries using only energy loss, enabling assessment of dielectric contributions and electrode shielding in surface ion traps.

Abstract: Electric-field noise in microfabricated surface ion traps contributes to gate errors in trapped-ion quantum computers, but this noise has been challenging to predict. Here, we present a method to predict the electric-field noise arising from fluctuations in the bulk of dielectric and metallic materials. The method is valid for arbitrary trap geometries, and the only relevant material property is energy loss. We apply it to an ion trap with an electrically floating electrode and find the contribution from dielectrics to be dominant. Our model predicts the observed noise for this trap within a factor of four. In addition, we apply our model to typical surface trap designs and analyze how the electrode structures shield the noise.

</details>


### [97] [Efficiently Optimizing the Quantum Value of Bell Inequalities using Batched Gradient Descent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01699)
*Xinyu Xu,Ping Zhu,Weikang Li,Pierre Pocreau,Dalu Ding,Dawei Ding*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a GPU-accelerated batched gradient descent (BGD) optimizer for the quantum value of Bell inequalities, using differentiable search over feasible quantum strategies and direct tensor contraction, achieving significant speedups over see-saw and scaling to thousands of inputs/outputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Studying large-scale Bell inequalities requires numerical methods that scale with the number of parties, inputs, and outputs. Such tools support designing experiments, enabling applications like randomness generation and multi-agent coordination with restricted communication, and tackling problems beyond analytic reach. Existing general-purpose optimizers, notably the see-saw method, struggle with even a dozen inputs and outputs.

Method: A BGD optimizer performing differentiable search over feasible quantum strategies, in which states and projective measurements are generated from unconstrained variables. The Bell expression is evaluated via direct tensor contraction, avoiding construction of the prohibitively large Bell operator. This formulation makes parallel random restarts inexpensive and is naturally GPU-implemented. Classical values for large inequalities are computed by GPU strategy enumeration or as a mixed-integer linear program.

Result: Compared against the see-saw method, the BGD optimizer yields significant speedup across a wide variety of Bell inequality families. On GPU, it optimizes Bell inequalities with more than a thousand inputs or outputs within minutes in the numerical experiments.

Conclusion: The optimizers can serve as numerical evaluation tools for Bell experiments and multi-agent coordination problems modeled by Bell inequalities with many inputs and outputs, a common feature of real-world scenarios such as high frequency trading and distributed systems.

Abstract: Exploring the world of Bell inequalities requires numerical methods that scale effectively with the number of parties, inputs, and outputs. Such methods can be used to study large-scale Bell inequalities beyond the reach of analytic methods, to design experiments for violating more complicated Bell inequalities, and to enable applications of Bell inequality violation. Such applications include randomness generation and multi-agent coordination with restricted communication. However, current general purpose optimizers such as the see-saw method struggle to handle Bell inequalities with only a dozen inputs and outputs. We introduce an optimizer for the quantum value of a Bell inequality based on batched gradient descent (BGD). More precisely, our BGD optimizer is a differentiable search over feasible quantum strategies in which states and projective measurements are generated from unconstrained variables. The Bell expression is evaluated by a direct tensor contraction without forming the prohibitively large Bell operator. This formulation makes parallel random restarts inexpensive and is thus naturally implemented by a GPU. We evaluate our optimizer against the see-saw method and find a significant speedup for a wide variety of Bell inequality families. Implemented on a GPU, our BGD optimizer can optimize Bell inequalities in our numerical experiments with more than a thousand inputs or outputs within minutes. For these large-scale Bell inequalities, we also efficiently compute the classical value by enumerating possible strategies using a GPU or expressing the optimization as a mixed-integer linear program. Our optimizers can serve as numerical evaluation tools for Bell experiments and multi-agent coordination problems modeled by Bell inequalities with many inputs and outputs, a common feature of real-world scenarios such as high frequency trading and distributed systems.

</details>


### [98] [Exact first-detection probability in a locally monitored solvable quantum circuit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01703)
*Cecilia De Fazio,Igor Lesanovsky,Gabriele Perfetto*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper derives exact analytical results for the statistics of first detecting a monitored subset of qubits returning to their initial state in the deterministic Floquet-quantum East model, revealing two regimes governed by the ratio of measurement probing time to circuit relaxation timescale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding how local stroboscopic measurements and their backaction reshape detection statistics in interacting quantum many-body systems is a central open question, and exact characterizations of this interplay are rare. The authors aim to provide a solvable setting for this problem.

Method: They study the Floquet-quantum East model under local stroboscopic measurements. A renewal equation is derived that links the first-detection probability of a qubit subset returning to its initial state to the subsystem Loschmidt echo, which encodes the effective open dynamics of the monitored qubits, yielding exact analytical results.

Result: The first-detection statistics exhibit two regimes determined by the competition between the measurement probing time and the relaxation timescale of the unitary circuit dynamics: infrequent monitoring produces geometric first-detection statistics, while frequent monitoring induces correlations that cause deviations from geometric behavior. Return probabilities are controlled by the size of the monitored subregion.

Conclusion: The work provides a rare exact characterization of how local measurement backaction reshapes detection statistics in an interacting quantum many-body system, and the local protocol makes these return probabilities accessible on digital quantum simulators via local mid-circuit measurements.

Abstract: We study the interplay between local stroboscopic measurements and the unitary quantum circuit dynamics of the deterministic Floquet-quantum East model. We derive exact analytical results for the probability of first detecting a subset of qubits back in their initial state. This is achieved via a renewal equation linking the first-detection probability to the subsystem Loschmidt echo, which encodes the effective open dynamics of the monitored qubits. The resulting statistics exhibit two regimes set by the competition between the measurement probing time and the relaxation timescale of the unitary circuit dynamics: while infrequent monitoring leads to geometric first-detection statistics, frequent monitoring induces correlations that manifest as deviations from this geometric behavior. These results provide a rare exact characterization of how local measurement backaction reshapes detection statistics in an interacting quantum many-body system. In our local protocol, return probabilities are controlled by the size of the monitored subregion and can therefore be accessed on digital quantum simulators via local mid-circuit measurements.

</details>


### [99] [Less precise but less noisy: local circuits for momentum-space state preparation and measurement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01704)
*Etienne Granet,Henrik Dreyer*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: For tight-binding chain ground state preparation on Quantinuum H2, beyond a system size N adiabatic evolution reaches lower energies than the Fermionic Fourier Transform (FFT) at equal gate count and depth; this is attributed to FFT's high momentum resolution requiring long-range couplings that propagate noise faster. A cheaper, less noisy momentum measurement scheme is introduced and outperforms FFT for spectral function measurement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum algorithms are conventionally optimized for gate count or circuit depth, but this ignores noise sensitivity, which can dominate performance on real hardware. The authors aim to show that noise sensitivity is a distinct and important optimization criterion.

Method: They compare adiabatic evolution and the Fermionic Fourier Transform (FFT) for tight-binding chain ground state preparation on the Quantinuum System Model H2 quantum computer at matched gate count and circuit depth, and analyze how momentum resolution relates to the range of real-space couplings and error propagation speed. They also design a lower-cost momentum measurement scheme.

Result: Beyond a critical system size N, adiabatic evolution reaches significantly lower energies than FFT with the same number of gates and circuit depth. FFT's ability to resolve momenta by 1/N requires long-range couplings that propagate errors faster, causing high noise sensitivity. The proposed momentum measurement scheme, though less precise than FFT, achieves better performance than FFT for spectral function measurement on the H2 device.

Conclusion: Noise sensitivity, beyond gate count or circuit depth, is a crucial metric for quantum algorithm design; trading unnecessary high momentum resolution for lower noise sensitivity can improve practical performance.

Abstract: Quantum algorithms are usually optimized for gate count or circuit depth. We find on Quantinuum System Model H2 quantum computer that for a tight-binding chain ground state preparation, there is a system size $N$ beyond which the adiabatic evolution reaches significantly lower energies than the Fermionic Fourier Transform (FFT), with the same number of gates, and with the same circuit depth. We attribute this high noise sensitivity of the FFT to its high precision, being able to distinguish momenta by $1/N$. This high resolution in momentum space requires long-range couplings in real space, which propagates errors faster. In contrast, although local and physical circuits such as the adiabatic evolution have a coarser momentum resolution, they also propagate errors more slowly. For physical applications, high momentum resolution is rarely required and is often worth trading for low noise sensitivity. We also introduce a momentum measurement scheme that although less precise than FFT, is less costly and less noisy. We show that it achieves better performance than FFT for spectral function measurement on Quantinuum System Model H2 quantum computer. Our work emphasizes the importance of reducing the noise sensitivity of quantum algorithms, beyond the number of gates or circuit depth.

</details>


### [100] [Near-optimal quantum query lower bounds on bipartiteness and expansion testing in the bounded-degree graph model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01752)
*Chandrima Kayal,Sayantan Sen,Dániel Szabó*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work establishes tight quantum query lower bounds of Θ̃(N^(1/3)) for bipartiteness and expansion testing in the bounded-degree graph model, resolving a long-standing open question and fully characterizing their quantum query complexity up to polylogarithmic factors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bipartiteness and expansion testing are canonical graph property testing problems. Classically, both require Θ̃(√N) queries. Ambainis, Childs, and Liu (2011) gave quantum algorithms with Õ(N^(1/3)) queries and an Ω̃(N^(1/4)) lower bound for expansion testing, leaving open whether bipartiteness admits an exponential quantum advantage. No improvement had been made in over a decade, motivating a complete characterization.

Method: The authors employ the polynomial method, as in Ambainis–Childs–Liu, but introduce intermediate problems that are related to the main problems via reductions. They then perform a more precise analysis of the resulting polynomials to derive the near-optimal lower bounds.

Result: Essentially tight Ω̃(N^(1/3)) quantum query lower bounds are proven for both bipartiteness and expansion testing, matching the known upper bounds up to polylogarithmic factors.

Conclusion: The quantum query complexity of bipartiteness and expansion testing in the bounded-degree model is completely characterized as Θ̃(N^(1/3)), closing the open question about a possible exponential quantum advantage for bipartiteness and improving upon the decade-old results.

Abstract: In this work, we study bipartiteness and expansion testing, two canonical problems in graph property testing in the bounded-degree model through the lens of quantum query complexity. In the classical setting, it is known that $\widetildeΘ(\sqrt{N})$ queries are necessary and sufficient for both these testing problems (Goldreich and Ron, 1999, 2000 & 2002), where $N$ denotes the number of vertices of the input graph. Due to their significance, (Ambainis, Childs, and Liu, 2011) initiated the study of these problems in the quantum setting and designed quantum algorithms for bipartiteness and expansion testing that perform $\widetilde{O}(N^{1/3})$ queries, showing a polynomial speedup. They also proved that $\widetildeΩ(N^{1/4})$ queries are necessary for expansion testing, but the possibility of an exponential quantum advantage for bipartiteness testing remained open. Despite significant effort, there has been no improvement in these results in the last decade and a half. In this work, we prove essentially tight $\widetildeΩ(N^{1/3})$ quantum query lower bounds for both bipartiteness and expansion testing, thereby completely characterizing the quantum query complexity of these problems up to polylogarithmic factors. While our proofs use the polynomial method similarly to Ambainis, Childs, and Liu, we use intermediate problems that we relate to the main problems via reductions, and perform a more precise analysis of the resulting polynomials, leading to the near-optimal lower bounds.

</details>


### [101] [Efficient quantum phase estimation with adaptive entanglement-assisted Hadamard test](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01772)
*Hengzhun Chen,Benchi Zhao,Yingzhou Li*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes the adaptive entanglement-assisted Hadamard test (AEHT), which iteratively refines the reference phase to enable progressively stronger amplification, achieving efficient high-precision quantum phase estimation and suppressing bias from imperfect eigenstate preparation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The conventional entanglement-assisted Hadamard test (EHT) amplifies the phase signal but its feasible amplification is fundamentally limited by reference phase accuracy, making it inefficient in the high-precision regime. Additionally, imperfect eigenstate preparation introduces an unavoidable systematic bias in conventional EHT.

Method: AEHT iteratively refines the reference phase so that amplification strength grows progressively across iterations. The authors also adopt device-restart count, rather than shot count, as the cost metric for quantum phase estimation to reflect physical implementation considerations, and validate the approach via numerical experiments.

Result: Numerical experiments confirm the effectiveness of AEHT compared with conventional methods under the device-restart-count cost measure, and the systematic bias from imperfect eigenstate preparation can be suppressed by AEHT.

Conclusion: By unlocking the full amplification power of large entangled states, AEHT offers an efficient method for estimating high-precision quantum phases on near-term quantum processors.

Abstract: The entanglement-assisted Hadamard test (EHT) is a practical method for estimating a quantum phase by amplifying the phase signal. However, the feasible amplification is fundamentally limited by the accuracy of the reference phase, such that the method is inefficient in the high-precision regime. In this work, we propose an algorithm, called adaptive entanglement-assisted Hadamard test (AEHT), that iteratively refines the reference phase, enabling progressively stronger amplification as the iteration goes by. We further consider the imperfect eigenstate preparation scenario, where a systematic bias is unavoidable when estimating the quantum phase with the conventional EHT. Such a bias can be suppressed by the proposed AEHT. Moreover, taking physical implementation into consideration, we adopt device-restart count to measure the cost of quantum phase estimation, rather than shot count. The numerical experiments confirm the effectiveness of the proposed AEHT compared with conventional methods under this measure. By unlocking the full amplification power of large entangled states, this work offers an efficient method to estimate high-precision quantum phase on near-term quantum processors.

</details>


### [102] [QUFIG: GNN-Based Prediction of Quantum Fault Injection Vulnerabilities with Gate-Level Precision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01777)
*Shihan Zhao,Qiying Li,Ben Dong,Qian Wang,Yuntao Liu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: QUFIG is a learning-guided framework that prioritizes gate-fault pairs in quantum circuits by vulnerability score to enable efficient, gate-level fault identification under limited fidelity budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The scale and accessibility of quantum hardware have introduced reliability and security challenges, such as run-time fault injection attacks in cloud-based quantum platforms. Existing approaches fail to pinpoint vulnerabilities at gate-level precision or adapt to run-time environments.

Method: The work formulates gate-level fault analysis as a learning-guided prioritization problem under restricted fidelity budgets. A circuit-DAG-based GNN backbone predicts a vulnerability score for each gate-fault pair, defined as the impact of that pair on circuit fidelity, and pairs are then ranked by this score.

Result: Experiments on QASMbench and HamLib MaxCut show QUFIG recovers high-impact vulnerable gate-fault pairs with fewer inspections than random and depth-based heuristics, reducing the number of gates requiring inspection by 2.9--19.8% while maintaining effective fault identification.

Conclusion: QUFIG enables quantum circuit designers to identify vulnerabilities and apply targeted defenses more efficiently by focusing inspection effort on the most impactful gate-fault pairs.

Abstract: The growing scale and accessibility of quantum hardware exposed new reliability and security challenges in the quantum computing workflow, such as the run-time fault injection attacks in cloud-based quantum computing platforms. However, existing works fail to identify vulnerabilities with gate-level precision or adapt to run-time environments. In this work, we formulate gate-level fault analysis as a learning-guided prioritization problem under restricted fidelity budgets. The framework uses a circuit-DAG-based GNN backbone to predict the vulnerability score of each gate to each type of injected fault, defined as the impact of the gate-fault pair on circuit fidelity. The gate-fault pairs are then ranked by their vulnerability score. Experiments on QASMbench and HamLib MaxCut show that QUFIG recovers high-impact vulnerable gate-fault pairs with fewer inspections than random and depth-based heuristics. Our results show that QUFIG can reduce the number of gates requiring inspection by 2.9--19.8% while maintaining effective fault identification, allowing quantum circuit designers to identify vulnerabilities and apply targeted defenses more efficiently.

</details>


### [103] [Non-Markovian effects on informational steady states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01782)
*Jacob Werner*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work studies informational steady states (ISSs) in continuously monitored collision models, showing that steady-state information gain per measurement is negatively correlated with the degree of non-Markovianity, and characterizes how interaction parameters affect both quantities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ISSs are well understood in Markovian settings, but how memory effects (non-Markovianity) influence their formation and steady-state properties remains largely unexplored.

Method: The environment is modeled as a sequence of two-qubit ancillas within a continuously monitored collision-model framework, and numerical analysis is used to relate non-Markovianity to steady-state information gain.

Result: For this model, the steady-state information gain per measurement is negatively correlated with the degree of non-Markovianity; system-ancilla and ancilla-ancilla interaction parameters are shown to influence both the degree of non-Markovianity and the steady-state information gain.

Conclusion: Memory effects measurably shape ISS formation and properties, with non-Markovianity trading off against steady-state information gain in the studied collision model.

Abstract: Informational steady states (ISSs) arise when the rate at which information is gained from continuous measurements is exactly balanced by the rate at which information from earlier measurements is lost to the environment. While ISSs have been studied in Markovian settings, relatively little is known about how memory effects influence their formation and steady-state properties. In this work, we investigate non-Markovian effects on ISSs within the framework of continuously monitored collision models. We model the environment as a sequence of two-qubit ancillas and find that, for this specific model, the steady-state information gain per measurement is negatively correlated with the degree of non-Markovianity. Numerical results further characterize how the system-ancilla and ancilla-ancilla interaction parameters influence both the degree of non-Markovianity and the steady-state information gain.

</details>


### [104] [Learnt Attacks on Quantum Key Distribution under Channel Noise and Device Drift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01792)
*Marcel Mordarski,Benjamin Gras,Abdelrahman Shehata,Daniel Budina,Roberto Bondesan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper quantifies the advantage of adaptive eavesdropping on QKD links whose channels drift between recalibrations, modeling the attacker as a constrained Markov decision process and learning gate circuits that outperform the best fixed attack.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: QKD security analyses assume stationary channels, but real devices suffer channel drift between recalibrations. It was unknown whether an eavesdropper constrained to not alter the channel's intrinsic noise could benefit by tracking this drift. The paper aims to quantify and bound this adaptation gain.

Method: Adaptive eavesdropping is formulated as a constrained Markov decision process: the attacker picks one circuit per round, noise evolves as an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process, and the abort condition is a per-block budget of rounds. Gate structure and rotation angles are searched jointly (unlike prior fixed-template training), yielding compact circuits that form a discrete action set applicable to channels without a known template, e.g., amplitude damping. Results are bounded below by the best fixed circuit and above by a dynamic-programming upper bound.

Result: On device-independent E91 under bilateral depolarising noise, the RL attacker raises Holevo information from 0.135 (best fixed circuit) to 0.348 at zero detection, reaching 98% of the upper bound. On BB84 under a drifting bit-flip channel, it beats a conservative noise-indexed rule by 0.024 in fidelity, reaching 99% of the upper bound. Under stationary noise, the gain from basis asymmetry flips sign between an averaged and a per-basis error-rate constraint. Starting from random gate sequences, the search recovers analytical cloners and the collective-attack key rate, meeting the Winick-Lutkenhaus-Coles lower bound from above.

Conclusion: Adaptive, learning-based eavesdropping can substantially exceed stationary-channel assumptions in drifting QKD links, approaching dynamic-programming bounds, and the joint gate/angle search generalizes to channels lacking known templates while recovering known analytical attacks.

Abstract: Quantum key distribution (QKD) links are provisioned from security analyses of stationary channels, whereas the devices that determine the channel drift between recalibrations. Whether an eavesdropper who cannot alter the channel's own noise gains by following that drift has not been quantified. Adaptive eavesdropping is posed here as a constrained Markov decision process in which the attacker selects one circuit per round while the noise level follows an Ornstein--Uhlenbeck process and the abort condition is a budget over each block of rounds. The value of adaptation is bounded by the best fixed circuit and a dynamic-programming upper bound. The actions are learnt attacks. Whereas Decker et al. trained a parametrised circuit on a fixed gate template against a fixed channel, here the gate structure and rotation angles are searched jointly. This yields circuits compact enough to form a discrete action set, extending the construction to noise models lacking a known template, including the amplitude damping channel. On device-independent E91 under bilateral depolarising noise, a reinforcement-learning attacker raises her Holevo information from $0.135$ for the best fixed circuit to $0.348$ at zero detection, $98\%$ of the upper bound. On BB84 under a drifting bit-flip channel, she exceeds a conservative noise-indexed rule by $0.024$ in fidelity, reaching $99\%$ of the upper bound. Under stationary noise, the attacker's gain from basis asymmetry changes sign between an averaged and a per-basis error-rate constraint. The search, started from random gate sequences, recovers the analytical cloners and the collective-attack key rate, and meets the lower bound of the Winick--Lütkenhaus--Coles objective from above.

</details>


### [105] [Collision models: Markovian and Non-Markovian impurity models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01814)
*Jacob Werner*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces Markovian impurity collision models where information backflow is periodically blocked, showing that non-Markovianity is monotonic in the impurity number p and identifying a critical transition point.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Collision models offer a flexible framework for open quantum systems and non-Markovianity, but scenarios where information backflow is periodically blocked are not yet captured. This motivates a new class of models inspired by such physical settings.

Method: The authors define Markovian impurity models within the collision-model framework and use the Breuer-Laine-Piilo (BLP) measure of non-Markovianity to analyze them. They derive a monotonicity relation between p-impurity and (p+1)-impurity models and perform numerical simulations to study how the critical p depends on system-ancilla and ancilla-ancilla interaction strengths.

Result: If a p-impurity model is non-Markovian, the corresponding (p+1)-impurity model is also non-Markovian, implying a critical p marking the Markovian-to-non-Markovian transition. Simulations show that, with exceptions, increasing either interaction strength generally lowers the critical p, while sufficiently weak ancilla-ancilla interactions yield a regime that remains Markovian even without impurities.

Conclusion: The work establishes Markovian impurity models as a tractable class exhibiting a critical impurity number for non-Markovianity, and briefly introduces a complementary non-Markovian impurity model.

Abstract: Collision models provide a flexible framework for studying open quantum systems and introducing non-Markovianity. We present a novel class of collision models, which we term Markovian impurity models, motivated by physical scenarios in which information backflow is periodically blocked. Using the Breuer-Laine-Piilo (BLP) measure of non-Markovianity, we show that if a $p$-impurity model is non-Markovian, then the corresponding $(p+1)$-impurity model is also non-Markovian, implying the existence of a critical $p$ at which the process transitions from Markovian to non-Markovian. Numerical simulations are used to investigate how this critical parameter depends on the system-ancilla and ancilla-ancilla interaction strengths. We find that, altough exceptions exist, increasing either interaction strength generally lowers the critical $p$, while sufficiently weak ancilla-ancilla interactions lead to a regime in which the dynamics remain Markovian even in the absence of impurities. Finally, we briefly introduce the complementary non-Markovian impurity model.

</details>


### [106] [QPI-DeepONet-MAC: A Scalable and Stable Hybrid Classical-Quantum Architecture for Physics-Informed Deep Operator Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01824)
*Said Lantigua,José Valencia,Gilson Giraldi,Renato Portugal,Jonas Maziero*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: QPI-DeepONet-MAC is a hybrid quantum-classical physics-informed operator network with provable universal approximation, barren-plateau-avoiding gradient bounds, sensor-count lower bounds, and a coherence-based regularization scheme.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Solving parametric PDEs with operator learning is a core AI-for-physics challenge. PI-DeepONets embed governing equations but suffer optimization difficulties in high dimensions, while quantum extensions add representational capacity but are prone to barren plateaus that impede trainability.

Method: The authors propose QPI-DeepONet-MAC, integrating parameterized quantum circuits into PI-DeepONets via multiplicative-and-additive coupling (MAC) of classical representations and quantum expectation values. They analytically prove universal operator approximation, derive quantum parameter gradient bounds giving explicit barren-plateau-avoidance conditions and a trainability scaling criterion, establish a lower bound on the number of sensors for target accuracy, and introduce an informational regularization scheme based on quantum coherence and state fidelity dynamics.

Result: The architecture retains universal operator approximation capabilities. Gradient bounds yield explicit conditions to avoid barren plateaus plus a scaling criterion for trainability. The sensor lower bound links sampling requirements to input function regularity, domain dimension, and PDE dynamical growth. The coherence/fidelity-based regularization prevents coherence loss and preserves quantum structure during training.

Conclusion: These results provide a theoretical foundation for quantum-enhanced physics-informed operator learning and position QPI-DeepONet-MAC as a scalable, stable framework for solving parametric PDEs.

Abstract: General operator learning for parametric partial differential equations (PDEs) is a fundamental challenge at the intersection of artificial intelligence and physics-based modeling. Physics-informed Deep Operator Networks (PI-DeepONets) incorporate governing equations into learning, but face optimization difficulties in high dimensions. Quantum extensions offer additional representational capacity, yet their trainability can be hindered by barren plateaus. Here, we introduce QPI-DeepONet-MAC, a hybrid quantum-classical architecture that integrates parameterized quantum circuits into PI-DeepONets through a multiplicative-and-additive coupling (MAC) of classical representations and quantum expectation values. We prove analytically that the proposed architecture retains universal operator approximation capabilities. Furthermore, we derive bounds on quantum parameter gradients, establishing explicit conditions to avoid barren plateaus alongside a scaling criterion for trainability. Additionally, we derive a lower bound on the number of sensors required for a target accuracy, directly linking sampling requirements to input function regularity, domain dimension, and PDE dynamical growth. Finally, we propose an informational regularization scheme based on quantum coherence and state fidelity dynamics to prevent coherence loss and preserve quantum structure during training. Together, these results establish a theoretical foundation for quantum-enhanced physics-informed operator learning and position QPI-DeepONet-MAC as a scalable, stable framework for solving parametric PDEs.

</details>


### [107] [Experimental realization of Wheeler's delayed-choice experiment with dual selections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01832)
*Xiaowan Yang,Xinglei Yu,Liangsheng Li,Xinzhi Zhao,Tianle Zheng,Chengjie Zhang,Chuan-Feng Li,Guang-Can Guo*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work implements Wheeler's delayed-choice experiment with dual selections using entangled photons, quantum-controlling both beam splitters in a Mach-Zehnder interferometer to reveal wave-particle behavior and wave-particle quantum superposition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wheeler's delayed-choice experiment demonstrates wave-particle duality using different Mach-Zehnder configurations, and its quantum version controls photon behavior via the second beam splitter. A dual-selection extension controlling both beam splitters is pursued to explore richer wave-particle behavior.

Method: The authors realize a delayed-choice experiment with dual selections based on entangled photons, experimentally controlling both beam splitters of a Mach-Zehnder interferometer, with the presence and absence of the second beam splitter simultaneously governed through path encoding.

Result: The experiment reveals the wave-particle behavior of single photons under different configurations of the two beam splitters. It further discusses the scenario where, when both beam splitters are in a quantum superposition of present and absent, photons reside in a wave-particle quantum superposition state.

Conclusion: Dual-selection control of both beam splitters in the delayed-choice experiment is achievable and exposes wave-particle behavior across configurations, extending toward photons in a wave-particle quantum superposition state.

Abstract: Wheeler's delayed-choice experiment demonstrates wave-particle duality of particles using different experimental configurations of a Mach-Zehnder interferometer. In a quantum version of this experiment, the wave-particle behavior of photons can be observed by controlling presence or absence of the second beam splitter. Here, we implement a delayed-choice experiment with dual selections based on entangled photons, experimentally controlling both of two beam splitters in the Mach-Zehnder interferometer, where the presence and absence of the second beam splitter are simultaneously controlled through path encoding. Our experiment reveals the wave-particle behavior of single photons under different configurations of two beam splitters. We further discuss the scenario when two beam splitters are in a quantum superposition of being present and absent, photons will be in a wave-particle quantum superposition state.

</details>


### [108] [Toward Optimal Circuit Depth for Geometrically Local Hamiltonian Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01839)
*Zhenyu Shen,Yusen Wu,Penghui Yao,Xiao Yuan,Yukun Zhang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a nearest-neighbor quantum circuit for simulating finite-range lattice Hamiltonians with depth O(T log(nT/ε)) and gate count O(nT log(nT/ε)), reducing prior polylogarithmic overhead to a single logarithm, and proves a matching Ω(log n / log log n) lower bound for uniform XXZ Hamiltonians, establishing near-optimal circuit depth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Locality in geometrically local Hamiltonians suggests digital simulation should preserve much of the parallelism of the underlying dynamics, so a natural goal is to determine how much circuit depth and precision actually cost. Prior work left a polylogarithmic overhead that did not exploit spatial localization's control over the cost of high precision, and no unconditional depth lower bound was known for this regime.

Method: The authors build a nearest-neighbor circuit using the Lieb-Robinson decomposition into logarithmic-scale blocks. On each block, a constant-order product formula is corrected so that its error is compensated, yielding exponential accuracy; the correction is implemented with depth proportional to the block diameter, adding no extra asymptotic depth beyond that required by spatial localization. They also prove an unconditional circuit-depth lower bound for uniform XXZ Hamiltonians using an information-theoretic/light-cone argument in the stated regime.

Result: For an n-qubit, time-independent, finite-range Hamiltonian with normalized local interaction strength on a fixed-dimensional lattice and time T ≥ 1, the construction achieves target accuracy ε with depth O(T log(nT/ε)) and gate count O(nT log(nT/ε)), reducing the polylogarithmic overhead of Haah, Hastings, Kothari, and Low to a single logarithm. For uniform XXZ Hamiltonians at fixed evolution time T and sufficiently small fixed ε, any nearest-neighbor circuit requires depth Ω(log n / log log n), matching the O(log n) upper bound up to a doubly logarithmic factor.

Conclusion: Spatial localization controls both the cost of parallelism and the cost of high precision, allowing exponential accuracy via a diameter-depth correction with no additional asymptotic depth. These results clarify the roles of locality and precision in circuit complexity of quantum dynamics and establish near-optimal circuit depth for lattice Hamiltonian simulation in this regime.

Abstract: Understanding the dynamics of interacting quantum systems is a central goal of quantum simulation. For geometrically local Hamiltonians, locality suggests that digital simulation should retain much of the parallelism of the underlying dynamics. For a target accuracy $ε$, we construct a nearest-neighbor quantum circuit that simulates the dynamics of an $n$-qubit, time-independent, finite-range Hamiltonian with normalized local interaction strength on a fixed-dimensional lattice for time $T\ge1$, with circuit depth $O(T\log(nT/ε))$ and gate count $O(nT\log(nT/ε))$. This reduces the polylogarithmic overhead of Haah, Hastings, Kothari, and Low to a single logarithm. On logarithmic-scale blocks from the Lieb--Robinson decomposition, a shallow but highly accurate implementation is essential for reducing the circuit depth. Our key observation is that spatial localization also controls the cost of high precision. In each block, the error of a constant-order product formula is compensated for by a correction, yielding exponential accuracy. The correction can be implemented with depth proportional to the block diameter, introducing no additional asymptotic depth scale beyond that required by spatial localization.
  Furthermore, we prove an unconditional circuit-depth lower bound for uniform XXZ Hamiltonians: at fixed evolution time $T$ in the stated regime and sufficiently small fixed error $ε$, any nearest-neighbor quantum circuit simulating these dynamics requires depth $Ω(\log n/\log\log n)$. This matches our $O(\log n)$ upper bound up to a doubly logarithmic factor, establishing near-optimal circuit depth for lattice Hamiltonian simulation in this regime. Together, these results clarify the role of locality and precision in determining the circuit complexity of quantum dynamics and suggest broader principles for spatially constrained quantum computation.

</details>


### [109] [Trapdoored Clifford Operators and Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01848)
*Minki Hhan,Hojune Lee*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces trapdoored Clifford operator distributions that are computationally indistinguishable from uniformly random Cliffords but can be sampled and implemented in near-linear time given a trapdoor, enabling faster random-Clifford protocols and worst-case-to-average-case reductions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uniformly random n-qubit Clifford operators are central to randomized benchmarking, classical shadows, and quantum authentication, but sampling and implementing them incurs near-quadratic complexity because of the large size of the Clifford group. The authors seek a cryptographic approach to bypass these barriers.

Method: They construct a distribution of trapdoored Clifford operators that is computationally indistinguishable from uniform Cliffords yet admits near-linear-time sampling and implementation under a variant of the learning parity with noise (LPN) assumption. The construction supports fast tableau action on Pauli labels for classical simulation and can be optimized for polylogarithmic-depth implementation. As a building block, they construct trapdoored matrices over finite fields supporting efficient multiplication by both a matrix and its inverse, resolving an open question of Vaikuntanathan and Zamir [SODA'26].

Result: The constructions yield faster protocols based on random Cliffords. They also support worst-case-to-average-case reductions for matrix and Clifford problems, including iterated matrix multiplication and Clifford circuit synthesis. In particular, they show the hardness of batching Clifford circuits: synthesizing circuits that apply the same Clifford to multiple registers is at least as hard as worst-case matrix multiplication, even when synthesis succeeds on only a small constant fraction of random Cliffords. This extends to approximate implementations by general quantum circuits.

Conclusion: Trapdoored Clifford distributions provide a cryptographic route to circumvent the near-quadratic cost of uniform Clifford sampling/implementation, yielding faster random-Clifford protocols and new hardness results, notably for batching Clifford circuits, while also resolving an open problem on trapdoored finite-field matrices.

Abstract: Random Clifford operators have numerous applications in quantum computing, including randomized benchmarking, classical shadows, and quantum authentication. However, sampling and implementing uniformly random $n$-qubit Clifford incur near-quadratic complexity due to the size of Clifford group.
  We introduce a cryptographic way to overcome these barriers: trapdoored Clifford operator distributions whose samples are computationally indistinguishable from uniformly random Cliffords, yet implementing them can be much faster given the trapdoor. We construct a distribution of trapdoored Clifford operators whose elements can be sampled and implemented in near-linear time under a variant of the learning parity with noise assumption. Our constructions allow fast tableau action on Pauli labels for classical simulation, and also can be optimized to admit polylogarithmic-depth implementation. Along the way, we construct trapdoored matrices over finite fields that support efficient multiplication by both a matrix and its inverse, resolving an open question left by Vaikuntanathan and Zamir [SODA'26].
  We use these constructions to obtain faster protocols based on random Cliffords. We also explore their applications to the worst-case to average-case reductions for matrix and Clifford problems including the iterated matrix multiplication and Clifford circuit synthesis. In particular, we show the hardness of batching Clifford circuits: synthesizing circuits that apply the same Clifford to multiple registers is at least as hard as worst-case matrix multiplication, even when synthesis succeeds on a small constant fraction of random Cliffords. This extends to approximate implementations by general quantum circuits.

</details>


### [110] [Quantum Optimal Transport Barycenters: Existence, Duality, and Gaussian Rigidity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01855)
*Augusto Gerolin,Zhiyi Lin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops a Quantum Optimal Transport barycenter framework on separable Hilbert spaces, establishing existence and duality for general (possibly unbounded) costs, and proves a Gaussian rigidity theorem for 2-quantum Wasserstein barycenters: with a faithful Gaussian input, the barycenter is unique among all quantum states and Gaussian.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wasserstein barycenters are central in classical optimal transport, and quantum analogues (2-quantum Wasserstein distances) have recently been introduced, but a general barycenter theory with existence/duality and a rigidity theory for Gaussian inputs was missing. A key obstacle is that uniqueness of the optimal covariance need not imply uniqueness of the underlying quantum state.

Method: The authors formulate a Quantum Optimal Transport barycenter problem for quantum states via cost operators on separable Hilbert spaces, proving existence and duality for a broad class of possibly unbounded transport costs. Specializing to canonical quadratic cost operators recovers 2-quantum Wasserstein barycenters in both state and channel formulations. For Gaussian inputs they reduce the problem to finite-dimensional convex optimization over covariance matrices, and introduce a state-reconstruction principle under covariance complementary slackness to lift covariance uniqueness to state uniqueness.

Result: The barycenter problem admits a Gaussian minimizer and reduces to a finite-dimensional convex program in covariance matrices. Under covariance complementary slackness, uniqueness of the optimal covariance upgrades to uniqueness of the full quantum state. Consequently, if at least one Gaussian input is faithful, the barycenter is unique among all quantum states and is Gaussian (global rigidity). Faithfulness is sufficient but not necessary: pure input families still yield a unique barycenter, while partially pure nonfaithful Gaussian inputs can admit multiple barycenters.

Conclusion: The framework unifies 2-quantum Wasserstein barycenters across state and channel formulations and provides general existence/duality results. Gaussian rigidity holds under a faithful Gaussian input, with the covariance-state gap closed by complementary slackness. Nonfaithful cases exhibit a dichotomy: pure families are rigid, whereas partially pure nonfaithful Gaussian inputs may fail uniqueness.

Abstract: We develop a Quantum Optimal Transport (QOT) barycenter framework for quantum states, as an analog of Wasserstein barycenters~\cite{AguCar}, and establish existence and duality results for a broad class of possibly unbounded transport costs on separable Hilbert spaces. Our framework provides, in particular, a unified treatment of $2$-quantum Wasserstein (QW) barycenters in both the quantum-state and quantum-channel formulations by specializing to the canonical quadratic cost operators associated with the $2$-quantum Wasserstein distances of Caglioti--Golse--Mouhot--Paul~\cite{caglioti2021towards,Golse-Mouhot-Paul:2016} and De Palma--Trevisan~\cite{DPaTre19}.
  The central results concern Gaussian input states and, in particular, Gaussian rigidity: whether Gaussian input states force the 2-QW barycenter itself to be Gaussian and uniquely determined when the minimization is taken over all quantum states. We first show that the barycenter problem admits a Gaussian minimizer and reduces to a finite-dimensional convex optimization problem over covariance matrices.
  The main difficulty is that uniqueness of the optimal covariance does not, in general, imply uniqueness of the underlying quantum state. We bridge this gap through a state-reconstruction principle under covariance complementary slackness that upgrades uniqueness of the optimal covariance to uniqueness of the full quantum state, and thereby prove a global rigidity theorem: if at least one Gaussian input is faithful, then the barycenter is unique among all quantum states and is necessarily Gaussian. Faithfulness is sufficient but not necessary: families of pure inputs still determine a unique barycenter, whereas partially pure nonfaithful Gaussian inputs may admit multiple barycenters.

</details>


### [111] [Approximation theorems for fermionic Gaussian states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01860)
*Amir-Reza Negari,Farzin Salek,Zoltán Zimborás,Aram Harrow,Patrick Hayden,Jens Eisert*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper shows that a single covariance-level constraint—monogamy of two-point correlations—yields product-state, de Finetti, and recoverability approximations for fermionic Gaussian states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The general many-body principle that widely distributed interactions or correlations reduce to an effective mean-field description is qualitative. The authors aim to give a quantitative, structural version of this principle directly for fermionic Gaussian states, at the level of Majorana covariance matrices.

Method: They exploit the admissibility condition for fermionic covariance matrices, interpreted as a quantitative monogamy constraint on two-point correlations (a bounded covariance budget of a fixed region across many disjoint regions). This principle is applied to: (i) quadratic fermionic Hamiltonians on high-degree finite simple graphs, via product-state approximations to ground-state energy and finite-temperature free energy; (ii) a finite fermionic Gaussian de Finetti theorem using graded fermionic copy permutations and exchangeability; and (iii) spatial fermionic Gaussian states with exponentially clustering mutual information, combined with recoverability.

Result: For quadratic fermionic Hamiltonians on D-regular graphs, product-state approximations achieve error of order D^{-1/2} for both ground-state energy and finite-temperature free energy. A k-copy Gaussian state that is n-exchangeable under graded fermionic copy permutations is O(k/n)-close in trace norm to the product of its one-copy marginal, and infinite exchangeability within the Gaussian class implies exact product structure. For spatial fermionic Gaussian states with exponentially clustering mutual information, the conditional mutual information across a separating buffer decays as O(r^{-1}), giving an O(r^{-1/2}) trace-norm recovered-state approximation.

Conclusion: Product and de Finetti approximations and recoverability estimates for fermionic Gaussian systems can all be derived directly from covariance-level structure, via the monogamy of two-point correlations.

Abstract: A recurring principle in many-body physics is that sufficiently distributed interactions or correlations lead to an effectively mean-field description. For fermionic Gaussian states, we establish a quantitative version of this principle directly at the level of Majorana covariance matrices. The main observation is that the admissibility condition for fermionic covariance matrices imposes a quantitative monogamy constraint on two-point correlations: a fixed region has only a bounded covariance budget to distribute among many disjoint regions. We use this principle in three directions. First, for quadratic fermionic Hamiltonians on finite simple graphs of high degree, we obtain product-state approximations to both the ground-state energy and the finite-temperature free energy, with error of order \(D^{-1/2}\) for \(D\)-regular graphs. Second, we prove a finite fermionic Gaussian de Finetti theorem: a \(k\)-copy Gaussian state that is \(n\)-exchangeable under graded fermionic copy permutations is \(O(k/n)\)-close in trace norm to the product of its one-copy marginal. In particular, infinite exchangeability within the Gaussian class implies exact product structure. Third, for spatial fermionic Gaussian states with exponentially clustering mutual information, we prove that the conditional mutual information across a separating buffer decays as \(O(r^{-1})\), and hence, by recoverability, the state admits an \(O(r^{-1/2})\) trace-norm approximation by a recovered state. These results show that product and de Finetti approximations and recoverability estimates for fermionic Gaussian systems can all be derived directly from covariance-level structure.

</details>


### [112] [Fourier Symmetrization for Geometric Quantum Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01874)
*Letao Wang,Abdel Lisser,Sreejith Sreekumar,Zeno Toffano*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies how symmetry shapes the expressivity of quantum Fourier models (QFMs) via their Fourier spectrum, showing symmetrization reorganizes frequencies into orbits and controls coefficient variance. It introduces randomized encoding for invariant models and demonstrates QPINNs achieving lowest errors on two PDE benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Geometric quantum machine learning embeds symmetry into quantum models, but the mechanisms by which symmetry influences expressivity and guides effective model design are not well understood, creating a need for a principled framework connecting symmetry to model behavior.

Method: The authors analyze QFMs through their Fourier representation, where symmetry partitions the frequency spectrum into orbits and sums Fourier coefficients within each orbit into symmetrized coefficients. They derive a variance identity when trainable layers form independent exact 2-designs, and bound the deviation for single-layer QFMs with epsilon-approximate 2-design layers. They examine hyperoctahedral group orbit growth and symmetrization over an elementary abelian 2-group yielding multivariate Chebyshev bases. They further introduce randomized encoding to implement invariant models without ancilla qubits or extra quantum twirling depth, and evaluate via QPINNs on screened Poisson and Hamilton-Jacobi equations under hard boundary constraints.

Result: When trainable layers are independent exact 2-designs, each symmetrized coefficient's variance equals the sum of coefficient variances in its orbit; for epsilon-approximate 2-designs the deviation is bounded, with a bound for individual Fourier coefficients that can be exponentially tighter than an existing bound. Hyperoctahedral orbit growth can mitigate vanishing expressivity of symmetrized coefficients, and abelian 2-group symmetrization yields pure multivariate Chebyshev polynomial bases. QPINNs using exact symmetrization and randomized encoding achieve the lowest mean errors on the two benchmarks, respectively.

Conclusion: Symmetry's effect on QFM expressivity is characterized through orbit-structured Fourier coefficients and variance identities, providing design principles and a randomized encoding scheme that avoids ancilla qubits and extra twirling depth, with empirical success in physics-informed neural network benchmarks.

Abstract: Geometric quantum machine learning incorporates symmetry into quantum models, but how symmetry shapes their expressivity and guides effective model design remains insufficiently understood. We address this question through the Fourier representation of quantum Fourier models (QFMs). Symmetry organizes the frequency spectrum into orbits and sums the Fourier coefficients within each orbit into a symmetrized coefficient. When QFM trainable layers form independent exact 2-designs, the variance of each symmetrized coefficient equals the sum of the coefficient variances in its orbit. For single-layer QFMs with $\varepsilon$-approximate 2-design trainable layers, we bound the deviation from this identity. The resulting bound for individual Fourier coefficients can be exponentially tighter than an existing bound. The hyperoctahedral group provides an example of orbit growth that can mitigate vanishing expressivity of the symmetrized coefficients. Symmetrization of QFMs over an elementary abelian 2-group also yields pure multivariate Chebyshev polynomial basis functions. We introduce randomized encoding, which implements invariant models without ancilla qubits or the additional circuit depth for quantum twirling. We evaluate the models as quantum physics-informed neural networks (QPINNs) on two-dimensional screened Poisson and stationary viscous Hamilton-Jacobi equations. Under hard boundary constraints, QPINNs using exact symmetrization and randomized encoding achieve the lowest mean errors in the two benchmarks, respectively.

</details>


### [113] [Floquet-Universal Hamiltonian Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01878)
*Emilio Onorati,Harriet Apel,Michael M. Wolf,Toby Cubitt*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops a theory of Floquet simulation, using periodically driven Hamiltonians to synthesize time-independent Hamiltonians. It characterizes Floquet-universal Hamiltonians that can produce any target Hamiltonian using only O(1) local interaction strengths, requiring driving frequencies scaling polynomially with system size for k-local lattice Hamiltonians, with complexity-theoretic implications including BQP-completeness and QMA-hardness results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Analogue and digital Hamiltonian simulation represent two distinct approaches to replicating physical properties across quantum systems, rooted in time-independent Hamiltonians versus fully controllable time-dependent quantum circuits. Time-periodic Hamiltonians represent an intermediate regime that has remained underexplored theoretically. The work aims to establish a rigorous theory of Floquet simulation bridging these regimes, addressing the impractical multi-scale local interaction strengths often required in analogue simulation.

Method: The authors establish a theory of Floquet simulation using periodically driven Hamiltonians to synthesize time-independent ones. They prove that a set of interactions S can Floquet-simulate every Hamiltonian in the Lie algebra Lie(S), providing a complete and constructive characterization of Floquet-universal Hamiltonians. Their construction uses only O(1) local interaction strengths and their ratios, requiring a range of driving frequencies that scales polynomially with system size for classes such as k-local lattice Hamiltonians.

Result: The paper shows a set of interactions S can Floquet-simulate every Hamiltonian in Lie(S), yielding a complete and constructive characterization of Floquet-universal Hamiltonians able to produce any target Hamiltonian. The construction uses only O(1) local interaction strengths, avoiding impractical multi-scale strengths, with driving frequencies scaling polynomially with system size for important classes like k-local lattice Hamiltonians. This produces BQP-completeness and QMA-hardness results for natural physical properties and quantities in Floquet physics.

Conclusion: Floquet simulation constitutes an intermediate regime between analogue and digital Hamiltonian simulation. The established theory provides a complete and constructive characterization of Floquet-universal Hamiltonians with practical O(1) interaction strengths and polynomially scaling driving frequencies, with immediate complexity-theoretic consequences including BQP-completeness and QMA-hardness results for natural properties studied in Floquet physics.

Abstract: Analogue and digital Hamiltonian simulation represent two distinct approaches to making one quantum system replicate the physical properties of another, with consequences ranging from practical applications to complexity theory and quantum gravity. They are rooted in two distinct regimes of quantum dynamics: time-independent Hamiltonians for the former; fully controllable time-dependent quantum circuits in the latter. Time-periodic Hamiltonians represent an intermediate regime. In this work, we establish a theory of Floquet simulation where we use periodically driven Hamiltonians to synthesise time-independent ones.
  We show that a set of interactions $S$ can Floquet-simulate every Hamiltonian in the Lie algebra $\mathrm{Lie}(S)$, and consequently we give a complete and constructive characterisation of Floquet-universal Hamiltonians that are able to produce any target Hamiltonian. Notably, our construction uses only $O(1)$ local interaction strengths and ratios thereof, avoiding the impractical multi-scale local interaction strengths often required in analogue simulation via time-independent Hamiltonians. Instead, the construction requires a range of driving frequencies which scales polynomially with system size for important classes such as $k$-local lattice Hamiltonians. This has immediate complexity-theoretic implications, including numerous BQP-completeness and QMA-hardness results for natural physical properties and quantities studied in Floquet physics.

</details>


### [114] [Exponential quantum advantages for decoded quantum interferometry in the streaming setting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01902)
*Kewen Wu,Guangxu Yang*

Main category: quant-ph
