TL;DR: In the streaming model, an adaptation of the Decoded Quantum Interferometry (DQI) algorithm solves a generalization of optimal polynomial intersection (OPI) using one pass, polylogarithmic space, and polylogarithmic time per entry, satisfying 93% of constraints; any classical algorithm satisfying just 76% requires polynomial space. This yields unconditional, provable quantum advantages for DQI in memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While DQI (Jordan et al., Nature 2025) gives polynomial-time quantum approximation guarantees for OPI where known classical algorithms need exponential time, space is a critical resource given fault-tolerant qubit overhead. The paper asks whether DQI also yields unconditional quantum advantages in memory.

Method: The authors study a natural generalization of OPI via Hermite interpolation and Hasse derivatives: find a low-degree polynomial satisfying as many value and derivative constraints as possible. They adapt the DQI algorithm to the streaming setting and prove a space-separation theorem.

Result: Quantum efficiency: an adapted DQI algorithm satisfies 93% of constraints with a single pass, polylogarithmic space, and polylogarithmic per-entry time. Classical hardness: any classical algorithm satisfying only 76% of constraints requires polynomial space, even with polynomially many passes and unlimited time. The result yields a complete tradeoff curve over the tunable parameters.

Conclusion: DQI provides provable, unconditional quantum advantages in memory for the original OPI problem, establishing a streaming quantum-classical space separation.

Abstract: Decoded quantum interferometry (DQI) is a polynomial-time quantum algorithm introduced by Jordan et al. (Nature 2025). For a natural optimization problem, known as optimal polynomial intersection (OPI), it achieves approximation guarantees in regimes where all known classical algorithms require exponential time.
  Besides time, space is another central resource: storing and manipulating a massive input can be very challenging, especially when logical qubits carry substantial fault-tolerant implementation overhead. This motivates the following question: does DQI yield quantum advantages in memory, and can we prove it unconditionally?
  We give an affirmative answer to this question in the streaming setting. In particular, we consider a natural generalization of OPI using Hermite interpolation and Hasse derivatives, which asks for a low-degree polynomial satisfying as many constraints on its values and derivatives as possible. As a concrete example, we show
  [Quantum efficiency.] An adaptation of the DQI algorithm produces a polynomial satisfying $93\%$ of the constraints; moreover, it only reads the input stream in one pass, uses polylogarithmic space, and has polylogarithmic computation time per stream entry.
  [Classical hardness.] Any classical algorithm that produces an answer satisfying just $76\%$ of the constraints requires polynomial space, even if it can read the input stream with polynomially many passes and can use unlimited time.
  Our result provides a complete tradeoff curve for the tunable parameters, and implies that DQI has provable quantum advantages for the original OPI problem.

</details>


### [115] [Loss-tolerant distributed lattice surgery using fusion networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01923)
*Felix Burt,Richard Meister,Sheng-Ku Lin,Kuan-Cheng Chen,Michael Hanks,Roberto Bondesan,M. S. Kim,Kin K. Leung*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work uses ZX calculus to design hybrid syndrome extraction protocols for interfacing networked matter-based QPUs, showing that fusion-based and cluster-state protocols reach a 50% interface-erasure threshold and offer improved interface distance over Bell-pair interfaces, though these advantages only manifest at low local noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Networking matter-based QPUs is a promising path to scalable fault-tolerant quantum computing, but distributed logical operations over photonic links suffer from photon loss and probabilistic linear-optical operations, whose noise is stronger and characteristically different from local QPU noise. Measurement- and fusion-based quantum computing are naturally robust to these effects and may complement circuit-based error correction at the interface between networked QPUs.

Method: The authors employ ZX calculus transformations to construct hybrid syndrome extraction protocols and apply them to distributed rotated surface code lattice surgery via linear cluster states and fused Bell pairs. They compute erasure thresholds, map correctable error regions under resource-state errors, local circuit noise, and fusion erasure, convert erasure thresholds into photon-loss thresholds, and probe subthreshold performance with fusion boosting.

Result: Several protocols achieve a 50% interface-erasure threshold in the absence of local noise, including circuit-based lattice surgery with fused Bell pairs and hybrid protocols using linear cluster states. Compared to a straight Bell-pair interface, the hybrid protocols raise the merge-observable interface distance from d+1 to 2d+1 and restore the perpendicular-observable interface distance from floor((d+1)/2) to d. Thresholds degrade with truncated resource states and under resource-state errors, local circuit noise, and fusion erasure. At circuit and resource-state error rates of 10^-3, local errors largely mask the interface-distance advantage; as local noise drops to 10^-4 and below, linear-chain protocols improve more rapidly with decreasing erasure.

Conclusion: Hybrid measurement- and fusion-based protocols, designed via ZX calculus, can interface networked matter-based QPUs while improving interface distance relative to Bell-pair interfaces. Their interface-distance advantage becomes effective only in low local-noise regimes (near or below 10^-4), indicating that reducing local noise is key to realizing the benefit of these hybrid interfaces.

Abstract: Networking matter-based quantum processing units (QPUs) offers a promising route to scaling fault-tolerant quantum computers. This requires distributed logical operations to be performed across photonic links, where noise is characteristically different from and stronger than in local QPUs owing to photon loss and probabilistic linear-optical operations. Measurement- and fusion-based quantum computing are designed to be robust against loss and probabilistic photonic operations, suggesting they could complement circuit-based error correction at the interface between networked QPUs. We use ZX calculus transformations to construct hybrid syndrome extraction protocols and apply them to distributed rotated surface code lattice surgery, demonstrating that several protocols attain a $50\%$ interface-erasure threshold when local noise is absent, including circuit-based lattice surgery using fused Bell pairs and hybrid protocols using linear cluster states. Relative to a straight Bell-pair interface geometry, the hybrid protocols increase the merge-observable interface distance from $d+1$ to $2d+1$ and restore the perpendicular-observable interface distance from $\lfloor(d+1)/2\rfloor$ to $d$. We calculate how the threshold decreases when using truncated resource states and map correctable regions under resource-state errors, local circuit noise, and fusion erasure. We then convert these erasure thresholds into photon-loss thresholds and probe subthreshold performance using fusion boosting. At circuit and resource-state error rates of $10^{-3}$, local errors largely mask the interface-distance advantage. As local noise is reduced to $10^{-4}$ and below, the linear-chain protocols improve more rapidly with decreasing erasure, suggesting that interface distance enhancements are effective in low local noise regimes.

</details>


### [116] [Avoiding Exponentially Large Groups with Open Quantum System Technology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01932)
*Jihong Cai,Advith Govindarajan,Marius Junge*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes state preparation via open quantum systems: instead of using the full unitary group on n qubits, it uses a small poly(n)-dimensional Lie group G acting on (n+3) qubits, combined with partial trace and environmental state preparation, to approximately reach any state; it also identifies an open-system magic interaction Hamiltonian enabling transitivity over all densities and achieves channel universality with a larger environment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional state preparation relies on the full unitary group on n qubits, which is prohibitively large. The authors aim to exploit open quantum systems—specifically partial trace and environmental state preparation—to find a much smaller, efficient control group that still suffices for universal state preparation.

Method: They identify a small Lie group G acting on (n+3) qubits whose Lie algebra has dimension poly(n), derived from Lie algebras of local Pauli strings. Any state can be approximated from any fixed input state by repeatedly applying group operations together with partial trace and state preparation in the environment. They further identify a single open-system magic interaction Hamiltonian whose unitary group, combined with open-system techniques, yields transitivity on the space of all density operators, and show channel universality using a larger environment.

Result: The small poly(n)-dimensional Lie group on (n+3) qubits enables approximate preparation of any state. A single open-system magic interaction Hamiltonian combined with open-system technology achieves transitivity on all densities, and all channels on n qubits can be approximated using a larger environment. The identified groups yield a new landscape of cheap and expensive states, densities, and channels.

Conclusion: Full unitary control on n qubits is not necessary for universal state preparation. Open quantum system resources—partial trace, environmental state preparation, and a small poly(n)-dimensional Lie group from local Pauli strings, plus a single magic interaction Hamiltonian—suffice for universal state and channel approximation, introducing a new resource-theoretic classification of cheap versus expensive states, densities, and channels.

Abstract: We propose a novel approach to state preparation by embracing the full power of open quantum systems. Instead of working with the whole unitary group in $n$ qubits, we identify a small Lie group $G$ in $(n+3)$ qubits. The dimension of its Lie algebra is $\text{poly}(n)$. Every state can be approximated starting from any fixed input state by repeatedly using partial trace and state preparation in the environment alongside group operations. Moreover, we also identify a single (open system magic) interaction Hamiltonian whose unitary group can be combined with open system quantum technology mentioned above to achieve transitivity on the space of all densities. Similarly, we show that all channels on $n$ qubits can be approximated by using a larger environment, achieving channel universality. The interesting small groups we identify are derived from Lie algebras of local Pauli strings and lead to a new landscape of cheap and expensive states, densities, and channels.

</details>


### [117] [Optimal query complexity for fractional quantum evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01940)
*Anthony Yuezhang Liu,Adam Wesołowski,Jayne Thompson,Mile Gu,Lirandë Pira*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proves a matching lower bound for the fractional quantum query problem, showing that the QSVT-based upper bound O((1/δ)log(1/ε)) is asymptotically optimal, with query complexity Θ_τ((1/δ)log(1/ε)).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The fractional query problem asks how many queries to an unknown unitary U=e^{iH} are needed to implement a noninteger power U^t=e^{itH} (0<t<1) when the spectrum is separated from the branch cut by a gap δ. While quantum singular value transformation (QSVT) provides an upper bound of O((1/δ)log(1/ε)) queries for approximation error ε, no matching lower bound was known, leaving the optimality of the QSVT construction unestablished.

Method: The authors reduce any N-query circuit to the approximation of e^{itθ} by a trigonometric polynomial of degree bounded by O(N), then apply the Remez inequality to derive the lower bound. They additionally provide an alternative proof by constructing a linear functional that annihilates the approximant space.

Result: They establish a lower bound of Ω_τ((1/δ)log(1/ε)), matching the QSVT upper bound. The alternative linear-functional argument yields a Ω_τ(log(1/ε)) bound that is uniform in δ.

Conclusion: The optimal query complexity for the fractional query problem is Θ_τ((1/δ)log(1/ε)), proving that the known QSVT construction is asymptotically optimal.

Abstract: Given oracle access to an unknown unitary $U=e^{iH}$ , the fractional query problem asks how many queries are required to implement a noninteger power $U^t=e^{itH}$, $0<t<1$, when the spectrum is separated from the branch cut by a gap $δ$. Quantum singular value transformation gives an upper bound of $O\!\left(\frac{1}δ\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$ queries for approximation error $\varepsilon$. We prove a matching lower bound for arbitrary query algorithms. Our argument reduces any $N$-query circuit to the approximation of $e^{itθ}$ by a trigonometric polynomial with degree bounded by $O(N)$, together with Remez inequality. This allows us to establish the lower bound of $Ω_τ\!\left(\frac{1}δ\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$. Consequently, the optimal query complexity for fractional query problem is $Θ_τ\!\left(\frac{1}δ\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$, showing that the known QSVT construction is asymptotically optimal. We also give an alternative lower bound proof based on constructing a linear functional that annihilates the approximant space, yielding a $Ω_τ\!\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$ bound uniform to $δ$.

</details>


### [118] [Universality Sacrifices Reliability in Classical-Quantum Channel Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01941)
*Kaito Watanabe,Masahito Hayashi,Takaya Matsuura,Hao-Chung Cheng*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Universal channel coding achieves capacity and optimal high-rate reliability for classical channels, but this compatibility fails for classical-quantum (CQ) channels. The optimal channel-aware reliability is not always universally achievable due to ignorance of the output unitary rotation. For unitary-invariant decoders, a converse bound is derived that can be strictly below the channel-aware error exponent; a channel-independent code matches this bound at high rates. The channel-aware and universal exponents are governed by Petz and sandwiched Rényi divergences, which coincide for commuting outputs but differ otherwise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether universal coding—achieving both capacity and optimal high-rate reliability without full channel description—remains compatible in the classical-quantum setting. The work identifies and quantifies the fundamental reliability cost of universality for classical-quantum channels.

Method: Constructs a family of CQ channels demonstrating that no fixed coding scheme attains channel-aware optimal reliability. Derives a converse bound on reliability for unitary-invariant decoders. Constructs a channel-independent encoder-decoder pair achieving a universally achievable reliability bound matching the converse in the high-rate regime, via Petz and sandwiched Rényi divergence analysis.

Result: The channel-aware optimal reliability is not always achievable with universal coding for CQ channels. A converse bound for unitary-invariant decoders can be strictly smaller than the optimal channel-aware error exponent. A universal encoder-decoder pair matches this converse bound in the high-rate regime, characterizing optimal universal reliability. Channel-aware and universal exponents are governed by Petz and sandwiched Rényi divergences, respectively.

Conclusion: Universality preserves optimal reliability classically (where the two Rényi divergences coincide for commuting outputs) but can reduce it quantumly (where they differ for noncommuting outputs), revealing a fundamental reliability cost of universal CQ channel coding.

Abstract: Universal channel coding enables communication without a complete description of the channel. For classical channels, universal codes can attain both capacity and the optimal high-rate reliability. We show that this compatibility fails for classical-quantum channels in general; that is, the optimal reliability in the channel-aware scenario is not always achievable with universal coding due to the ignorance of the unitary rotation of the output system. We exhibit a family of classical-quantum channels for which one cannot achieve the channel-aware optimal reliability by a fixed coding scheme. We further derive a converse bound on the reliability for unitary-invariant decoders, a natural assumption for the universal coding scheme, that can be strictly smaller than the optimal channel-aware error exponent. Conversely, we construct a channel-independent encoder-decoder pair and establish a universally achievable bound on the reliability that matches this converse bound in the high-rate regime, thereby characterizing the optimal universal reliability. Specifically, the channel-aware and universal exponents are governed by the Petz and sandwiched Rényi divergences, respectively. These divergences coincide for commuting outputs but differ for noncommuting ones, explaining why universality preserves optimal reliability classically but can reduce it quantumly. Our results showcase the fundamental reliability cost of performing the classical-quantum channel coding task universally.

</details>


### [119] [Hardware-Efficient Ground-State Preparation using Variational Imaginary-Time Majorana Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01954)
*Federico Santona,Manuel G. Algaba,Aeishah Ameera Anuar,Anna M. Wernbacher,Prachi Sharma,Fedor Šimkovic*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: VIME is a classical pre-training algorithm that extends operator-projected variational quantum imaginary-time evolution to molecular electronic structure via a single compiled Majorana-propagation graph, paired with a compressed tiled Unitary Product State (c-tUPS) ansatz to cut simulation and state-preparation costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Compact ground-state preparation circuits are essential for early fault-tolerant quantum chemistry, motivating resource-efficient approaches to molecular ground-state preparation.

Method: VIME extends operator-projected variational quantum imaginary-time evolution (OVQITE) to molecular electronic structure using a single compiled Majorana-propagation (MP) surrogate graph. It is paired with a compressed tiled Unitary Product State (c-tUPS) ansatz developed to reduce both classical simulation and quantum state-preparation costs.

Result: For the strongly correlated ruthenium complex TLD-1411, active-space calculations up to 52 qubits reach chemical precision against DMRG reference energies, with energy errors ~400x smaller than ADAPT-VMPE. Across the multireference acene series up to heptacene (60 qubits), VIME matches or improves MP-reported energy accuracy of ADAPT-VMPE with up to 26x fewer CNOTs and 167x lower two-qubit depth.

Conclusion: Imaginary-time classical pre-training of compressed variational ansätze is a promising route to resource-efficient molecular ground-state preparation.

Abstract: Compact ground-state preparation circuits are essential for early fault-tolerant quantum chemistry. We introduce Variational Imaginary-time Majorana Evolution (VIME), a classical pre-training algorithm that extends operator-projected variational quantum imaginary-time evolution (OVQITE) to molecular electronic structure using a single compiled Majorana-propagation (MP) surrogate graph. We pair VIME with a compressed tiled Unitary Product State (c-tUPS) ansatz that we develop to reduce both classical simulation and quantum state-preparation costs. In the strongly correlated ruthenium complex TLD-1411, active-space calculations requiring up to 52 qubits achieve chemical precision against DMRG reference energies, with energy errors approximately $400\times$ smaller than those from ADAPT-VQE-based variational Majorana-propagation (ADAPT-VMPE). Across the strongly multireference acene series up to heptacene (60 qubits), VIME matches or improves on the MP-reported energy accuracy of the ADAPT-VMPE comparison points with up to $26\times$ fewer CNOTs and $167\times$ lower two-qubit depth. These results establish imaginary-time classical pre-training of compressed variational ansätze as a promising route to resource-efficient molecular ground-state preparation.

</details>


### [120] [Fully tolerant product state testing and closest product state learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01979)
*Zongbo Bao,Jonas Helsen,Tuyen Nguyen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies tolerant testing of whether an unknown n-qudit state is close to or far from any product state (in state overlap) and closest product state learning. It gives a time-efficient tester using an n-independent number of copies via a random coloring argument, and an improved learner using O~(nd)^2 · 2^{O~(1/ε^8)} copies to produce an ε-approximately optimal product state.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Testing and learning product states is a fundamental problem in quantum property testing and quantum learning theory. Prior work (e.g., Harrow & Montanaro's product state test) lacked an efficient tolerant tester, and closest product state learning algorithms required a large number of copies or were not time-efficient. The authors aim to overcome these limitations by removing the dependence on n in the copy complexity and substantially improving sample complexity for learning.

Method: The authors introduce a random coloring argument showing that for any unknown state there exists a partition of [n] into q parts such that the squared overlap with the closest product state for that partition is at most an additive O(1/q) larger than optimal. This reduces the problem to tolerant testing of q parties with growing local dimensions. They combine this with blockwise spectral projection arguments to prove that the natural k-copy generalization of Harrow & Montanaro's product state test is an efficient tolerant tester. For learning, they use the same random coloring and blockwise spectral projection plus a qudit variant of the Bakshi et al. high-fidelity product state learning algorithm and a sampling technique based on Werner's optimal cloning channel.

Result: A time-efficient tolerant tester for product-state proximity using an n-independent number of copies, and an algorithm for closest product state learning that takes O~((nd)^2) · 2^{O~(1/ε^8)} copies of the unknown state and outputs an ε-approximately optimal product state.

Conclusion: The random coloring and blockwise spectral projection techniques yield both an efficient tolerant product state tester with copy complexity independent of n and a substantially improved closest product state learner, advancing the state of the art in quantum product state testing and learning.

Abstract: We address the problem of testing whether an unknown $n$-qudit state $ρ$ is $a$-close to a product state or $b$-far away from any product state, as measured in terms of state overlap. We provide a time-efficient algorithm to solve this problem that requires an $n$-independent number of copies of the unknown state. Our random coloring argument shows that for any unknown state there always exists a partition of $[n]$ into $q$ parts such that the square of the overlap with the closest product state with respect to this partition is only an additive factor $O(1/q)$ larger. This reduces the problem to tolerant testing of $q$ parties, with potentially growing local dimensions. Combining this insight with blockwise spectral projection arguments we can show that the natural $k$-copy generalization of Harrow & Montanaro's product state test provides an efficient tolerant tester.
  We use the same random coloring and blockwise spectral projection techniques to obtain a substantially improved algorithm for closest product state learning. In particular we give an algorithm that takes in $\widetilde{O}\big((nd)^2\big)\, 2^{\widetilde{O}(1/\varepsilon^8)}$ copies of the unknown state and produces an $\varepsilon$-approximately optimal product state. The key technical components of this learner are a qudit variant of the Bakshi et al. high-fidelity product state learning algorithm and a sampling technique based on Werner's optimal cloning channel.

</details>


### [121] [On the Metastability of the Mean-Field Interchange Model for Local Dimension $\geq 3$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01987)
*Sergio Escobar,Lin Lin,Michael Ragone,Kevin D. Stubbs*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The Davies dynamics of the mean-field interchange model (local dimension d ≥ 3, single-site generalized Pauli couplings) exhibits a re-entrant thermalization pattern: the spectral gap is polynomially bounded at both low and high temperatures, but vanishes exponentially in system size within a metastable interval between two inverse temperatures β_min(d) and β = d.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many quantum systems are believed to thermalize slowly at low temperatures because metastable phases exist. Yet there are cases where further cooling restores polynomial-time thermalization, and the authors aim to identify and rigorously characterize such a dynamical phase diagram with a controllable model.

Method: The authors analyze the Davies generator of the mean-field interchange model with local dimension d ≥ 3 and single-site generalized Pauli couplings. Using Schur-Weyl duality, they decompose the generator into a classical Markov generator acting on Young diagrams plus a restriction to the remaining symmetry sectors, whose spectral gap is Ω(n^{-1}). The gap of the Young-diagram chain is then bounded to locate the metastable region.

Result: The dynamical phase diagram is governed by two inverse temperatures, β_min(d) and β = d, with the static first-order phase transition lying between them. Outside the interval (β_min(d), d), the spectral gap of the Davies generator is bounded below by an inverse polynomial in system size n; inside this interval, the gap vanishes exponentially in n, signaling metastability. Exponential slowdown therefore originates entirely from the Young diagram chain.

Conclusion: Cooling a system can non-monotonically restore fast (polynomial-time) thermalization: the mean-field interchange model exhibits re-entrant thermalization with a metastable window bounded by β_min(d) and β = d, and its slowdown is fully attributable to the classical Young diagram dynamics within the Schur-Weyl decomposition.

Abstract: Many quantum systems are believed to thermalize slowly at low temperatures due to the existence of metastable phases. However, there are cases where further cooling can restore polynomial-time thermalization: we demonstrate that the Davies dynamics of the mean-field interchange model with local dimension $d\geq 3$ and single-site generalized Pauli couplings exhibits this behavior. The dynamical phase diagram is beholden to two distinguished inverse temperatures, $β_{\mathrm{min}}(d)$ and $β=d$, with the static first-order phase transition lying between them. At low and high temperatures surrounding the interval $(β_{\mathrm{min}}(d),d)$, the spectral gap of the Davies generator is bounded below by an inverse polynomial in system size $n$. Meanwhile, in this interval, the gap vanishes exponentially in $n$, indicating metastability. The proof uses Schur-Weyl duality to decompose the Davies generator into a classical Markov generator on Young diagrams, and the restriction to the remaining symmetry sectors whose gap is $Ω(n^{-1})$. Thus, exponential slowdown arises entirely from the Young diagram chain.

</details>


### [122] [Geometry of Knill-Laflamme Coefficients for Pauli Error Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01992)
*Baisong Sun,Ningping Cao,Yiu Tung Poon,Bei Zeng*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Knill-Laflamme coefficients are recast as joint higher-rank numerical ranges whose norm image is a signature spectrum; representation multiplicity is shown to lift active expectation data to exact scalar compressions, yielding convex connected coefficient ranges and interval signature spectra, with applications to commuting Pauli families and subsystem stabilizer codes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Knill-Laflamme coefficients characterize exact quantum error detection via scalar compressions of error operators onto the code space, but the geometry of attainable coefficient vectors—how operator representation, code dimension, and detection constraints shape these ranges—was not systematically understood.

Method: The authors formulate attainable coefficient vectors for prescribed Pauli observables and fixed code dimension as a joint higher-rank numerical range, whose Euclidean norm image is the signature spectrum. They prove a main theorem identifying representation multiplicity as sufficient to lift active expectation data to exact scalar compressions, and analyze the resulting geometry (convexity, connectivity, signature spectrum) across commuting Pauli families, subsystem stabilizer codes, parent-Hamiltonian constructions, and a three-qubit example with noncommuting detection constraints.

Result: A lift from active expectation data to exact scalar compressions exists whenever each occupied block has multiplicity at least the code dimension times the rank of the corresponding active state. When multiplicity suffices to realize all active states, the coefficient range equals the full active expectation body, hence is convex and connected with a closed interval as its signature spectrum. Sufficient common-eigenspace degeneracy in commuting Pauli families yields polytopes; subsystem stabilizer codes supply multiplicity spaces for noncommuting gauge observables. Examples show increasing code dimension can shrink, collapse, or empty a range, while increasing multiplicity can turn a Bloch sphere into a filled ball; extra noncommuting detection constraints reduce a tetrahedral range to its barycenter plus four vertices, giving a disconnected signature spectrum.

Conclusion: The work provides a structural framework unifying the geometry of Knill-Laflamme coefficients under varying operator representation, code dimension, and detection constraints, and motivates sharper criteria for connected coefficient ranges and interval signature spectra under structured error models.

Abstract: Knill-Laflamme coefficients characterize exact quantum error detection through scalar compressions of error operators to the code space. For prescribed Pauli observables and a fixed code dimension, attainable coefficient vectors form a joint higher-rank numerical range, whose Euclidean norm image is the signature spectrum. We study how operator representation, code dimension, and detection constraints govern this geometry. Our main theorem identifies representation multiplicity as sufficient to lift active expectation data to exact scalar compressions: a lift exists whenever each occupied block has multiplicity at least the code dimension times the rank of the corresponding active state. When multiplicity suffices to realize all active states, the coefficient range is the full active expectation body and is therefore convex and connected, with a closed interval as its signature spectrum. This framework unifies commuting Pauli families, where sufficient common-eigenspace degeneracy yields polytopes, and subsystem stabilizer codes, where protected logical subsystems supply multiplicity spaces for noncommuting gauge observables. Explicit examples show how increasing the code dimension can make a range shrink, collapse, or become empty, while increasing multiplicity can turn a Bloch sphere into a filled ball. In the subsystem setting, parent-Hamiltonian constructions realize exact detecting codes as degenerate ground spaces, selecting distinguished vectors and continuous paths within these ranges. A final three-qubit example shows additional noncommuting detection constraints reduce a tetrahedral range to its barycenter and four vertices, producing a disconnected signature spectrum. The results provide a structural framework for Knill-Laflamme coefficient geometry and motivate sharper criteria for connected coefficient ranges and interval signature spectra under structured error models.

</details>


### [123] [Operational transformation rates of quantum states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02025)
*Giulia Mazzola,Renato Renner*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper relaxes the iid assumption for quantum state copies by defining an operational set of states indistinguishable from a tensor power, and shows via a de Finetti-type argument that this set is asymptotically almost-iid, introducing operational analogues of asymptotic transformation rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assuming independent and identically distributed (iid) copies of a quantum state is not operationally justified, since an experimentalist cannot generally rule out imperfections at unknown positions or correlations between copies.

Method: The authors relax the iid assumption by considering the whole set of resource states that are fundamentally indistinguishable from a tensor power state, i.e., that look like many copies to the experimentalist, and apply a de Finetti-type argument.

Result: The operational set of states can asymptotically be represented by almost-iid states. As an application, operational analogues of asymptotic transformation rates, such as entanglement cost and distillation, are introduced.

Conclusion: The introduced operational rates raise the question of whether they coincide with their iid counterparts.

Abstract: Typically, multiple copies of a quantum state are described by a tensor power of that state. However, from an operational point of view, an experimentalist cannot, in general, rule out imperfections at unknown positions or correlations between copies, so assuming independent and identically distributed (iid) copies is not justified a priori. In the following, we relax the iid assumption by considering a whole set of resource states that are fundamentally indistinguishable from a tensor power state, and in that sense look like many copies of the quantum state to the experimentalist. Using a de Finetti-type argument, we show that this operational set of states can asymptotically be represented by almost-iid states. As an application, we introduce operational analogues of asymptotic transformation rates, such as entanglement cost and distillation. This raises the question of whether these rates coincide with their iid counterparts.

</details>


### [124] [(A Variant of) Clifford Circuit Synthesis is NP-Complete](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02029)
*Luna Lima Keller,Richard Kueng*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Optimal Clifford circuit synthesis (finding the shortest-depth circuit for a given functionality) is proven NP-hard, via a reduction from 3-edge colorability on 3-regular graphs, with the result that adding Hadamard, phase, and CNOT gates cannot improve the optimal depth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Optimal circuit synthesis—finding the shortest-depth circuit for a given functionality over a fixed gate set—is central to quantum and classical hardware design. The classical version is well understood (heuristics and hardness), but little is known about optimal quantum circuit synthesis. For optimal Clifford synthesis, heuristics (e.g., via 3-SAT reduction) exist, but no matching hardness result was known. This paper aims to close that gap.

Method: The proof proceeds in two parts: (i) reduce 3-edge colorability on 3-regular graphs to a circuit synthesis problem involving only CZ gates; (ii) prove that additional elementary Clifford gates—most notably Hadamard, phase, and CNOT—cannot lead to further improvements in optimal circuit depth.

Result: A variant of optimal Clifford synthesis is NP-hard, providing a matching hardness result to the known heuristics. This sharpens the complexity-theoretic picture: simulation and equivalence checking of Clifford circuits are in P, whereas deciding whether a Clifford unitary admits an implementation within a prescribed depth is NP-complete.

Conclusion: Optimal Clifford circuit synthesis is NP-hard (deciding prescribed-depth implementability is NP-complete), complementing the polynomial-time tractability of Clifford simulation and equivalence checking and the existing heuristic approaches.

Abstract: Optimal circuit synthesis is the problem of finding the shortest-depth circuit representation of a given functionality with respect to a pre-specified elementary gate set. This is a central problem in both quantum and classical hardware design. While the classical version is very well understood -- both in terms of heuristics and rigorous hardness assertions -- much less is known about optimal quantum circuit synthesis.
  We focus on optimal Clifford circuit synthesis, for which various heuristics are known, e.g. via reduction to 3-SAT. Our main result supplies a matching hardness result: a variant of optimal Clifford synthesis is NP-hard. The proof proceeds in two parts: (i) reduce 3-edge colorability on 3-regular graphs to a circuit synthesis problem that only involves CZ gates, (ii) prove that the availability of additional elementary Clifford gates -- most notably: Hadamard, phase and CNOT -- cannot lead to further improvements of the optimal circuit depth. Our work sharpens the complexity-theoretic understanding of Clifford circuits: simulation and equivalence checking are in P, whereas deciding whether a Clifford unitary admits an implementation within a prescribed depth is NP-complete.

</details>


### [125] [Sparse Hamiltonian simulation with optimal dependence on the maximum column Euclidean norm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02030)
*Zecheng Li,Chunhao Wang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a quantum algorithm for simulating d-sparse Hermitian Hamiltonians with query complexity O(tΛ√d + √d log(2/ε)), eliminating the subpolynomial overhead of prior work and achieving optimality, with applications to sparse linear systems and black-box unitary implementation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work by Low (STOC 2019) suffered from a subpolynomial overhead that prevented matching the worst-case lower bound. The authors aim to remove this overhead and achieve a query-complexity-optimal simulation for d-sparse Hamiltonians, as well as to resolve an open question on black-box unitary implementation posed by Berry and Childs (QIC 2012).

Method: The algorithm simulates a d-sparse Hermitian Hamiltonian H using a known upper bound Λ on its maximum column Euclidean norm. It employs sparse-oracle queries and achieves operator-norm error ε. The approach replaces the subpolynomial overhead with an additive logarithmic precision term. A spectral-norm upper bound may substitute for Λ. The method is also adapted to solve d-sparse quantum linear systems and to implement black-box unitaries with sparse access.

Result: For tΛ ≥ 1/2, simulation with error ε uses O(tΛ√d + √d log(2/ε)) sparse-oracle queries, removing subpolynomial overhead. For d > 1 and tΛ ≥ log(2/ε), it matches the worst-case lower bound. Gate count is linear in query scale up to oracle and polynomial overhead. Applications yield O(κ√d polylog(κ/ε)) queries for d-sparse linear systems and O(√d log(2/ε)) queries for black-box unitaries, which is optimal at constant error, giving Θ(√N) for arbitrary N×N unitaries.

Conclusion: The paper achieves an optimal query complexity for simulating d-sparse Hamiltonians, removing the subpolynomial overhead of Low's algorithm. It resolves the open question on black-box unitary implementation by Berry and Childs, achieving Θ(√N) queries for N×N unitaries. The results extend to quantum linear systems and gate-efficient unitary implementations.

Abstract: We give a quantum algorithm for simulating a $d$-sparse Hermitian Hamiltonian $H$, assuming a known upper bound $Λ$ on its maximum column Euclidean norm $\|H\|_{1\to2}$. For $tΛ\ge1/2$, simulation with operator-norm error $ε$ uses \[
  O\!\left(tΛ\sqrt d+\sqrt d\log(2/ε)\right) \] sparse-oracle queries. This removes the subpolynomial overhead in Low's algorithm [STOC 2019], replacing it with an additive logarithmic precision term. For $d>1$ and $tΛ\ge\log(2/ε)$, the bound matches the worst-case lower bound. A known spectral-norm upper bound may also be used in place of $Λ$. The number of 1- and 2-qubit gates is linear in the query scale, up to oracle costs and polynomial overhead in the input bit lengths and logarithmic precision parameters.
  As applications, we obtain $O(κ\sqrt d\,\mathrm{polylog}(κ/ε))$ queries for solving $d$-sparse quantum linear systems with $\|A\|\le1$ and $\|A^{-1}\|\leκ$, under standard sparse and state-preparation access. We also give a gate-efficient implementation of black-box unitaries with at most $d$ nonzero entries per row and column using $O(\sqrt d\log(2/ε))$ queries, given sparse access to the unitary and its adjoint. At constant error, the query bound is optimal and yields $Θ(\sqrt N)$ queries for arbitrary $N\times N$ unitaries, resolving the open question on black-box unitary implementation posed by Berry and Childs [QIC 2012].

</details>


### [126] [Adaptivity is all you need: Optimal stabilizer learning using just single-copy measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02031)
*L. Bittel,J. Eisert,W. Gong,A. A. Mele,L. Schatzki*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Adaptive single-copy Clifford measurements learn any n-qubit stabilizer state from Θ(n) copies, matching Bell sampling and closing the adaptivity gap; the method also yields optimal testing tradeoffs and extends to low stabilizer nullity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stabilizer states underpin quantum error correction, benchmarking, and classical simulation, yet their learnability has a pronounced gap: two-copy Bell measurements achieve Θ(n) sample complexity, while non-adaptive single-copy measurements need Ω(n²). It is important to determine whether this gap is intrinsic to single-copy access or merely an artifact of non-adaptivity.

Method: The paper designs a polynomial-time adaptive algorithm that performs single-copy Clifford measurements. Adaptivity is used to coherently steer the measurement strategy across rounds, enabling efficient reconstruction of an arbitrary stabilizer state without multi-copy measurements. The same adaptive framework is applied to tolerant testing with limited quantum memory and generalized to states of bounded stabilizer nullity.

Result: The adaptive algorithm learns any n-qubit stabilizer state from Θ(n) single-copy Clifford measurements in polynomial time, matching the optimal Bell-sampling sample complexity without multi-copy measurements. It also gives a sample-optimal single-copy tolerant tester and, with k qubits of quantum memory, the optimal testing tradeoff Θ(n−k+1/ε) at infidelity ε. For states of stabilizer nullity at most r, including Clifford circuits with a bounded number of T gates, O(n2^r) single-copy measurements suffice.

Conclusion: Adaptivity completely closes the single-copy vs. two-copy sample-complexity gap for stabilizer states, yielding optimal learning and testing procedures under single-copy access. The results further show that the adaptive mechanism extends beyond exact stabilizer states to low-stabilizer-nullity states.

Abstract: Stabilizer states are central to quantum computing, underlying quantum error correction, benchmarking, and efficient classical simulation. Yet their learnability exhibits a striking gap: an $n$-qubit stabilizer state can be learned from $Θ(n)$ copies using two-copy Bell measurements, whereas non-adaptive single-copy measurements require $Ω(n^2)$ copies. Here we show that adaptivity completely closes this gap. We give a polynomial-time adaptive algorithm that learns an arbitrary $n$-qubit stabilizer state from $Θ(n)$ single-copy Clifford measurements, matching the optimal sample complexity of Bell sampling without any multi-copy measurements. The same ideas yield a sample-optimal single-copy tolerant tester and, with $k$ qubits of quantum memory, the optimal testing tradeoff $Θ(n-k+1/\varepsilon)$ at infidelity $\varepsilon$. Finally, we show that this adaptive mechanism extends beyond exact stabilizer states: states of stabilizer nullity at most $r$, including states prepared by Clifford circuits with a bounded number of $T$ gates, can be learned using $O(n2^{r})$ single-copy measurements.

</details>


### [127] [Nonlocality without entanglement for multipartite quantum measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02032)
*Satyaki Manna,Debashis Saha*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper establishes a measurement analogue of nonlocality without entanglement, showing product measurements can be globally distinguishable yet LOCC-indistinguishable, with a hierarchy of adaptive strategies and intrinsic directionality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonlocality without entanglement is well-known for quantum states, but the measurement counterpart is largely unexplored. The authors aim to show that this phenomenon can emerge in a much simpler setting for measurements than for states, and to characterize the structure of LOCC discrimination of measurements.

Method: The authors analyze bipartite product measurements under global versus LOCC discrimination, comparing adaptive and non-adaptive LOCC protocols and examining the role of which party initiates the protocol. They also generalize the construction to n-qubit product measurements that are globally but not bipartition-locally distinguishable, and propose an optical realization.

Result: A pair of two-qubit product measurements is perfectly distinguishable jointly but not under LOCC. A set of six bipartite qutrit product measurements shows adaptive LOCC strictly outperforming non-adaptive. LOCC distinguishability is intrinsically directional, and n-qubit constructions are globally distinguishable yet indistinguishable across any bipartition even with arbitrary joint operations. Quantum data hiding based on process nonlocality is realized with elementary optical components.

Conclusion: Measurement nonlocality without entanglement emerges in simple settings, exhibits adaptive and directional hierarchies, and supports quantum data hiding realizable with elementary optical components.

Abstract: We show that a pair of two-qubit product measurements can be perfectly distinguished when accessed jointly, yet cannot be perfectly distinguished when their local components are distributed between distant parties restricted to local quantum operations and classical communication (LOCC). This establishes a measurement analogue of nonlocality without entanglement that, unlike its state-based counterpart, emerges in a remarkably simple setting. We further uncover a hierarchy of LOCC discrimination strategies: adaptive protocols, in which the probe used at one site may depend on classical information obtained from another, can strictly outperform restricted non-adaptive protocols, as demonstrated by a set of six bipartite qutrit product measurements. We also show that LOCC distinguishability of bipartite measurements can be intrinsically directional, depending on which party initiates the protocol. More generally, we construct pairs of $n$-qubit product measurements that are globally perfectly distinguishable but cannot be perfectly distinguished across any bipartition, even when all parties on either side of the bipartition are allowed arbitrary joint quantum operations. Finally, we show how quantum data hiding based on quantum-process nonlocality without entanglement can be realized using elementary optical components.

</details>


### [128] [Complexity and Applications of Nearest Stabilizer Product State Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02037)
*Daniel Grier,Hakop Pashayan,Luke Schaeffer*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies maximizing the overlap between an n-qubit stabilizer state and single-qubit stabilizer product states, giving a complete complexity classification across 9 distinct sets S (after Clifford symmetry), showing all but the two simplest are NP-complete, with applications to simulation bounds, entanglement measures, and low-rank matrix completion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The nearest stabilizer product state problem appears in multiple computational and quantum-information contexts, yet its complexity was not fully understood. A systematic classification would clarify when such optimization is tractable versus intractable and connect it to practical settings like classical simulation and entanglement quantification.

Method: The authors formulate an optimization problem over stabilizer product states: given an n-qubit stabilizer state |ψ⟩ and a set S of single-qubit stabilizer states, maximize |⟨ψ|φ_1,...,φ_n⟩|^2 over |φ_i⟩ in S. They vary S and analyze the resulting problems, classifying them up to Clifford group symmetries, which reduce the distinct sets S to 9. They then prove complexity results for each case.

Result: Accounting for Clifford group symmetries, there are 9 distinct possible sets S. All but the two simplest of these sets yield NP-complete nearest stabilizer product state problems. Solutions to these problems yield tighter runtime bounds for certain classical simulation algorithms, measures of entanglement, and bear on the complexity of low-rank matrix completion.

Conclusion: The nearest stabilizer product state problem admits a complete complexity classification: after Clifford symmetry reduction, 9 cases exist and 7 are NP-complete, with only the two simplest tractable. This classification has practical implications for classical simulation, entanglement measures, and low-rank matrix completion.

Abstract: Consider the following optimization problem over stabilizer product states: given an $n$-qubit stabilizer state $\left|ψ\right\rangle$ and a set of single-qubit stabilizer states $S$, maximize $\left|\langle ψ| φ_1, \ldots, φ_n \rangle\right|^2$ over single-qubit stabilizer states $\left|φ_i\right\rangle \in S$. By varying the set $S$, we show that solutions to this problem can be useful in a variety of settings: tighter runtime bounds for certain classical simulation algorithms; measures of entanglement; and the complexity of low-rank matrix completion. Moreover, we give a complete complexity classification of this nearest stabilizer product state problem. After accounting for the symmetries in the Clifford group, there are $9$ distinct possible sets $S$, and we show that all but the two simplest of these are $\NP$-complete.

</details>


### [129] [Fermionic Gaussianity can be tested with mode-independent sample-complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02050)
*Maxwell West,Martin Larocca*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a general technique for upper-bounding the acceptance probability of 2-copy tests on states far from a target family, thereby bounding sample complexity. It proves that testing fermionic Gaussianity via Bell sampling is optimal at Θ(ε⁻²) copies, and extends the result to Slater determinant states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deciding whether an unknown quantum state belongs to or is far from a given family is a fundamental problem in quantum information theory. While natural 2-copy tests often always accept states within the family, bounding acceptance probability for far states—and thus the sample complexity—is typically much harder. The authors seek a general, simple framework to address this gap.

Method: The authors develop a new technique for upper-bounding the acceptance probability of general 2-copy tests when the state is far from the target family, which in turn upper-bounds the sample complexity of the decision problem. They apply this framework to the Bell sampling procedure for testing fermionic Gaussianity and to the analogous problem for Slater determinant states.

Result: The framework shows that deciding whether an unknown pure n-mode state is fermionic Gaussian or at least ε-far in trace distance from all Gaussian states requires the optimal Θ(ε⁻²) copies. The analysis proves Bell sampling is optimal, even against protocols using arbitrary collective and adaptive measurements. The analogous decision problem for Slater determinant states is also solvable with mode-independent sample complexity.

Conclusion: The proposed technique provides an effective general tool for bounding sample complexity of quantum state decision problems. It establishes the optimality of Bell sampling for fermionic Gaussianity testing and shows mode-independent sample complexity for Slater determinant testing.

Abstract: Deciding whether an unknown quantum state either belongs to, or is far from, a given family of states is a natural question of quantum information theory. In many such cases, there is a natural 2-copy test which always accepts whenever the state indeed belongs to the target family; it is often much more difficult, however, to bound the probability of acceptance for states that are far from the family. Here we develop a simple new technique for upper bounding this probability for general families, and therefore upper bounding the sample-complexity of the decision problem itself. As a particularly striking example of this framework, we show that deciding whether an unknown pure $n$-mode state is fermionic Gaussian, or at least $\varepsilon$-far in trace distance from all Gaussian states, can be accomplished using the optimal number $Θ(\varepsilon^{-2})$ of copies. Remarkably, our analysis applies to the well-known Bell sampling procedure for testing fermionic Gaussianity, which we therefore show to be optimal, even compared to protocols making arbitrary collective and adaptive measurements. As a second example, we show that the analogous decision problem for the family of Slater determinant states can also be solved with mode-independent sample-complexity.

</details>


### [130] [Sequential Capacity of Quantum Processes with Finite Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02068)
*Yibin Wang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces "sequential response capacity" to quantify how many adaptive testing stages can distinguish quantum processes via a fixed-gap response probability. For fixed system/memory sizes, capacity scales as K log K with run length K and decreases with probability resolution; a single visible qubit with time-dependent phase rotations achieves this without extra memory, while fixed-basis classical processes scale only linearly. Under stored-label phase sequences, residual phase-flip noise limits the logarithmic enhancement, with the inverse residual phase-flip probability acting as the coherence timescale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum devices run for longer durations with fixed internal memory, raising the question of how complex their responses can become and how this complexity compares with classical stochastic processes under the same testing framework.

Method: The authors define sequential response capacity as the number of adaptive testing stages (each using a fresh run) that can separate possible processes by a prescribed gap in response probabilities. They derive tight scaling laws between capacity, run length, and probability resolution, construct a scheme using time-dependent phase rotations on one visible qubit with no added memory, and analyze the effect of known independent Pauli noise for phase sequences selected by a stored classical label, proving matching bounds under ideal controls and weak residual phase noise.

Result: At fixed resolution, capacity grows on the order of K log K, where K is the number of timesteps per run; the construction attains this growth with tests yielding response probabilities exactly zero or one. Classical stochastic processes measuring in a fixed basis each step have only linear capacity at fixed sizes and resolution. With residual phase noise, the inverse residual phase-flip probability sets the coherence timescale limiting the extra logarithmic growth, and matching capacity bounds hold at a fixed small probability gap.

Conclusion: Quantum devices with fixed memory can exhibit a logarithmic enhancement in sequential response capacity over classical fixed-basis processes, but this enhancement is bounded by residual phase-flip noise, which defines the relevant coherence timescale.

Abstract: How complex can the responses of a quantum device become as it runs longer with a fixed internal memory? We quantify this complexity through sequential response capacity: how many adaptive testing stages, each using a fresh run, can continue to separate possible processes by a prescribed gap in response probabilities. For fixed system and memory sizes, we establish a tight law relating this capacity to run length and probability resolution. At fixed resolution, the capacity grows on the order of $K\log K$, where $K$ is the number of time steps in each run. Our construction attains this growth using time-dependent phase rotations on a single visible qubit with no additional internal memory; its tests give response probabilities exactly zero or one. Under the same tests, classical stochastic processes that measure in a fixed basis at every step have only linear capacity at fixed sizes and resolution. For phase sequences selected by a stored classical label, we then quantify how known independent Pauli noise changes this logarithmic enhancement. With ideal controls and weak residual phase noise after correction, we prove matching capacity bounds at a fixed small probability gap. These bounds identify the inverse residual phase-flip probability as the coherence timescale that limits the extra logarithmic growth.

</details>


### [131] [Universal Bound and Phase Transition in Many-Body Fermionic Non-Gaussianity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02075)
*Masahiro Hoshino,Ryota Matsuda,Yuto Ashida*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper bounds and characterizes fermionic non-Gaussianity via magic Rényi entropy (MRE), showing Haar-random states saturate an MRE density of ln(4/3) per Majorana, and reveals a first-order transition in MRE growth driven by distinct imaginary- and real-time evolution under the SYK Hamiltonian.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fermionic non-Gaussianity is a resource for universal quantum computation, and interactions in quantum many-body systems can generate it. A quantitative understanding of how much non-Gaussianity typical states possess and how it dynamically grows is needed, which motivates using MRE as a measure.

Method: The authors use magic Rényi entropy (MRE) as a non-Gaussianity measure. They derive a universal upper bound and Haar mean for MRE, and evolve a Gaussian state in both imaginary and real time under the Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK) Hamiltonian, tuning the durations.

Result: Typical Haar-random states attain the maximal MRE density of ln(4/3) per Majorana in the large-system limit. MRE can differ extensively between states with identical covariance matrices. Varying imaginary- and real-time durations yields a first-order transition marked by a kink in MRE density and spontaneous breaking of permutation symmetry among the four copies used in MRE evaluation.

Conclusion: This transition represents a qualitative change in the state's non-Gaussianity that is not reflected in the thermal free energy, distinguishing non-Gaussianity structure from thermodynamic signatures.

Abstract: Fermionic non-Gaussianity is a resource for universal quantum computation that can be generated by interactions in quantum many-body systems. Using the magic Rényi entropy (MRE) as a measure of non-Gaussianity, we derive its universal upper bound and Haar mean, proving that typical Haar-random states attain the maximal MRE density of $\ln(4/3)$ per Majorana in the large-system limit. We also show that the MRE can differ extensively between states with identical covariance matrices. To investigate how non-Gaussianity grows toward this maximal density, we evolve a Gaussian state in imaginary and real time under the Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev Hamiltonian. By varying the imaginary- and real-time durations, we identify a first-order transition marked by a kink in the MRE density and spontaneous breaking of the permutation symmetry among the four copies used to evaluate the MRE. This transition represents a qualitative change in the non-Gaussianity of the state that is not reflected in the thermal free energy.

</details>


### [132] [A computational phase diagram for the transverse field Ising model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02079)
*Thuy-Duong Vuong*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies the transverse field Ising model and establishes a sharp threshold based on the condition Δ(J)·tanh(βη)/η ≤ 1, under which the partition function and Gibbs state observables can be efficiently approximated classically, while beyond this threshold even exponential-factor approximation is NP-hard.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding when quantum many-body systems, specifically the transverse field Ising model, admit efficient classical simulation is a central question. The paper aims to identify a precise regime—expressed via the spectral width Δ(J) and parameters β, η—that separates classically tractable from computationally hard instances, thereby delineating the boundary of efficient classical approximation for partition functions and Gibbs state observables.

Method: For the Hamiltonian H = ½∑ J_ij Z_i Z_j + ∑ h_i^z Z_i + η∑ X_i, the authors design randomized classical algorithms. In the regime Δ(J)·tanh(βη)/η ≤ 1, they approximate the partition function Z(β)=Tr(e^{-βH}) to relative error ε in time polynomial in n, β, model parameters, and ε^{-1}. They also provide an algorithm approximating Pauli string observables of the Gibbs state ρ_β within arbitrarily small additive error; for observables diagonal in the X-basis (P ∈ {I,X}^{⊗n}), relative error is achieved.

Result: When Δ(J)·tanh(βη)/η ≤ 1, the partition function can be approximated to relative error ε in polynomial time. When Δ(J)·tanh(βη)/η > 1, approximating Z(β) within an exp(o(n))-multiplicative factor is NP-hard, implying no efficient classical or quantum algorithm is likely under standard assumptions. Additionally, in the tractable regime, Pauli string observables of the Gibbs state are efficiently approximable with additive error, and with relative error when diagonal in the X-basis.

Conclusion: The condition Δ(J)·tanh(βη)/η ≤ 1 serves as a sharp threshold for the classical approximability of the transverse field Ising model's partition function and Gibbs state observables. Below the threshold, efficient randomized classical algorithms exist; above it, the problem becomes NP-hard, precluding efficient quantum or classical solutions under standard complexity assumptions.

Abstract: We study the transverse field Ising model, defined by the Hamiltonian $H =\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i, j\in [n]} J_{ij} Z_i Z_j +\sum_{i=1}^n h_i^z Z_i + η\sum_{i} X_i$ where $J $ is the symmetric interaction matrix, and $η$ is the transverse field strength. Let $Δ(J)=λ_{\max}(J)-λ_{\min}(J)$ be the spectral width of $J.$ When the inverse temperature $β\geq0$ satisfies $Δ(J)\cdot\frac{\tanh(βη)}η\leq1$, we give a randomized classical algorithm that approximates the partition function $Z(β)=\operatorname{Tr}(e^{-βH})$ to a given relative error $ε\in(0,1)$ in time polynomial in $n$, $β$, the model parameters, and $ε^{-1}$. When $ Δ(J) \cdot \frac{\tanh(βη)}η > 1 ,$ we show that approximating $ Z(β)$ within an $\exp(o(n))$-multiplicative factor is $\textbf{NP}$-hard, and thus unlikely to admit an efficient classical or quantum algorithms under standard complexity theoretic assumptions. Furthermore, in the regime $Δ(J)\cdot \frac{\tanh(βη)}η\leq 1,$ we provide an efficient randomized classical algorithm that approximates Pauli string observables of the Gibbs state $ ρ_β= \frac{e^{-βH}}{\operatorname{Tr}(e^{-βH})}$ within an arbitrarily small additive error. In the special case when the observable is also diagonal in the $X$-basis, i.e. $P \in \{I, X\}^{\otimes n}$, the algorithm further achieves arbitrarily small relative error.

</details>


### [133] [Strong Simulation of 1D Quantum Circuits via Reduced Transition Matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02082)
*Matilde Grassi,Stefano Carignano,Luca Tagliacozzo,Jacopo De Nardis*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Sweeping RTM algorithm, a transverse tensor-network contraction using reduced transition matrices, to approximately strong-simulate specific output probabilities of 1D chaotic brick-wall circuits at finite relative precision, finding subexponential bond-dimension growth over accessible times.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spatial entanglement growth severely limits direct evolution of pure states under chaotic unitary circuits, making full tensor-network simulation impractical. A more targeted task—approximate strong simulation of a specified output probability at finite relative precision—may circumvent this entanglement bottleneck.

Method: Given input and output bit strings y and x, evaluate p(x|y)=|<x|U(T)|y>|^2 via the Sweeping RTM algorithm, a transverse tensor-network contraction based on reduced transition matrices (RTMs). The algorithm jointly compresses left and right temporal boundary states, seeking a probability that converges across spatial cuts and as bond dimension increases.

Result: For chaotic 1D brick-wall circuits at fixed relative target precision, numerical evidence shows the bond dimension required for stable estimates grows subexponentially over the accessible time window.

Conclusion: The findings open a direct route to classical probability queries for chaotic quantum circuits, with potential applications to benchmarking and learning tasks.

Abstract: Tensor networks are powerful tools for simulating quantum many-body systems, but the growth of spatial entanglement severely limits the direct evolution of pure states under chaotic unitary circuits. Here we consider a more targeted task: the approximate strong simulation of a specified output probability of a 1D chaotic brick-wall circuit at finite relative precision. Given input and output bit strings $\by$ and $\bx$, we evaluate $p(\bx|\by)=|\bra{\bx}U(T)\ket{\by}|^2$ using the Sweeping RTM algorithm, a transverse tensor-network contraction based on reduced transition matrices (RTMs). The algorithm compresses the left and right temporal boundary states jointly, seeking an output probability that converges across spatial cuts and as the bond dimension is increased. For chaotic one-dimensional brick-wall circuits at a fixed relative target precision, we find numerical evidence that the bond dimension required to obtain stable estimates grows subexponentially over the accessible time window. Our findings open a direct route to classical probability queries for chaotic quantum circuits, with potential applications to benchmarking and learning tasks.

</details>


### [134] [Stationarity as a One-Mode Constraint on Quantum Correlation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02090)
*Itai Panas*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Stationary quantum states exhibit a single dynamical phase and scalar energy even with vast internal Hilbert-space response. This work argues for the "one-ness" of stationary evolution (not absolute phase value). Using regularized Hartree-Fock (reg-HF), it derives a Gaussian relative-coordinate measure that is simultaneously minimum-Fisher-information and maximum-entropy, introduces a one-mode exclusion axiom, and tests it on helium, reproducing He-like coefficients and energies while showing spectral complexity is separable from one-mode energetic sufficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A stationary quantum state has only one dynamical phase factor and one scalar energy, yet its internal response may span a large Hilbert space. The paper seeks to explain how this "one-ness" of stationary evolution arises, distinguishing the argument from questions about the value or observability of an absolute phase.

Method: In regularized Hartree-Fock (reg-HF), the connection to the stationary mean field is made before any one-mode reduction. The primitive-product relative-coordinate Gaussian measure induced by the Gaussian HF representation fixes a covariance at which the distribution is simultaneously minimum-Fisher-information, maximum-Shannon-entropy, and the equality case of the Stam bound. A compatible Gaussian scale resolution of the Coulomb operator and virial completion convert the regularized pair displacement into a stationary-compatible response. Stationarity motivates a one-mode constraint, formalized as a one-mode exclusion axiom: covariance from response components orthogonal to the retained pair mode is excluded from the terminal scalar energy property, without claiming the unrestricted response is one-dimensional.

Result: Helium is the minimal nontrivial test. The parameter-free retained Coulomb carrier $-δ/e$ and finite-$Z$ insertion $8δ/9$ closely reproduce independent He-like coefficients and energies. An explicit Bethe-Goldstone calculation shows a broad non-single-denominator return spectrum despite its scalar energy being exactly moment-compressible.

Conclusion: The results separate microscopic spectral complexity from one-mode energetic sufficiency: a stationary state's scalar energy can be captured by a single mode even when the underlying return spectrum is spectrally complex.

Abstract: A stationary quantum state has one dynamical phase factor and one scalar energy even when its internal response spans a large Hilbert space. Our argument concerns not the value or observability of an absolute phase, but the one-ness of stationary time evolution. In regularized Hartree--Fock (reg-HF), the connection to the stationary mean field is established before any one-mode reduction: the primitive-product relative-coordinate Gaussian measure induced by the Gaussian HF representation fixes a covariance and, at that covariance, is simultaneously the minimum-Fisher-information and maximum-Shannon-differential-entropy distribution and the equality case of the Stam bound. The Coulomb operator admits a compatible Gaussian scale resolution, and virial completion converts the resulting regularized pair displacement into a stationary-compatible response. Stationarity motivates a one-mode constraint on the terminal scalar correlation response. The decisive reg-HF hypothesis is a one-mode exclusion axiom: covariance carried by response components orthogonal to the retained pair mode is excluded from the terminal scalar energy property, without asserting that the unrestricted response is one dimensional. Helium provides the minimal nontrivial test. The parameter-free retained Coulomb carrier $-δ/e$ and finite-$Z$ insertion $8δ/9$ closely reproduce independent He-like coefficients and energies, while an explicit Bethe--Goldstone calculation displays a broad non-single-denominator return spectrum even though its scalar energy is exactly moment-compressible. The result separates microscopic spectral complexity from one-mode energetic sufficiency.

</details>


### [135] [One-Way Quantum Symmetric Private Information Retrieval Protocol From A Single Database Server Using NISQ Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02093)
*Xiang Zou,H. F. Chau*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Symmetric private information retrieval (SPIR) is impossible with a single server for unrestricted quantum adversaries, but becomes possible with information-theoretic security when both server and user are limited to NISQ devices that lack long-term quantum memory and measure each qubit on arrival, even though the eavesdropper remains all-powerful.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single-server SPIR is impossible for parties with unrestricted quantum power. The authors seek a feasible, information-theoretically secure SPIR protocol under a stricter memory restriction (the bounded/noisy-quantum-storage models) where only the legitimate server and user are NISQ-limited while the eavesdropper is all-powerful.

Method: The server one-way sends BB84 states; the user performs unambiguous discrimination and learns a random subset of bits. After a joint random permutation into blocks, the user announces the conclusive locations of one privately chosen block. The server hashes and takes a random parity of those locations in every block; the permutation makes it exponentially unlikely that one location set fits two blocks. Security is proven for secrecy against the eavesdropper, database privacy via an explicit size rule for the announced set, and user privacy against an honest server preparing the prescribed BB84 states. The only hardware addition to BB84 equipment is a passive linear-optical measurement with one ancillary mode.

Result: For a 10^4-bit database and database privacy 10^-6, blocks of about a thousand qubits suffice over a tolerated noise channel for a user applying the prescribed measurement. Certifying an arbitrary memoryless user and committing to the permutation require larger auxiliary transmissions, quantified with finite keys. Database privacy extends to decoy-state weak coherent pulses, and simulations show the bound is nearly tight.

Conclusion: Information-theoretic single-server SPIR is achievable under a NISQ device memory restriction with an all-powerful eavesdropper, using essentially standard BB84 equipment plus a passive linear-optical measurement. The scheme provides proven secrecy, database privacy, and user privacy, with finite-key quantification, decoy-state extension, and near-tight performance bounds.

Abstract: Symmetric private information retrieval (SPIR) lets an inquirer obtain one specific bit only, without leaking any information about its index to the database owner. With a single server, this is impossible for parties with unrestricted quantum power. We show that it becomes possible, with information-theoretic security, when the server and the user hold only noisy intermediate-scale quantum (NISQ) devices without long-term quantum memory and measure each qubit on arrival, while the eavesdropper remains all-powerful; this is a stricter form of the memory restriction of the bounded- and noisy-quantum-storage models. The server sends BB84 states one way; the user discriminates them unambiguously and so knows a random subset of the bits. After a joint random permutation into blocks, the user announces the conclusive locations of one privately chosen block, and the server hashes and takes a random parity of these locations in every block; the permutation makes it exponentially unlikely that one set of locations fits two blocks. We prove secrecy against the eavesdropper, database privacy through an explicit size rule for the announced set, and user privacy against a server who prepares the prescribed BB84 states and announces truthfully, exactly for single photons and up to a small multi-photon term over a tolerated noise channel. The only addition to BB84 equipment is a passive linear-optical measurement with one ancillary mode. For a $10^{4}$-bit database and database privacy $10^{-6}$, blocks of about a thousand qubits suffice over a tolerated noise channel for a user who applies the prescribed measurement; certifying an arbitrary memoryless user and committing to the permutation need larger auxiliary transmissions, which we quantify with finite keys. Database privacy extends to decoy-state weak coherent pulses, and simulations show that the bound is nearly tight.

</details>


### [136] [Quantum state preparation for weighted d-DNNF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02094)
*Steef Hegeman,Joon Hyung Lee,Alfons Laarman*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Weighted d-DNNF state descriptions admit quantum state preparation circuits computable in linear time (up to complex arithmetic).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The quantum state preparation problem—efficiently generating a quantum circuit that computes a given quantum state from its description—is fundamental, and identifying classical description classes that permit efficient preparation is of both theoretical and practical interest.

Method: The authors consider quantum states described by weighted d-DNNF (deterministic, decomposable pseudo-Boolean circuits) and construct a quantum circuit computing the state from this description, analyzing its time complexity.

Result: For quantum states described by weighted d-DNNF, a quantum circuit computing the state can be obtained in linear time, up to complex arithmetic.

Conclusion: Weighted d-DNNF provides a class of state descriptions for which quantum state preparation is efficient, achieving linear-time construction up to complex arithmetic.

Abstract: The quantum state preparation problem is to, given a description of a quantum state, efficiently generate a quantum circuit computing the state. We show that for quantum states described by weighted d-DNNF (deterministic, decomposable pseudo-Boolean circuits) a quantum circuit computing the state can be obtained in linear time up to complex arithmetic.

</details>


### [137] [A No-Go Theorem for Order-Two Clifford Electric-Magnetic Duality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02097)
*Shunta Takahashi,Zhi Li,Beni Yoshida*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies whether the emergent order-two electromagnetic duality of toric codes can be realized microscopically as an order-two local Clifford operation, proving it is impossible for Z2 and even-N ZN toric codes but possible for odd N.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Electromagnetic duality in the toric code is order two on anyon types and acts as a Hadamard-type logical transformation on encoded quantum information. The authors ask whether this order-two structure can also be realized microscopically as an order-two locality-preserving Clifford operation, i.e., whether the group law of the emergent duality lifts faithfully to its microscopic realization.

Method: For the Z2 toric code, the authors give a general proof that any locality-preserving Clifford realization of electric-magnetic exchange cannot have order two. The proof is fully general: it assumes neither translation symmetry with restricted system sizes nor a fixed pairing between vertex and plaquette stabilizers. Geometrically, it locally emulates the unavoidable crossing between electric and magnetic strings, akin to a cross-cap in a non-orientable manifold. The problem is then extended to the ZN toric code, where the existence of an order-two Clifford realization is analyzed as a function of N.
