Result: For the Z2 toric code, no locality-preserving Clifford realization of electric-magnetic exchange can have order two. For the ZN toric code, an order-two Clifford realization exists for odd N, whereas it is impossible for even N.

Conclusion: The group law of an emergent electromagnetic duality need not lift faithfully to its microscopic Clifford realization. The order-two duality can fail to be realizable as an order-two Clifford operation microscopically, with the obstruction depending on N in the ZN toric code.

Abstract: Electromagnetic duality in the toric code has order two at the level of anyon types and acts as a Hadamard-type logical transformation on the encoded quantum information. Here, we ask whether it can likewise be realized microscopically as an order-two Clifford operation. For the $\mathbb{Z}_2$ toric code, we prove that any locality-preserving Clifford realization of electric-magnetic exchange cannot have order two. Our proof is fully general and requires neither translation symmetry with restricted families of system sizes nor a fixed pairing between vertex and plaquette stabilizers. Geometrically, the proof locally emulates the unavoidable crossing between electric and magnetic strings, similar to a cross-cap in a non-orientable manifold. Extending the problem to the $\mathbb{Z}_N$ toric code, we find that an order-two Clifford realization exists for odd $N$, whereas it is impossible for even $N$. Our results show that the group law of an emergent electromagnetic duality need not lift faithfully to its microscopic Clifford realization.

</details>


### [138] [An exponential separation between entanglement-assisted and unassisted one-way quantum communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02099)
*Ryan Anselm,Srijita Kundu,Olivier Lalonde,Ashwin Nayak*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper resolves a longstanding open question in quantum communication complexity by giving the first exponential separation for a total Boolean function, showing an O(log n) vs Ω(n^{1/3}) gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A central open question in quantum communication complexity asks whether a task can be cheap in the presence of prior entanglement but much more expensive without it. Such separations were known for relational problems and, in the SMP model, for partial functions, but it remained unresolved whether any separation exists for a total Boolean function.

Method: The authors exhibit a family of total Boolean functions f_n mapping {0,1}^n × {0,1}^n to {0,1}, constructed as a special case of the subgroup membership problem (first studied in the communication setting by Aaronson, Le Gall, Russell, and Tani), and analyze its one-way communication complexity with and without prior entanglement.

Result: The function family can be computed with O(log n) bits of one-way classical communication given prior entanglement, but requires Ω(n^{1/3}) qubits of one-way quantum communication without entanglement—an exponential separation in the one-way setting.

Conclusion: This provides the first exponential separation between the entanglement-assisted and entanglement-free models for a total Boolean function, resolving the longstanding question.

Abstract: A longstanding question in quantum communication complexity is whether some task can be accomplished with a small amount of communication in the presence of entanglement, yet require much more quantum communication in the absence of entanglement. Separations of this nature were previously known for relational problems and, in the simultaneous message passing model, for partial functions. But it has remained unresolved whether any such separation exists for a total Boolean function. We resolve this question with an exponential separation in the one-way setting: we exhibit a family of total Boolean functions $f_n\colon \{0,1\}^n \times \{0,1\}^n \to \{0,1\}$ that can be computed with $O(\log n)$ bits of one-way classical communication given prior entanglement, but that require $Ω(n^{1/3})$ qubits of one-way quantum communication without entanglement. Our function is a special case of the subgroup membership problem, first studied in the communication setting by Aaronson, Le Gall, Russell, and Tani.

</details>


### [139] [On the pseudorandomness of simple quantum processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02100)
*Jesko Dujmovic,Jonas Haferkamp,Alexander Poremba*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work studies the unitary quantum analog of Gowers' conjecture on random reversible operations. It shows that statistical moment matching (unitary designs) does not generically imply pseudorandomness, refuting the unitary analog of the Hoory–Magen–Myers–Rackoff conjecture, and proposes new conjectures for how pseudorandomness can emerge in simple quantum circuits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gowers conjectured that repeatedly composing local random reversible operations can produce global permutations indistinguishable from random. The paper aims to make progress on this longstanding conjecture by studying its unitary quantum analog, and to understand whether unitary designs (moment matching) suffice for pseudorandomness.

Method: Constructs efficiently samplable families of distributions over one- and two-qubit gates, analyzes resulting n-qubit ensembles via statistical moment matching (unitary t-designs) versus query-complexity-based pseudorandomness, compares local random walks against highly structured ensembles, and tests these against efficient quantum distinguishing algorithms.

Result: For every fixed t, there is an efficiently samplable family of one- and two-qubit gate distributions such that after T=O_t(n^2 log^2 n) independent steps the ensemble is an approximate unitary t-design with negligible error exp(-Ω(log^2 n)), yet an efficient quantum algorithm distinguishes it from random with only O_t(log^2 n) queries. This refutes the unitary analog of the Hoory–Magen–Myers–Rackoff conjecture. A stronger separation between unitary designs and pseudorandom unitaries is also obtained at polynomially bounded moments, though requiring highly structured ensembles.

Conclusion: Statistical moment matching (unitary designs) does not generically lead to pseudorandomness, even for simple local quantum processes. Caution is advised when using unitary designs to model information scrambling in black-hole physics, since even maximally scrambled systems can retain structure accessible to efficient experiments. New conjectures are proposed for how pseudorandomness can plausibly emerge within simple quantum processes such as random quantum circuits.

Abstract: Can simple processes appear highly complex? Gowers (Comb. Prob. Comp. '96) conjectured that repeatedly composing local random reversible operations can yield global permutations that are indistinguishable from random. In this work, we study the unitary quantum analog of this question, in an attempt to make new progress on this longstanding conjecture.
  Our first result shows that statistical moment matching in the form of unitary designs does not generically lead to pseudorandomness---even for the simplest quantum processes: for every fixed $t$, we give an efficiently samplable family $\{ν_n\}_n$ of distributions on one- and two-qubit gates such that, after $T=O_t(n^2\log^2 n)$ independent steps, the resulting $n$-qubit ensemble is an approximate unitary $t$-design with negligible error $\exp(-Ω(\log^2 n))$, yet an efficient quantum algorithm distinguishes it from random using only $O_t(\log^2 n)$ queries. This refutes the unitary analog of the Hoory--Magen--Myers--Rackoff conjecture (ICALP '04) for permutations.
  Our second result is a stronger separation between unitary designs and pseudorandom unitaries at polynomially bounded moments; our counterexample, however, requires highly structured ensembles, in contrast with the simple local walks from before. This suggests caution when using unitary designs to model information scrambling in black-hole physics, as even maximally scrambled systems can exhibit structure which is accessible to efficient experiments. Motivated by these findings, we then propose new conjectures for how pseudorandomness can plausibly emerge within simple quantum processes, such as random quantum circuits.

</details>


### [140] [Time-space lower bounds for breaking quantum cryptography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02101)
*Fangqi Dong,Alex Lombardi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proves near-optimal time-space lower bounds for breaking quantum cryptography in the random oracle model, showing a new advantage of quantum over classical cryptography in resisting preprocessing attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish tight time-space lower bounds for breaking quantum cryptography (specifically, recovering a random key from binary phase states) in the random oracle model, and to demonstrate a security advantage of quantum cryptography over post-quantum (classical) one-way functions in the presence of preprocessing attacks.

Method: Express the optimal preprocessing attack as the operator norm of a random matrix; bound this in expectation over the random oracle using the trace-moment method. Interpret trace moments via compressed oracles (Zhandry, Crypto 2019) for analysis, generalizing and simplifying Liu's (Eurocrypt 2023) approach.

Result: A $T$-query adversary with $S$ qubits of non-uniform advice recovers a random key from an $n$-qubit binary phase state with probability at most $O((T^2 + \sqrt{ST})/N)$ for $N=2^n$, versus $O((T^2 + ST)/N)$ for post-quantum one-way functions (whose bound is trivial at $S=N$). Thus $n$ qubits of communication secure against preprocessing up to space $N^2$. Additional results: (1) tightened QROM bound for post-quantum PRGs of $O(T^2/N + \sqrt{ST/N})$; (2) extended Lombardi-Ma-Wright (STOC 2024) one-query lower bound for unitary synthesis to adversaries with one arbitrary function query plus polynomially many adaptive random oracle queries; (3) tight $O(\sqrt{S}/N)$ bound for pseudorandomness of random binary phase states against space-$S$ distinguishers.

Conclusion: Quantum cryptography with $n$-qubit binary phase states achieves security against preprocessing attacks with space up to $N^2$, outperforming classical/post-quantum security (which degrades trivially at $S=N$). The compressed-oracle trace-moment framework yields a simple, general technique that tightens prior bounds (Liu's QROM PRG analysis) and reinterprets and extends existing results (LMW24 unitary synthesis lower bound).

Abstract: We prove near-optimal time-space lower bounds for breaking quantum cryptography in the random oracle model. Specifically, we show that a $T$-query adversary with $S$ qubits of non-uniform advice can recover a random key $k$ from the $n$-qubit binary phase state $|ψ_k\rangle \propto \sum_{x} R(k,x) |x\rangle$ with probability at most $O(\frac{T^2 + \sqrt{ST}}{N})$ for $N=2^n$. In contrast, the best known bound for post-quantum one-way functions is $O(\frac{T^2 + ST}{N})$, with a trivial attack at $S = N$. This demonstrates a new advantage of quantum cryptography over classical cryptography: $n$ qubits of communication suffice for security against preprocessing attacks with space up to $N^2$ rather than $N$. Our methodology is simple: express the optimal preprocessing attack as the operator norm of a random matrix, and bound this value in expectation over the random oracle via the trace-moment method. These trace moments have a natural interpretation using compressed oracles [Zhandry, Crypto 2019], which we then analyze. This can be viewed as a simplification and generalization of the approach of Liu [Eurocrypt 2023] for proving time-space tradeoffs for breaking post-quantum cryptography. We also prove the following results: (1) We tighten Liu's analysis of post-quantum PRGs in QROM, achieving a distinguishing advantage bound of $O(\frac{T^2}N + \sqrt{\frac{ST}N})$. (2) For unitary synthesis, we extend the one-query lower bound of Lombardi-Ma-Wright [STOC 2024] to hold against adversaries that can make one arbitrary function query along with polynomially many (adaptive) queries to the random oracle, either before or after the function query. This also interprets the original LMW24 result in terms of compressed oracles. (3) Finally, we prove a tight $O(\frac{\sqrt{S}}N)$ bound for the pseudorandomness of random binary phase states against space $S$ distinguishers.

</details>


### [141] [Unitary Schur Sampling of Qudits via Random SWAP Tests: Hunt for Antisymmetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02103)
*Shrigyan Brahmachari,David Jakab,Henry D. Pfister,Iman Marvian*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Unitary Schur sampling of arbitrary permutation-invariant mixed states on n qudits can be implemented using only pairwise SWAP tests, achieving error ε in diamond distance with O(n·min{n,d}³·log²(n/ε)) random SWAP tests, by repeatedly extracting the largest antisymmetric subsystem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Schur sampling is a fundamental primitive for extracting permutation-invariant information from many-body quantum systems, with broad applications in quantum information science. Existing circuit-based implementations typically rely on coherent representation-theoretic operations (Clebsch-Gordan transforms, generalized phase estimation, or quantum Fourier transforms over the symmetric group), which are complex and resource-intensive. A simpler, measurement-based approach is desirable.

Method: The protocol replaces coherent representation-theoretic operations with only pairwise SWAP tests, i.e., two-qudit projective measurements onto the symmetric and antisymmetric subspaces. The central idea is to repeatedly identify and extract the largest antisymmetric subsystem, turning unitary Schur sampling into a search for antisymmetry and giving the algorithm a natural interpretation as a stochastic traversal of a Young diagram. The protocol preserves the SU(d)-irrep state while preparing a canonical pure state in the multiplicity subsystem.

Result: The protocol achieves error ε in diamond distance using O(n·min{n,d}³·log²(n/ε)) random SWAP tests. As an application to quantum purity amplification (QPA), the optimal purified state can be obtained by retaining a single designated output qudit and discarding the rest.

Conclusion: Unitary Schur sampling can be realized solely through pairwise SWAP tests, drastically simplifying its implementation compared to coherent representation-theoretic approaches. This antisymmetry-search formulation, interpretable as a stochastic traversal of a Young diagram, also yields an efficient strategy for quantum purity amplification.

Abstract: Schur sampling is a fundamental primitive for extracting permutation-invariant information from many-body quantum systems and has broad applications in quantum information science. Circuit-based implementations typically rely on coherent representation-theoretic operations such as Clebsch-Gordan transforms, generalized phase estimation, or quantum Fourier transforms over the symmetric group. We show that unitary Schur sampling of arbitrary permutation-invariant mixed states on $n$ $d$-dimensional qudits can instead be implemented using only pairwise SWAP tests, namely, two-qudit projective measurements onto the symmetric and antisymmetric subspaces. Our protocol achieves error $ε$ in diamond distance using $O(n\min\{n,d\}^{3}\log^2(n/ε))$ random SWAP tests. The central idea is to repeatedly identify and extract the largest antisymmetric subsystem. This turns unitary Schur sampling into a search for antisymmetry and gives the algorithm a natural interpretation as a stochastic traversal of a Young diagram. The protocol preserves the $\mathrm{SU}(d)$-irrep state while preparing a canonical pure state in the multiplicity subsystem. As an application, we consider quantum purity amplification (QPA), in which multiple noisy copies of a pure quantum state are combined to produce a state of higher purity. We show that the optimal purified state can be obtained by retaining a single designated output qudit and discarding the rest.

</details>


### [142] [Quantum Advantage for Two-Party Differential Privacy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02113)
*Daniel Alabi,Emil T. Khabiboulline*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces information-theoretically private quantum protocols for two-party Hamming distance estimation where both parties output the same estimate, achieving O(1) error (matching classical computational security) via O(n)-communication quantum protocols under quantum differential privacy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classically, information-theoretic protocols for two-party Hamming distance require Ω(√n) error under pure differential privacy and Ω(√n/log n) under strong approximate differential privacy, while O(1) error is only achievable under computational assumptions. The authors aim to close this accuracy gap information-theoretically by exploiting quantum communication.

Method: Under Klauck's honest, nonpreemptive, message-preserving model, the authors construct an O(n)-communication quantum protocol using a guarded coherent round trip and an equal-Gram rigidity principle that prevents an honest player from retaining input-dependent complementary information. They analyze pure ε quantum differential privacy (QDP) and approximate (ε, δ) QDP via exact hockey-stick divergence calculations.

Result: The protocol achieves pure ε QDP with expected error at most 2/sinh ε + γ for every γ > 0. For approximate (ε, δ) QDP, the hockey-stick divergence calculation yields strictly smaller error while preserving the O(1) versus Ω(√n/log n) separation for δ = o(1/n). Thus quantum communication attains O(1) information-theoretic error, matching classical accuracy achievable only under computational assumptions.

Conclusion: The authors separate their model from weaker prescribed-channel privacy (which admits an exact classical realization) and from fully retention-robust security (against which measurement-and-abort attacks remain possible). They identify the preservation of non-orthogonal quantum messages as a privacy resource.

Abstract: We introduce information-theoretically private quantum protocols for two-party Hamming distance when both parties must output the same estimate. Classically, for input length $n$, information-theoretic protocols require $Ω(\sqrt{n})$ error under pure differential privacy and $Ω(\sqrt{n}/\log n)$ error under strong approximate differential privacy, whereas computational security permits $O(1)$ error. In Klauck's honest, nonpreemptive, message-preserving model, we give an $O(n)$-communication quantum protocol with pure $\varepsilon$ quantum differential privacy (QDP) and expected error at most $\frac{2}{\sinh \varepsilon}+γ$, for every $γ>0$. For approximate $(\varepsilon, δ)$ QDP, an exact hockey-stick divergence calculation yields strictly smaller error, while preserving the $O(1)$-versus-$Ω(\sqrt{n}/\log n)$ separation for $δ=o(1/n)$. Thus, quantum communication achieves $O(1)$ information-theoretic error, matching the accuracy available classically only under computational assumptions.
  The main construction uses a guarded coherent round trip and an equal-Gram rigidity principle that prevents an honest player from retaining input-dependent complementary information. We separate this model from weaker prescribed-channel privacy, which already admits an exact classical realization, and from fully retention-robust security, against which measurement-and-abort attacks remain possible. Therefore, we identify preservation of non-orthogonal quantum messages as a resource for privacy.

</details>


### [143] [Local random quantum circuits converge to the Porter-Thomas distribution in polynomial depth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02125)
*Aniruddha Sen,Nicholas Hunter-Jones*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Polynomial-depth brickwork random circuits converge inverse-polynomially in total variation distance to the Porter-Thomas distribution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Porter-Thomas statistics characterize the output of random quantum states and chaotic many-body systems, and are central to random circuit sampling and quantum advantage experiments. Low-depth random circuits are expected to be approximately Porter-Thomas, but no rigorous convergence proof existed. This work provides such a proof.

Method: The authors analyze the output probability distribution over a fixed bitstring of a local random quantum circuit built from nearest-neighbor Haar random gates. Their proof combines moment bounds from approximate designs, analytic estimates for characteristic functions, and a local anticoncentration property for inverse moments.

Result: For any m ≥ 0, the distribution corresponding to circuits of depth O(n^{2m+1} log n) is at most O(1/n^m) far from the Porter-Thomas distribution in total variation distance. This establishes inverse-polynomial convergence at polynomial depth.

Conclusion: Polynomial-depth brickwork random circuits converge inverse-polynomially in total variation distance to Porter-Thomas, providing a rigorous foundation for the Porter-Thomas assumption underlying random circuit sampling and quantum advantage demonstrations.

Abstract: Porter-Thomas statistics are a characteristic feature of the output distribution of random quantum states and, more broadly, chaotic quantum many-body systems. Convergence to Porter-Thomas plays a central role in random circuit sampling and experimental demonstrations of quantum advantage, where the output statistics of low-depth random quantum circuits are expected to be approximately Porter-Thomas, despite the absence of a rigorous proof of convergence. We show that the output distribution of polynomial-depth brickwork random circuits converges inverse-polynomially in total variation distance to the Porter-Thomas distribution. Specifically, consider the output probability distribution over a fixed bitstring of a local random quantum circuit, constructed from nearest-neighbor Haar random gates. Then, for any $m \geq 0$, the distribution corresponding to circuits of depth $O(n^{2m+1}\log(n))$ is at most $O(1/n^m)$ far in total variation distance from the Porter-Thomas distribution. Our proof uses moment bounds from approximate designs, analytic estimates for characteristic functions, and a local anticoncentration property for inverse moments.

</details>


### [144] [Spiking neural networks for streaming qubit readout](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02129)
*Barry M. Dillon,Aqib Javed,Jim Harkin,Patryk Dabkowski,Benjamin Lienhard*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Spiking neural network (SNN) discriminators enable streaming, low-latency superconducting qubit readout, outperforming matched filtering and approaching full-trace ANN accuracy on FPGA hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fast and accurate qubit-state assignment is essential for feedback, calibration, and error correction in quantum processors, but frequency-multiplexed readout makes the task inherently multivariate since measured traces encode crosstalk, relaxation events, and transient nonidealities not fully captured by conventional matched filtering.

Method: The authors introduce spiking neural network discriminators that process the measurement window in successive time chunks, exploiting temporal structure and updating classification scores as data arrive. They employ quantisation-aware training and hls4ml synthesis for FPGA deployment.

Result: The spiking networks outperform matched-filter discrimination and approach the accuracy of a full-trace artificial neural network, while providing a streaming, time-resolved estimate of the qubit state that evolves as the readout signal is acquired. Each FPGA inference update can be completed before the next readout chunk arrives.

Conclusion: Spiking neural networks establish a promising route to low-latency, real-time qubit readout on FPGA hardware, with broader implications for time-critical quantum-control and scientific-inference applications.

Abstract: Fast and accurate qubit-state assignment is essential for feedback, calibration, and error correction in quantum processors. In superconducting platforms, frequency-multiplexed readout makes this task intrinsically multivariate as measured traces can encode crosstalk, qubit-state relaxation events, and other transient nonidealities that are not fully captured by conventional matched filtering. Here, we introduce spiking neural network (SNN) discriminators for superconducting qubit readout. By processing the measurement window in successive time chunks, the networks exploit temporal structure and update classification scores as data arrive, rather than waiting until the end of the readout window. The spiking networks outperform matched-filter discrimination and approach the accuracy of a full-trace artificial neural network. Beyond reaching the performance of artificial neural networks, the key advantage of SNNs is that they provide a streaming, time-resolved estimate of the qubit state that evolves as the readout signal is acquired. Using quantisation-aware training and hls4ml synthesis, we further demonstrate that each FPGA inference update can be completed before the next readout chunk arrives. These results establish spiking neural networks as a promising route to low-latency, real-time qubit readout on FPGA hardware, with broader implications for time-critical quantum-control and scientific-inference applications.

</details>


### [145] [Quantum impurity models: easy at equilibrium, universal in motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02130)
*Srinivasan Arunachalam,Sergey Bravyi,Anirban Chowdhury,Arkopal Dutt,Alexandru Gheorghiu,Zhi Li*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies the computational complexity of quantum impurity models, proving a sharp dichotomy: equilibrium properties (ground energy, free energy, thermofield double state) are classically efficiently approximable, while time evolution is BQP-complete.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum impurity models describe a small interacting subsystem embedded in a large bath of free fermions. Understanding whether their static and dynamic properties can be efficiently computed classically versus requiring quantum computation is a fundamental question at the intersection of quantum many-body physics and computational complexity.

Method: The authors analyze a Hamiltonian H of an impurity model with n fermionic modes and a constant-size impurity. They develop classical algorithms exploiting exponential suppression of multi-particle bath excitations in a basis organized by energy scale and Krylov depth. For the universality result, they construct a stationary quantum processor whose program arrives in a stream of freely propagating fermions to realize BQP-completeness of time evolution.

Result: (1) The ground energy of H can be approximated to additive error ε by a classical algorithm with runtime poly(n, 1/ε), improving on the quasi-polynomial runtime of the best previously known algorithm. (2) At inverse temperature β, the Helmholtz free energy and a classical description of the thermofield double state can be computed to precision ε in time poly(n, β, 1/ε). (3) Simulating the time evolution e^{-iHt} is BQP-complete for time-independent H with fixed, constant impurity size.

Conclusion: The work reveals a sharp contrast in quantum impurity models: equilibrium properties are classically tractable, whereas time evolution can implement universal quantum computation. This establishes a complexity-theoretic separation between static and dynamical properties of these models.

Abstract: A quantum impurity model describes a small interacting subsystem embedded into a large bath of free fermions. Here we study the computational complexity of calculating the ground energy, thermal equilibrium, and dynamical properties of these models. Our work reveals a sharp contrast: equilibrium properties can be efficiently approximated by classical means, whereas time evolution can implement a universal quantum computation. More precisely, let $H$ be the Hamiltonian of an impurity model with $n$ fermionic modes and a constant-size impurity. We show that: (1) the ground energy of $H$ can be approximated to additive error $\varepsilon$ by a classical algorithm with runtime $\textsf{poly}(n,1/\varepsilon)$, improving on the quasi-polynomial runtime of the best previously known algorithm; (2) at inverse temperature $β$, the Helmholtz free energy and a classical description of the thermofield double state can be computed to precision $\varepsilon$ in time $\textsf{poly}(n,β,1/\varepsilon)$; (3) simulating the time evolution $e^{-iHt}$ is $\textsf{BQP}$-complete, for $H$ that is time-independent and has a fixed, constant impurity size. Our algorithms exploit exponential suppression of multi-particle bath excitations in a basis organized by energy scale and Krylov depth. Our universality construction realizes a stationary quantum processor whose program arrives in a stream of freely propagating fermions.

</details>


### [146] [Optimal transducers using symmetries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02133)
*Benoît Dubus,Julien Ladeuze,Jérémie Roland*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper shows that leveraging the symmetry group of state-conversion problems simplifies the construction of optimal quantum transducers, yielding optimal transducers with optimal constants for common primitives like unstructured search, amplitude amplification, and amplitude estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transducers are a powerful quantum algorithm design framework where optimal transduction complexity equals the adversary bound (Las Vegas complexity), and they compose exactly, limiting error-control overhead. However, constructing efficient or optimal transducers remains hard because it still requires solving the adversary SDP and explicitly constructing the unitary.

Method: Using the symmetry group of state-conversion problems, the authors (1) prove via a symmetrization argument that an optimal catalyst can always be chosen covariant under a representation of the symmetry group, and (2) prove the transducer intertwines two different group representations, so it can be chosen block diagonal in the isotypic decomposition of the Hilbert space.

Result: The methods yield optimal transducers with optimal constants for widely used quantum algorithmic primitives, including unstructured search, amplitude amplification, and amplitude estimation.

Conclusion: The work extends prior use of representation theory for computing adversary lower bounds (Høyer–Lee–Špalek 2007; Ambainis–Magnin–Roetteler–Roland 2011) to the systematic construction of optimal quantum algorithms.

Abstract: Transducers (Belovs, Jeffery and Yolcu, 2024) are a quantum computing framework describing a quantum algorithm as a unitary converting an input state into a target state using a catalyst, an auxiliary vector that is left unchanged. They are a powerful tool in quantum algorithm design, especially in the context of quantum query complexity: feasible points of the (dual) adversary semidefinite program directly translate into transducers and the optimal transduction complexity is equal to the adversary bound, i.e. the Las Vegas complexity, which is known to characterize bounded-error quantum query complexity. Moreover, contrary to bounded-error algorithms, transducers compose exactly, which limits overheads due to controlling errors in algorithms constructed by composition. Constructing efficient, let alone optimal, transducers in terms of quantum query complexity nevertheless remains a hard task since it still requires solving the adversary SDP and constructing the unitary to obtain an explicit algorithm. In this paper, we show how using the symmetry group of state-conversion problems simplifies both steps. First, using a symmetrization argument, we prove an optimal catalyst can always be chosen covariant under a representation of the symmetry group. Second, we prove that the transducer intertwines two different representations of the group and can thus be chosen block diagonal in the isotypic decomposition of the Hilbert space. Using those methods, we then derive optimal transducers, with optimal constants, for different widely used quantum algorithmic primitives, such as unstructured search, amplitude amplification and amplitude estimation. Our approach extends previous work on the use of representation theory to compute adversary lower bounds (Høyer, Lee, and {\v S}palek, 2007; Ambainis, Magnin, Roetteler and Roland, 2011) to the systematic construction of optimal algorithms.

</details>


### [147] [A Generalized quantum Stein lemma on von Neumann algebras](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02134)
*Li Gao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proves a generalized quantum Stein lemma: for i.i.d. normal states tested against convex, tensor-stable families on arbitrary von Neumann algebras, the worst-case type-II error exponent equals the regularized relative entropy with a strong converse, provided an alternative state with finite relative entropy from the null state exists.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum Stein's lemma characterizes optimal asymptotic hypothesis-testing error exponents. Extending it beyond the standard finite-dimensional or specific-algebra settings to arbitrary von Neumann algebras and general convex, tensor-stable alternative families broadens the applicability of the theory to more general quantum statistical models.

Method: The proof combines three ingredients: (1) the integral representation of relative entropy via hockey-stick divergences, (2) a modular testing bound, and (3) a convex minimax argument. The setting is i.i.d. normal states against convex, tensor-stable families on arbitrary von Neumann algebras.

Result: Assuming the existence of an alternative state with finite relative entropy from the null state, at every type-I error tolerance ε ∈ (0,1) the worst-case type-II error exponent is achieved with the regularized relative entropy, and a strong converse holds.

Conclusion: The generalized quantum Stein lemma holds in this broad von Neumann algebraic setting, with the regularized relative entropy governing the optimal achievable exponent and the strong converse property.

Abstract: We prove a generalized quantum Stein lemma for i.i.d. normal states against convex, tensor-stable families on arbitrary von Neumann algebras. Assuming the existence of an alternative state with finite relative entropy from the null state, we show that, at every type-I error tolerance $\varepsilon\in (0,1)$, the worst case type-II error exponent is achieved with the regularized relative entropy with a strong converse. The proof combines the integral representation of relative entropy by hockey-stick divergences, a modular testing bound, and convex minimax argument.

</details>


### [148] [Random Quantum Circuits Beyond Moment Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02135)
*Shih-Han Hung*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work quantifies how accurately random quantum circuits that form approximate unitary k-designs reproduce the full distribution of individual output probabilities, showing optimal Kolmogorov-distance bounds versus the Porter-Thomas distribution and exponential improvements under local invariance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Random quantum circuits are used to efficiently mimic ideal random quantum evolution, often via approximate unitary designs that match Haar-random moments up to order k. However, it was unclear how accurately such moment-matching designs reproduce the full distributions of individual output probabilities, not just their moments.

Method: The authors derive quantitative distributional guarantees for strong ε-approximate unitary k-designs on n qubits. They bound the Kolmogorov distance between each individual output probability distribution and the finite-dimensional Porter-Thomas distribution (a beta distribution with parameters 1 and 2^n−1). They then analyze designs with local invariance under unitary translations on log k + O(1) qubits, obtaining improved bounds in Kolmogorov and total variation distance. They also relate marginal-distribution accuracy to expected Shannon entropy.

Result: For every strong ε-approximate unitary k-design on n qubits, each output probability distribution lies within O(√(2^n/(k(k+2^n))) + ε) in Kolmogorov distance of the Porter-Thomas distribution, and this bound is optimal up to constant factors. Local invariance yields exponential improvement in k: error 2^{−Ω(k)} + O(ε) in Kolmogorov distance and 2^{−Ω(k)} + O(ε log(2/ε)) in total variation. As an application, if each output probability marginal is within η in Kolmogorov distance of its Haar counterpart, the expected Shannon entropy differs from its Haar value by O(√η).

Conclusion: Moment matching alone guarantees a distributional accuracy that is optimal up to constants, while additional structure—specifically local invariance—is required to substantially improve it. These results characterize both the distributional accuracy ensured by approximate unitary k-designs and the structure that enhances it, with implications for output entropy estimation.

Abstract: Random quantum circuits aim to efficiently reproduce the statistical properties of ideal random quantum evolution. One approach is to construct approximate unitary designs, which match the moments of Haar-random unitaries up to a prescribed order with controlled error. In this work, we establish quantitative guarantees for how accurately these designs reproduce the full distributions of individual output probabilities. We show that, for every strong $\varepsilon$-approximate unitary $k$-design on $n$ qubits, the distribution of each individual output probability is within $O\left(\sqrt{\frac{2^n}{k(k+2^n)}}+\varepsilon\right)$ in Kolmogorov distance of the finite-dimensional Porter-Thomas distribution, a beta distribution with parameters $1$ and $2^n-1$. This bound is optimal up to constant factors, implying that a substantially better uniform bound requires additional structure. We further show that local invariance yields an exponential improvement in the dependence on $k$, with guarantees also in the stronger metric of total variation: every strong $\varepsilon$-approximate unitary $k$-design invariant under unitary translations acting on $\log k+O(1)$ qubits achieves error $2^{-Ω(k)}+O(\varepsilon)$ in Kolmogorov distance and $2^{-Ω(k)}+O(\varepsilon\log(2/\varepsilon))$ in total variation distance. These results identify both the distributional accuracy guaranteed by moment matching and the additional structure that substantially improves it. As an application, we show that if the marginal distribution of each output probability of a strong approximate design is within Kolmogorov distance $η$ of its Haar counterpart, then the expected Shannon entropy of the output distribution differs from its Haar value by $O(\sqrtη)$.

</details>


### [149] [One-Shot any Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02137)
*Andrew C. Yuan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Any single-shot code is self-correcting; any [[n,k]] CSS QLDPC code can be converted into an [[nm,k]] CSS QLDPC code that achieves single-shot quantum error correction with an efficient O(log m)-parallel-time local decoder. Below a constant threshold p_RG under joint local stochastic physical and measurement noise, the logical failure probability over T rounds is O(Tn)exp[−Ω(m^α)]; if the input code has a single-shot decoder with threshold p_c and suppression Ω(n^β), the output enhances suppression to Ω(m^α n^β) while keeping the decoder-independent threshold p_RG, becoming "enhanced" when p_c < p_RG.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to establish that single-shot codes are inherently self-correcting and to provide a general transformation that equips arbitrary CSS QLDPC codes with single-shot quantum error correction and efficient local decoding.

Method: A simple proof shows any single-shot code is self-correcting. A transformation converts any [[n,k]] CSS QLDPC code into an [[nm,k]] CSS QLDPC code with single-shot correction and a local decoder running in O(log m) parallel time.

Result: Below a constant threshold p_RG>0 for joint local stochastic physical and measurement noise, logical failure probability over T correction rounds is bounded by O(Tn)exp[−Ω(m^α)] for constant α>0, giving threshold p_RG and error suppression Ω(m^α). If the input code has a single-shot decoder with threshold p_c>0 and suppression Ω(n^β), the output enhances suppression to Ω(m^α n^β), maintaining decoder-independent threshold p_RG, thus being "enhanced" if p_c < p_RG.

Conclusion: Single-shot codes are self-correcting, and the presented transformation universally upgrades CSS QLDPC codes to single-shot correctable codes with efficient local decoders, constant threshold, and improved error suppression, including enhancement over suitable input decoders.

Abstract: We first provide a simple proof showing that any single-shot code is self-correcting. We then show that any $[[n,k]]$ CSS quantum low-density parity-check (QLDPC) code can be transformed into an $[[nm,k]]$ CSS QLDPC code with single-shot (SS) quantum error correction and an efficient local decoder which runs in $O(\log m)$ parallel time. Below a constant threshold $p_{\rm RG}>0$ for joint local stochastic physical and measurement noise, the logical failure probability over $T$ correction rounds is bounded by \begin{equation*}
  O(T n)\exp[-Ω(m^α)] \end{equation*} for a constant $α>0$; we say that the \textit{threshold} is $p_{\rm RG}$ and \textit{error suppression} is $Ω(m^α)$. Moreover, if the input code is equipped with a single-shot decoder with threshold $p_c>0$ and error suppression $Ω(n^β)$, then the output code \textit{enhances} the error suppression to $Ω(m^α n^β)$, while maintaining the decoder-independent threshold $p_{\rm RG}$, and thus \textit{enhanced} if $p_c < p_{\rm RG}$.

</details>


### [150] [A provable quantum advantage for approximate optimization via decoded quantum interferometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02145)
*Maximilian J. Kramer,Elies Gil-Fuster,Benjamin D. M. Jones,Jens Eisert,Franz J. Schreiber*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work establishes a provable quantum advantage for Decoded Quantum Interferometry (DQI) over all polynomial-time classical algorithms in an oracle setting, using a task called folded optimal polynomial intersection (folded OPI).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DQI offers strong performance guarantees for approximate optimization by exploiting the duality between optimization and coding theory, but it was previously unclear whether it could provably outperform all polynomial-time classical algorithms. This paper addresses that central question.

Method: The authors study folded OPI, an optimization task with randomly chosen acceptance sets accessed via membership oracles. Their proof builds on Jordan et al.'s DQI framework, extends the classical lower-bound method behind Yamakawa and Zhandry's exact-search oracle separation to the approximation regime, and—drawing on work by Sun and Wootters, Horinaga and Yamakawa, and Jo—constructs a modified DQI algorithm.

Result: A strict gap is established between the approximation ratio of any polynomial-time classical algorithm and that of DQI. The modified DQI algorithm yields an even larger gap. Concretely, for code rate 0.3, DQI and the modified algorithm achieve expected scores of roughly 0.85 and 0.95, whereas exceeding the classical threshold of 0.65 by any fixed amount with constant probability requires super-polynomially many classical membership queries.

Conclusion: DQI provably outperforms all polynomial-time classical algorithms on folded OPI in the oracle setting, and a modified variant achieves an even stronger quantum separation.

Abstract: Decoded quantum interferometry (DQI) is a novel paradigm for tackling approximate optimization problems on quantum computers. This framework comes with strong performance guarantees and exploits a well-established duality between optimization and coding theory. A central question, however, is whether DQI can actually provably outperform all polynomial-time classical algorithms. In this work, we establish such an advantage in an oracle setting: we consider an optimization task called folded optimal polynomial intersection (folded OPI), where the acceptance sets are chosen randomly and accessed through membership oracles. We establish a strict gap between the approximation ratio achievable by any polynomial-time classical algorithm and the approximation ratio achieved by the DQI algorithm. Our proof builds on Jordan et al.'s DQI framework for approximate optimization and extends the classical lower-bound method underlying Yamakawa and Zhandry's exact-search oracle separation to approximation. Building on recent developments by Sun and Wootters, Horinaga and Yamakawa, and Jo, we further show that a modified version of the DQI algorithm achieves a strictly larger gap on the folded OPI problem, yielding an even stronger quantum separation. As a concrete example, for code rate $0.3$, DQI and the modified algorithm achieve expected scores of approximately $0.85$ and $0.95$, respectively. In contrast, exceeding the classical threshold of $0.65$ by any fixed amount with constant probability on sampled instances requires super-polynomially many classical membership queries.

</details>


### [151] [Polynomial-time additive-error estimation of output probabilities for shallow quantum circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02146)
*Matthew Coudron,Michael J. Gullans,Jon Nelson,Joel Rajakumar,Shi Jie Samuel Tan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A deterministic polynomial-time classical algorithm estimates output probabilities of constant-depth bounded-fan-in quantum circuits with arbitrary connectivity, improving over previous quasi-polynomial and circuit-specific results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior classical simulation algorithms for estimating |⟨x|U|0^n⟩|^2 in constant-depth circuits required super-polynomial time: n^{O(log n)} in general, n^{O(log log n)} for geometrically local circuits, and n^{O(1)} only for 2D geometrically-local circuits. The goal is to achieve efficient poly(n, 1/ε) estimation under the most general connectivity.

Method: A deterministic classical algorithm that estimates the squared overlap |⟨x|U|0^n⟩|^2 to additive error ε, running in poly(n, 1/ε) time, for constant-depth quantum circuits with bounded fan-in gates and arbitrary connectivity.

Result: The algorithm achieves poly(n, 1/ε) time, improving over the prior state of the art: n^{O(log n)} general, n^{O(log log n)} for geometrically local, and n^{O(1)}-only-for-2D-local circuits.

Conclusion: Constant-depth bounded-fan-in circuits with arbitrary connectivity admit deterministic polynomial-time classical estimation of output probabilities, substantially narrowing the regime where such circuits evade efficient classical simulation.

Abstract: We give a deterministic classical algorithm that estimates $|\langle x|U|0^n\rangle|^2$ to additive error $\varepsilon$ in $\mathrm{poly}(n, 1/\varepsilon)$ time, where $U$ is a constant-depth quantum circuit comprised of gates with bounded fan-in and arbitrary connectivity, and $x$ is an arbitrary $n$-bit output string. This improves over prior state-of-the-art algorithms that takes $n^{O(log(n))}$ time for the same task, $n^{O(log(log(n))}$ when $U$ is geometrically local, and $n^{O(1)}$ for 2D geometrically-local circuits.

</details>


### [152] [The Robustness of QAC0](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02154)
*Daniel Grier,Jackson Morris,Kewen Wu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies the robustness of QAC^0, showing that its inherent error can be eliminated via exact amplitude amplification, enabling exact simulation of TC^0 and computation of total Boolean functions outside AC^0[p]; it also shows QAC^0 is robust to gate-set restrictions, being efficiently approximable by circuits using only generalized Toffoli, S, and Hadamard gates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work is motivated by two questions about the robustness of QAC^0: (1) whether the non-zero error typically allowed for QAC^0 circuits computing Boolean functions is truly necessary, and (2) to what extent the computational power of QAC^0 depends on the availability of arbitrary single-qubit gates at any point in the circuit.

Method: For the first question, the inherent error in the parallel W-test is eliminated entirely via a novel application of exact amplitude amplification in the many-copies context. For the second question, the paper analyzes gate-set restrictions by constructing approximating circuits.

Result: QAC^0 can exactly simulate TC^0 with polynomially many copies of the classical input; for every fixed prime p, exact QAC^0 (EQAC^0) can compute total Boolean functions outside AC^0[p]. Additionally, every QAC^0 circuit can be approximately implemented by a QAC^0 circuit consisting solely of generalized Toffoli, S, and Hadamard gates, and this approximating circuit can be constructed efficiently from a classical description of the original circuit.

Conclusion: QAC^0 is robust with respect to error tolerance and modifications to its gate-set: the error usually permitted is unnecessary, and arbitrary single-qubit gates can be restricted to generalized Toffoli, S, and Hadamard gates without substantial loss of power, with efficient classical construction of the approximating circuit.

Abstract: In this work we study the robustness of $\mathsf{QAC}^0$ with respect to error tolerance and modifications to its gate-set. First, we investigate whether the non-zero error typically allowed for $\mathsf{QAC}^0$ circuits computing Boolean functions is truly necessary. We show that the error inherent in the parallel $W$-test of \cite{grier_morris_wu} can be eliminated entirely via a novel application of exact amplitude amplification in the many-copies context. Consequently, we find that $\mathsf{QAC}^0$ can \textit{exactly} simulate $\mathsf{TC}^0$ with polynomially many copies of the classical input and that for every fixed prime $p$ exact $\mathsf{QAC}^0$, $\mathsf{EQAC}^0$, can compute total Boolean functions outside of $\mathsf{AC}^0[p]$.
  Second, we ask to what extent the computational power of $\mathsf{QAC}^0$ follows from the fact that arbitrary single-qubit gates may be used at any point in the circuit. We find that $\mathsf{QAC}^0$ is in fact robust to restrictions on which single-qubit gates are permitted: every $\mathsf{QAC}^0$ circuit can be approximately implemented by a $\mathsf{QAC}^0$ circuit consisting of just generalized Toffoli, $S$, and Hadamard gates. Moreover, this approximating circuit can be constructed efficiently from a classical description of the original circuit.

</details>


### [153] [Learning Many-Body Hamiltonians Using a Local Probe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02157)
*Suying Liu,Zitai Xu,Alexey V. Gorshkov,Xiaodi Wu,Yu-Xin Wang,Zhi-Yuan Wei*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work shows that a single measurable qubit, combined with fast single-qubit control and a connected reference backbone, is sufficient to learn all O(N) independent parameters of a bounded-degree two-body Hamiltonian on N qubits at the Heisenberg limit, using robust SWAP gates via quantum signal processing and a parallel learning architecture achieving Õ(N) total query time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Hamiltonian learning protocols typically assume direct measurement access to the entire system, which limits scalability. The authors seek a scalable route to learning an extensive number of many-body Hamiltonian parameters through only a local measurement interface.

Method: The protocol uses fast single-qubit control and a connected reference backbone so a single measurable qubit suffices. It employs robust SWAP gates synthesized by quantum signal processing to coherently transfer states evolving under distant Hamiltonian parameters to the measurable qubit, requiring no prior calibration of intermediate link parameters. A parallel learning architecture is developed, and the framework extends to arbitrary bounded-degree interaction graphs.

Result: A single measurable qubit learns all O(N) independent parameters of a bounded-degree two-body Hamiltonian on N qubits at the Heisenberg limit. On an N-qubit chain, total query time is Õ(N) while retaining Heisenberg-limited precision scaling, matching fundamental precision and information-propagation lower bounds up to logarithmic factors (with extension to arbitrary bounded-degree interaction graphs).

Conclusion: The results establish a scalable route to learning an extensive number of many-body Hamiltonian parameters through only a local measurement interface.

Abstract: Hamiltonian learning provides a systematic framework for reconstructing unknown quantum dynamics. However, existing protocols typically assume direct measurement access to the entire system. With fast single-qubit control and a connected reference backbone, we show that a single measurable qubit suffices to learn all $O(N)$ independent parameters of a bounded-degree two-body Hamiltonian on $N$ qubits at the Heisenberg limit. Crucially, our protocol uses robust SWAP gates synthesized by quantum signal processing, enabling coherent transfer of states evolving under distant Hamiltonian parameters to the measurable qubit. This transfer requires no prior calibration of the Hamiltonian parameters of the intermediate links. A parallel learning architecture achieves total query time $\widetilde{O}(N)$ for an $N$-qubit chain, while retaining Heisenberg-limited precision scaling. On the chain, these scalings match the fundamental precision and information-propagation lower bounds up to logarithmic factors. The framework further extends to arbitrary bounded-degree interaction graphs. Our results establish a scalable route to learning an extensive number of many-body Hamiltonian parameters through only a local measurement interface.

</details>


### [154] [Polynomial-time classical and quantum simulation of quantum impurity models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02167)
*Jiaqing Jiang,Nathan Ju,Ojas Parekh,Chaithanya Rayudu,Andrew Zhao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper maps the classical/quantum complexity boundary for quantum impurity models: static properties (ground-state energy, partition function) are classically tractable in polynomial time, while dynamical properties (nonequilibrium Green's functions) are classically hard but quantumly easy, capturing full quantum computational power even at finite temperature.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum impurity models are paradigmatic for interacting quantum matter and serve as key primitives in modern electronic-structure methods, describing a small interacting fermionic subsystem coupled to a large noninteracting bath. Despite their importance, the boundary between classical and quantum tractability for simulating them was not well delineated, with prior ground-state energy algorithms only achieving quasipolynomial complexity and no rigorous polynomial-time guarantees for thermal equilibrium simulation.

Method: The authors perform a comprehensive computational-complexity study of simulating impurity models, giving classical algorithms for static properties and complexity-theoretic arguments (hardness constructions) for dynamical properties, alongside quantum algorithms for the latter.

Result: Static properties are classically efficient: (1) ground-state energy estimated to additive precision δ in poly(n, δ⁻¹) time, improving prior quasipolynomial bounds to polynomial; (2) partition function at inverse temperature β to relative precision δ in poly(n, β, δ⁻¹) time—the first rigorous polynomial-time guarantees for thermal-equilibrium simulation. Dynamical properties are classically hard but quantumly easy: computing nonequilibrium Green's functions captures the full power of quantum computation, even at finite temperature.

Conclusion: Static properties admit no superpolynomial quantum speedup, whereas simulating impurity physics out of equilibrium offers a viable avenue for quantum advantage.

Abstract: Quantum impurity models are paradigmatic models of interacting quantum matter, as well as key computational primitives for modern electronic-structure methods. They describe a small subsystem of interacting fermions coupled to a large, noninteracting bath. We perform a comprehensive study of the computational complexity of simulating impurity models, delineating the boundary between classical and quantum tractability for this class of problems. Our main finding is that static properties of quantum impurity models can be calculated efficiently on a classical computer. Specifically, we give classical algorithms that (1) estimate the ground-state energy to additive precision $δ$ in time $\mathrm{poly}(n,δ^{-1})$, and (2) estimate the partition function at inverse temperature $β$ to relative precision $δ$ in time $\mathrm{poly}(n,β,δ^{-1})$, where $n$ is the system size. These results improve the previous best-known complexity for ground-state energy estimation from quasipolynomial to polynomial time, while establishing for the first time rigorous polynomial-time guarantees for simulating impurity models in thermal equilibrium. On the other hand, we find that simulating dynamical properties of impurity models is hard for classical computers but easy on a quantum computer. As a canonical example, we show that computing their nonequilibrium Green's functions captures the full power of quantum computation, even at finite temperature. Taken together, our results rule out superpolynomial quantum speedups for computing static properties, but provide an avenue for quantum advantage in simulating impurity physics out of equilibrium.

</details>


### [155] [Robust exponential lower bounds for fermionic and bosonic Gaussian ranks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02172)
*Fuchuan Wei,Kong-Wing Wu,Zhengwei Liu,Zi-Wen Liu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper establishes robust exponential lower bounds on Gaussian rank for bosonic and fermionic systems, proving that any pure non-Gaussian state has tensor powers whose approximate border Gaussian rank grows at least exponentially, thereby limiting decomposition-based classical simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coherent decomposition into classically tractable free states is a leading paradigm for classical simulation of quantum systems, where the decomposition rank sets simulation complexity. Proving strong lower bounds on this rank is notoriously difficult, as in the qubit stabilizer rank problem. The Gaussian analogue of this problem in bosonic and fermionic systems lacked robust lower bounds.

Method: The proofs combine a reduction to four modes with Majorana spectral bounds for fermions, and Gaussian postselection with an entropy-based rank bound for bosons.

Result: For every pure non-Gaussian state on finitely many modes (with definite parity in the fermionic case), the approximate border Gaussian rank of its tensor powers grows at least exponentially at any fixed norm error below one. Explicit exponential lower bounds are derived for the four-mode fermionic GHZ state and the bosonic single-photon state; the bosonic results require no energy assumption on the target state.

Conclusion: Non-Gaussianity universally entails exponential Gaussian decomposition complexity, establishing fundamental limitations on decomposition-based classical simulation of bosonic and fermionic systems.

Abstract: The power and limitations of classical simulation are central to understanding quantum computational advantages. A leading simulation paradigm is based on coherent decomposition into classically tractable free states where the decomposition rank determines the simulation complexity. Proving strong lower bounds on this number is a notoriously difficult and mathematically rich problem, as exemplified by the qubit stabilizer rank problem. Here, we study the Gaussian version of this problem in both bosonic and fermionic systems and establish robust exponential lower bounds on Gaussian rank. In particular, we prove that for every pure non-Gaussian state on finitely many modes, with definite parity in the fermionic case, the approximate border Gaussian rank of its tensor powers grows at least exponentially at any fixed norm error below one. Our proofs combine reduction to four modes with Majorana spectral bounds for fermions, and Gaussian postselection with an entropy-based rank bound for bosons. As concrete examples, we derive explicit exponential lower bounds for the four-mode fermionic GHZ state and the bosonic single-photon state. The bosonic results require no assumption on the energy of the target state. Our results show that non-Gaussianity universally entails exponential Gaussian decomposition complexity, setting fundamental limitations on decomposition-based classical simulation of bosonic and fermionic systems.

</details>


### [156] [From Permutation Symmetry to Communication Bounds and Additivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02176)
*Zahra Baghali Khanian,Debbie Leung,Graeme Smith*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Permutation invariance eliminates the asymptotic advantage of correlations across channel uses: the optimized coherent information per use converges to the single-use maximum, while superadditivity at finite block lengths remains possible.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Correlations across channel uses can boost quantum communication rates, so determining quantum capacity requires optimization over arbitrarily large blocks—a central difficulty. The paper aims to show how symmetry (full permutation invariance) constrains this advantage for all finite-dimensional memoryless channels.

Method: The authors exploit full permutation invariance and De Finetti reduction within a shared variational framework, combined with permutation covariance, to analyze optimized coherent information per use, pure-source entanglement generation, completely bounded norms, sandwiched Rényi channel quantities, and output entropy under permutation-invariant inputs.

Result: The optimized coherent information per use converges to the single-use maximum and attains its supremum at finite block length, so finite-block superadditivity persists while the asymptotic optimization reduces to a single channel use. A strong converse is established for pure-source entanglement generation with permutation-invariant inputs: above the single-use coherent-information maximum, fidelity tends to zero. A common structure is identified underlying completely bounded norms and sandwiched Rényi channel quantities, yielding systematic proofs of their multiplicativity and additivity. For permutation-invariant inputs, correlations cannot asymptotically reduce output entropy per use below the single-use minimum, for Rényi orders greater than one and von Neumann entropy.

Conclusion: Permutation invariance limits the benefit of inter-use correlations, reducing asymptotic quantum-capacity optimization to a single channel use and extending the role of symmetry from communication bounds to controlling output entropy.

Abstract: Correlations across channel uses can improve quantum communication rates, making optimization over arbitrarily large blocks a central difficulty in determining quantum capacity. We show how full permutation invariance limits this advantage for every finite-dimensional memoryless channel. The optimized coherent information per use converges to the single-use maximum and consequently attains its supremum at a finite block length. Thus finite-block superadditivity remains possible, while the asymptotic optimization reduces to a single channel use. We also establish a strong converse for pure-source entanglement generation with permutation-invariant inputs: at any fixed rate above the single-use coherent-information maximum, the fidelity tends to zero. Beyond these communication bounds, we identify a common structure underlying completely bounded norms and the sandwiched Rényi channel quantities governing discrimination against a replacer and entanglement-assisted communication. De Finetti reduction and permutation covariance yield systematic alternative proofs of their known multiplicativity and additivity results within a shared variational framework. We further show that, for permutation-invariant inputs, correlations cannot sustain an asymptotic reduction in output entropy per use below the single-use minimum. This entropy limit holds for Rényi orders greater than one and for the von Neumann entropy, extending the role of symmetry from communication bounds to the control of output entropy.

</details>


### [157] [Learning SYK Hamiltonians](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02178)
*Anurag Anshu,Srinivasan Arunachalam,Sitan Chen,Yeongwoo Hwang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies learning the dense SYK Hamiltonian from Gibbs state copies and shows that, despite the high interaction overlap that defeats locality-based methods, the random mean-field structure enables efficient learning: polynomially many samples suffice for inverse-polynomial accuracy at constant temperature, and a quasipolynomial-time algorithm is constructed for sufficiently small constant inverse temperature.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Hamiltonian learning algorithms rely on geometric locality or bounded interaction degree, so they do not apply to the SYK model, where each quartic interaction overlaps with Θ(n^3) others. The paper aims to overcome this obstruction by exploiting the random mean-field structure of the model.

Method: The paper exploits the random mean-field structure of the SYK model rather than geometric locality or bounded degree. At any constant temperature it analyzes learning from copies of the Gibbs state, proving that with high probability over the SYK couplings the entire Hamiltonian can be learned to inverse-polynomial accuracy. For sufficiently small constant inverse temperature, it constructs a quasipolynomial-time learning algorithm that is qualitatively different from the sample-efficient algorithm.

Result: At any constant temperature, with high probability over the SYK couplings, the entire Hamiltonian can be learned to inverse-polynomial accuracy using polynomially many samples. When the inverse temperature is restricted to a sufficiently small constant, a quasipolynomial-time learning algorithm is constructed, which is qualitatively different from the sample-efficient algorithm.

Conclusion: The high-overlap obstruction of the dense SYK Hamiltonian can be overcome by leveraging its random mean-field structure. Learning is sample-efficient at constant temperature, achieving inverse-polynomial accuracy with polynomially many Gibbs state copies, and a distinct quasipolynomial-time algorithm exists for sufficiently small constant inverse temperature.

Abstract: We study the problem of learning the dense Sachdev--Ye--Kitaev (SYK) Hamiltonian from copies of its Gibbs state. Existing algorithms for Hamiltonian learning typically rely on geometric locality or bounded interaction degree and therefore do not apply to SYK, where each quartic interaction overlaps with $Θ(n^3)$ others. We show that this obstruction can be overcome by exploiting the random mean-field structure of the model. At any constant temperature, we prove that with high probability over the SYK couplings, the entire Hamiltonian can be learned to inverse-polynomial accuracy using polynomially many samples. Furthermore, when the inverse temperature is restricted to be a sufficiently small constant, we construct a quasipolynomial-time learning algorithm which is qualitatively different from the sample-efficient algorithm.

</details>


### [158] [Single-Particle Spectral Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02183)
*Adrian Chapman,Charles Derby,Steven T. Flammia,Yeongwoo Hwang,Joel Klassen,Calum McCartney*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies Hamiltonian learning when a non-interacting structure is obfuscated by an unknown global unitary. It proves this problem is DQC1-hard and introduces SPICES, an efficient method combining a Hadamard test with classical postprocessing to recover the spectral distribution in Wasserstein distance under natural assumptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning the weights of a Hamiltonian whose non-interacting structure is hidden by a global unitary U is well-motivated by materials modeling, where such Hamiltonians underpin classical methods like mean-field theory, Green's functions, and density functional theory. The goal is to recover the energies E_i without reconstructing U, exploiting the promise of an underlying non-interacting structure.

Method: The paper first establishes the computational hardness of the problem, showing it is DQC1-hard despite the apparent simplification from the non-interacting structure. It then introduces SPICES (single-particle inference via contour estimators), which combines a Hadamard test with a novel classical postprocessing procedure. The method exploits the correspondence between single-particle energies of individual quasiparticles and the Fourier frequencies governing the zeros of the partition function at complex inverse temperature.

Result: SPICES efficiently recovers the spectral distribution in Wasserstein distance under certain natural assumptions, overcoming the DQC1-hardness obstacle for the practically relevant spectral task.

Conclusion: Since quasiparticle spectra feature ubiquitously in downstream materials calculations, and SPICES is simple and practical to execute, it is expected to be a promising route to quantum applications in materials simulation.

Abstract: We study Hamiltonian learning in a novel setting, where we are promised a physical structure that is obfuscated by a global unitary. In particular, we consider the task of learning the weights $E_i$ of a Hamiltonian $H=U^{\dagger}(\sum_{i \in I} E_i n_i + H_O)U$ comprised of a set of non-interacting modes $I$ and an arbitrary ``spectator'' $H_O$ acting on a disjoint set of modes $O$. The non-interacting structure is hidden by an unknown global unitary $U$, and our goal is to recover the $E_i$ without reconstructing $U$. Such Hamiltonians are well-motivated in the setting of materials modeling, where they find application for classical methods such as mean-field theory, Green's functions, and density functional theory. We show that this problem is DQC1-hard, despite the apparent simplification given by the non-interacting structure. We then introduce SPICES (single-particle inference via contour estimators), which combines a Hadamard test with a novel classical postprocessing procedure to efficiently recover the spectral distribution in Wasserstein distance under certain natural assumptions. The method exploits the correspondence between single-particle energies of the individual quasiparticles and the Fourier frequencies governing zeros of the partition function at complex inverse temperature. As quasiparticle spectra feature ubiquitously in downstream materials calculations, and with SPICES being simple and practical to execute, we expect that SPICES is a promising route to quantum applications in materials simulation.

</details>
